# What a GB behind-the-meter BESS model must contain

*Derived from the literature, independently of any implementation.*

## 0. How to use this checklist, and how it was built

This is an **audit instrument**. Each row states something a correct GB behind-the-meter
battery model must, should, or might do, with the source that establishes it and the error
that follows from omitting it. An implementation is audited against it **row by row**: an
item the pipeline does not satisfy is either implemented, or recorded as a known limitation
that caps the confidence band. It is never silently absent.

**It was built repo-blind, deliberately.** The surveying agents were forbidden from reading
this codebase, any of its documentation, or any prior analysis. The reason is confirmation
bias: a reviewer who can see the existing implementation tends to write a checklist that
ratifies what was built and stays silent about what was omitted — precisely 
the failure this
document exists to prevent. Every requirement therefore describes what the *literature*
requires, with no knowledge of what we happen to have.

**Obligation levels** are derived from the strength of the evidence: `settled` in the
literature → **MUST**; `majority-view` → **SHOULD**; `contested` or `emerging` →
**CONSIDER**. The mapping is stated so it can be challenged.

### Coverage and its gap

- **386 distinct requirements** and **511 sources**, from
  14 independent research lenses plus a critic panel and a 
gap-filling second sweep.
- **The degradation lens did not complete** (an API failure mid-run). 
Section C is therefore
  populated only by degradation requirements that surfaced incidentally in 
other lenses, and
  is **known-incomplete**. Cycle-life, calendar ageing, rainflow counting, warranty
  throughput caps and augmentation economics need a dedicated pass before 
this checklist can
  be called complete.
- Three critic angles and one gap-filling sweep completed; three further gap batches were
  lost to a usage limit, so the second sweep is partial.

---

## 1. The checklist

### A. Data requirements and admission

#### A-01 · MUST · Require a full year of real, half-hourly site load data as an input gate; do not synthesise or annualise from a shorter sample without declaring it and testing sensitivity.

- **Why:** Every BTM value stream (self-consumption, peak shaving against capacity charges, DUoS red-band avoidance, the headroom left over for ancillary services) is driven by the shape of the site load, not its total. Site-specific shape cannot be recovered from an annual kWh figure or a standard profile.
- **Error if omitted:** The peak-shaving and self-consumption lines are fabricated. Because the capacity-charge saving depends on the single highest half-hour in each billing period, a smoothed or synthesised profile systematically understates the peak and therefore the achievable saving, while overstating the residual headroom available to sell as ancillary capacity.
- **How to implement:** Input contract: a 17,520-row (or 17,568 in a leap year) half-hourly kWh series aligned to GB settlement periods and clock changes, with gaps flagged rather than interpolated. If a synthesised profile is used, run the MILP on both the synthesised and any available real partial data, and report the divergence in peak-shaving revenue as a model risk. Note that Market-wide Half Hourly Settlement makes real half-hourly data increasingly obtainable for GB sites.
- *Source:* Mariaud, A. et al., 'Integrated optimisation of photovoltaic and battery storage systems for UK commercial buildings', Applied Energy 199:466-478, 2017 (UK commercial building MILP driven by measured demand profiles), DOI 10.1016/j.apenergy.2017.04.067
- *Source:* Fioriti, D., Pellegrino, L., Lutzemberger, G., Micolano, E., Poli, D., 'Optimal sizing of residential battery systems with multi-year dynamics and a novel rainflow-based model of storage degradation', Electric Power Systems Research 203:107675, 2021 (399 real measured load profiles at 15-minute resolution over multi-year horizons), DOI 10.1016/j.epsr.2021.107675

#### A-02 · MUST · Model the delivered-cost stack as a time-varying forward path with the correct charging-year and tariff-period boundaries, not a single flat annual number held constant across the asset life.

- **Why:** Every component steps on a different calendar: DUoS and TNUoS on 1 April (TNUoS final tariffs published late January); BSUoS on 1 April and 1 October; CCL on 1 April; RO obligation year 1 April–31 March; CfD ILR and Nuclear RAB quarterly; CM delivery year 1 October–30 September. And the steps are large: the TNUoS demand residual rose from £3,836.05m (2025/26) to £6,265.17m (2026/27), +63%, with the NGED-area HV2 TNUoS residual going from £62.80 to £117.15 per site per day (+87%).
- **Error if omitted:** A flat-tariff model gets both the level and the direction of the cost stack wrong. Because the growing components are the NON-AVOIDABLE ones (fixed residuals) while the avoidable one (DUoS red-band spread) is comparatively static, holding tariffs flat systematically overstates the battery's relative value over time.
- **How to implement:** Represent charges as a table keyed by (component, effective_from, effective_to, region, band) and resolve per settlement period. Underwriting cases must include a tariff-escalation scenario set: at minimum P50 = published + CPI, P90 = published + observed 3-year CAGR of the residual components, with the residual escalation applied as a cost, not a saving.
- *Source:* NESO, January 2026 Final TNUoS Tariffs for 2026/27, Tables 8 and 10, https://www.neso.energy/document/376336/download
- *Source:* NESO, January 2025 Final TNUoS Tariffs for 2025/26, Table 10, https://www.neso.energy/document/353086/download
- *Source:* NESO, BSUoS Fixed Tariff 6, December 2024 (six-monthly tariff periods; CMP408/CMP415 move to a 12-month fixed price period with 3 months' notice, published each December), https://www.neso.energy/document/350411/download

#### A-03 · MUST · Model distribution line loss factors on the import volume, and recognise that the settled (and therefore charged) volume differs from the metered volume for wholesale/BSUoS purposes.

- **Why:** Each DNO publishes a Schedule of line loss factors (NGED publishes a 'Revised Annex 5 — Schedule of adjustment factors to be made for Distribution Losses' each year). Metered site consumption is grossed up by the LLF for the class when allocated at the GSP, so the volume on which the supplier incurs wholesale, BSUoS and levy cost is larger than the site's metered kWh, and this differential is passed through in supply contracts.
- **Error if omitted:** Systematically understates the cost of every imported kWh by the LLF uplift (typically ~1–8% depending on voltage and LLFC), which compounds directly onto the battery's charging losses. Understates the round-trip loss penalty by the same proportion.
- **How to implement:** settled_import[t] = metered_import[t] × LLF[llfc, period(t)]. Apply wholesale, BSUoS and levy rates to settled_import where the contract passes losses through; apply DUoS unit rates to metered import (DUoS unit charges are levied on metered kWh). Require the LLFC and the DNO's LLF table as model inputs.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, section 4 'Schedule of line loss factors' and Annex 5, https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677
- *Source:* National Grid, Charging statements and methodology (Revised Annex 5 — Schedule of adjustment factors for Distribution Losses, per licence area), https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/our-network/use-of-system-charges/charging-statements-and-methodology

#### A-04 · MUST · Use half-hourly settlement-period granularity aligned to UK clock time with 46/50-period days at the DST transitions, and reconcile the modelled annual bill to an actual invoice before producing any P50.

- **Why:** All GB charges in this lens are defined on settlement periods in UK clock time. NGED's time-band table states explicitly 'All the above times are in UK Clock time'. DST transition days have 46 and 50 settlement periods. A model that runs on 17,520 UTC periods will misallocate red/amber/green bands and misalign Triad and CM peak windows.
- **Error if omitted:** One-period band misallocation across the seven BST months mis-prices roughly 30% of red half-hours. DST-day period-count errors corrupt annual energy totals and any per-period indexing. Without an invoice reconciliation there is no evidence the stack is right at all.
- **How to implement:** Index on (settlement_date, settlement_period) per BSC convention, with period counts 46/48/50. Provide a mandatory validation step: rebuild the customer's historic annual bill from the model's tariff stack and baseline HH data, and require agreement within a stated tolerance (e.g. ±2% on total, ±5% on each named component) before any savings case is produced.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, Annex 1 notes ('All the above times are in UK Clock time'), https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677
- *Source:* Elexon, Balancing and Settlement Code — BSC Guidance Notes, https://www.elexon.co.uk/bsc/bsc-and-codes/bsc-guidance-notes/

#### A-05 · MUST · Require and validate the full set of site-specific charging inputs before running; refuse to produce a lender-grade output on defaults.

- **Why:** Every number in this lens is keyed to site-specific identifiers that cannot be inferred: DNO licence area, LLFC / DUoS Tariff ID, TCR residual band, MIC (kVA), TNUoS demand zone, connection voltage, presence and capacity of an export MPAN, EII/CCA certification status, and the supply contract's per-component pass-through terms. NESO states residual band mapping is published in the 'Schedule of charges and other tables' spreadsheet alongside 'the mapping between the DUoS Tariff name and TNUOS site charging band'.
- **Error if omitted:** Defaults silently substitute an average site. Given that the HH TNUoS locational tariff varies from £0.00 to £14.13/kW across zones, the DUoS red rate from 2.837 to 7.777 p/kWh across voltages in a single DNO, and the TNUoS residual from £5.80 to £5,698/day across bands, a defaulted model can be wrong by an order of magnitude on the network component and no reviewer will see it.
- **How to implement:** Mandatory input schema: {dno_id, llfc, duos_tariff_id, tcr_band, mic_kva, mec_kva, tnuos_demand_zone, connection_voltage, has_export_mpan, eii_certificate, cca_holder, contract_passthrough_map, hh_import_kwh[], hh_export_kwh[], hh_reactive_kvarh[]}. Hard-fail on any missing field. Cross-check tcr_band against llfc using NESO's published DUoS-tariff-to-TNUoS-band mapping table and raise an error on mismatch.
- *Source:* NESO, January 2026 Final TNUoS Tariffs for 2026/27, para on Demand Residual Tariffs ('the mapping between the DUoS Tariff name and TNUOS site charging band'), https://www.neso.energy/document/376336/download
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, paras 2.28–2.32 and 2.57–2.58, https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677

#### A-06 · MUST · Split capex into a power-related component (£/kW) and an energy-related component (£/kWh) plus fixed site costs, and never scale total capex linearly with either MW or MWh alone.

- **Why:** The economics of duration selection depend entirely on this split. Modo's GB capex projections give 2-hour systems at £618/kW and 4-hour at £828/kW (2025 real), which decomposes to approximately £408/kW of power-related cost plus £105/kWh of energy-related cost. A model that scales capex on £/kWh alone will price a 4-hour system at twice a 2-hour system's cost when the market price ratio is 1.34.
- **Error if omitted:** Duration optimisation becomes meaningless. A £/kWh-only model will always choose the shortest duration; a £/kW-only model will always choose the longest. Both answers are wrong, and duration is one of the two decisions the model exists to make.
- **How to implement:** Capex = FixedSiteCost + PowerCost_per_kW x P_kW + EnergyCost_per_kWh x E_kWh. GB 2025 calibration derived from Modo's published points: PowerCost ~£408/kW, EnergyCost ~£105/kWh (arithmetic derivation from 2h £618/kW and 4h £828/kW: 2 x EnergyCost = 828-618 = £210/kW so EnergyCost = £105/kWh; PowerCost = 618 - 210 = £408/kW). 2030 projection: 2h £411/kW, 4h £610/kW implying ~£99.5/kWh energy and ~£212/kW power. Cross-check the total against the GB survey benchmark of £580k/MW average at 1.9h average duration. For sub-5 MW BTM, add a scale penalty: the GB survey explicitly finds larger projects achieve lower per-MW cost, and the survey population is grid-scale, so the £580k/MW figure is a floor not a central estimate for a 0.1-5 MW site.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Modelling Assumptions' (UK/Europe, Jul 2026 vintage) — BESS capex 2h £618/kW (2025) to £411/kW (2030); 4h £828/kW to £610/kW, real 2025 terms, from the Modo Energy BESS CapEx Survey, https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jul-2026/uk-europe/model-assumptions/
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'How much does it cost to build a battery energy storage system in 2024?' (BESS CapEx Survey, November 2024, GB, 30 respondents, 2.8 GW, average duration 1.9 hours) — average £580k/MW, 68% between £400k and £700k/MW, 2-hour median £650k/MW, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/battery-energy-storage-capex-containerised-bess-development-costs-oem-balance-plant-bop-grid-connections-survey-2024
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'November 2024: GB battery energy storage research roundup', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-november-2024-research-roundup-battery-energy-storage-capex-long-duration-carbon-emmisions-connections-reform-recycling-clean-power-2030

#### A-07 · MUST · Require half-hourly, multi-year, dated input data for prices and for host site load, and record the vintage and provenance of every series and every assumption in an auditable register.

- **Why:** A bankable model is one a technical and financial adviser can reproduce. Forecast providers publish vintage-stamped documentation (v3.0, v3.3, v3.4, monthly-dated assumption pages) precisely because a revenue curve without a date is meaningless — the Gresham House disclosure that curve declines cost 66.30p of NAV over three years is the proof.
- **Error if omitted:** Mixing a 2023-vintage revenue curve with 2026-vintage capex produces a project that looks investable and is not. Without a provenance register the error is undetectable, and the model cannot be re-run when the curve updates.
- **How to implement:** Every input carries {value, unit, geography, vintage/date, source reference, basis note}. Prices and host load at half-hourly granularity for at least one full year and preferably three or more, so seasonality and weather variation are captured. Produce a machine-readable assumptions register as a model output, and a diff against the previous run so that curve changes are visible as a named NAV/IRR movement, exactly as listed funds report them.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Modelling Assumptions' and 'Model Calibration' (vintage-dated documentation pages), https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jul-2026/uk-europe/model-assumptions/
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — Q4 2024 vs Q4 2025 revenue curves by duration, 2026-2049, with the NAV impact of the change attributed explicitly, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf

#### A-08 · MUST · Report a defined output pack for credit: sources and uses, min/average DSCR, LLCR, gearing, tenor, debt tail, breakeven revenue, first-breach period, contracted share by year, and the full assumptions register with sources.

- **Why:** This is the deliverable an underwriter needs. Listed funds and regulators publish exactly this shape of disclosure — Gresham House reports net debt, net debt to NAV, EBITDA and EBITDA margin as key performance indicators explicitly because they 'underpin operating cash flow, debt covenants' and access to debt capital.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that outputs only an IRR cannot be underwritten. Every omitted ratio is a question the credit committee will ask that the model cannot answer, and each one is an opportunity for the model's hidden assumptions to be wrong.
- **How to implement:** Emit a fixed schema per run: {sources_and_uses, capex_build, opex_build, revenue_by_stream_by_year, CFADS_by_period, debt_schedule, DSCR_by_period (min, average, first_breach), LLCR, gearing_at_COD, tenor, debt_tail_years, contracted_share_by_year, contracted_only_DSCR_by_period, breakeven_revenue_per_MW_yr, project_IRR {basis}, equity_IRR {basis}, NPV_ex_terminal, NPV_inc_terminal, stress_results, assumptions_register}. Every numeric assumption in the register must carry a source string; refuse to produce a headline result if any required source is missing.
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — KPI set: NAV, operational MW/MWh, net debt and net debt to NAV, revenue and revenue per MW, contracted revenues per MW and as a percentage of total, EBITDA and EBITDA margin, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf
- *Source:* GreenBridge Infrastructure, 'Debt Sizing', https://greenbridgeinfra.com/resources/project-finance/debt-sizing

#### A-09 · MUST · Sample every stochastic input as contemporaneous multivariate blocks drawn from the same calendar timestamps. Build a single time-aligned matrix over the joint vector (day-ahead wholesale price, imbalance price, the six EAC dynamic-response clearing prices, reserve product prices, 1 Hz system frequency, site half-hourly load, on-site PV output, ambient temperature, and any DNO flexibility utilisation signal) and resample contiguous calendar blocks (day or week) of that matrix jointly. Prohibit independent per-stream resampling and prohibit mixing vintages across streams within a single scenario. Where a stream is only available for a different period, either restrict the joint sample to the overlapping window or fit a copula on standardised residuals and document the imputation explicitly.

- **Why:** GB revenue extremes are produced by co-movement, not by marginal dispersion. A cold, still winter evening simultaneously delivers high wholesale and imbalance prices, high BM offer value, an elevated dynamic response requirement, a DUoS red band, a C&I heating peak, near-zero PV and a cold-derated battery. Independent resampling destroys both tails. The statistical remedy is standard and long-established: the moving block bootstrap (Kunsch 1989) and the stationary bootstrap (Politis & Romano 1994) exist precisely to preserve serial and cross-sectional dependence in resampled time series; Gaussian-copula space-time trajectory methods (Tastu, Pinson & Madsen) are the accepted alternative where a parametric dependence structure is wanted.
- **Error if omitted:** The P90 is biased optimistically (too tight) because the joint bad day — host load collapse, negative price, network curtailment and cold derate arriving together — is never generated. Directionally, independent resampling shrinks the variance of the annual revenue total by removing positive cross-stream correlation; the annual-total standard deviation falls roughly as the square root of the sum of variances rather than including the 2*sum(cov) term, so with even moderate positive pairwise correlation the understatement of dispersion is material and one-sided. A DSCR sized off that P90 oversizes debt. The upper tail is also destroyed, which understates the value of the optimiser and of stacking.
- **How to implement:** Assemble a single dataframe indexed by settlement period with one column per stochastic driver, over the longest common historical window (minimum 3 years, preferably 5+ to span weather years). Scenario generation: (1) choose block length L (7 days is a defensible default for capturing weather persistence; alternatively use Politis-White automatic block-length selection on the first principal component); (2) draw ceil(365/L) blocks with replacement, preserving each block's internal cross-stream alignment and its calendar position (draw winter blocks for winter slots to preserve seasonality); (3) concatenate to a synthetic year; (4) repeat N>=500 times. Layer curve-level (vintage) uncertainty ON TOP as a separate multiplicative or additive shift applied consistently across all price streams within a scenario, so that level uncertainty and dependence structure are both present and separately attributable. Validation gate: compute the empirical correlation matrix of the synthetic ensemble and require each pairwise correlation to lie within the bootstrap confidence interval of the historical value; require the empirical distribution of daily wholesale-price spread conditional on the top decile of site load to match history. Report P50/P90 both with and without the dependence structure and quote the difference as a named model-risk line.
- *Source:* H. R. Kunsch, 'The Jackknife and the Bootstrap for General Stationary Observations', Annals of Statistics 17(3):1217-1241, 1989, DOI 10.1214/aos/1176347265
- *Source:* D. N. Politis and J. P. Romano, 'The Stationary Bootstrap', Journal of the American Statistical Association 89, 1994, DOI 10.1080/01621459.1994.10476870
- *Source:* J. Tastu, P. Pinson, H. Madsen, 'Space-Time Trajectories of Wind Power Generation: Parametrized Precision Matrices Under a Gaussian Copula Approach', in Modeling and Stochastic Learning for Forecasting in High Dimensions, Springer, 2015, DOI 10.1007/978-3-319-18732-7_14
- *Source:* P. Pinson et al., 'RESGen: Renewable Energy Scenario Generation Platform', DTU Orbit, https://orbit.dtu.dk — multivariate predictive densities and space-time interdependencies
- *Source:* Elexon BSC / NESO Data Portal — time-aligned raw series for imbalance price, system frequency and balancing service outturn (data source, not a claim)

#### A-10 · MUST · Treat co-located rooftop PV as a stochastic, physical asset with error bars, not a deterministic input. Require a bankable yield assessment giving P50/P75/P90 with a stated uncertainty decomposition and a named TMY dataset; interannual variability as a distribution sampled contemporaneously with price and load; module degradation over the full underwriting horizon; soiling and shading losses; inverter/PCS availability; DC:AC ratio and clipping; and an explicit AC- versus DC-coupling decision. Capture the PV's FiT/RO/REGO/SEG accreditation status as a required input, because it is the trigger for both the Capacity Market Low Carbon Exclusion and the co-location metering constraints.

- **Why:** The model nets PV against load at half-hourly resolution to derive the net profile the battery optimises against, so every PV error propagates straight into dispatch, avoided-import value, export exposure and charging cost. Giving price a full distribution while giving PV a point estimate is internally inconsistent. Module degradation is well characterised and material over 15 years: Jordan and Kurtz assembled nearly 2000 measured degradation rates and found 'a median value of 0.5%/year', with crystalline silicon at ~0.5%/yr and other technologies nearer 1.0%/yr; the later NREL compendium extends this to over 11,000 rates across ~200 studies in 40 countries. Compounded at 0.5%/yr, year-15 output is 0.995^15 = 92.8% of year 1; at 1.0%/yr it is 86.0% — a 7-point spread on the single input that sets the battery's charging opportunity. The coupling decision is not cosmetic either: DC coupling lets PV charge the battery without passing through the PV inverter, which changes both round-trip efficiency and the metering topology that the FiT/RO rules and the ancillary services depend on.
- **Error if omitted:** The net-load profile is wrong with unknown and unquantified error, and the error is systematically one-directional if a P50 TMY is used without interannual variability (a P50 year is by construction exceeded half the time, so a model run on P50 PV will overstate self-consumption savings in the lower half of the distribution and never generate the low-irradiance year that stresses the DSCR). Ignoring degradation overstates year-10-to-15 PV output by 5-14%, which in turn overstates avoided import and understates the battery's grid-charging requirement in exactly the years where the debt tail sits. Omitting the accreditation input leaves the Capacity Market Low Carbon Exclusion test un-triggerable and the FiT/RO metering constraint undiscovered.
- **How to implement:** Inputs: a PVsyst (or equivalent) yield report giving P50, P75 and P90 with the uncertainty budget decomposed into resource interannual variability, model uncertainty and equipment uncertainty; the TMY dataset named (Solargis, PVGIS or Meteonorm) and cross-checked against measured site generation where the array already exists; soiling and shading loss assumptions; DC:AC ratio and modelled clipping losses; inverter availability. Modelling: sample the annual irradiance multiplier as part of the contemporaneous multivariate block (so a low-sun year coincides with the price and load of that same year, not an independent draw); apply module degradation as a compounding annual factor with the rate itself uncertain (median 0.5%/yr, sensitivity at 1.0%/yr); apply inverter availability as timed outages, not an annual scalar. Coupling decision as a binary: DC-coupled (PV charges the battery through a shared DC bus, avoiding one AC conversion, recapturing clipped energy above the inverter rating, but sharing the inverter's apparent-power budget and changing where the generation meter can sit) versus AC-coupled (independent, simpler metering, one extra conversion). Solve both, and check each against the FiT/RO metering constraint, because DC coupling frequently puts the battery upstream of the generation meter and therefore triggers the grid-charging prohibition. Validation gate: where the array exists, backcast the model against at least 12 months of measured generation and report the bias; refuse a bankable output if the bias exceeds a stated tolerance.
- *Source:* D. C. Jordan and S. R. Kurtz, 'Photovoltaic Degradation Rates — An Analytical Review', Progress in Photovoltaics: Research and Applications, 2013 (NREL/JA-5200-51664, June 2012), https://www.nrel.gov/docs/fy12osti/51664.pdf — nearly 2000 degradation rates assembled, 'showing a median value of 0.5%/year'; crystalline silicon ~0.5%/yr, other technologies ~1.0%/yr
- *Source:* D. C. Jordan, S. R. Kurtz et al., 'Compendium of Photovoltaic Degradation Rates', NREL — over 11,000 degradation rates across ~200 studies in 40 countries, with analysis of sampling bias
- *Source:* IEC 61724 series, 'Photovoltaic system performance' — monitoring, capacity evaluation and energy evaluation methods
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Guidance for generators: Co-location of electricity storage and hydrogen production under the RO, FIT, REGO and SEG', v6.2, May 2025 — establishes why PV accreditation status is a required model input (metering constraints and payment eligibility)
- *Source:* EMR Delivery Body, 'Capacity Market Rules' — Low Carbon Exclusion at prequalification for CMUs including FiT- or RO-accredited plant [primary code document; obtain current version]
- *Source:* PVGIS (European Commission JRC) and Solargis TMY datasets — resource data sources for cross-checking a PVsyst P50

#### A-11 · SHOULD · Itemise capex into at least: battery containers (cells/modules/BMS/HVAC), power conversion system, transformer and HV/LV switchgear, protection and control, metering, civils and groundworks, grid or site connection works, development and consenting, EPC margin, owner's costs, contingency and interest during construction — each separately sourced.

- **Why:** A single blended £/kWh hides the components that behave differently: cells are falling fast and are commodity-priced; civils, protection, metering and connection are site-specific, largely fixed, and dominate at small BTM scale. The GB capex survey collected balance of plant, grid connection, development cost and time-to-COD as separate categories precisely because they diverge.
- **Error if omitted:** At 0.1-5 MW the fixed and site-specific components are a much larger share of total cost than at 50-200 MW. A model carrying a grid-scale blended £/kWh will understate BTM capex by a wide margin and will not be able to explain to an investment committee where the money goes or which line to attack.
- **How to implement:** Structure the capex build as a table of line items each with {value, unit (£, £/kW, £/kWh, £/site), source, vintage}. Require that the sum reconciles to a total £/MW that can be compared to the GB benchmark of £580k/MW (1.9h) and £650k/MW (2h median). Add an explicit scale factor for sub-5 MW. Include interest during construction as a separate funded cost — Ofgem's indicative IDC rates for storage are 6.03% (1-3 year tenor) and 6.11% (5-7 year tenor).
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'How much does it cost to build a battery energy storage system in 2024?' (BESS CapEx Survey, November 2024, GB), https://modoenergy.com/research/en/battery-energy-storage-capex-containerised-bess-development-costs-oem-balance-plant-bop-grid-connections-survey-2024
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Decision - Financial Framework: LDES Window 1 Cap and Floor regime', September 2025 — indicative IDC rate 6.03% (1-3yr) and 6.11% (5-7yr), https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2025-09/LDES%20Window%201%20Financial%20Framework%20Decision.pdf

#### A-12 · SHOULD · Build opex from itemised, sourced lines — O&M/LTSA, insurance, business rates, land or roof rent, metering and comms, DNO/supplier standing charges, SPV administration, optimiser fee — not as a percentage of capex.

- **Why:** Opex is where BTM diverges most from grid-scale and where percentage-of-capex rules of thumb fail. Gresham House explicitly attributes margin improvement to reduced insurance costs and to acquiring land to remove rental expense, showing these are separately managed, material lines. Its implied all-in opex is roughly £24.6k/MW/yr in 2025 (revenue £68,600/MW/yr at a 64.2% EBITDA margin) and roughly £22.4k/MW/yr in 2024.
- **Error if omitted:** A percentage-of-capex opex rule ties opex to a falling capex number, so as cell prices fall the model automatically forecasts falling opex — which is false, since rates, insurance, rent and O&M labour do not track cell prices. It also makes the model unable to answer 'what if the host charges rent' or 'what if rates are reassessed', both of which are live BTM issues.
- **How to implement:** Opex_y = sum of tagged lines, each with its own unit and escalation index. GB grid-scale sanity check: total ~£22-25k/MW/yr (2024-2025, derived from Gresham House revenue and EBITDA margin). Expect a materially higher £/MW figure at 0.1-5 MW because of fixed-cost dilution. Insurance benchmark: 0.5-3% of total system value per annum for commercial installations, with 10 MW+ projects at the lower end and LFP attracting 10-20% lower premiums — this is a broker source and should be treated as indicative only.
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — EBITDA margin 64.2% (2025) vs 62.5% (2024); revenue £68,600/MW/yr; margin improvement attributed to lower insurance costs and land acquisition removing rent, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf
- *Source:* Solarif, 'How much does battery storage fire insurance cost?' — BROKER/VENDOR SOURCE, indicative only: 0.5-3% of total system value annually for commercial installations, https://solarif.com/academy-article/how-much-does-battery-storage-fire-insurance-cost/

#### A-13 · SHOULD · Include UK business rates as an explicit opex line, computed on the contractor's basis, and flag the 2026 revaluation and multiplier change.

- **Why:** Battery storage is a rateable hereditament in England and Wales (Special Category Code 733) and is valued on the contractor's basis using the Rating Cost Guide, with a decapitalisation rate applied to adjusted capital cost. The 2026 rating list has an antecedent valuation date of 1 April 2024 and takes effect 1 April 2026 with a new five-tier multiplier structure.
- **Error if omitted:** Business rates are a fixed, index-linked, non-avoidable cost that runs for the asset's life and does not fall when revenue falls. Omitting it overstates CFADS in exactly the periods where DSCR is tested. It is also the opex line most likely to step up unexpectedly, because the 2026 revaluation resets values against April 2024 costs.
- **How to implement:** RateableValue ~ (adjusted replacement cost of rateable plant and buildings, after obsolescence allowance) x DecapitalisationRate + land value adjustment. Decapitalisation rates for the 2026 list remain at 2.6% and 4.4% (the 4.4% standard rate applies outside education/healthcare). Annual bill = RateableValue x Multiplier, with 2026/27 England non-RHL multipliers of 0.430 (RV under £51,000), 0.480 (RV £51,000-£499,999) and 0.508 (RV £500,000+), plus transitional relief caps of 5%/15%/30% by RV band. Model the rate as a stepped, CPI-adjusted line with a revaluation step every three years. For a BTM asset, flag whether the battery is assessed as a separate hereditament or merged into the host's assessment — this is unresolved and changes who bears the cost.
- *Source:* Valuation Office Agency, 'Rating Manual section 6 part 3: valuation of all property classes - power generators' — Special Category Code 733 'Battery Storage' for 2017 and subsequent rating lists; contractor's basis with Rating Cost Guide stage 1 costs, https://www.gov.uk/guidance/rating-manual-section-6-part-3-valuation-of-all-property-classes/power-generators
- *Source:* MHCLG, 'Business Rates Information Letter 3/2025: Confirmation of Decapitalisation Rates' — 2.6% and 4.4% retained, https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/22025-confirmation-of-decapitalisation-rates
- *Source:* Knight Frank, 'A Guide to 2026 Business Rates in England', 2026 — multipliers and transitional relief caps, https://www.knightfrank.co.uk/commercial/guides/business-rates-england
- *Source:* Valuation Office Agency, 'Revaluation 2026 - everything you need to know', 29 September 2025 — AVD 1 April 2024, effective 1 April 2026, https://valuationoffice.blog.gov.uk/2025/09/29/revaluation-2026-everything-you-need-to-know/


### B. MILP formulation and physics

#### B-01 · MUST · Model state of energy with two separate non-negative power variables and place charge efficiency as a MULTIPLIER and discharge efficiency as a DIVISOR, so that round-trip efficiency is applied exactly once per full cycle.

- **Why:** The single most common formulation error is applying round-trip efficiency to both legs (charging it twice) or to a single net-power variable (charging it zero or once regardless of direction). Every authoritative formulation uses the asymmetric form.
- **Error if omitted:** Applying eta_RT to both legs turns a true 88% round-trip into an effective 77.4% — understating deliverable energy and arbitrage revenue by ~12%. Applying no efficiency at all overstates spread capture by ~1/eta_RT - 1 = ~13.6% and, worse, removes the loss term that is one of the three published sufficient conditions for the LP relaxation to be exact.
- **How to implement:** E_{t+1} = E_t + eta_c * P^ch_t * dt - (1/eta_d) * P^dis_t * dt, with 0 <= P^ch_t <= Pbar_ch, 0 <= P^dis_t <= Pbar_dis, and eta_RT = eta_c * eta_d. Hornek et al. write it as e_p = e_{p-1} + (b_p*eta_b - s_p/eta_s)*dt_p with eta_RT = eta_b*eta_s. Garifi et al. write E^(t+1) = E^(t) + eta_c*dt*P_ch - eta_d*dt*P_dis with the constraint 0 < eta_c < 1 < eta_d (i.e. their eta_d is the reciprocal of a discharge efficiency). Symmetric split: eta_c = eta_d = sqrt(eta_RT).
- *Source:* Hornek, T., Lee, Y., Potenciano Menci, S., Pavic, I., 'The Value of Battery Energy Storage in the Continuous Intraday Market: Forecast vs. Perfect Foresight Strategies', IEEE PowerTech 2025, eqs (1)-(6), DOI 10.1109/POWERTECH59965.2025.11180315, arXiv:2501.07121v2
- *Source:* Garifi, K., Baker, K., Christensen, D., Touri, B., 'Control of Energy Storage in Home Energy Management Systems: Non-Simultaneous Charging and Discharging Guarantees', 2018, eqs (4a)-(4d), arXiv:1805.00100v2
- *Source:* Hesse, H.C., Kumtepeli, V., Schimpe, M., Reniers, J., Howey, D., Tripathi, A., Wang, Y., Jossen, A., 'Ageing and Efficiency Aware Battery Dispatch for Arbitrage Markets Using Mixed Integer Linear Programming', Energies 12(6):999, 2019, eqs (8)-(19), DOI 10.3390/en12060999

#### B-02 · MUST · Set big-M values equal to the tightest valid physical bound (the power rating), never an arbitrary large number, and keep the model's coefficient range inside six orders of magnitude.

- **Why:** Gurobi's integrality tolerance means a binary can be 1e-5 away from zero; the physical leakage in a big-M constraint is M multiplied by that tolerance. With arbitrary M this silently reintroduces simultaneous charge/discharge even with the binary present.
- **Error if omitted:** With M = 1e6 kW and the default integrality tolerance of 1e-5, up to 10 kW of 'phantom' power passes the binary gate per period. Across 17,520 half-hours that is a systematically overstated revenue line that no feasibility check will catch, because the solution is feasible to tolerance.
- **How to implement:** 0 <= P^ch_t <= Pbar_ch * u_t with M := Pbar_ch (MW), and 0 <= P^dis_t <= Pbar_dis * (1 - u_t) with M := Pbar_dis. Work in MW and MWh, not kW/kWh, to compress the range. Gurobi guidance: 'scale the matrix coefficients so that their range is contained in six orders of magnitude or less, and hopefully within [1e-3, 1e6]'; RHS values and variable bounds 'should be scaled so that they are on the order of 1e6 or less'; objective values ideally between 1 and 1e6. Defaults: primal/dual feasibility tolerance 1e-6, integrality tolerance 1e-5. Prefer SOS1 or indicator constraints over big-M where the solver supports them.
- *Source:* Gurobi Optimization, 'Numerics Guide — Tolerances and User-Scaling', Gurobi Optimizer documentation (current release, accessed 2026), https://docs.gurobi.com/projects/optimizer/en/current/concepts/numericguide/tolerances_scaling.html
- *Source:* Gurobi Optimization, 'Numerics Guide' (sources of numerical instability; reformulation preferred over parameter tuning), https://docs.gurobi.com/projects/optimizer/en/current/concepts/numericguide.html

#### B-03 · MUST · Convert power to energy with the explicit half-hour time step and state units in the model; do not assume dt = 1.

- **Why:** GB settlement is half-hourly and NESO's Enduring Auction Capability service windows are 30 minutes. Any formulation ported from hourly US literature will double every energy quantity if dt is left at 1.
- **Error if omitted:** A factor-of-two error in state of energy, cycle count, degradation and revenue. It is usually caught, but the same error in the ancillary energy-reserve constraint (e.g. Dynamic Containment's 15-minute requirement) is not, because 15 minutes is 0.25 h, not half a period.
- **How to implement:** dt = 0.5 h for GB half-hourly settlement periods (17,520 or 17,568 periods per year; 46 and 50 periods on clock-change days). Energy in MWh = power in MW * dt. Keep the reserve sustain requirement in its own units: E_reserve_required = (sustain_minutes / 60) * contracted_MW.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Enduring Auction Capability (EAC)', accessed 2026 — 'the platform operates on 30-minute service windows', https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/enduring-auction-capability-eac
- *Source:* Hornek, T. et al., 2025, eq. (5) with explicit dt_p per product duration, arXiv:2501.07121v2

#### B-04 · MUST · State initial and terminal state-of-energy conditions explicitly, and either impose a cyclic condition or attach a terminal value to stored energy.

- **Why:** An unconstrained terminal condition makes the optimiser empty the battery in the final periods regardless of price, because stored energy has zero value at T. Every published formulation pins both ends.
- **Error if omitted:** For a full-year run the end effect is negligible; for a rolling-horizon controller with a short window it is severe and recurs at every window boundary, systematically biasing dispatch toward discharge near each horizon end and overstating revenue in the terminal periods of every window.
- **How to implement:** E_0 = E_init (Hesse et al. use E_bat(0) = 0; Xu et al. set e_{1,j} = e^0_j per aging segment). Terminal: sum_j e_{T,j} >= E^final (Xu eq. 16), or cyclic E_T = E_0, or add a terminal value term -V(E_T) to the objective. For rolling horizons, optimise over a long window and IMPLEMENT only the first sub-window, discarding the tail — Hornek et al. optimise 32 quarter-hourly products (8 h) and trade only the 3 nearest; for auction markets they optimise 48 hourly products two days ahead and trade 24.
- *Source:* Xu, B. et al., 2018, eqs (15)-(16), IEEE TPWRS 33(2):2248-2259
- *Source:* Hesse, H.C. et al., 2019, eqs (16)-(18), Energies 12(6):999
- *Source:* Hornek, T. et al., 2025, Section IV-B: n^opt = 48 (hourly), n^trade = 24; CID n^opt = 32, n^trade = 3, arXiv:2501.07121v2

#### B-05 · MUST · Constrain the usable state-of-charge window and the C-rate explicitly, and separate energy rating from usable energy.

- **Why:** Manufacturers restrict operating SoC to preserve warranty and cell life; published case studies operate 10-15% to 85-95%. Nameplate MWh is not dispatchable MWh.
- **Error if omitted:** Overstating usable energy by 10-30%. A '2 MW / 4 MWh' asset modelled at 100% depth delivers roughly 3.2-3.4 MWh in practice; every duration-dependent revenue stream (arbitrage spread capture, Dynamic Regulation's 60-minute sustain) is inflated proportionally.
- **How to implement:** E_min <= E_t <= E_max with E_min = SOC_min * E_nom, E_max = SOH_t * E_nom * SOC_max. Published windows: 15%-95% (Xu et al., 20 MW / 12.5 MWh NMC); 10%-95% with C-rate <= 1 (Hesse et al., LFP); 15%-85% (Garifi et al., 5 kWh residential). C-rate cap: P_max <= C_rate_max * E_nom. Hesse et al. also show cyclic capacity fade rising steeply above C-rate 1 (from ~4e-4 %/h at rated to ~8e-4 %/h above rated), so a C-rate cap is both a warranty constraint and a degradation driver.
- *Source:* Xu, B. et al., 2018, Section V-A: maximum SoC 95%, minimum SoC 15%, IEEE TPWRS 33(2):2248-2259
- *Source:* Hesse, H.C. et al., 2019, Section 2.2: 10-95% SOC, C-rates <= 1, Fig. 3b, Energies 12(6):999
- *Source:* Garifi, K. et al., 2018, Section V: 5 kWh ESS restricted to 15%-85% of maximum SOC to preserve lifetime, arXiv:1805.00100v2

#### B-06 · MUST · Optimise the full year at half-hourly resolution rather than on representative/typical days; if representative periods are used, implement inter-period state-of-energy linking and report the induced error.

- **Why:** Representative-day formulations break the state-of-energy chain at each period boundary, which is precisely the coupling that gives storage its value. The dedicated literature exists specifically because naive typical-day models mis-size and mis-value storage.
- **Error if omitted:** Storage is valued as if it had to return to a fixed SoC every representative day, destroying multi-day and seasonal arbitrage and mis-sizing the asset. Tejada-Arango et al. report that an enhanced representative-period method with a transition matrix and cluster indices reduces storage representation error to 'almost 10 times lower' than the traditional approach — implying the traditional approach's error is roughly an order of magnitude larger.
- **How to implement:** Preferred: a single MILP over all 17,520 half-hours (17,568 in a leap year) with a continuous SoC chain. If representative periods are unavoidable, use a two-level SoC: an intra-period SoC that resets each representative period and an inter-period SoC that accumulates across the chronological sequence of periods, E^inter_{p+1} = E^inter_p + dE(rep(p)), with the physical bounds applied to the SUM E^inter_p + E^intra_{p,t}. Report objective error against a full-chronology reference run.
- *Source:* Tejada-Arango, D.A., Domeshek, M., Wogrin, S., Centeno, E., 'Enhanced Representative Days and System States Modeling for Energy Storage Investment Analysis', IEEE Transactions on Power Systems 33(6), 2018, DOI 10.1109/TPWRS.2018.2819578
- *Source:* Hoffmann, M., Kotzur, L., Stolten, D., Robinius, M., 'A Review on Time Series Aggregation Methods for Energy System Models', Energies 13(3):641, 2020, DOI 10.3390/en13030641
- *Source:* Gabrielli, P., Gazzani, M., Martelli, E., Mazzotti, M., 'Optimal design of multi-energy systems with seasonal storage', Applied Energy 219:408-424, 2018, DOI 10.1016/j.apenergy.2017.07.142
- *Source:* Blanke, T., Schmidt, K.S., Goettsche, J., Doering, B., Frisch, J., van Treeck, C., 'Time series aggregation for energy system design: review and extension of modelling seasonal storages', Energy Informatics 5, 2022, DOI 10.1186/s42162-022-00208-5

#### B-07 · MUST · Write the site power balance explicitly, with separate import and export variables at the supply meter, distinct import and export prices, and a hard export limit.

- **Why:** BTM economics are driven by the spread between the retail import tariff and the export price, not by the wholesale price alone. The two must be separate variables because they carry different prices and different constraints.
- **Error if omitted:** Netting import and export into one variable at one price destroys the entire BTM value proposition (avoided retail tariff plus network charges) and removes the price asymmetry that is one of the published sufficient conditions for the LP relaxation to be exact.
- **How to implement:** 0 = -P_grid_t + P_load_t - (P_sol_t - P_curt_t) - P^dis_t + P^ch_t  [Garifi et al. eq. 5]. Split: P_grid_t = P^imp_t - P^exp_t, with P^imp_t, P^exp_t >= 0, P^imp_t <= MIC (maximum import capacity), P^exp_t <= MEC or the G99/G100 export limit (often zero for an export-limited BTM site). Objective: + c^imp_t * P^imp_t * dt - c^exp_t * P^exp_t * dt. The condition c^imp_t > c^exp_t for all t makes simultaneous charge/discharge suboptimal without a binary (Engels et al.); if the site is export-limited to zero, the Garifi no-export case applies instead.
- *Source:* Garifi, K. et al., 2018, eq. (5) and Propositions 1-2 (no-export and net-metering cases treated separately), arXiv:1805.00100v2
- *Source:* Engels, J. et al., 2019, Section V-B: 'As long as c_cons > c_inj, there is a cost for consuming energy and an optimal solution will always set P^{sc,c}_k * P^{sc,d}_k = 0, for all k', arXiv:1710.08331

#### B-08 · MUST · Add a strictly positive throughput or cycling penalty term to the objective even when degradation is priced elsewhere, sized to be economically negligible but numerically decisive.

- **Why:** Garifi et al.'s Proposition 2 and Remark 1 show that when the energy price can be zero (or the site can curtail freely, or export at parity), a strictly positive cycling penalty alpha + beta > 0 is what restores the guarantee of a non-simultaneous optimum. It also breaks degenerate ties that otherwise produce arbitrary, unstable schedules.
- **Error if omitted:** Degenerate optima with meaningless cycling. Garifi et al. demonstrate that with alpha = beta = 0 and high PV the relaxed model simultaneously charges and discharges; setting alpha = 0.001 (per kWh, against an electricity price of $0.11/kWh, i.e. under 1% of the price) restores correct behaviour.
- **How to implement:** objective += alpha * sum_t P^ch_t + beta * sum_t P^dis_t with alpha, beta > 0. Keep alpha + beta at roughly 0.1-1% of the typical energy price so it does not distort the economics but does break ties. This is in ADDITION to, not instead of, the piecewise-linear cycle-aging cost.
- *Source:* Garifi, K. et al., 2018, eq. (6), Proposition 2, Remark 1, and Fig. 4 (alpha = 0.001 with c_e = $0.11/kWh restores non-simultaneous operation), arXiv:1805.00100v2

#### B-09 · MUST · Assert solver status and optimality gap on every solve, and refuse to report revenue from any solve that did not reach a stated gap.

- **Why:** A large annual MILP with binaries in every half-hour will frequently hit a time limit. A time-limited solution with a 5% gap silently understates or (via the bound) overstates revenue, and no downstream cashflow check can detect it.
- **Error if omitted:** P50 revenue carrying an unstated 2-10% optimality gap on top of every other modelling error, varying unpredictably between runs and between sites, which destroys comparability across the portfolio.
- **How to implement:** After solve: require status == OPTIMAL, or status == TIME_LIMIT with MIPGap <= a declared threshold (e.g. 0.1%), else raise. Record and report objbound, objval and the achieved gap alongside every revenue number. Gurobi defaults: MIPGap 1e-4 (0.01%), primal/dual feasibility tolerance 1e-6, integrality tolerance 1e-5. Never report a revenue figure from a solve whose status is INFEASIBLE, INF_OR_UNBD, or SUBOPTIMAL without flagging it.
- *Source:* Gurobi Optimization, 'Parameters' reference (MIPGap, MIPGapAbs, FeasibilityTol, IntFeasTol, OptimalityTol, TimeLimit; status codes OPTIMAL, SUBOPTIMAL, TIME_LIMIT, INFEASIBLE, INF_OR_UNBD), https://docs.gurobi.com/projects/optimizer/en/current/reference/parameters.html
- *Source:* Gurobi Optimization, 'Numerics Guide — Tolerances and User-Scaling', https://docs.gurobi.com/projects/optimizer/en/current/concepts/numericguide/tolerances_scaling.html

#### B-10 · MUST · Model round-trip efficiency as a first-order value driver and test IRR sensitivity to it explicitly, because arbitrage value is super-linear in efficiency.

- **Why:** Efficiency does not scale value proportionally: a less efficient device must charge for more hours, and the additional hours are at progressively worse prices. Sioshansi et al. document the multiplier effect directly.
- **Error if omitted:** A 2-3 point error in assumed round-trip efficiency, which is well within the range between DC-DC cell efficiency and true AC-AC system efficiency including auxiliaries and transformer losses, moves arbitrage value by considerably more than 2-3%. Quoting cell-level efficiency instead of AC-AC system efficiency is the standard way this error enters.
- **How to implement:** Run the MILP across a grid of eta_RT and report the elasticity. Published anchor: 'increasing the efficiency of a 20-hour device from 70% to 80% results in a more than 30% increase in arbitrage value from $60/kW-year to $80/kW-year' (PJM 2006 average hourly prices). Ensure the efficiency entered is AC-AC at the point of common coupling, including inverter and transformer losses, and state whether auxiliaries are inside or outside it.
- *Source:* Sioshansi, R., Denholm, P., Jenkin, T., Weiss, J., Energy Economics 31(2):269-277, 2009, Section 2 and Fig. 5, DOI 10.1016/j.eneco.2008.10.005
- *Source:* Hesse, H.C. et al., 2019, Fig. 4 (separate battery, power-electronics and overall round-trip efficiency curves), Energies 12(6):999
- *Source:* Hornek, T. et al., 2025, Section IV-B (uniform round-trip efficiency 92% assumed for all cases, aligned to a 2h Tesla Megapack specification), arXiv:2501.07121v2

#### B-11 · MUST · State the objective as profit net of losses and degradation, not gross revenue, and report the gross-to-net bridge.

- **Why:** The gross arbitrage revenue and the net profit differ by a large, model-dependent amount, and a model that optimises gross revenue produces a different schedule from one that optimises net profit.
- **Error if omitted:** Hesse et al. quantify it: 'losses in battery ageing and efficiency reduce the attainable profit by up to 36% for the most profitable arbitrage use case considered'. A model reporting gross arbitrage revenue as the revenue line is overstating the cashflow by up to that margin, and is also dispatching sub-optimally because the schedule that maximises gross revenue is not the schedule that maximises profit.
- **How to implement:** min -J_total = sum_k ( c_en(k) * P_AC(k) * dt + c_ag * (-dSOH(k)) ), where c_en is the energy price (EUR/kWh) and c_ag the cost of unit ageing (EUR per unit dSOH), with dissipation losses embedded in the system dynamics rather than as a separate objective term. Report the bridge explicitly: gross market revenue -> less conversion losses -> less degradation cost -> less auxiliary load -> less network and supply charges on imports -> net margin.
- *Source:* Hesse, H.C. et al., 2019, eqs (1)-(3), (7), and abstract (36% profit reduction; arbitrage gross revenue up to 8762 EUR/MWh installed capacity per month versus 7716 EUR/MWh for primary control reserve in the same month, German 2017 data), Energies 12(6):999, DOI 10.3390/en12060999

#### B-12 · MUST · Enforce non-simultaneous charge/discharge and non-simultaneous import/export with binaries wherever a time-varying charge differs between import and export, rather than relying on the optimiser to avoid it.

- **Why:** In GB the import and export legs face different, asymmetric tariffs: import pays positive DUoS unit rates plus the whole levy stack; export receives negative DUoS generation rates and no levies. Whenever the export credit in a period exceeds the import cost (which happens at LV in red periods: import 7.777 p/kWh vs generation credit 7.711 p/kWh, and can invert under other DNOs or with a high SEG/PPA price), a linear relaxation will simultaneously import and export to harvest the spread — pure arbitrage of the tariff structure, not physics.
- **Error if omitted:** The MILP returns physically impossible schedules that manufacture revenue from tariff asymmetry. This is a silent failure: the solution is feasible in the relaxed model and only detectable by inspecting simultaneous imp[t]>0 and exp[t]>0.
- **How to implement:** Binary z[t] ∈ {0,1}: imp[t] ≤ M·z[t], exp[t] ≤ M·(1−z[t]). Similarly binary y[t] for charge/discharge: c[t] ≤ P_max·y[t], d[t] ≤ P_max·(1−y[t]). Add a post-solve assertion Σ_t 1[imp[t]>ε ∧ exp[t]>ε] == 0. If binaries are dropped for tractability, prove ex-post that the relaxation is tight for the specific tariff set in use — do not assume it.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, Annex 1 (import tariffs positive, generation tariffs negative), https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677

#### B-13 · MUST · Reconcile the dispatch schedule against the battery warranty's cycle cap and depth-of-discharge limits, and treat warranty invalidation as a lender event, not a modelling detail.

- **Why:** This is the storage-specific bankability risk the GB legal literature dwells on: a warranty 'permits only a certain number of cycles of charging and discharging (limited per annum and/or within a pre-determined period)' and recommends a maximum depth of discharge; 'a usage pattern that maximises short-term revenues may not be the optimum use of the technology and may accelerate degradation of the battery or invalidate the warranties'. If the warranty is invalidated, lenders lose asset security, warranty reliance and the cashflow servicing their debt simultaneously.
- **Error if omitted:** An unconstrained MILP will cycle to the revenue-maximising level, which routinely exceeds warranty limits. The resulting revenue is not financeable at all — it is revenue that voids the security package. Reporting it as the base case is not conservative-by-a-margin, it is structurally invalid.
- **How to implement:** Add to the MILP a hard constraint: sum over the warranty period of discharge_energy <= CycleCap x UsableEnergy, plus per-day and per-year variants; and SoC bounds enforcing the warranted DoD window (typically avoiding the extremes where degradation accelerates). Typical constraint: 1-2 equivalent full cycles per day. Modern LFP warranties run 15-25 years with 10,000-12,000 cycle guarantees, so calibrate the cap to the actual LTSA. Report warranty headroom (actual cycles / permitted cycles) each year as a covenant-style output. Where the optimiser wants to breach, either bind the constraint or model an explicit warranty-waiver case with a prepayment provision, as the legal literature describes.
- *Source:* Osborne Clarke / Practical Law, 'Structuring a bankable project: energy storage', 2022, https://www.osborneclarke.com/system/files/documents/22/06/15/Structuring%20a%20bankable%20project%20energy%20storage%20(w-025-5057)%202022.pdf
- *Source:* DNV, 'Financing the energy transition: building bankable battery projects' — LFP warranties now 15-25 years, cycle warranties improved from 6,000-8,000 to 10,000-12,000 cycles, https://www.dnv.com/article/financing-energy-transition-building-bankable-battery-projects/
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Modelling Assumptions' (UK/Europe, Jul 2026 vintage) — typical constraint 1-2 cycles daily, https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jul-2026/uk-europe/model-assumptions/

#### B-14 · MUST · Quantify and remove perfect-foresight bias in the dispatch optimiser, either by using a rolling-horizon formulation with realistic forecast error or by an explicit, sourced foresight haircut.

- **Why:** The GB calibration attributes roughly 10% of the modelled-to-achieved revenue gap to imperfect foresight: 'real-time trading lacks future price visibility'. A deterministic annual MILP over known prices is an upper bound, not an estimate.
- **Error if omitted:** Roughly 10% revenue overstatement, which is not conservative and compounds with the availability and degradation effects. If the calibration factor is applied without knowing whether the MILP already has foresight bias, the model risks either double-counting or under-counting.
- **How to implement:** Preferred: rolling-horizon MILP with a lookahead of L periods, re-solved every R periods, using forecast prices with realistic error and carrying state of charge forward. Report the ratio of rolling-horizon revenue to perfect-foresight revenue as the foresight factor. Acceptable alternative: apply a foresight haircut of ~10% and document that the MILP is deterministic. Never do both without netting them off.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Model Calibration' (GB, Jan 2026 vintage) — imperfect foresight ~10% impact, https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jan-2026/uk-europe/gb/model-calibration/
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Introduction to our forecasting' v3.0 — battery dispatch model uses mixed integer linear programming into day-ahead markets, https://forecastdocs.modoenergy.com/v3.0/docs/introduction

#### B-15 · MUST · Model the round-trip efficiency at the point of connection, including auxiliary and transformer losses, and apply it to charging cost rather than assuming a nameplate cell efficiency.

- **Why:** Forecast providers require the user to input 'AC useable energy capacity at point of connection (POC)' and 'round-trip efficiency (RTE) at POC' as distinct from cell-level figures, because auxiliaries (HVAC, BMS) and transformation losses are material and are borne continuously.
- **Error if omitted:** Using a cell-level RTE overstates discharge per unit of charge and understates auxiliary consumption, which for a BTM asset paying full retail import prices on that consumption is a compounding error on the most expensive energy in the model.
- **How to implement:** Discharge_hh = Charge_hh x RTE_POC, with RTE_POC defined at the metering point. Model auxiliary load as a separate always-on consumption (kW) charged at the import tariff, not netted into RTE, so that standby losses in low-activity periods are visible. Degrade RTE with age if the warranty or manufacturer data supports it. State the RTE basis explicitly (AC-AC at POC, including auxiliaries and transformer) in the assumptions register.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Modelling Assumptions' (UK/Europe, Jul 2026 vintage) — users input AC useable energy capacity at point of connection and round-trip efficiency at POC, https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jul-2026/uk-europe/model-assumptions/

#### B-16 · MUST · Make rated power P_rated (MW) and usable energy E_usable (MWh) explicit decision variables in a build-and-dispatch model, subject to GB integrality: (a) the ancillary-sellable quantity must be an integer number of MW with a floor of 1 MW; (b) BM Physical Notification MW must be integer; (c) the DFS-sellable quantity is continuous to one decimal place with a 0.1 MW floor and 100 MW cap; (d) CM eligibility and duration binning. Handle the resulting bilinear terms (degraded_capacity_y x SoC_fraction, and reserved_MW x delivery_duration in the energy-adequacy constraint) either by normalising SoC to [0,1] and multiplying by the capacity variable under McCormick envelopes, or by enumerating a discrete candidate grid of (P,E) and solving each to optimality.

- **Why:** The stated task is 'what to build, and how to dispatch it', and duration is set by a price ratio between MW-denominated and MWh-denominated revenue, so it is an output not an input. GB rules make the feasible set discrete: NESO Response Services Provider Guidance states 'A minimum response capacity of 1 MW is required per response unit' and 'Sell orders may only be submitted in whole MWs'; EAC child orders are accepted 'at any integer amount between 0MW and 20MW'; BM Physical Notifications require that 'MW values must be declared as integers'. The energy-adequacy constraint is itself written as a product in NESO's rules: Response Energy Volume = Contracted Quantity x Delivery Duration, with Energy Recovery = 20% of Response Energy Volume per Settlement Period.
- **Error if omitted:** Two distinct errors. (1) The model cannot answer the question asked — it ratifies an assumed duration rather than deriving it. (2) A continuous-relaxation sizing sweep lands on unbuildable points: a 1.7 MW asset can sell only 1 MW into EAC, stranding 0.7 MW (41% of nameplate) from all six dynamic response products and from BM headroom, while still paying for it. Overstatement of ancillary revenue is therefore proportional to the fractional MW, up to ~50% of the ancillary line for assets just above an integer boundary. Ignoring the bilinear terms and fixing capacity instead biases the build toward whatever was assumed.
- **How to implement:** Decision variables P_rated in R+, E_usable in R+, plus integer n_MW in Z+ with n_MW <= P_rated and n_MW >= 1 for any EAC participation (binary z_eac with n_MW >= z_eac and n_MW <= M*z_eac). Ancillary contracted quantity per EFA block per product: q_{a,e} in Z+, sum_a q_{a,e} <= n_MW. Energy adequacy per EFA block: sum_a q_{a,e} * D_a <= E_avail_e, where D_a = 0.25 h (DC), 0.5 h (DM), 1.0 h (DR). Energy recovery: reserve 0.2 * sum_a q_{a,e} * D_a of recoverable headroom per Settlement Period. SoC as fraction: s_t in [SoC_min, SoC_max]; stored energy e_t = s_t * C_y where C_y = degraded capacity in year y. Linearise e_t = s_t * C_y with McCormick envelopes over s_t in [s_lo,s_hi] and C_y in [C_lo,C_hi]: e_t >= s_lo*C_y + C_lo*s_t - s_lo*C_lo; e_t >= s_hi*C_y + C_hi*s_t - s_hi*C_hi; e_t <= s_hi*C_y + C_lo*s_t - s_hi*C_lo; e_t <= s_lo*C_y + C_hi*s_t - s_lo*C_hi. Because C_y is constant within a project year, the exact and preferred implementation is to fix C_y per annual solve, which makes the product linear and the envelope exact — do this, and use McCormick only where capacity is a within-solve variable (e.g. augmentation sizing). For CM: duration_h = E_usable_final_year / P_rated, floored to the published duration bin, then de-rated capacity = P_rated * DF(bin). Oversize at BOL so that E_usable(final agreement year, after degradation) still clears the duration bin used at prequalification and the Extended Performance Test obligation. Solve the build layer as a discrete grid over (P_rated in {1,2,...}, duration in {0.5,1,1.5,2,...}) with full-year hourly/half-hourly dispatch at each node, and report the efficient frontier rather than a single point.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services (DC/DM/DR) Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download — 'A minimum response capacity of 1 MW is required per response unit'; 'Sell orders may only be submitted in whole MWs'; 'Aggregation is possible when assets sit behind same GSP Group'; 'For BM, MW values must be declared as integers'; Response Energy Volume = Contracted Quantity x Delivery Duration; Energy Recovery = 20% of Response Energy Volume
- *Source:* NESO, 'Enduring Auction Capability (EAC) Auction Rules / market design pack', https://www.neso.energy/document/277671/download — child orders 'can be accepted for any integer volume between 0 and the total offered quantity'; parent orders non-curtailable; Slow Reserve Flexible Group requires >=4 consecutive service windows (2 hours)
- *Source:* NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service Procurement Rules', v5.0, effective 18 May 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/379391/download — Minimum Submission Size 0.1 MW, Maximum Submission Size 100 MW, Registered Unit Capacity rounded to one decimal place (paras 4.2, 11.5.7, Schedule 1)
- *Source:* G. P. McCormick, 'Computability of global solutions to factorable nonconvex programs: Part I — Convex underestimating problems', Mathematical Programming 10(1):147-175, 1976, DOI 10.1007/BF01580665
- *Source:* Energy-Storage.news, 'UK system operator reassessing outdated Capacity Market de-rating factors for storage', reporting NESO Electricity Capacity Report (May 2023) — storage de-rating is duration-dependent: ~4-5% at 0.5h, 19-22% (T-1) / 30-40% (T-4) at 2h, ~90-95% at 8h+ [industry analytics, secondary]

#### B-17 · MUST · Write the inverter apparent-power constraint explicitly as P(t)^2 + Q(t)^2 <= S_rated^2 and linearise it as a regular polygon inner approximation with 8-16 segments so it stays inside the MILP. Bind Q(t) to (a) the G99 reactive capability requirement at the connection point, (b) any DNO connection-agreement power-factor band or reactive-import restriction, and (c) the reactive volume actually needed to avoid the DNO excess-reactive charge in that half hour. Then propagate the reduced real-power headroom sqrt(S^2 - Q(t)^2) to EVERY MW-denominated quantity in the same period: ancillary contracted quantity, BM MEL/MIL, CM deliverability, DNO flexibility, and DFS Service Volume.

- **Why:** 'Model reactive behaviour' is not implementable as written; the constraint is the model. ENA EREC G99 requires that Power Generating Modules between 1 MW and 10 MW, when operating at Registered Capacity, are capable of continuous operation between 0.95 lagging and 0.95 leading power factor at the connection point — that is a mandated Q capability of +/-0.3122*S, which is precisely the region where the real-power derate becomes non-trivial. If the inverter is used for power-factor correction (a common and often decisive part of the C&I value proposition and the mechanism that actually avoids the DNO excess-reactive charge), the MW it can simultaneously sell is reduced.
- **Error if omitted:** Systematic double-counting of the inverter. The model sells power headroom it does not physically have AND separately books the reactive-charge saving that consumes that same headroom. Magnitude, exactly: at Q = 0.3122*S (the G99 0.95 pf point) available P = 0.95*S, a 5.0% derate; at Q = 0.5*S available P = 0.866*S, a 13.4% derate; at Q = 0.6*S, 20.0%. That derate applies simultaneously to every MW-denominated revenue line, so the revenue error is roughly linear in the derate across the whole ancillary and CM stack, not a small correction to one line. It is also time-varying, because the host's reactive demand is time-varying, so a single average derate misstates the periods that matter most.
- **How to implement:** Polygon inner approximation: for k = 1..K (K = 12 is a good default; error of the regular K-gon inner approximation is 1 - cos(pi/K), i.e. 3.4% at K=8, 1.5% at K=12, 0.9% at K=16), impose P(t)*cos(2*pi*k/K) + Q(t)*sin(2*pi*k/K) <= S_rated * cos(pi/K). This is a set of K linear constraints per period, exact-from-inside (never sells headroom that does not exist), and its conservatism is bounded and reportable. Declare S_rated from the PCS nameplate and derate it for ambient temperature and AC voltage using the vendor's PQ capability chart — do not assume the chart is temperature-independent. Add: (i) G99 capability check |Q| >= 0.3122 * P at Registered Capacity as a design gate; (ii) the DNO connection agreement's pf band as -Q_max <= Q(t) <= +Q_max; (iii) reactive dispatch Q(t) chosen to hold site pf at or above the DNO's threshold (commonly 0.95) so the excess-reactive charge is zero, with the residual charge computed by the DNO's published formula where it is not. Then replace every occurrence of P_rated in the revenue constraints with P_avail(t) = the polygon-feasible P at the chosen Q(t). Sanity gate: assert that (sum of contracted ancillary MW) + |charge/discharge MW| <= P_avail(t) in every period, and fail the run if it does not.
- *Source:* Energy Networks Association, 'Engineering Recommendation G99', Issue 2 (2025), https://dcode.org.uk/assets/250307ena-erec-g99-issue-2-(2025).pdf — reactive capability requirements and PQ capability chart framework at the connection point
- *Source:* Aurora Power Consulting, 'G99 / Grid Code — Reactive Power Requirements', https://aurora-power.co.uk/g99-reactive-power/ — 'For Power Generating Modules between 1 MW and 10 MW when operating at registered capacity they must be capable of continuous operation between 0.95 lagging to 0.95 leading power factor' [industry technical consultancy, secondary]
- *Source:* The relevant DNO's Use of System Charging Statement (excess-reactive formula and the power-factor threshold) — site-specific, must be obtained per project [UNSOURCED as a general constant; each DNO publishes its own]
- *Source:* The specific PCS vendor's PQ capability chart with its temperature and AC-voltage dependence — project-specific vendor technical documentation [UNSOURCED as a general constant]

#### B-18 · SHOULD · Treat GB negative prices and free PV curtailment as explicit breakers of the LP-exactness conditions, and test for them.

- **Why:** Both Garifi's and Xu's relaxation conditions require strictly positive (or at least non-negative) energy prices. GB day-ahead, intraday and imbalance prices are negative in a material number of half-hours; a BTM site with a curtailment variable on its rooftop PV reproduces exactly Garifi's Fig. 3 failure case.
- **Error if omitted:** The model will produce physically impossible simultaneous charge/discharge in precisely the periods that matter most for a modern GB revenue stack (negative-price charging hours), and those periods will carry fabricated value.
- **How to implement:** Add an assertion over the solved schedule: for all t, min(P^ch_t, P^dis_t) <= tol. If prices can be negative or a curtailment variable P_c_t >= 0 exists, force the binary formulation. Do not rely on the relaxation.
- *Source:* Garifi, K. et al., 2018, Fig. 3 and accompanying text: with high PV penetration, free curtailment and alpha=beta=0, simultaneous charging and discharging is the optimal solution to the relaxed LP; setting alpha=0.001 restores non-simultaneous operation, arXiv:1805.00100v2
- *Source:* Xu, B. et al., 2018, IEEE TPWRS 33(2):2248-2259, statement that binary relaxation requires non-negative market clearing prices

#### B-19 · SHOULD · Represent efficiency as power-dependent (part-load) via a piecewise-linear loss curve, or at minimum state and defend the constant-efficiency error at low dispatch levels.

- **Why:** Laboratory-parametrised measurements show AC-AC round-trip efficiency is strongly non-constant: it collapses at low part load because inverter losses are largely fixed. A constant round-trip efficiency systematically overvalues small, frequent trades — exactly the trades a half-hourly optimiser will choose.
- **Error if omitted:** Hesse et al. report that ignoring both ageing and conversion losses overstates attainable arbitrage profit by up to 36% (German intraday 2017 data). Their measured overall system efficiency peaks near 91% at roughly one third of rated power, falls to about 87% at rated power (36 kW inverter), and collapses below about 60% for dispatch under roughly 2 kW; the round-trip loss factor exceeds 50% at very low power. A flat 88-92% assumption prices the low-power tail of the dispatch as if it were free.
- **How to implement:** Battery ohmic loss: P^b_loss(P_AC) = R_CH/DCH * I_b(P_AC)^2 (measured R_CH = 46.66 mOhm, R_DCH = 50.29 mOhm, U_OCV = 3.30 V for a muRata/Sony Fortelion LFP cell). Approximate the quadratic and the inverter look-up table by piecewise-linear (PWA) segments over |P_AC|. Couple AC and DC sides: P_AC = P^+_AC - P^-_AC; P^+_AC = P^+_b + L^+; P^-_AC = P^-_b - L^-; 0 <= P^+_AC, P^-_AC <= P_invmax. Hesse et al. PWA-approximate three separate components: battery dissipation, inverter dissipation, and charging cycle ageing.
- *Source:* Hesse, H.C. et al., 'Ageing and Efficiency Aware Battery Dispatch for Arbitrage Markets Using Mixed Integer Linear Programming', Energies 12(6):999, 2019, Section 2.5, eqs (6)-(15), Figs 3-4, and abstract (36% profit reduction), DOI 10.3390/en12060999
- *Source:* Kumtepeli, V., Hesse, H.C., Schimpe, M., Tripathi, A., Wang, Y., Jossen, A., 'Energy Arbitrage Optimization With Battery Storage: 3D-MILP for Electro-Thermal Performance and Semi-Empirical Aging Models', IEEE Access 8, 2020, DOI 10.1109/ACCESS.2020.3035504

#### B-20 · SHOULD · Run the realistic case as a rolling-horizon MILP with an explicit optimisation window, re-optimisation frequency and commitment window, and document all three.

- **Why:** 'Rolling horizon' is not a single thing. The bias depends on window length relative to the storage duration, on how much of each window is actually committed, and on whether the forecast is genuine or a backcast.
- **Error if omitted:** Two silently different models both called 'rolling horizon' can differ by more than the perfect-foresight premium itself. Without documenting the three parameters the P50 is not reproducible and cannot be defended to a lender.
- **How to implement:** Parameterise: H = optimisation horizon (periods), C = commitment window (periods actually implemented, C < H), F = re-optimisation frequency. Published choices: Hornek et al., auction markets H = 48 hourly products (2 days), C = 24, F = daily; continuous intraday H = 32 quarter-hours (8 h), C = 3 products, F = every 5 minutes. Xu et al. use a 24-hour horizon for all simulations. Sioshansi et al. optimise in two-week blocks using the previous two weeks' prices, then evaluate against actual prices. Rule of thumb from the literature: H should be at least several times the storage duration and should span at least one full diurnal price cycle.
- *Source:* Hornek, T. et al., 2025, Algorithm 1 and Section IV-B, arXiv:2501.07121v2
- *Source:* Xu, B. et al., 2018, Section V ('the optimization period is 24 hours for all simulations'), IEEE TPWRS 33(2):2248-2259
- *Source:* Sioshansi, R. et al., 2009, Section 3 (two-week backcast design), Energy Economics 31(2):269-277

#### B-21 · SHOULD · Include the parasitic auxiliary load (thermal management, HVAC, controls) as a continuous site-meter draw, separate from conversion losses.

- **Why:** Round-trip efficiency measured at the AC terminals of the inverter does not include container HVAC and BMS/EMS parasitics, which run whether or not the battery is cycling. Hesse et al. explicitly grey these components out of their model and flag it as a simplification.
- **Error if omitted:** A standing load of even 1-2% of rated power, drawn 8,760 hours a year at a retail BTM import tariff, is a material cost line that the AC-AC round-trip efficiency does not capture. Omitting it overstates net margin, and the error grows with low utilisation.
- **How to implement:** Add P_aux (MW, constant or temperature-dependent) into the site balance: 0 = -P_grid_t + P_load_t + P_aux + ... , so it is charged at the site import tariff. Distinguish from the conversion loss terms L^+ and L^- which scale with dispatch. Source the value from the vendor's datasheet at the site's design ambient conditions, and state it.
- *Source:* Hesse, H.C. et al., 2019, Section 2.1 and Fig. 1 — the energy management system (EMS) and thermal management system (TMS) 'are greyed out and not considered further in this work', i.e. explicitly excluded from the modelled efficiency, Energies 12(6):999

#### B-22 · SHOULD · Include ramp and minimum-dispatch constraints only where a market rule or inverter limit actually imposes them, and state which; do not import thermal-generator unit-commitment constructs by default.

- **Why:** Lithium-ion inverters ramp within seconds, so physical ramp limits are not binding at half-hourly resolution. Xu et al. note the battery's advantage over generators precisely on these grounds: 'it does not have a minimum stable generation, it can start immediately, and can remain idle until called'. Minimum up/down and minimum non-zero dispatch enter through market rules (minimum bid sizes, minimum contract capacity, linked service windows), not physics.
- **Error if omitted:** In the wrong direction, spurious ramp and min-up/min-down constraints add binaries, slow the solve, and understate revenue by preventing legitimate fast switching. In the right direction, omitting the market-imposed minimums (e.g. Balancing Reserve's 1 MW minimum contract capacity, EAC linked service windows) overstates the granularity at which capacity can be sold.
- **How to implement:** Physical ramp (only if the inverter datasheet gives one): |P_net_t - P_net_{t-1}| <= R_max * dt. Market minimum bid: r_{s,w} = 0 or r_{s,w} >= r_min_s, enforced with a binary z_{s,w}: r_min_s * z_{s,w} <= r_{s,w} <= Pbar * z_{s,w}. Linked windows: r_{s,w} = r_{s,w+1} within the linked block. Do NOT add minimum stable generation or start-up costs for a battery.
- *Source:* Xu, B. et al., 2018, Section V-D ('A BES is more flexible than generators at providing reserves because it does not have a minimum stable generation, it can start immediately, and can remain idle until called'), IEEE TPWRS 33(2):2248-2259
- *Source:* NESO, 'Balancing Reserve' (minimum contract capacity of 1MW or above), https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/balancing-reserve
- *Source:* NESO, 'Enduring Auction Capability (EAC)' (Linked Service Windows, default 2-hour minimum duration with consistent volumes), https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/enduring-auction-capability-eac

#### B-23 · CONSIDER · Enforce a per-period binary preventing simultaneous charge and discharge, OR prove for the specific instance that the LP relaxation is exact under a published sufficient condition, AND verify ex post that P^ch_t * P^dis_t = 0 in every period.

- **Why:** This is the well-studied result the brief asks for. Garifi et al. (2018) prove via KKT conditions that simultaneous charge/discharge is suboptimal — but only under stated conditions, and they exhibit a counterexample where it IS optimal. Arroyo (2022) published a direct counterexample to a claimed binary-free convex storage model.
