A relaxation that is exact for a wholesale-only arbitrage problem is not automatically exact for a BTM problem with PV curtailment, export limits and negative prices.
- **Error if omitted:** Silent physical infeasibility: the solver simultaneously charges and discharges to burn energy at zero cost, producing a dispatch that cannot be executed and an objective value that is an upper bound on nothing physical. Garifi et al. Fig 3 shows exactly this occurring under high PV penetration with free curtailment and zero cycling penalty — the reported schedule is unimplementable. In an underwriting context this is a fabricated revenue line.
- **How to implement:** Binary form: 0 <= P^ch_t <= u_t * Pbar_ch ; 0 <= P^dis_t <= (1 - u_t) * Pbar_dis ; u_t in {0,1}. PUBLISHED EXACTNESS CONDITIONS, state precisely: (Garifi Prop. 1) if c_e^(t) > 0 for ALL t, alpha,beta >= 0 (cycling penalties), and 0 < eta_c < 1 < eta_d, then any solution with 0 < P^ch_t, P^dis_t <= Pmax is suboptimal, in either (1) the no-export case with grid import P_grid^(t) > 0 for all t, or (2) the net-metering case. Proof rests on the KKT contradiction beta + lambdabar_dis + (eta_d/eta_c)(alpha + lambdabar_ch) + (eta_d/eta_c - 1)*c_e = 0, which is impossible because every term is >= 0 and the last is > 0. (Garifi Prop. 2) under the same efficiency and price conditions with no export, there EXISTS an optimal solution that is non-simultaneous, for any alpha,beta >= 0. (Garifi Remark 1) if c_e^(t) = 0 for some t, or under net metering/export, the guarantee requires alpha + beta > 0. (Engels et al., separate condition) splitting self-consumption power into charge and discharge parts, 'as long as c_cons > c_inj there is a cost for consuming energy and an optimal solution will always set P^{sc,c}_k * P^{sc,d}_k = 0 for all k' — i.e. import price strictly above export price is sufficient for a BTM site. FALLBACK if conditions fail: keep the binary, or add epsilon*(P^ch_t + P^dis_t) with epsilon > 0 to the objective and assert complementarity ex post.
- *Source:* Garifi, K., Baker, K., Christensen, D., Touri, B., 2018, Propositions 1 and 2, Remark 1, and Fig. 3 counterexample, arXiv:1805.00100v2
- *Source:* Arroyo, J.M., 'Ensuring Physically Realizable Storage Operation in the Unit Commitment Problem', IEEE Transactions on Power Systems 37(6):4966-4969, 2022, DOI 10.1109/TPWRS.2022.3193035
- *Source:* Engels, J., Claessens, B., Deconinck, G., 'Combined Stochastic Optimization of Frequency Control and Self-Consumption With a Battery', IEEE Transactions on Smart Grid 10(2):1971-1981, 2019, Section V-B, DOI 10.1109/TSG.2017.2785040, arXiv:1710.08331
- *Source:* Pozo, D., 'Convex Hull Formulations for Linear Modeling of Energy Storage Systems', IEEE Transactions on Power Systems 38(6), 2023, DOI 10.1109/TPWRS.2023.3304131
- *Source:* Xu, B., Zhao, J., Zheng, T., Litvinov, E., Kirschen, D.S., 'Factoring the Cycle Aging Cost of Batteries Participating in Electricity Markets', IEEE Transactions on Power Systems 33(2):2248-2259, 2018 (note: 'If some sufficient conditions are satisfied, for example the market clearing prices should not be negative, the binary variable v_t can be relaxed')


### C. Degradation, lifetime and warranty

#### C-01 · MUST · Price battery degradation inside the objective using a CONVEX piecewise-linear marginal cycle-aging cost with enough segments, not a flat per-MWh throughput charge.

- **Why:** Cycle life is strongly non-linear in cycle depth: Xu et al. report a 7 Wh NMC cell delivering over 50,000 cycles at 10% depth (35 kWh lifetime throughput) but only 500 cycles at 100% depth (3.5 kWh lifetime throughput) — a 10x difference in lifetime throughput for the same cell. A flat per-MWh cost cannot represent this and systematically misprices shallow cycling.
- **Error if omitted:** Quantified by Xu et al. on a 20 MW / 12.5 MWh BES against full-year 2015 ISO-NE SE-MASS prices: with NO aging cost the annual prorated profit was -$2101.3k and battery life expectancy 1.1 years; with a 1-segment cost, +$222.5k and 8.2 years; with a 16-segment cost, +$276.3k and 8.0 years (5-minute RTM). In the day-ahead market the no-cost model gave -$775.0k and 2.9-year life versus +$10k and 9.7 years with 16 segments. A single-segment approximation overstates marginal aging cost across most depths and leaves the battery idle unless price deviations are very large; relative error versus ex-post rainflow is roughly 35% at 1 cost block and negligible by 16.
- **How to implement:** Cycle depth stress function (NMC 18650, fitted): Phi(delta) = 5.24e-4 * delta^2.03, delta in [0,1], returning % of life lost per full cycle. Marginal cost of segment j: c_j = ( R / (eta_dis * E_rate) ) * J * [ Phi(j/J) - Phi((j-1)/J) ], where R is battery replacement cost ($) and J the number of segments. Piecewise cost c(delta_t) = c_j for delta_t in [(j-1)/J, j/J]. Decompose SoC by segment: e_{t,j} - e_{t-1,j} = M*(p^ch_{t,j}*eta_ch - p^dis_{t,j}/eta_dis); d_t = sum_j p^ch_{t,j}; g_t = sum_j p^dis_{t,j}; 0 <= e_{t,j} <= ebar_j; E_min <= sum_j e_{t,j} <= E_max. Aging cost C = sum_t sum_j M * c_j * p^dis_{t,j}. CRITICAL CONDITION (Xu Theorem 1): the segment costs must be non-decreasing, c_1 <= c_2 <= ... <= c_J (convex aging curve); only then does the LP naturally discharge from the shallowest segment first and reproduce rainflow. Theorem 2: as J -> infinity, C^pwl converges exactly to the rainflow benchmark C^ben.
- *Source:* Xu, B., Zhao, J., Zheng, T., Litvinov, E., Kirschen, D.S., 'Factoring the Cycle Aging Cost of Batteries Participating in Electricity Markets', IEEE Transactions on Power Systems 33(2):2248-2259, 2018, eqs (1)-(7), (24)-(27), Theorems 1-2, Table I, Fig. 5. DOI 10.1109/TPWRS.2017.2733339; arXiv:1707.04567v2

#### C-02 · MUST · Model calendar (shelf) ageing separately from cycle ageing, and feed cumulative state of health back into usable energy capacity in later years.

- **Why:** Cycle-only degradation models overstate late-life capacity; calendar-only models understate the cost of hard cycling. Both act on the same capacity variable, and the usable energy window shrinks over the asset life.
- **Error if omitted:** Year-10 revenue is computed on year-1 capacity. With Xu's 10%/year calendar loss alone, a 12.5 MWh nameplate is delivering materially less usable energy by year 8; combined with cycle fade, a lender's P50 built on constant capacity overstates late-life cashflow and therefore backend DSCR.
- **How to implement:** E_max(k) = SOH(k) * E_nom * (SOC_max - SOC_min)  [Hesse et al. eq. 19]. Life expectancy L^exp = 100% / (dL^cal + dL^cycle) where dL^cal is annual calendar loss (Xu et al. use 10%/yr, i.e. 10-year shelf life) and dL^cycle is annual cycle loss from the rainflow/PWL model. Degradation cost per unit SOH: C^ageing_usage = ( -dSOH / (1 - k_EOL) ) * C^inv_bat with k_EOL = 0.8 (end of life at 80% SOH) [Hesse et al. eq. 5]. Re-solve the dispatch MILP each project year with the degraded E_max and any retained power derating.
- *Source:* Xu, B. et al., 2018, Section V-A (battery shelf life 10 years; 10% annual self life loss) and eq. (27), IEEE TPWRS 33(2):2248-2259
- *Source:* Hesse, H.C. et al., 2019, eqs (4), (5), (19), Energies 12(6):999, DOI 10.3390/en12060999

#### C-03 · MUST · Validate the dispatch model against an independent ex-post recomputation of degradation and losses from the resulting schedule, not just against the objective value.

- **Why:** The optimiser's own objective is computed with its own approximations. Xu et al. build exactly this check: take the optimal schedule, convert it to a percentage SoC series, run true rainflow counting on it, and compare the resulting life loss to what the model charged for.
- **Error if omitted:** The model can be internally consistent and externally wrong. Xu et al. show relative error against the rainflow benchmark of roughly 35% with a single cost block, falling to near zero by 16 blocks — without the ex-post check there is no way to know which regime the model is in.
- **How to implement:** After solve: sigma_t = sum_j ehat_{t,j} / E_rate (percentage SoC series); run rainflow on sigma to obtain cycle depths delta_i; compute benchmark life loss L = sum_i Phi(delta_i); relative error epsilon = |Chat - R*L| / (R*L) where Chat is the aging cost the model charged. Require epsilon below a stated threshold, and increase the number of PWL segments until it is met. Apply the same idea to conversion losses: recompute total losses from the schedule using the measured loss curve and compare to the modelled losses.
- *Source:* Xu, B. et al., 2018, eqs (25)-(26), Fig. 5, and Section V-C, IEEE TPWRS 33(2):2248-2259

#### C-04 · MUST · Model degradation as a state variable that reduces usable energy capacity and therefore revenue capability over time, and fund augmentation as a scheduled capital event within the debt term.

- **Why:** Modo's assumption set has roughly 67% of original capacity remaining after 10,000 cycles (~12-15 years), with 88% at 2,500 cycles, 79% at 5,000 and 72% at 7,500. Degradation is separately identified as roughly 3% of the gap between modelled and achieved revenues. Augmentation is real cash: Gore Street budgeted £18-22m to take 129.8 MW from 1 hour to 2 hours, implying roughly £139-169/kWh of incremental installed energy capacity.
- **Error if omitted:** Holding capacity flat overstates late-life revenue and understates capex, compounding into a large NPV error and a materially overstated terminal value. Worse, it hides the mid-life cash call that lenders will want reserved for, which is the difference between a covenant breach and a smooth amortisation.
- **How to implement:** Track SoH_y as a function of cumulative equivalent full cycles and calendar age: SoH = f(EFC, t) calibrated to the manufacturer curve. UsableEnergy_y = E_nameplate x SoH_y. Revenue capability scales with UsableEnergy (for energy-limited streams) and with P_rated (for power-limited streams) separately. Model augmentation as a decision: at the year where SoH falls below a trigger (or where the Capacity Market derating or contracted duration obligation would be breached), incur AugmentationCapex = incremental_kWh x £/kWh_at_that_date, funded from reserve, from a capex facility, or from equity. Reduce the trigger year's revenue for augmentation downtime — Gore Street selected sites specifically for 'relatively quick upgrade times and limited downtime'.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Modelling Assumptions' (UK/Europe, Jul 2026 vintage) — ~67% of original capacity after 10,000 cycles (~12-15 years); 88% at 2,500, 79% at 5,000, 72% at 7,500 cycles; BESS project life 15 years, https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jul-2026/uk-europe/model-assumptions/
- *Source:* Gore Street Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Audited Full Year Results to 31 March 2025', 2025 — £18-22m to augment Stony (79.9MW) and Ferrymuir (49.9MW) from 1h to 2h, https://www.gsenergystoragefund.com/docs/librariesprovider22/archive/results/prelims-2025.pdf
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Model Calibration' (GB, Jan 2026 vintage) — capacity degradation ~3% of the modelled-to-actual gap, https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jan-2026/uk-europe/gb/model-calibration/

#### C-05 · SHOULD · If a warranty imposes an annual throughput or equivalent-cycle cap, model it as a hard constraint OR as the priced degradation cost — never both, and never neither.

- **Why:** Double-counting a cycling limit (as both a constraint and a cost) understates revenue; omitting both lets the optimiser cycle the asset out of warranty. Xu et al. specifically criticise the practice of artificially capping cycles per day because it 'prevents operators from taking advantage of a BES's operational flexibility and significantly lessens its profitability'.
- **Error if omitted:** Without either, the no-cost dispatch drives battery life expectancy from 8.0 years to 1.1 years (Xu et al., ISO-NE 5-minute RTM, full-year 2015) while reporting revenue as if the asset survived. With both, the asset is over-constrained and revenue is understated by an unquantified margin.
- **How to implement:** Full equivalent cycles: FEC = ( 1 / (2 * E_nom) ) * sum_t ( P^ch_t * eta_c + P^dis_t / eta_d ) * dt  [Hornek et al. eq. 7; Hesse et al. eq. 4 gives the equivalent integral form FEC(t) = 0.5 * integral |dSOC/dtau| dtau]. Warranty constraint: FEC_year <= N_warranty. Decide explicitly whether the warranty cap binds (constraint) or the economics bind (priced degradation), and document which. If the warranty cap is binding in the solution, that is a signal the asset is mis-sized, not a result to report.
- *Source:* Hornek, T. et al., 2025, eq. (7), arXiv:2501.07121v2
- *Source:* Hesse, H.C. et al., 2019, eq. (4), Energies 12(6):999
- *Source:* Xu, B. et al., 2018, Section II-A (critique of fixed cycle caps) and Table I (life expectancy 1.1 vs 8.0 years), IEEE TPWRS 33(2):2248-2259

#### C-06 · SHOULD · Re-run the dispatch optimisation for each project year on that year's degraded capacity and that year's forward price curve, rather than scaling a single year's result.

- **Why:** Degradation shrinks the energy window and price curves change shape (spreads, negative-price frequency, ancillary saturation). The relationship between capacity and revenue is non-linear, so a scalar degradation factor applied to year-1 revenue is not a valid approximation.
- **Error if omitted:** Late-year revenue error in an unknown direction. Because arbitrage value is concave in duration (Sioshansi et al. show 9-10 hours captures 90% of the maximum value, so the marginal value of energy capacity falls steeply), a 1-4 hour asset losing capacity is on the steep part of that curve and loses revenue faster than proportionally.
- **How to implement:** For y in 1..N: solve the full-year MILP with E_max(y) = SOH(y) * E_nom * (SOC_max - SOC_min) and Pbar(y) from any power derating, on year-y prices; feed the resulting net margin into the cashflow. Update SOH(y+1) from the cycle count actually produced by year y plus calendar ageing — this closes the loop between dispatch and degradation that a fixed degradation curve breaks.
- *Source:* Hesse, H.C. et al., 2019, eq. (19), Energies 12(6):999
- *Source:* Xu, B. et al., 2018, eq. (27), IEEE TPWRS 33(2):2248-2259
- *Source:* Sioshansi, R. et al., 2009, Figs 4-5 (marginal arbitrage value of energy capacity; 90% of maximum value at 9-10 hours of storage across efficiencies from 50% to 90%), Energy Economics 31(2):269-277

#### C-07 · SHOULD · Treat the degradation price as a SHADOW PRICE derived inside the model, not an exogenous £/MWh input. Formulate the multi-year problem with a single end-of-life or warranty constraint linking all years (cumulative equivalent full cycles, or cumulative capacity fade to the 80%-of-BOL threshold), extract the dual variable on that constraint as the marginal value of remaining state of health (£ per % SoH, convertible to £/MWh throughput), and iterate to consistency: assume a price, solve all years, check the realised end-of-life date against the assumed one, update, repeat until the assumed and realised retirement dates agree to within one year. Report the converged shadow price as a model output. Where a hard annual throughput cap is used instead, extract and report the dual on the cap and verify that it reproduces the priced-degradation solution.

- **Why:** A myopic year-by-year solve gives year 1 no incentive to preserve life for year 8, so the intertemporal opportunity cost of a cycle is simply absent from the objective. The cap and the price are Lagrangian duals of each other — the correct constraint-side and price-side formulations of the same problem — so offering them as unrelated alternatives without extracting the dual discards the one number that makes the model self-consistent. Xu et al. established the semi-empirical rainflow cycle-counting degradation model that makes the constraint tractable; Shi, Xu, Tan, Kirschen and Zhang showed the resulting degradation cost is convex and can be embedded in the control objective with a characterisable optimality gap.
- **Error if omitted:** An arbitrary exogenous price fails in one of two directions and the model cannot tell you which. Too low: the asset is cycled to death in the first high-price years, the warranty is breached, and the terminal years of the DSCR profile — precisely the years a lender cares about — are empty. Too high: the asset sits idle through years of positive-margin arbitrage. Neither error is detectable from the objective value alone, because both are optimal for the price that was assumed. The same missing number also breaks the build decision: the shadow price of SoH is exactly what determines whether to buy more MWh at BOL or accept deeper cycling, and it is the natural input to sizing the augmentation reserve.
- **How to implement:** Variables: throughput T_y (MWh discharged) per project year y; capacity C_y with C_y = C_0 * f(cumulative stress) or a linearised C_y = C_{y-1} - a*T_y - b (calendar term). Constraint (EOL): C_Y >= 0.80 * C_0 at the end of the underwriting horizon, OR sum_y T_y <= T_max from the warranty. Solve the full multi-year MILP (or a Benders/rolling decomposition with a coupling variable) and read lambda = dual of the EOL constraint. Convert: marginal degradation cost per MWh discharged = lambda * (dC/dT) = lambda * a, in £/MWh. Fixed-point loop: initialise lambda_0 (e.g. from replacement capex / expected lifetime throughput), solve, compute realised EOL year, set lambda_{k+1} by secant update on (realised EOL - target EOL), stop when |change| < 1 year and |lambda change| < 5%. Cross-validation gate (mandatory): run the identical model twice — once with the hard cap, once with lambda priced into the objective and the cap removed — and assert that the cap's dual equals the priced lambda to within tolerance and that the dispatch profiles match. If they do not, the degradation model is not convex in the throughput variable and must be reformulated (piecewise-linear convex is the standard fix). Report lambda in the underwriting pack alongside the DSCR.
- *Source:* B. Xu, A. Oudalov, A. Ulbig, G. Andersson, D. S. Kirschen, 'Modeling of Lithium-Ion Battery Degradation for Cell Life Assessment', IEEE Transactions on Smart Grid 9(2):1131-1140, 2018, DOI 10.1109/TSG.2016.2578950
- *Source:* Y. Shi, B. Xu, Y. Tan, D. Kirschen, B. Zhang, 'Optimal Battery Control Under Cycle Aging Mechanisms in Pay for Performance Settings', IEEE Transactions on Automatic Control, 2018, arXiv:1709.05715 — degradation cost shown convex with characterised optimality gap
- *Source:* Y. Shi, B. Xu, Y. Tan, D. Kirschen, B. Zhang, 'Optimal Regulation Response of Batteries Under Cycle Aging Mechanisms', IEEE CDC 2017, DOI 10.1109/CDC.2017.8263750
- *Source:* Code reference: https://github.com/Yuanyuan-Shi/Cycle-based-Battery-Controller


### D. Revenue streams — GB market mechanics

#### D-01 · MUST · Model the BM as a pay-as-bid, per-BM-Unit instruction mechanism operating after Gate Closure, not as a cleared market or a price-taking arbitrage opportunity. BM revenue must be computed as accepted deviation from the unit's Final Physical Notification, priced at the unit's own submitted bid/offer price for the relevant Bid-Offer pair.

- **Why:** The BM 'solves a multitude of energy and system needs' and is explicitly described by LCP Delta as 'an imperfect market'. Prices are pay-as-bid per BOA, and the settlement cashflow is defined in BSC Section T paragraph 3.11. Treating BM revenue as volume × system price, or as a clearing market, overstates both volume and price.
- **Error if omitted:** Overstates BM revenue in both directions. A model that clears the asset at the marginal system price whenever in-merit will assume ~100% acceptance where reality in 2023 was 6–7% for batteries, and will attribute the marginal price rather than the unit's own submitted price.
- **How to implement:** For each settlement period t and BM Unit u: BM_revenue(u,t) = Σ_p [ Offer_volume(u,p,t) × Offer_price(u,p) − Bid_volume(u,p,t) × Bid_price(u,p) ], where p indexes accepted Bid-Offer pairs and volumes are measured as the area between the FPN trajectory and the BOA-instructed trajectory over the period (BSC Section T §3.11). Offer volumes are positive deviations (export up / import down), Bid volumes negative deviations.
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report — Report prepared for NESO', 2024, pp.9, 14, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* Elexon, 'BSC Section T: Settlement and Trading Charges', v34.0, https://bscdocs.elexon.co.uk/bsc/bsc-section-t-settlement-and-trading-charges/v-34-0/id-6393286b3bed430c2acd1836
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB Methodology — Indices', accessed 2026, https://modoenergy.com/methodology/gb

#### D-02 · MUST · Represent the timing architecture explicitly: Gate Closure at 1 hour ahead of the settlement period, a BM Window of 89 minutes, PN/FPN submission, and Bid-Offer Data submission by 11:00 day-ahead with defaulting rules. The model must not allow BM decisions to be re-optimised with hindsight inside the delivery period.

- **Why:** NESO states that gate closure of 60 minutes from the end of the current settlement period means the earliest an instruction can be sent is 89 minutes prior to delivery; the BM Window is 89 minutes long. GC0166 confirms indicative parameters are submitted before 11:00 day-ahead and defaulted if not submitted.
- **Error if omitted:** A perfect-foresight MILP that co-optimises wholesale and BM within the same time index will manufacture spreads that no real operator can capture, systematically inflating IRR. It also mis-states the deliverability test: any BOA a unit must be able to honour was issued up to 89 minutes ahead.
- **How to implement:** Two-stage decision structure. Stage 1 (by gate closure, information set I_{t-1h}): choose FPN(t) and the BOD price/volume ladder. Stage 2 (in-window): NESO chooses BOA(t) from the ladder. Model Stage 2 as an exogenous stochastic acceptance process conditional on Stage 1, never as a decision variable of the asset owner. Enforce that BOD prices are constant across the settlement period once submitted.
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, pp.9, 26, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'GC0166: Introducing new Balancing Mechanism Parameters for Limited Duration Assets — Final Modification Report', 8 July 2025, pp.9-10, https://www.neso.energy/document/364346/download

#### D-03 · MUST · Declare MEL and MIL as the maximum power the unit can sustain for 30 minutes (the '30-minute rule', effective 11 March 2024), not nameplate power. MEL/MIL must therefore be a function of state of charge and duration, and the model must recognise that this convention reduces declared availability for short-duration assets.

- **Why:** Modo Energy documents that before 11 March 2024 MEL/MIL were declared as the maximum power deliverable for 15 minutes, and after that date for 30 minutes. NESO's GC0166 report confirms NESO 'use Maximum Import Limits (MIL) and Maximum Export Limits (MEL) and the "30-minute" rule (previously "15-minute" rule)'. Modo explicitly notes total declared power availability falls under the 30-minute convention.
- **Error if omitted:** Declaring nameplate MEL for a 1-hour battery overstates the MW the unit can offer into the BM by up to ~50% at low SoC, and overstates the volume the control room believes is available. Conversely, a model that keeps the pre-2024 15-minute convention understates the accessible BM volume, which Modo estimated the 30-minute change could triple.
- **How to implement:** MEL(t) = min( P_rated , (SoC(t) − SoC_min) × E_cap × η_dis / 0.5h ) and MIL(t) = min( P_rated , (SoC_max − SoC(t)) × E_cap / (0.5h × η_ch) ), with 0.5h replaced by 0.25h for pre-March-2024 backtests. Feed MEL/MIL as the upper bound on offer/bid volume available in the merit stack.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism: shift to 30-minute rule announced by ESO', Feb 2024, https://modoenergy.com/research/balancing-mechanism-30-minute-rule-change-battery-energy-storage-skip-rates
- *Source:* NESO, 'GC0166 Final Modification Report', 8 July 2025, p.4, https://www.neso.energy/document/364346/download

#### D-04 · MUST · Implement GC0166's replacement of the 30-minute rule: Maximum Delivery Offer (MDO) and Maximum Delivery Bid (MDB) in MWh, plus a Future State of Energy (FSoE) model, and phase them in on the published timeline (Authority approval 22 Oct 2025; transition from June 2026; full implementation by early November 2026). Model MDO/MDB as net of energy reserved for ancillary service commitments.

- **Why:** GC0166 introduces MDO/MDB as the declared maximum energy (MWh) deliverable against offers/bids in the BM Window, superseding the crude MEL/MIL 30-minute proxy, with defaults of +9999/−9999 MWh for non-energy-limited units. The definition is explicitly net of 'the volume of energy required to satisfy any other Ancillary Service commitments'. NESO plans up to 33 hours ahead using the FSoE model.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that assumes the 30-minute MEL derating persists past 2026 will understate accessible BM volume for 2h+ assets (GC0166's stated purpose is 'increased economic dispatch of Electricity Storage Modules' and longer dispatches). A model that ignores the netting of ancillary commitments will double-count the same MWh in DC/DR/DM and BM.
- **How to implement:** Add per-period declared energy limits: Σ_τ∈window Offer_MWh(τ) ≤ MDO(t) and Σ_τ∈window Bid_MWh(τ) ≤ MDB(t), where MDO(t) = f(SoC(t), η, P_rated) − E_reserved_for_AS(t). Model FSoE forecast error as ≤10% to 4h ahead and ≤20% for 4–33h ahead, per NESO's accuracy requirement. Allow MDO/MDB redeclaration only on the four permitted triggers (technical fault, BOA issued, frequency event depleting AS energy, non-zero PN after BM Window change).
- *Source:* NESO, 'GC0166: Introducing new Balancing Mechanism Parameters for Limited Duration Assets — Final Modification Report', 8 July 2025, pp.3-11, https://www.neso.energy/document/364346/download
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'GC0166 Grid Code changes' (Authority approval 22 October 2025), https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/gc0166-grid-code-changes
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'NESO Roadmap 2026: Key dates for battery energy storage stakeholders in Great Britain', 2026, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-neso-national-energy-system-operator-2026-energy-storage-roadmap-p462-connections-reform-balancing-mechanism

#### D-05 · MUST · Model the full Bid-Offer Data ladder (multiple bid-offer pairs per settlement period), not a single price point, and evaluate skip/acceptance separately for each price band.

- **Why:** LCP Delta states BMU operators submit Bid-Offer pairs 'for each settlement period (up to 5 pairs)', and explicitly changed methodology in Phase 2 because 'The ESO considers all BOD price bands separately' — Phase 1's use of only the first, lowest-cost pair was a known defect.
- **Error if omitted:** Collapsing the ladder to one price makes skip rate look like a single scalar when in fact acceptance is band-specific; it also removes the model's ability to test the commercially important question of whether pricing more aggressively buys acceptance.
- **How to implement:** Index BOD by (unit u, pair p ∈ {1..5}, period t) with price π(u,p) and volume band [V_{p-1}, V_p]. Build the in-merit stack from all bands. Acceptance probability α(u,p,t) is band-specific. BM volume = Σ_p α(u,p,t)·V_band(u,p,t).
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, pp.9, 18, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download

#### D-06 · MUST · Adopt NESO's published definition of skip rate exactly and state which of the two variants and which of the five exclusion stages the model is parameterised on. Never mix stages or variants.

- **Why:** NESO defines two: 'All BM Skip Rate' = (volume of units in merit not taken) / (total volume of accepted units, including system actions), and 'Post System Actions (PSA) Skip Rate' = (volume of units in merit not taken) / (total volume of units accepted to manage energy imbalance). LCP Delta then reports five nested exclusion stages. The same underlying data yields all-technology Offer skip rates of 71% (Stage 1) down to 48% (Stage 5) for 2023 — a 23 percentage-point spread purely from definition.
- **Error if omitted:** Silently importing a headline percentage from a press article and applying it as an acceptance derating can be wrong by 20–45 percentage points. Applied to the Gale et al. sensitivity (~£12k/MW/yr per 10 points), that is a £24k–£54k/MW/yr revenue error — enough to invert a financing decision.
- **How to implement:** Store skip rate as a tagged object: {variant ∈ {AllBM, PSA}, stage ∈ {1..5}, direction ∈ {bid, offer}, technology, period}. Skip_Rate = Volume_in_merit_not_taken / Denominator(variant). Require the tag to be present before the number can be used; reject untagged constants at model load.
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, pp.8, 32, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Skip rates', industry information page, accessed 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/skip-rates

#### D-07 · MUST · Split BM volume into 'system-flagged / constraint' actions and 'energy balancing' actions, and apply a much higher skip rate to the former. Do not allow a BTM asset to earn constraint-management revenue unless it is electrically located to relieve the constraint.

- **Why:** LCP Delta excludes system-flagged acceptances at Stage 3 because they 'are taken to alleviate transmission constraints in specific regions (so only a subset of assets located in that region can be utilised)'. Modo measured system-flagged skip rates of 97% (Dec 2023) falling only to 91% (Aug 2024) versus energy actions falling from >90% to 76% — an 18 percentage-point gap — and noted constraint actions were not dispatched via the Open Balancing Platform. System-flagged actions were 39% of total BM volume.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that applies a single blended skip rate to all BM volume will credit a battery in, say, the Thames Valley with revenue that only a Scottish or constrained-boundary asset can earn, or vice versa. Since ~39% of BM volume is system-flagged, this is a first-order error, and it is the single most location-sensitive line in the stack.
- **How to implement:** Partition per-period BM requirement R(t) = R_sys(t) + R_energy(t). Apply s_sys(region, t) and s_energy(t) separately. For a BTM site, set the accessible share of R_sys to zero unless the site's GSP group / constraint boundary is demonstrated to be on the correct side of an active constraint. Default for an unconstrained-region BTM asset: accessible BM volume = R_energy(t) only.
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, pp.24, 27, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Skip rates: How have these improved for BESS in 2024?', 2024, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/bess-skip-rates-balancing-mechanism-2024-update-system-energy-actions
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism: how deep is the market for battery energy storage?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/10374

#### D-08 · MUST · Model the dynamic-parameter exclusion tests that NESO's own skip-rate methodology applies, and confirm the modelled asset passes them: MZT, MNZT, NDZ, SEL/SIL and run-up/run-down rates. A battery must be parameterised so it is not excluded at Stage 2 or Stage 5.

- **Why:** LCP Delta's exclusions remove volumes where MZT or MNZT ≥ 12 hours or NDZ ≥ 90 minutes (Stage 2), and at Stage 5 remove actions that would sync a unit with MNZT or NDZ ≥ 31 minutes or desync a unit with MZT ≥ 31 minutes, plus volumes outside the SEL–MEL / SIL–MIL ranges. NESO's dispatch-transparency 'Incomplete' and 'Unit commitment' reason codes turn on the same parameters, and BODs with 'insufficient performance in speed, minimum change, commitment, and less than 1MW' are excluded from being counted as alternative actions.
- **Error if omitted:** Mis-declared parameters silently make the asset invisible to the control room's optimiser, producing zero BM revenue in reality while the model shows healthy revenue. It also means the model cannot explain why a real comparable asset underperforms.
- **How to implement:** Assert at model build: MZT ≤ 5 min, MNZT ≤ 5 min, NDZ ≤ 2 min, SEL ≤ 25 MW (trivially satisfied at 0.1–5 MW), and run-up/run-down rate ≥ P_rated / NTO. Explicitly reject any parameter set that would trip the ≥31-minute Stage 5 exclusion. Add a hard model gate: if the parameter set fails, BM revenue = 0.
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, pp.23-24, 29, 54, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Balancing Reserve Guidance Document', Version 7, June 2026, pp.8-10, https://www.neso.energy/document/303546/download

#### D-09 · MUST · Model bid and offer skip rates separately, with separate parameters, and never average them.

- **Why:** LCP Delta's results are consistently different by direction: battery Offer skip 93% (2023) / 83% (2024 ytd) vs Bid skip 94% / 78% at Stage 3; at Stage 5, Offer 92%/81% vs Bid 94%/77%. All-technology Bid skip rates actually rose in 2024 at Stages 1–2 (81%→84%, 80%→83%) while Offer rates fell.
- **Error if omitted:** A charging-side (bid) strategy and a discharging-side (offer) strategy have materially different acceptance economics and different trajectories over time. Averaging hides the fact that in some periods bid skip rates worsened while offer skip rates improved.
- **How to implement:** Maintain s_bid(·) and s_offer(·) as distinct time series in the parameter store; require both to be sourced from the same variant/stage; disallow a single scalar 'skip rate' input anywhere in the code path.
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, pp.2, 32-34, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download

#### D-10 · MUST · Gate BM revenue on a demonstrated route to BM access. For a BTM C&I asset this means Virtual Lead Party + Secondary BM Unit under P344, with a minimum Secondary BMU size of 1 MW, aggregated only within a single GSP Group. Assets below 1 MW that cannot be aggregated within one GSP Group must be modelled with zero BM revenue.

- **Why:** P344 created the VLP role and Secondary BMUs with a minimum size of 1 MW; SVA half-hourly metered generation/demand 'within a single GSP Group' can be allocated to a Secondary BM Unit. NESO's registration guide confirms Wider Access 'introduc[es] the concept of a Virtual Lead Party (VLP) who will be able to register BMUs as small as 1MW' and that only a VLP can register Secondary BM Units.
- **Error if omitted:** For a 0.1–1 MW BTM battery this is a binary revenue gate. Modelling BM revenue for a standalone 500 kW site with no aggregation partner in the same GSP Group is not a small error — it is revenue that legally cannot be earned.
- **How to implement:** Boolean gate: BM_eligible = (P_site ≥ 1 MW) OR (Σ P_portfolio_in_same_GSP_group ≥ 1 MW AND aggregation agreement in place). If false, BM_revenue = 0 for all t and the model must say so explicitly in the output rather than silently returning zero.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Registration guide: Balancing Mechanism Units (BMUs)', June 2026, pp.5, 10, https://www.neso.energy/document/290631/download
- *Source:* Elexon, 'P344 Wider Access and Project TERRE' / Secondary BM Units, https://www.elexon.co.uk/bsc/operational/balancing-mechanism-units/
- *Source:* Watt-Logic, 'Opening access to the Balancing Mechanism for smaller aggregated assets', 3 October 2018, https://watt-logic.com/2018/10/03/balancing-mechanism/

#### D-11 · MUST · For a battery physically behind the customer's supply meter, model the P375 Asset Metering VLP route: an Asset Metering System with its own AMSID behind the boundary point, settled either by direct allocation or by differencing against the boundary-point MSID. Include the cost and obligation of the asset meter, meter operator agent and half-hourly data collector.

- **Why:** The AMVLP role 'allows the participant to register SBMUs associated with meters behind the site Boundary Point'. Two methods exist: direct allocation of the AMSID pair volume to the SBMU, or differencing where the AMSID volume is deducted from the boundary-point MSID volume. The AMVLP must be qualified through self-assessment and must appoint a Meter Operator Agent (or Asset Metering MOA) and a Half Hourly Data Collector.
- **Error if omitted:** Without asset metering, a BTM battery's BM delivery is invisible in settlement — the boundary point only shows net site flow, so battery output is confounded with the customer's load. The model would credit BM delivery that cannot be evidenced, and would omit the metering capex/opex and the agent appointments.
- **How to implement:** Add a BTM_metering_capex and annual BTM_metering_opex line (asset meter, MOA, HHDC). Add a settlement-basis flag ∈ {direct_allocation, differencing}. Under differencing, delivered BM volume = BoundaryPoint_MSID_volume − AMSID_volume, so site load noise enters the delivery measurement — model a delivery-verification risk margin. Under direct allocation, delivered volume = AMSID volume.
- *Source:* Enegen, 'An explanation of the Asset Metering Virtual Lead Party or AMVLP role', https://enegen.co.uk/content/generation/Asset-Metering-Virtual-Lead-Party-AMVLP.shtml
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'P375 Final Authority Decision', February 2021, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/system/files/docs/2021/02/p375_final_authority_decision.pdf
- *Source:* Elexon, 'Issue 70: Settlement of Secondary BM Units using metering at...', https://www.elexon.co.uk/bsc/documents/change/issues/51-100/issue-70-issue-report/

#### D-12 · MUST · Model the BM registration and connection lead times and the associated systems obligations as a hard constraint on the revenue start date: EDT, EDL (or Wider Access API), control/system telephony, and operational metering. Minimum 6 months from BMU ID request where new EDT/EDL/control telephony is needed; minimum 2 months otherwise; SMP application at least 30 working days before the Elexon Effective From Date; fixed-line circuits can take up to 6 months to install.

- **Why:** NESO's registration guide states the NESO BMU ID 'should be obtained a minimum of 6 months in advance of your anticipated connection date' to allow installation of EDT/EDL/control telephony; at least 2 months for data upload if no new comms are needed; and 'the application should be completed in SMP at least thirty working days prior to the ELEXON "Effective From Date"'. Active BMUs must supply operational metering data; the Grid Code standard for BM participation is 1 Hz.
- **Error if omitted:** Assuming BM revenue from COD overstates year-1 revenue by up to 6 months of BM income and mis-times the debt service coverage in the first period — precisely the period lenders scrutinise. It also omits the WA API / EDL / telephony capex and the operational metering solution.
- **How to implement:** BM_revenue_start = max(COD, BMU_ID_request_date + 6 months if new_comms else 2 months, SMP_application + 30 working days). Add capex/opex lines for EDT/EDL or WA API connectivity, control telephony, and operational metering (1 Hz per Grid Code BC4.4, with sub-asset-level relaxation possible).
- *Source:* NESO, 'Registration guide: Balancing Mechanism Units (BMUs)', June 2026, pp.6, 8, 11, https://www.neso.energy/document/290631/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Balancing Reserve Guidance Document', Version 7, June 2026, p.8 (Grid Code BC4.4, 1 Hz operational metering), https://www.neso.energy/document/303546/download

#### D-13 · MUST · Model the imbalance/cash-out regime correctly for the entity bearing the risk: single imbalance price (SBP = SSP), PAR = 1 MWh since 1 November 2018, VoLL £6,000/MWh. Then model the fact that under P344 a VLP is liable only for Trading Charges relating to BM participation, so the Secondary BMU's BM volumes flow through ABSVD rather than creating imbalance exposure for the VLP.

- **Why:** Elexon confirms a single price calculation so SBP = SSP, PAR of 1 MWh from 1 November 2018 (a volume-weighted average of the most expensive 1 MWh of actions), and VoLL set at £6,000/MWh from 1 November 2018. Elexon further states 'the principle behind the P344 solution is that the VLP is responsible only for Trading Charges relating to participation in the BM', with ABSVD adjusting the Supplier's position.
- **Error if omitted:** Two opposite errors. (i) Modelling imbalance exposure on the VLP overstates risk and understates value. (ii) Ignoring imbalance entirely misses the real exposure for a supplier-route or self-supplied BTM asset, where PAR1 makes cash-out extremely volatile (Jan 2025 monthly average long £76.08/MWh vs short £179.38/MWh, against MIP £114.09/MWh) and capped only at VoLL £6,000/MWh.
- **How to implement:** If route = VLP/Secondary BMU: imbalance_charge = 0 for BM-instructed volumes; VLP pays Trading Charges only; the Supplier's position is adjusted by ABSVD (and the model must confirm the VLP submits delivered-volume data, which Elexon flags is not consistently done). If route = Supplier Additional BMU: Imbalance_cost(t) = (Metered_volume(t) − Contracted_volume(t) − BOA_volume(t)) × SIP(t), with SIP derived on PAR1 and capped at ±£6,000/MWh.
- *Source:* Elexon, 'Imbalance Pricing', BSC settlement page, https://www.elexon.co.uk/bsc/settlement/imbalance-pricing/
- *Source:* Elexon, 'Imbalance Pricing Guidance' v15.0, 25 June 2020, https://www.elexon.co.uk/bsc/documents/training-guidance/bscguidance-notes/imbalance-pricing/
- *Source:* Elexon, 'Understanding Applicable Balancing Services Volume Data for Secondary BM Units', https://www.elexon.co.uk/bsc/article/understanding-applicable-balancing-services-volume-data-for-secondary-bm-units/
- *Source:* Elexon, 'System Prices Analysis Report: January 2025', https://elexon-backup.elexonhostings.co.uk/data/system-prices-analysis-report/

#### D-14 · MUST · Where the BTM asset also trades wholesale, model P415 (implemented 7 November 2024) as the route by which a VLP can monetise flexibility in the wholesale market via Deviation Volumes, with its compensation arrangements to the affected Supplier.

- **Why:** P415 'Facilitating access to wholesale markets for flexibility dispatched by VLPs' was implemented on 7 November 2024, allowing VLPs to trade Deviation Volumes independently of the customer's Supplier, and includes compensation arrangements for impacts of VLP actions. Before P415, BSC assigned all customer flexibility to the Supplier except where instructed in the BM or Replacement Reserve.
- **Error if omitted:** A pre-2024 model will either assume the BTM battery's wholesale value must be shared with or routed through the site's Supplier, or will assume free wholesale access that did not exist. Both mis-price the wholesale leg of the stack, which together with the BM is ~60% of the GB BESS revenue stack.
- **How to implement:** Route flag ∈ {supplier_route, VLP_BM_only, VLP_with_P415}. Under VLP_with_P415, wholesale revenue accrues to the VLP on Deviation Volumes net of the P415 compensation payable to the Supplier; model the compensation as a per-MWh deduction. Do not enable this route for settlement dates before 7 November 2024 in backtests.
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Decision on P415 Facilitating Access to Wholesale Markets for Flexibility Dispatched by VLPs', https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/ofgem-decision-p415-facilitating-access-wholesale-markets-flexibility-dispatched-vlps
- *Source:* Elexon, 'P415 Facilitating access to wholesale markets for flexibility dispatched by Virtual Lead Parties', https://www.elexon.co.uk/bsc/mod-proposal/p415/
- *Source:* Engage Consulting, 'A summary of BSC Modification P415', https://www.engage-consulting.co.uk/industry-news/a-summary-of-bsc-modification-p415-facilitating-access-to-wholesale-electricity-markets-for-flexibility-dispatched-by-virtual-lead-partners/

#### D-15 · MUST · Model Balancing Reserve as a distinct, BM-gated revenue line with an availability payment (£/MW/h, single clearing price) plus utilisation via BOA, and enforce its eligibility conditions — minimum 1 MW, response ≤10 minutes including NTO/NTB and ramping, NTO/NTB ≤2 minutes, and (for dispatch from zero) SEL ≤25 MW with MNZT ≤5 minutes / SIL ≤25 MW with MZT ≤5 minutes.

- **Why:** NESO's Balancing Reserve Guidance v7 (June 2026) specifies minimum contract size 1 MW, provider eligibility 'BM Units with a back up means of dispatch (control or system telephony) during contracted windows', response times up to 10 minutes including NTO/NTB and ramping, dispatch via BOA to contracted BM units, and the SEL/MNZT and SIL/MZT thresholds. A single clearing price in £/MW/hour is paid to all successful providers.
- **Error if omitted:** Balancing Reserve is only available to BM-registered units, so it must be gated on the same BM-access test. Modelling it as a standalone ancillary revenue for a non-BM BTM asset is simply wrong. Conversely, omitting it understates the value of achieving BM access, since it converts skip-exposed energy revenue into a contracted availability payment.
- **How to implement:** BR_revenue = Σ_windows [ ContractedMW × ClearingPrice(£/MW/h) × hours ] + Σ BOA utilisation. Gate on BM_eligible = TRUE and on the parameter tests above. Critically, reserve the energy: subtract contracted BR headroom/footroom from MDO/MDB and from the SoC available for wholesale arbitrage — NESO requires the unit 'be able to deliver the full contracted capacity per Service Window'.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Balancing Reserve Guidance Document', Version 7, June 2026, pp.6-10, https://www.neso.energy/document/303546/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Balancing Reserve', service page, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/balancing-reserve

#### D-16 · MUST · Model the current reserve product set with correct effective dates and BM/non-BM eligibility: Quick Reserve (Phase 2 open to non-BM units from the 2 September 2025 auction), Slow Reserve (Ofgem approval 9 October 2025), and STOR ending at 05:00 on 1 April 2026. Do not model STOR revenue beyond that date.

- **Why:** NESO states Quick Reserve Phase 2 went live with the first auction accessible to non-BM units on 2 September 2025; Ofgem approved Slow Reserve Service Terms and Procurement Rules on 9 October 2025; and 'the STOR service will end at 05:00 1st April 2026' per the Slow Reserve Transition Plan. From January 2026 DC, DM and DR are activated directly within the Open Balancing Platform.
- **Error if omitted:** A stack built on the 2019–2022 product set (FFR, STOR, DC-only) will carry revenue lines that no longer exist and will miss the non-BM routes that are the only reserve access for sub-1 MW BTM assets. Frequency response fell from 87% of GB BESS revenue in 2020–2022 to ~33% gross.
- **How to implement:** Product calendar table keyed on date, with eligibility flags {BM_required: bool}. Zero out STOR after 2026-04-01T05:00. Enable Quick Reserve for non-BM units from 2025-09-02. Co-optimise reserve commitments against MDO/MDB energy netting so the same MWh is not sold twice.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Quick Reserve', https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/quick-reserve
- *Source:* NESO, 'Slow Reserve', https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/slow-reserve
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'NESO Roadmap 2026', 2026, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-neso-national-energy-system-operator-2026-energy-storage-roadmap-p462-connections-reform-balancing-mechanism
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'How does a battery energy storage system make money?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/how-does-battery-energy-storage-make-money

#### D-17 · MUST · Enforce state-of-charge feasibility across the whole BM commitment: the asset must be able to honour any BOA issued from the Bid-Offer ladder it submitted, at any point in the 89-minute BM Window, given its SoC trajectory. Reserve headroom and footroom accordingly.

- **Why:** GC0166 exists precisely because 'Electricity Storage Modules ... can only import or export until their limited storage capacity is either fully charged or fully depleted', and NESO requires 'an explicit statement of the available energy for an offer or a bid' inside the BM Window because otherwise NESO would be 'making decisions that could affect the BM Units commercial position'. LCP Delta lists 'State of Charge' as a known limitation of its own analysis — each 5-minute period is treated independently.
- **Error if omitted:** A MILP that offers full power in every period without reserving energy will produce a schedule that is physically infeasible if accepted, and in reality generates non-delivery, breach of Grid Code obligations and potential loss of Balancing Reserve availability payments. It also inflates modelled BM volume.
- **How to implement:** For every period t and every submitted offer band: SoC(t) − Σ_{τ=t}^{t+89min} Offer_MWh(τ)/(E_cap·η_dis) ≥ SoC_min, and symmetrically SoC(t) + Σ Bid_MWh(τ)·η_ch/E_cap ≤ SoC_max. If the model submits volume it cannot deliver, it must be infeasible — do not soft-penalise.
- *Source:* NESO, 'GC0166 Final Modification Report', 8 July 2025, pp.3, 8, https://www.neso.energy/document/364346/download
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, p.42, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download

#### D-18 · MUST · Include a per-period binary preventing simultaneous charge and discharge, and prevent the model from booking a bid and an offer in the same direction-inconsistent way in the same period, while allowing for BOA 'unwind' actions as a distinct, contingent action type.

- **Why:** LCP Delta explicitly excludes 'unwinds' at Stage 4 because 'BOAs to reverse out (unwind) a previously accepted action ... only become available if the opposing bid or offer action is accepted' — i.e. reverse actions are real, but are contingent, not free-standing. Without the charge/discharge binary a MILP with round-trip losses can generate phantom revenue by charging and discharging simultaneously in negative-price periods.
- **Error if omitted:** Simultaneous charge/discharge is the classic MILP artefact: with negative wholesale prices (20 negative-price hours in October 2025, 54 in September 2025) a relaxed formulation will burn energy for profit. Separately, treating unwinds as freely available BM volume overstates BM revenue.
- **How to implement:** Introduce binary z(t) ∈ {0,1}: P_ch(t) ≤ P_max·z(t), P_dis(t) ≤ P_max·(1−z(t)). Model unwind volume U(t) as available only conditional on a prior BOA in the opposing direction: U(t) ≤ Σ_{τ<t} BOA_opposing(τ). If the binary is dropped for tractability, the model must prove no negative-price period allows simultaneous operation to pay.
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, pp.24, 28, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'ME BESS GB: Revenues climb to £77k/MW/year in October 2025', 6 November 2025, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/battery-energy-storage-revenues-gb-october-2025-record-balancing-mechanism-dispatch-rates

#### D-19 · MUST · Model BM revenue and wholesale revenue as co-optimised but sequentially constrained, with the FPN as the linking variable. The FPN chosen for wholesale purposes determines the volume and direction available to the BM.

- **Why:** LCP Delta: 'The BM generally becomes active post-gate closure, one hour ahead of delivery when all BMUs with their Final Physical Notification (FPN) flag set to true are required to provide the ESO with their FPNs. At this point all positions of assets are final with no further trading or self-dispatch optimisation available.' BM offer volume is measured from the FPN; a unit already at full export has no offer headroom.
- **Error if omitted:** Modelling the two streams additively double-counts the same MW. The peer-reviewed result that BM participation raises revenue 'by up to 250% relative to providing wholesale arbitrage alone' is a co-optimised result, not an additive one.
- **How to implement:** Offer_available(t) = MEL(t) − FPN(t); Bid_available(t) = FPN(t) − MIL(t) (with sign conventions for import). Single MILP over both markets with FPN(t) as a shared decision variable fixed at gate closure, then BM acceptance applied stochastically on top. Constrain: FPN(t) ∈ [MIL(t), MEL(t)].
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, p.9, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* Gale et al., 'Balancing with batteries', J. Energy Storage 166, 2026, DOI 10.1016/j.est.2026.122328, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2352152X26019924

#### D-20 · MUST · Source skip-rate parameters from NESO's published datasets (Skip Rate Summary; In-Merit All-BM; In-Merit Post System Action; Exclusion Reasons; Stage 5 PSA Skip Rate by Technology; and the separate Demand Side Flexibility Unit datasets), and record that no NESO-consistent data exists before December 2024.

- **Why:** NESO publishes these datasets on its Data Portal at 30-minute granularity, updated daily, with technology-specific skip rate data from December 2024 onwards; NESO states historical data before December 2024 is unavailable due to incomplete data storage. NESO has reported skip rates by technology at the weekly Operational Transparency Forum since June 2025.
- **Error if omitted:** Using a headline percentage from a news article rather than the underlying dataset loses the variant, the stage, the direction and the technology tag — the four things that determine what the number means. It also loses the ability to condition the skip rate on period, price and system state, which is required for the Monte Carlo formulation above.
- **How to implement:** Ingest CSVs from https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/skip-rates. Build s(variant, stage, direction, technology, settlement_period). For a BTM asset routed via a VLP, check whether it is classified in the Demand Side Flexibility Unit datasets rather than the battery technology dataset — the applicable skip rate may differ. For pre-Dec-2024 backtests use LCP Delta Phase 2 published annual figures and label them as a different vintage/methodology.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Skip rates' data portal, https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/skip-rates
- *Source:* NESO, 'Skip rates', industry information page, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/skip-rates
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Skip rates: Understanding NESO's new methodology and datasets', 28 March 2025, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/battery-energy-storage-skip-rates-balancing-mechanism-neso-methodology-explainer-march-2025-gb-great-britain

#### D-21 · MUST · Never mix Modo's 'dispatch rate' / 'in-merit dispatch rate' with NESO's 'skip rate'. They are different metrics with different denominators and are not complements.

- **Why:** Modo defines dispatch rate as 'how much of a battery's available capacity gets used for BM actions' (10 MW at 25% dispatch rate = 2.5 MW average), and separately reports 'in-merit dispatch rate' rising from 11% to 18% between September and October 2025 — a period in which NESO-basis battery skip rates were around 38–49%. If they were complements, an 18% dispatch rate would imply an 82% skip rate.
- **Error if omitted:** Silently substituting one for the other produces errors of 30–60 percentage points in the acceptance parameter — by far the largest single sensitivity in BM revenue.
- **How to implement:** Two distinct named parameters with unit tests: skip_rate_PSA_stage5 (denominator = in-merit volume for energy balancing) and dispatch_rate (denominator = available capacity). Assert in code that dispatch_rate ≠ 1 − skip_rate. Prefer 'MWh dispatched per MW offered per day' (~0.4 MWh/MW/day empirically) as the direct volumetric calibration target for the revenue model, and use skip rate as the mechanism/scenario driver.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism' documentation, April 2026, https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/apr-2026/uk-europe/gb/bm/
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism availability: how much are batteries getting dispatched?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/balancing-mechanism-availability-dispatch-battery-energy-storage
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'ME BESS GB: Revenues climb to £77k/MW/year in October 2025', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/battery-energy-storage-revenues-gb-october-2025-record-balancing-mechanism-dispatch-rates

#### D-22 · MUST · Model cycling and throughput arising from BM dispatch as an input to the degradation model, and constrain BM participation by any warranty cycle limit. Do not let BM revenue be earned on cycles the warranty does not permit.

- **Why:** NESO's GC0166 workgroup explicitly identified 'daily cycle limit' as a required parameter of the Future State of Energy model. LCP Delta lists as a limitation of the skip-rate methodology that tracking state of charge 'would introduce additional assumptions around cycling limits, round-trip efficiency and state of health'. Modo's index computes cycles as Total Energy Throughput / (Energy Capacity × Days) but explicitly excludes degradation from its methodology.
- **Error if omitted:** BM dispatch is short, frequent and unpredictable. A model that adds BM throughput on top of a wholesale cycling schedule without a cycle constraint will breach warranty limits, invalidating the availability assumptions and the capacity retention curve used in later years — a classic lender diligence finding.
- **How to implement:** Add: Σ_t (P_ch(t)+P_dis(t))·Δt / (2·E_cap) ≤ Cycles_max_annual (warranty), and price marginal degradation into the BM bid/offer floor: π_offer_min = π_charge + c_deg where c_deg = (£/MWh) marginal degradation cost. Note that published GB benchmark indices exclude degradation — so the benchmark comparison must be made on a pre-degradation basis.
- *Source:* NESO, 'GC0166 Final Modification Report', 8 July 2025, p.12, https://www.neso.energy/document/364346/download
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, p.42, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB Methodology — Indices', https://modoenergy.com/methodology/gb

#### D-23 · MUST · Do not credit non-delivery-free BM revenue: model the operational obligation to respond to BOAs within the declared parameters, including the practical submission burden of MEL/MIL/MDO/MDB updates immediately before gate closure.

- **Why:** NESO records that current systems see a peak of 1,100 MEL submissions in the five-minute period before Gate Closure from a total of 135 batteries, and expects up to 8 MDO and 8 MDB submissions per BM Unit in that same five-minute window. Balancing Reserve providers must additionally maintain 'a back up means of dispatch (control or system telephony) during contracted windows'.
- **Error if omitted:** Underestimates the operational cost and technical requirement of BM participation for a small BTM asset — real-time systems, 24/7 cover or an aggregator's platform, telephony backup. For 0.1–5 MW assets this fixed cost is a material fraction of gross BM revenue and is the reason the aggregator route exists.
- **How to implement:** Add an annual BM operations opex line (platform, telephony, monitoring) and test BM net revenue against it: if BM_gross − BM_opex − optimiser_share < 0, the correct model output is to not participate. Where the aggregator bundles this, ensure it is inside the 3–10% share and not double-counted.
- *Source:* NESO, 'GC0166 Final Modification Report', 8 July 2025, p.12, https://www.neso.energy/document/364346/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Balancing Reserve Guidance Document', Version 7, June 2026, p.8, https://www.neso.energy/document/303546/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Registration guide: Balancing Mechanism Units (BMUs)', June 2026, p.6, https://www.neso.energy/document/290631/download

#### D-24 · MUST · Backtest the BM module against published fleet-level outcomes for at least one full year, and reject the model if it cannot reproduce the observed step change around Bulk Dispatch (December 2023) and the 30-minute rule (March 2024).

- **Why:** NESO reports that comparing the three months before OBP went live in December 2023 with June–August 2024, average battery dispatch volume rose from 659 to 2,527 MWh/day and daily instructions from 217 to 1,867, with expected consumer savings of £15m/yr. Modo records August 2024 as the highest ever battery volume dispatched at 81 GWh (73 GWh via OBP), and October 2025 at 227 GWh.
- **Error if omitted:** A BM model that cannot reproduce a known, well-documented 4x step change in dispatch volume is not calibrated and cannot be trusted to project forward through the GC0166 transition, which is expected to produce a comparable step.
- **How to implement:** Validation harness: run the model on 2023 and 2024 BMRS data with contemporaneous rules (15-minute MEL convention pre-March 2024, 30-minute after; pre/post Bulk Dispatch skip rates). Assert modelled fleet-level dispatched GWh is within a stated tolerance of the published monthly series. Fail the build if not.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Enhancing the use of storage assets in our balancing activities', 2024, https://www.neso.energy/news/enhancing-use-storage-assets-our-balancing-activities
- *Source:* NESO, 'First stages of Open Balancing Platform go live', 13 December 2023, https://www.neso.energy/news/first-stages-open-balancing-platform-go-live
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'September 2024: GB battery storage research round-up', https://modoenergy.com/research/september-2024-gb-great-britain-battery-energy-storage-research-roundup-balancing-mechanism-capex-skip-rates-buildout-capacity-market-revenues-long-duration-ramp-rates

#### D-25 · MUST · Compute de-rated capacity from a per-auction, per-0.5h-duration-bin de-rating factor table keyed on (auction type, delivery year, duration class) — never from a single scalar de-rating assumption.

- **Why:** Rule 2.3.4(d) sets the de-rating factor for a Duration Limited Storage Generating Technology Class to the Equivalent Firm Capacity (EFC) of that class, determined per auction under Schedule 3B by a time-sequential stochastic simulation. NESO publishes a distinct factor for every 0.5h bin from 0.5h to 12h, for every auction. The factors have changed by up to 3x between adjacent vintages.
- **Error if omitted:** Using a single fixed factor (e.g. the common 2019-era 4h assumption of ~0.70-0.95) against the 2027/28 T-4 actual of 0.3078 overstates CM revenue for a 4h asset by 2.3x-3.1x. For a 1h asset, using a DSR-like 0.85 instead of the 2029/30 T-4 actual 0.1099 overstates CM revenue by 7.7x.
- **How to implement:** DeRatedCapacity_MW = ConnectionCapacity_MW * DF[auction_id][duration_class]; duration_class = floor_to_0.5h(UsableEnergy_MWh / ConnectionCapacity_MW), clipped to [0.5h, 12h]. Source the DF table from the NESO CKAN datastore resource d94ff98d-39ba-40d6-9672-7433de0631ac (fields: 'Auction Name', 'Primary Fuel Type', 'Generating Technology Class', 'De-Rating Factor'); refresh annually because a new auction row appears each August/October.
- *Source:* NESO, Capacity Market De-Rating Factors (Data Portal dataset, resource d94ff98d-39ba-40d6-9672-7433de0631ac), accessed 2026, https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/capacity-market-register/capacity_market_de-rating_factors
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules (informal consolidation, 24 July 2026), Rule 2.3.4(d), Rule 2.3.4B, Schedule 3B, https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/6a68894dcc35a9471da4c18f/capacity-market-rules-consolidation-2026.pdf

#### D-26 · MUST · Model de-rated capacity as approximately PROPORTIONAL TO ENERGY (MWh) below the cap, i.e. DeRated_MW ~= k_auction * UsableEnergy_MWh, and only proportional to power (MW) above the duration knee.

- **Why:** The published EFC curves are exactly linear in duration for short durations. 2029/30 T-4: 1h 0.1099, 2h 0.2198, 3h 0.3297, 4h 0.4396, 7h 0.7693 — all exact integer multiples of 0.1099. The curve then becomes concave and plateaus at the TCWAA cap (0.9216 for 2029/30 T-4, at ~8.5h). Substituting DF = k*d into DeRated = CC * DF(E/CC) gives DeRated = k*E, independent of CC.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that sizes the battery on power and treats duration as a free parameter will mis-rank build options. Under-sizing energy to save capex removes CM revenue pound-for-pound; adding energy adds CM revenue with no incremental de-rating penalty until the knee. Omitting this hides the fact that CM revenue is an energy-capacity subsidy, which changes the optimal MWh/MW ratio for a BTM asset.
- **How to implement:** Verify per auction: k = DF[1h]; assert max_abs_error(DF[d] - k*d for d <= 4h) < 0.001. Then DeRated_MW = min(k * UsableEnergy_MWh, TCWAA_cap * ConnectionCapacity_MW). TCWAA_cap = max(DF over all duration bins) for that auction. For 2029/30 T-4: k = 0.1099/h, cap = 0.9216, knee at ~8.4h.
- *Source:* NESO, Capacity Market De-Rating Factors dataset (2029-30 T-4, 2028-29 T-4, 2026-27 T-1 rows), https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/capacity-market-register/capacity_market_de-rating_factors
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Schedule 3B (EFC outputs multiplied by the Technology Class Weighted Average Availability)

#### D-27 · MUST · Model the Rule 3.5.6 lever: a battery Storage Facility may NOMINATE a Connection Capacity for CM purposes, provided the nominated value is not less than 50% of the value that would otherwise be calculated under Rule 3.5.2/3.5.5. Treat nominated Connection Capacity as a decision variable.

- **Why:** Nominating a lower Connection Capacity raises the effective duration (E/CC), moving the CMU into a higher de-rating bin. Because de-rated capacity is bin-rounded (floor to 0.5h), the nomination can recover up to 0.5h * k * CC of de-rated capacity that rounding would otherwise destroy, and it simultaneously lowers the MW level the asset must sustain in the Extended Performance Test.
- **Error if omitted:** For a 2 MW / 3.4 MWh BTM battery, E/CC = 1.7h rounds down to the 1.5h bin: DF 0.1641 (2029/30 T-4) → 0.328 MW de-rated. Nominating CC = 1.7 MW gives exactly 2.0h → DF 0.2198 → 0.374 MW de-rated, a 14% uplift in CM revenue at zero capex. Omitting the lever leaves that on the table and overstates the EPT power requirement by 18%.
- **How to implement:** Decision variable CC_nom in [0.5 * CC_calc, CC_calc]. Objective term: CM_revenue = CC_nom * DF[floor_0.5h(E_usable / CC_nom)] * ClearingPrice * CPI_factor. Optimal choice: pick CC_nom so that E_usable / CC_nom is an exact multiple of 0.5h and <= the knee duration; among ties, choose the CC_nom that minimises the EPT power obligation (TCWAA * CC_nom). Enforce CC_nom >= 0.5 * CC_calc as a hard constraint.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rule 3.5.6 'Nomination of Connection Capacity for a battery Storage Facility'
- *Source:* NESO EMR Delivery Body, Prequalification 2026 Rule Changes ('Battery Storage Generating Technology Class' units may 'self-nominate a Connection Capacity in an Application'), https://emrdeliverybody.nationalenergyso.com/IG/s/article/Prequalification-2026-Rule-Changes

#### D-28 · MUST · Gate CM eligibility on the 1 MW minimum capacity threshold, applied to Connection Capacity (Generating CMU) or DSR Capacity (DSR CMU) — NOT to de-rated capacity. Below 1 MW, force an aggregation route or exclude CM revenue entirely.

- **Why:** Regulation 15 of the Electricity Capacity Regulations 2014 requires that a generating CMU's connection capacity, or a DSR CMU's DSR capacity, is at least the minimum capacity threshold of 1 MW. Generating Units may only be aggregated into one Generating CMU under Regulation 4 Conditions 1-4 (same registered trading unit, same connection point, aggregate under 50 MW, or hydro) — effectively same-site. DSR CMU Components may be aggregated across multiple sites.
- **Error if omitted:** For the 0.1-1 MW end of the BTM range this is the difference between all CM revenue and none. A model that awards CM revenue to a 500 kW single-site BTM battery as a standalone CMU is fabricating a revenue line that cannot be prequalified.
- **How to implement:** Boolean gate: cm_eligible_standalone = (ConnectionCapacity_MW >= 1.0). If false, either (a) set CM revenue to zero, or (b) require an explicit aggregator/portfolio DSR CMU assumption with a stated aggregator fee (typically taken as a % of CM revenue) and multi-site Component aggregation. Note the threshold is on CONNECTION capacity, so a 1.2 MW / 2.4 MWh battery qualifies even though its de-rated capacity is only ~0.26 MW.
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity Regulations 2014 (SI 2014/2043), regulation 15 (general eligibility; minimum capacity threshold 1 MW), https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2014/2043/regulation/15
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity Regulations 2014, regulation 4 (definition of generating CMU and aggregation Conditions 1-4), https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2014/2043/regulation/4

#### D-29 · MUST · For a BTM battery registered as a Generating CMU, compute delivered stress-event volume as Eij = A + B - C, where A is metered export, B is Baseline Demand under Schedule 2A, and C is metered consumption — i.e. give credit for avoided site import, not export only.

- **Why:** Rule 8.6.2 defines, for a Generating CMU that constitutes a Storage Facility, Eij = A + B - C. This is the specific provision that makes a behind-the-meter battery deliverable against a CM obligation at all: a battery sitting behind a large C&I load may never export, yet still discharges its obligation by displacing site import measured against the Schedule 2A baseline.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that measures CM delivery only at the export meter will conclude that a BTM battery at a site with load greater than the battery's power rating can never deliver its obligation, and will either exclude CM revenue or fabricate a spurious export requirement. Conversely, a model that assumes gross discharge counts will overstate delivery when site load simultaneously rises.
- **How to implement:** E_ij = ExportMeteredVolume_MWh + BaselineDemand_MWh - ImportMeteredVolume_MWh, all per half hour, adjusted to the Transmission boundary by Line Loss Factors under BSC K1.7 (Rule 8.6.4). For a DSR CMU instead use DSR Volume = sum(BaselineDemand) - sum(MeteredVolume), with net import positive and net export negative (Rules definitions, 'DSR Volume').
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rule 8.6.2, Rule 8.6.3, Rule 8.6.4, definition of 'DSR Volume'
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Schedule 2A: Baseline Methodology for Storage Facilities

#### D-30 · MUST · Implement the Schedule 2/2A baseline algorithm exactly: same-weekday for the last 6 weeks plus the last 10 Working Days (or last 10 non-Working Days), excluding periods with no data and any period during which a Capacity Market Notice was in force; then apply the Pre-CMN Adjustment from the 6 settlement periods before the CMN.

- **Why:** The baseline determines B in Rule 8.6.2 and the DSR Volume for a DSR CMU. It is a specified mean-of-samples methodology with a symmetric pre-event correction, not a generic 'typical day' profile. Schedule 2 paragraph 3.1 also adjusts each Demand Sample for any energy delivered under a Standard Condition C16 balancing service in that period.
- **Error if omitted:** Modelling the baseline as a smooth average profile will over- or under-state deliverable volume at exactly the periods that matter (winter evening peaks, when C&I load is atypical), and will miss the Pre-CMN Adjustment which systematically shifts the baseline once a CMN has been issued. For a BTM asset whose entire CM delivery is measured against this baseline, an error here is an error in the whole CM revenue line's deliverability test.
- **How to implement:** Samples S = {demand in the equivalent settlement period on the same weekday for weeks -1..-6 (dropping non-Working-Day matches when the target is a Working Day)} UNION {same settlement period on the last 10 Working Days, or last 10 non-Working Days}. Drop samples with no data or under a live CMN. AdjustedSample = Sample + energy from consumption reduction/generation increase - energy consumed/generation withheld for a C16 balancing service. ProvisionalBaseline = mean(AdjustedSamples). PreCMNAdjustment = mean over the 6 settlement periods immediately before the CMN of (ActualDemand - ProvisionalBaseline). Baseline = ProvisionalBaseline + PreCMNAdjustment.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Schedule 2: Baseline Methodology, paragraphs 2.1-3.4
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Schedule 2A: Baseline Methodology for Storage Facilities, paragraphs 2.1-3.4

#### D-31 · MUST · Constrain the dispatch optimiser so it does not inflate site demand in baseline sample periods in order to increase measured CM delivery — the CM Settlement Body is required to monitor for Baseline Manipulation and refer suspicions to Ofgem.

- **Why:** Schedule 2 paragraph 4.1 obliges the CM Settlement Body to monitor for 'any manipulation of Demand at any DSR CMU Component intended to give a false indication of the baseline Demand', including by checking demand in periods other than the Demand Samples and examining other meters. Paragraph 4.2 requires referral to the Authority.
- **Error if omitted:** An unconstrained MILP maximising CM delivery volume will discover that charging the battery from the grid during the 6-week/10-day baseline sample windows raises the baseline and therefore raises measured delivery during a stress event. That is a compliance breach with termination and Ofgem enforcement exposure, and an underwriting model that scores it as revenue is underwriting a fraud.
- **How to implement:** Add a soft constraint or post-hoc audit: for each settlement period that is a Schedule 2/2A Demand Sample candidate, penalise or forbid grid-import charging that is not justified by an arbitrage/service signal that would exist absent the CM. Simplest bankable form: exclude CM-delivery volume from the objective entirely (CM revenue is availability-based, not energy-based) and treat CM only as a constraint set plus a fixed cashflow.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Schedule 2 paragraphs 4.1-4.2 (Monitoring / Baseline Manipulation)

#### D-32 · MUST · Model CM revenue as an availability payment independent of dispatch: MCP_im = CO_ix * PE_ix * WF_mx, paid monthly across the Capacity Year (1 October - 30 September) using the demand-based monthly weighting factor, with NO energy-linked component.

- **Why:** Schedule 1 paragraphs 2 and 3 of SI 2014/2043 set ACP_ix = CO_ix * PE_ix and MCP_im = ACP_ix * WF_mx, where WF_mx is the share of GB electrical demand falling in the corresponding month over a rolling 3-year calculation period, computed to 3 decimal places and published at least 3 months before the capacity year.
- **Error if omitted:** Two errors. (i) Modelling CM as £/MWh dispatched creates a phantom incentive to discharge, distorting the whole optimisation. (ii) Spreading CM revenue evenly across 12 months misstates monthly cashflow: the weighting factors track GB demand, so winter months carry materially more than 1/12 and summer months materially less. For a DSCR test computed on a monthly or quarterly basis this misstates the minimum DSCR period.
- **How to implement:** ACP = CapacityObligation_MW * PE_per_MW_per_year. MCP[m] = ACP * WF[m], sum(WF) = 1 over Oct..Sep. Source WF from EMR Settlement's annual publication for the relevant delivery year; if unavailable, approximate WF[m] from GB monthly demand shares over the trailing 3 years and flag it as an approximation. Do NOT add any £/MWh term to the dispatch objective for CM.
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity Regulations 2014 (SI 2014/2043), Schedule 1 paragraphs 2 and 3, https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2014/2043/schedule/1
- *Source:* EMR Settlement Ltd, 'Weighting Factors for 2025/26 Delivery Year now published', https://www.emrsettlement.co.uk/weighting-factors-for-2025-26-delivery-year-now-published/

#### D-33 · MUST · Index T-4 capacity payments to CPI from the auction's base period; do NOT index T-1 capacity payments.

- **Why:** Schedule 1 paragraph 3(5): for a T-4 auction, PE_ix = CCP_i * (CPI_x / CPI_base), where CPI_base is the average monthly CPI over the base period determined under regulation 11 for that auction and CPI_x is the average monthly CPI over the winter ending 30 April preceding the start of year X. Paragraph 3(6): for a T-1 auction (or a DSR transitional auction), PE_ix = CCP_i, unindexed. The NESO auction static data records the base period explicitly (e.g. Winter 2024/2025 for the 2029-30 T-4).
- **Error if omitted:** A 15-year T-4 agreement modelled in nominal terms without indexation understates revenue by roughly (1+i)^15; at 2.5% CPI that is a 45% understatement of the final year and materially misstates equity IRR. Conversely, escalating a T-1 agreement's price overstates it. Both errors bite directly on DSCR in the outer years.
- **How to implement:** PE[year] = ClearingPrice * (CPI_index[winter ending 30 April before year start] / CPI_index[base period]) for T-4-awarded obligations; PE[year] = ClearingPrice for T-1-awarded obligations. Store the base period per auction from the NESO Capacity Market Auction Static Data 'Base Period for the Agreement' field (2026-27 T-4: Winter 2021/2022; 2027-28 T-4: Winter 2022/2023; 2028-29 T-4: Winter 2023/2024; 2029-30 T-4: Winter 2024/2025).
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity Regulations 2014 (SI 2014/2043), Schedule 1 paragraph 3(4)-(7)
- *Source:* NESO, Capacity Market Auction Static Data (Data Portal resource 784fa703-1460-42cd-a8bb-fba4dcabac8d), https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/capacity-market-register/capacity_market_auction_static_data

#### D-34 · MUST · Fix the Capacity Obligation in MW at the point of auction award for the whole agreement term — do NOT re-apply a changing de-rating factor to a live multi-year agreement.

- **Why:** The de-rating factor is applied at prequalification/auction to convert Connection Capacity into De-rated Capacity, which becomes the Auction Acquired Capacity Obligation recorded on the Capacity Market Register (Rule 8.5.3, definition of AACO). Capacity payments are then CO_ix * PE_ix. DESNZ separately confirmed in July 2024 that the TCWAA of a battery Storage CMU subject to a multi-year agreement 'should not change during the course of that Capacity Agreement'.
- **Error if omitted:** Re-deriving the obligation each year from a forecast de-rating curve makes a 15-year T-4 agreement look like a declining revenue stream (given the downward de-rating trend), understating both project IRR and the tail DSCRs, and destroying the main investment-grade characteristic of a T-4 agreement. Conversely, applying the CURRENT year's de-rating factor to a future auction entry is the correct treatment and must not be confused with the in-agreement case.
- **How to implement:** Two distinct objects: (1) auction-entry de-rating, DF(auction vintage), used only to compute the MW obligation bid and won; (2) contracted obligation CO, a constant MW for each delivery year of the agreement.
