Revenue[y] = CO * PE[y] * sum(WF) for the delivery year. De-rating uncertainty enters ONLY through the future-auction re-contracting scenarios after the current agreement expires.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rule 8.5.3 (AACO), definition of 'De-rated Capacity'
- *Source:* DESNZ, Capacity Market: Government response - 2023 Phase 2 Consultation and 2024 Rule amendments, July 2024, section on 'Clarity in respect of TCWAA'

#### D-35 · MUST · Enforce the Satisfactory Performance Day (SPD) obligation as a hard dispatch constraint: demonstrate capacity at or above the Capacity Obligation for at least one Settlement Period on three separate days during Winter (1 October - 30 April), of which at least one must fall between 1 January and 30 April.

- **Why:** Rule 13.4.1. Failure triggers Rule 13.4.1ZA: three additional SPDs between 1 May and 31 July AND suspension of the entitlement to capacity payments from 1 May until the third additional SPD is demonstrated (or the end of the delivery year). Rule 13.4.6 doubles the requirement to six SPDs if the CMU fails to achieve E_ij of at least 1 kWh in at least one stress event in each of two or more months in which stress events occurred.
- **Error if omitted:** The failure mode is a full-year loss of capacity payments from 1 May, not a marginal penalty. For a BTM asset whose CM revenue is the contracted backbone of the DSCR, an unmodelled SPD failure is a covenant breach. A model with no SPD constraint will also mis-price the operational cost of scheduling three winter demonstrations at full obligation MW.
- **How to implement:** Binary variables spd[d] for each Winter day d; require sum(spd[d] for d in Oct 1..Apr 30) >= 3 and sum(spd[d] for d in Jan 1..Apr 30) >= 1. spd[d] = 1 requires exists a settlement period t in day d with discharge_MW[t] >= CapacityObligation_MW. Notification to the Delivery Body by end of Winter (Rule 13.4.2(a)). If the model cannot satisfy this (e.g. site export constraint below CO), the CM revenue line must be removed, not haircut.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 13.4.1, 13.4.1ZA, 13.4.2, 13.4.5, 13.4.6
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity Regulations 2014 (SI 2014/2043), regulation 2 ('winter' = 1 October to the following 30 April; 'capacity year' = 1 October to the following 30 September)

#### D-36 · MUST · Enforce the Extended Performance Test (EPT): a storage CMU must sustain output at or above its Adjusted Connection Capacity (= Connection Capacity x TCWAA) for a number of consecutive Settlement Periods equal to the minimum duration of its declared Storage Generating Technology Class, on at least one Satisfactory Performance Day in the first Delivery Year and thereafter at least once in the Winter of every third Capacity Year.

- **Why:** Rule 13.4A.2-13.4A.4. This is the binding physical constraint on a duration-limited storage CMU: it must actually be able to run at ~92% of its nominated Connection Capacity for the full declared duration, in year 1 and again every third year, for the life of the agreement.
- **Error if omitted:** For a 15-year T-4 agreement on a 2h-class battery, the model must show usable energy >= TCWAA * CC_nom * 2h at years 1, 4, 7, 10 and 13 AFTER degradation. Omitting this produces a model that books 15 years of CM revenue from a battery that will fail the year-7 test and be terminated. It also omits the augmentation capex needed to pass, which is real, lumpy and typically occurs mid-agreement.
- **How to implement:** Constraint at each EPT year y in {1, 4, 7, 10, 13, ...}: UsableEnergy_MWh(y) * DischargeEfficiency >= TCWAA * CC_nom * DurationClass_h, where UsableEnergy_MWh(y) is the degraded, temperature- and DoD-window-adjusted energy at the start of Winter in year y. TCWAA = the plateau value of the auction's de-rating curve (0.9216 for the 2029/30 T-4 and 2026/27 T-1 vintages; 0.9299 for 2028/29 T-4). If the constraint binds, trigger an augmentation capex event.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 13.4A.1-13.4A.5 ('Demonstrating extended performance'; 'Adjusted Connection Capacity' = Connection Capacity x TCWAA calculated per Rule 2.3.5(a))
- *Source:* DESNZ, Capacity Market: Government response - 2023 Phase 2 Consultation and 2024 Rule amendments, July 2024, Questions 6-9 policy response on the EPT framework

#### D-37 · MUST · Model Permitted Battery Augmentation as a scheduled capex line whose timing is driven by the EPT constraint, and cap its effect: it may restore capacity but may not increase the Auction Acquired Capacity Obligation nor import capacity from another CMU.

- **Why:** DESNZ introduced a definition of 'Permitted Battery Augmentation' in the CM Rules in 2024, confirming augmentation of battery energy storage is general maintenance and allowing CMUs in a Duration Limited Storage GTC to replace and/or add batteries at an existing CMU site to maintain the level of capacity required to meet EPT requirements. Government explicitly rejected the alternative of submitting degradation profiles, and conceded the measures 'will not fully resolve the risk of termination for some storage CMUs, such as those with existing agreements who are unable to augment'.
- **Error if omitted:** A 15-year CM agreement modelled without augmentation capex overstates equity IRR. The magnitude is the cost of restoring roughly the fade accumulated between EPT dates: for a typical LFP asset cycled hard on arbitrage plus balancing services, this is a material cell-replacement or module-addition event, usually once or twice over 15 years. A model that instead assumes a degradation profile is accepted by the Delivery Body is modelling a policy that DESNZ specifically declined to adopt.
- **How to implement:** At each EPT year, if UsableEnergy < TCWAA * CC_nom * Duration, apply augmentation capex = (energy shortfall MWh) * (£/kWh installed cost in that year) and reset UsableEnergy. Constrain CO to remain unchanged. Confirm site export/import capacity and space constraints permit augmentation; if not, the agreement must be modelled as terminating at the first failed EPT with the associated termination fee.
- *Source:* DESNZ, Capacity Market: Government response - 2023 Phase 2 Consultation and 2024 Rule amendments, July 2024, 'Permitted Augmentation for Storage - battery CMUs'
- *Source:* DESNZ, Capacity Market (Amendment) Rules 2024, https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/669a2017ab418ab055592894/Capacity_Market__Amendment__Rules_2024.pdf

#### D-38 · MUST · Model the stress-event delivery obligation correctly: the obligation is the Adjusted Load Following Capacity Obligation (ALFCO) in a Settlement Period, and it only binds where a Capacity Market Notice was published at least four hours before the start of that Settlement Period.

- **Why:** Rule 8.5.1: during a System Stress Event a Capacity Provider must deliver the ALFCO, but has no obligation unless a CMN was issued and the stress event falls four or more hours after the expiry of the Settlement Period in which the CMN was published. A CMN is issued when NESO anticipates an Inadequate System Margin (System Margin below 500 MW) at least 4 hours ahead (Rules 8.4.6-8.4.7).
- **Error if omitted:** A model that constrains the battery to hold State of Charge at all times against a possible stress event destroys the arbitrage and balancing-service value that pays for the asset. A model that ignores the obligation entirely omits the state-of-charge repositioning cost. The correct treatment is a 4-hour-notice recourse constraint: given a CMN, the optimiser must be able to reach the required SoC within 4 hours.
- **How to implement:** Two-stage / recourse formulation. Base dispatch is unconstrained by CM. Add a feasibility constraint: for every settlement period t, SoC[t] + 4h * MaxChargePower * eta_c - (site import headroom limits) >= EnergyRequiredForObligation, i.e. the asset must be able to reach ALFCO-serving SoC within 8 settlement periods of a CMN. Note ALFCO is an MWh volume per settlement period, so EnergyRequired = ALFCO (MWh) for the single period, plus a modelled persistence (stress events can span consecutive settlement periods, each separately a System Stress Event per Rule 8.4.1).
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 8.4.1-8.4.7, 8.5.1, 8.5.2, 8.5.3
- *Source:* NESO, GB Electricity Capacity Market Notices, https://gbcmn.nationalenergyso.com/

#### D-39 · MUST · Apply the beta adjustment: where the CMU is providing a Relevant Balancing Service in a settlement period, its ALFCO is reduced by the sterilised capacity QBSCCC — so CM and NESO balancing services are compatible, not mutually exclusive, during a stress event.

- **Why:** Rule 8.5.2(b), for a DSR CMU or a Generating CMU that is not comprised of BM Units: ALFCO_ij = LFCO_ij - sum_k(beta_kj * QBSCCC_kj), where beta = 1 if the unit provided a Relevant Balancing Service in that period and 0 otherwise, and QBSCCC = Declared_Availability - Contracted_Output as defined in Schedule 4. For BM-Unit Generating CMUs, Rule 8.5.2(a) additionally adds back reduced output from negative Bid-Offer Acceptances and Applicable Balancing Services volumes.
- **Error if omitted:** Without this, a model concludes that holding a frequency-response or reserve contract creates CM non-delivery risk, and will artificially forbid revenue stacking across the winter peak — the single most valuable stacking window. That mis-prices the asset downward. Conversely, assuming full stacking with no obligation adjustment overstates over-delivery payments.
- **How to implement:** ALFCO[t] = LFCO[t] - beta[t] * max(0, DeclaredAvailability[t] - ContractedOutput[t]). beta[t] = 1 iff a service on the Relevant Balancing Services Guidelines list is being provided at t. Only balancing services on that published list qualify; verify service-by-service against the Delivery Body's Relevant Balancing Services Guidelines rather than assuming all NESO products qualify.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 8.5.2(a), 8.5.2(b), 8.5.4, Schedule 4 (Relevant Balancing Services)

#### D-40 · MUST · Require that NESO is notified of CM participation at the time of entering any balancing services contract (or at prequalification), otherwise QBSCCC is forced to zero and the beta relief is lost.

- **Why:** Rule 8.5.4(c): if the Capacity Provider has not notified the System Operator at the time of entering into the balancing services contract (or, if later, on prequalification) that a Generating Unit is participating in the Capacity Market, or if the BM Unit Lead Party has given a Section Q paragraph 6.4.5 notice, QBSCCC must be set to zero.
- **Error if omitted:** A purely administrative omission converts a fully-compatible revenue stack into full CM non-delivery exposure during a stress event. This is a process requirement that belongs in the model's compliance gate list and in the operating cost assumptions (asset manager / route-to-market scope).
- **How to implement:** Boolean compliance gate on the CM revenue line: `so_notified_of_cm_participation == True`. If False, set beta = 0 in the ALFCO calculation for all periods and re-run the stress-event feasibility test.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rule 8.5.4(c)

#### D-41 · MUST · Model non-delivery penalties with the statutory penalty rate PR_ix = PE_ix / 24 (£/MWh), the monthly cap MPC_im = 2 x MCP_im, and the annual cap of 100% of annual capacity payments.

- **Why:** SI 2014/2043 Schedule 1 paragraph 5: SPP_ij = PR_ix * (ALFCO_ij - AE_ij), with PR_ix = PE_ix * (1/24). Paragraph 6: the monthly penalty charge is the lesser of (SP_i/MaxSP_i) * min(MaxSP_i, MPC_im) and APC_ix - sum of prior months' charges, with MPC_im = 2 * MCP_im. NESO's Capacity Market Auction Static Data records Annual Penalty Cap = 100% and Monthly Penalty Cap = 200% for every auction from the 2016-17 Transitional auction through the 2029-30 T-4.
- **Error if omitted:** Two opposite errors. Understating: a model with no penalty term shows no downside from a discharge-constrained BTM site. Overstating: a model that applies an uncapped £/MWh penalty across a long outage produces an unbounded loss that would never occur — the annual exposure is capped at 100% of that year's capacity payments, i.e. the worst case is losing the CM revenue line, not paying more than it.
- **How to implement:** PenaltyRate_per_MWh = (ClearingPrice_per_MW_per_year * CPI_factor) / 24. SPP[t] = PenaltyRate * max(0, ALFCO[t] - E[t]). MonthlyCharge[m] = min(sum(SPP in m) scaled per Schedule 1 para 6(3), 2 * MCP[m], AnnualCap - sum(prior monthly charges)). AnnualCap = 1.00 * ACP. Downside bound for underwriting: CM net contribution in any delivery year >= -0 (i.e. worst realistic case is zero net CM revenue plus, in extremis, an equal amount payable).
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity Regulations 2014 (SI 2014/2043, as made), Schedule 1 paragraphs 5 and 6, https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2014/2043/pdfs/uksi_20142043_en.pdf
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity Regulations 2014, regulation 41(3A) (annual penalty cap 100%, monthly penalty cap 200%), https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2014/2043/regulation/41
- *Source:* NESO, Capacity Market Auction Static Data (Annual Penalty Cap / Monthly Penalty Cap columns, all auctions)

#### D-42 · MUST · Do NOT book over-delivery payments as expected revenue; model them as a zero-or-positive option with expected value near zero.

- **Why:** Schedule 1 paragraph 7: ODR_ix = min(PR_ix, TPR_x / TODV_x), where TPR_x is total penalty charge payments RECEIVED in year X and TODV_x is total MWh over-delivered by all CMUs. Over-delivery is funded entirely out of penalties collected. With zero System Stress Events since the CM began, TPR_x has been zero and therefore ODR has been zero.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that treats over-delivery as a stacked revenue line is booking a payment whose funding pool has never existed. It also creates a false dispatch incentive to over-deliver during stress events.
- **How to implement:** ODP[t] = min(PenaltyRate, TPR_year / TODV_year) * max(0, E[t] - ALFCO[t]); set TPR_year = 0 in the base case. Include over-delivery only in an explicitly labelled upside scenario.
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity Regulations 2014 (SI 2014/2043), Schedule 1 paragraph 7; regulation 42
- *Source:* DESNZ, Capacity Market Ten-Year Review, December 2024 ('Since the inception of the CM there have been no System Stress Events declared'), https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/675fee720fb02bbe4853ef3f/capacity-market-10-year-review.pdf

#### D-43 · MUST · Calibrate CM delivery-risk to the observed record: zero System Stress Events since 2014 and 13 Capacity Market Notices since 2019 (six of them in 2022 alone). Put the risk weight on test failure and termination, not on stress-event penalties.

- **Why:** DESNZ's statutory ten-year review of the Capacity Market (December 2024) records both figures and cites them as evidence the CM is meeting its security-of-supply objective.
- **Error if omitted:** A P90 case built around large stress-event penalties is directionally wrong and will lead a lender to demand the wrong reserve. The correct P90 stressor for a BTM CM line is: (i) failure to prequalify, (ii) failure to demonstrate three SPDs, (iii) failure of a triennial EPT after degradation, (iv) termination fees. All of these are asset- and site-specific and controllable, unlike a stress event.
- **How to implement:** Set the base-case expected penalty to zero and document it. Instead construct a P90 that switches CM revenue to zero from the first year in which the EPT constraint is infeasible without augmentation, and applies the applicable Termination Fee. Sensitivity: CMN frequency of 0-6 per year (2019-2024 observed range) driving only the SoC-repositioning cost, not penalties.
- *Source:* DESNZ, Capacity Market Ten-Year Review, December 2024, pp.13-14 and Figure 1 (Capacity Market Notices Instances 2014-2024)
- *Source:* NESO, GB Electricity Capacity Market Notices, https://gbcmn.nationalenergyso.com/

#### D-44 · MUST · Determine the available agreement length from Qualifying £/kW Capital Expenditure = Total Project Spend divided by DE-RATED Capacity — not by connection capacity, and not by installed MWh.

- **Why:** The CM Rules define 'Qualifying £/kW Capital Expenditure' for a New Build Generating CMU, a Refurbishing Generating CMU or an Unproven DSR CMU as the Total Project Spend divided by the De-rated Capacity of the CMU. The Maximum Obligation Period is then 15 Delivery Years if that figure meets the Fifteen Year Minimum £/kW Threshold, 9 years at the Nine Year Threshold (for eligible Declared Low Carbon CMUs), and 3 years at the Three Year Threshold or Three Year Zero Capex Threshold.
- **Error if omitted:** Dividing by connection capacity is the natural but wrong reading, and it is decisive. A 2 MW / 4 MWh BTM battery at £500/kW connection = £1.0m total project spend. Divided by connection capacity that is £500/kW — above the £350/kW 15-year threshold but only just. Divided by de-rated capacity (2 MW x 0.2198 = 0.4396 MW for the 2029/30 T-4) it is £2,275/kW — 6.5x the threshold. The wrong denominator would wrongly disqualify short-duration or low-capex assets from a 15-year agreement, understating contracted revenue tenor by 12 years and destroying the DSCR profile.
- **How to implement:** QualifyingCapexPerKW = TotalProjectSpend_GBP / (DeRatedCapacity_MW * 1000). AgreementYears = 15 if QualifyingCapexPerKW >= FifteenYearThreshold; else 9 if Declared Low Carbon and >= NineYearThreshold; else 3 if >= ThreeYearThreshold or a Three Year Zero Capex Threshold CMU; else 1. Thresholds (2029/30 T-4, held Feb/Mar 2026): 15-year £350/kW, 9-year £205/kW, 3-year £65/kW, 3-year zero-capex £0/kW. (2030/31 T-4, July 2026 letter): 15-year £360/kW, 9-year £215/kW, 3-year £65/kW, 3-year zero-capex £0/kW.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, definition of 'Qualifying £/kW Capital Expenditure'; definition of 'Maximum Obligation Period'; Rules 3.7.2(d), 3.10.1(aa)
- *Source:* DESNZ, Capacity Market auction parameters: letter from DESNZ to NESO, July 2025 - full details of auction parameters, https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/capacity-market-auction-parameters-letter-from-desnz-to-neso-july-2025/full-details-of-auction-parameters-and-interconnector-de-rating-factors
- *Source:* DESNZ, Capacity Market auction parameters: letter from DESNZ to NESO, July 2026, https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/capacity-market-auction-parameters-letter-from-desnz-to-neso-july-2026/full-details-of-auction-parameters-and-interconnector-de-rating-factors

#### D-45 · MUST · Model the T-4 vs T-1 decision explicitly, with different agreement lengths, indexation, target volumes and price distributions — and recognise that T-1 auctions do not award multi-year agreements.

- **Why:** DESNZ's parameter letters state for every recent T-1 that 'Multi-year agreements are not awarded in the T-1 auction'. T-1 targets are an order of magnitude smaller than T-4 (2026/27 T-1: 6.3 GW target, 7.2 GW procured, £5.00/kW/yr; 2029/30 T-4: 39.4 GW target, 40.1 GW procured, £27.10/kW/yr). T-1 prices are unindexed. T-1 de-rating factors for storage are systematically HIGHER than the corresponding T-4 factors (2026/27: T-1 1h 0.1361 vs T-4 1h 0.1181).
- **Error if omitted:** A model that treats CM as a single generic revenue line cannot represent the actual financing structure: a lender will size debt on a 15-year T-4 agreement and will not size debt on rolling one-year T-1 awards. Conflating them either fabricates bankable contracted revenue or destroys it.
- **How to implement:** Two revenue objects. T4Agreement{start_delivery_year, term_years in {1,3,9,15}, clearing_price, cpi_indexed=True, de_rating_vintage}. T1Agreement{delivery_year, term_years=1, clearing_price, cpi_indexed=False, de_rating_vintage}. Debt sizing uses only the contracted T-4 term; T-1 and post-expiry T-4 re-contracting go into the merchant tail with a price distribution.
- *Source:* DESNZ, Final auction parameters, T-1 and T-4 Capacity Market auctions (February 2026), https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/capacity-market-auction-parameters-letter-from-desnz-to-neso-february-2026/final-auction-parameters-t-1-and-t-4-capacity-market-auctions
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Capacity Market: 2026/27 T-1 auction clears at £5/kW/yr', March 2026, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-capacity-market-t1-2026-27-battery-energy-storage-march-2026
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Capacity Market: 2029/30 T-4 auction clears at £27/kW/yr', March 2026, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-capacity-market-t4-2029-30-battery-energy-storage-march-2026
- *Source:* NESO, Capacity Market De-Rating Factors dataset

#### D-46 · MUST · Model the prequalification and auction calendar as hard project gates: prequalification window opens ~T-31 weeks, closes ~T-23 weeks, results ~T-17 weeks, auction at T (typically February/March), first Delivery Year begins 1 October four years later for a T-4.

- **Why:** Rule 2.2.2 sets out this indicative timetable, which the Auction Guidelines must follow unless the Delivery Body or Secretary of State determines otherwise. Prequalification requires evidence in place (connection agreement, planning, metering, credit cover) many months before the auction.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that assumes a project can pick up CM revenue in the year it reaches financial close is wrong by 12-18 months at minimum, and by up to 4.5 years for a T-4 route. This directly misstates the revenue ramp and the first-year DSCR.
- **How to implement:** Gate: CM revenue for delivery year Y requires prequalification submitted by (auction date for Y) minus 23 weeks, and the auction for a T-4 Y occurs in approximately February of calendar year (Y_start - 4). Connection agreement or connection offer must be in force at the date the Application is made (Rule 3.5B.1(a)). Model a Long Stop declaration option: Declared 12-month or Declared 24-month long stop shifts first delivery to the second or third Delivery Year, with Rule 13.4.1 and Rule 13.4A relief in those years.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rule 2.2.2 (auction timetable), Rule 3.5B.1(a), Rules 13.4.1ZZA and 13.4A.2 (Declared Long Stop relief)
- *Source:* DESNZ, Capacity Market: Government response - 2023 Phase 2 Consultation, July 2024, section on 'Projects with long build times'

#### D-47 · MUST · Gate CM eligibility on the Low Carbon Exclusion: if the CMU is, or includes, plant accredited under the Feed-in Tariffs Order, the Renewables Obligation Order or the RHI Regulations, or holds a CfD, it is excluded from prequalification unless a non-support confirmation is provided.

- **Why:** Regulation 16 of SI 2014/2043 prevents prequalification where the applicant fails to provide a non-support confirmation by the prequalification deadline for a CMU holding FIT/RO/RHI accreditation, and excludes a CMU where a CfD has been entered into and remains unexpired and unterminated. A non-support confirmation declares that support periods will expire before the delivery period begins, or that the applicant will not seek relevant support during the delivery period.
- **Error if omitted:** This is directly binding on the co-located-rooftop-solar case in scope. If the site's PV is FIT- or RO-accredited and is brought into the CMU (e.g. as a Permitted On-Site Generating Unit inside a DSR CMU), the whole CMU can be excluded. A model that assumes solar + battery co-location is CM-neutral will book CM revenue that cannot be prequalified.
- **How to implement:** Boolean gate before any CM revenue is recognised: `not (site_pv_fit_accredited or site_pv_ro_accredited or site_holds_cfd) or non_support_confirmation_given`. Structure the battery as a distinct Generating Unit / CMU from the supported PV where possible, and record that decision explicitly in the model's assumption register.
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity Regulations 2014 (SI 2014/2043), regulation 16 (excluded CMUs; low carbon exclusion; non-support confirmation), https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2014/2043/regulation/16
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rule 3.4.7 (Low Carbon Exclusion and Low Carbon Grant status), Rule 3.9.2 (Permitted On-Site Generating Units)

#### D-48 · MUST · Model secondary trading as a real but constrained option: only obligation transfers under Rule 9.2.4(a) can be traded, only after the T-1 auction for the relevant Delivery Year has concluded, one Delivery Year at a time, subject to the 1 MW Minimum Capacity Threshold on both the traded part and the residual, and blocked while capacity payments are suspended for a performance failure.

- **Why:** Rules 9.2.4, 9.2.5 and 9.2.6. Additionally, DESNZ confirmed in July 2024 that the Rules would be amended so that the EPT MW requirement (Adjusted Connection Capacity) is reduced pro rata when obligation is traded away and increased when acquired, with a re-test required within 60 working days if the obligation rises above the level at the last EPT.
- **Error if omitted:** Two consequences. (i) Secondary trading is the standard mitigation for a delayed BTM project that has won a T-4 agreement but will not be commissioned in time; a model with no trading option overstates termination risk. (ii) A model that assumes an operator can permanently shed obligation to dodge the EPT is wrong — the relief is per-Delivery-Year and triggers a re-test on any subsequent increase.
- **How to implement:** Per delivery year, allow TradedObligation in [-CO, +available], with |trade| >= 1.0 MW and residual CO either 0 or >= 1.0 MW. Adjust EPT requirement: AdjustedConnectionCapacity_effective = CC * TCWAA * (CO_after_trade / CO_at_award). If CO in any later year exceeds CO at the last EPT, schedule a re-test within 60 working days or before the end of the Delivery Year, whichever is earlier. Model a bid-ask haircut on traded obligation price (no public index exists; treat as an assumption).
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 9.2.4, 9.2.5, 9.2.6, 13.4A.4(c)-(d), 13.4A.5(aa)
- *Source:* DESNZ, Capacity Market: Government response - 2023 Phase 2 Consultation, July 2024, 'Adjusting the Extended Performance Testing Requirement through Secondary Trading'

#### D-49 · MUST · Model the metering and registration prerequisites for a BTM CMU: half-hourly metering with registered MPANs, an Approved Metering Solution or Metering Test Certificate for non-BM CMUs, a completed Metering Assessment, and entry on the Capacity Market Metering Register.

- **Why:** Regulation 4 requires a generating CMU's net output to be measured by half-hourly meters in accordance with the CM Rules. Rules 8.3.3(b)/(ba)/(d) require a Metering Assessment and, where indicated, a Metering Test Certificate. Rule 13.2.5 requires MPANs and either a Metering Test Certificate or confirmation of the Approved Metering Solution before a DSR Test. Rule 7.4A requires MPANs to be entered on the Capacity Market Metering Register from Auction Results Day.
- **Error if omitted:** For BTM assets this is the most common practical failure point, because the battery sits behind the customer's supply meter and needs its own sub-metering to separate battery flows from site load. A model that ignores it omits both the capex (sub-metering, comms) and the risk that the metering configuration cannot support the Rule 8.6.2 A + B - C calculation at all. A change in metering configuration also invalidates the Metering Test Certificate and requires a new one (Rule 13.3).
- **How to implement:** Capex line: HH sub-metering + comms + Approved Metering Solution design, per CMU. Compliance gates: metering_assessment_complete, metering_test_certificate_valid (if required), mpans_on_cm_metering_register. Data requirement: the model must have half-hourly import AND export AND battery-terminal data separately, not a single net site series, to evaluate Eij.
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity Regulations 2014 (SI 2014/2043), regulation 4
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 7.4A, 8.3.3, 13.2.5, 13.2.12B-13.2.12E, 13.3
- *Source:* EMR Settlement Ltd, G1 Capacity Market Metering guidance, https://www.emrsettlement.co.uk/document/guidance/g1-capacity-market-metering/

#### D-50 · MUST · If the DSR route is modelled, implement the DSR Test and the Proven DSR Capacity formula: three separate historic Settlement Periods within the 2 years before the prequalification window in which a Baseline Demand can be calculated and a positive DSR Volume was delivered in discharge of a balancing services obligation; Proven DSR Capacity is TWICE the lowest of the three evidenced DSR Volumes.

- **Why:** Rule 13.2.6(a). The doubling converts an MWh-per-half-hour volume into MW. It also means the CMU's registered DSR Capacity is set by its WORST of three demonstrations, and requires the asset to already hold a balancing services obligation it has delivered against.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that assumes DSR Capacity equals the battery's rating will overstate the CM obligation the asset can win. It will also miss the chicken-and-egg constraint: to prequalify as a Proven DSR CMU the asset must already be operating and delivering a balancing service, which is impossible for a greenfield BTM battery in its first prequalification round (hence the Unproven DSR route with Applicant Credit Cover).
- **How to implement:** ProvenDSRCapacity_MW = 2 * min(DSRVolume_MWh over the three evidenced settlement periods), after Line Loss Factor adjustment evidenced by a DNO letter (Rule 13.2.6(a)(iii)). For a greenfield asset, force the Unproven DSR route with credit cover and a DSR Test no later than one month before the start of the Delivery Year (Rule 13.2.2(b)).
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 13.2.1-13.2.6, definitions of 'Proven DSR Capacity' and 'DSR Volume'

#### D-51 · MUST · Model Applicant Credit Cover as a real financing cost and a real risk of forfeiture: £5,000/MW for Unproven DSR CMUs (£10,000/MW where bidding for a multi-year obligation before partial release), £10,000/MW for other CMUs, rising to £15,000/MW for new build CMUs 12 months after auction results day in specified circumstances.

- **Why:** Regulation 59 of SI 2014/2043 sets these amounts and the 15-working-day provision deadlines. Rule 4.6 makes prequalification conditional on providing it. Rule 5.5.13 allows partial release where an Unproven DSR CMU nominates less than its original de-rated capacity under Rule 5.5.11.
- **Error if omitted:** Credit cover is working capital tied up from conditional prequalification until Substantial Completion, and is drawn down on non-delivery. It is per MW of DE-RATED capacity for the CMU. A model with no credit cover line understates the equity requirement and omits a genuine loss-given-failure component in the P90.
- **How to implement:** CreditCover_GBP = rate_per_MW * DeRatedCapacity_MW, posted at conditional prequalification (letter of credit or cash), with a cost-of-carry line (LC fee, typically 1-3%/yr of face) until release at Substantial Completion. Step up to £15,000/MW at auction results day + 12 months for new build. Model drawdown in the non-delivery scenario.
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity Regulations 2014 (SI 2014/2043), regulation 59, https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2014/2043/regulation/59
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 4.5A, 4.6, 5.5.11, 5.5.13, 6.7B

#### D-52 · MUST · Bound the CM clearing price distribution with the published auction parameters — Price Cap, Net CONE and Price Taker Threshold — and with the full observed history, not with a point estimate.

- **Why:** For the 2029/30 T-4, 2026/27 T-1, 2030/31 T-4 and 2027/28 T-1 auctions DESNZ set Price Cap £75/kW/yr, Net CONE £49/kW/yr and Price Taker Threshold £25/kW/yr. The auction is a descending clock with a series of price-spread bidding rounds (Rule 5.5.5). Observed clearing prices span £0.77/kW/yr (2019/20 T-1) to £75/kW/yr (2022/23 T-1, at cap).
- **Error if omitted:** Using a single CM price assumption for the merchant tail hides a 100x range in outcomes. P50/P90 revenue bands built without an explicit CM price distribution will be wrong in the years after the contracted agreement expires. Note also that the Price Taker Threshold constrains an existing CMU's ability to bid above £25/kW/yr without a Price-Maker Memorandum and Certificate (Rule 4.8) — relevant to re-contracting at T-1.
- **How to implement:** Sample CM clearing price from a distribution truncated at [0, PriceCap]. P50 anchored on the recent T-4 result (£27.10/kW/yr for 2029/30, cleared 2026) with an upside toward Net CONE (£49/kW/yr) and a downside toward recent T-1 outcomes (£5.00/kW/yr for 2026/27). Add a scenario flag for the Price Taker Threshold constraint on existing-CMU re-entry.
- *Source:* DESNZ, Capacity Market auction parameters letters to NESO, July 2025 and July 2026, https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/capacity-market-auction-parameters-letter-from-desnz-to-neso-july-2025/full-details-of-auction-parameters-and-interconnector-de-rating-factors
- *Source:* NESO, Capacity Market Auction Static Data (all clearing prices 2016-17 TR to 2029-30 T-4)
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 5.5.5-5.5.9, 4.8

#### D-53 · MUST · Ensure the site's physical export/import limits and the customer's connection agreement can actually support the Capacity Obligation, and reconcile the three distinct capacity concepts: Connection Capacity (Rule 3.5), Nominated Connection Capacity (Rule 3.5.6), and De-rated Capacity (the obligation).

- **Why:** Rule 3.5.2 derives Connection Capacity from the registered capacity or inverter rating in the Grid or Distribution Connection Agreement in force at the date of application; Rule 3.5B.1(b) requires the LOWEST value where a range is stated. The obligation to deliver is expressed against the De-rated Capacity. For a BTM asset, Rule 8.6.2's A + B - C means the obligation can be discharged partly through avoided import, so a low export limit does not automatically disqualify — but the SPD test under Rule 13.4.1 requires demonstrating capacity at or above the Capacity Obligation.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that uses the battery's nameplate inverter rating rather than the connection-agreement value will overstate Connection Capacity and therefore De-rated Capacity, producing a CM revenue line the CMU cannot prequalify for. Where the DNO agreement states a range, using the top of the range rather than the bottom is a direct overstatement.
- **How to implement:** ConnectionCapacity_MW = min(registered capacity or inverter rating in the connection agreement, applying the LOWEST value if a range is stated). Then CC_nom in [0.5 * CC, CC]. Then CO = CC_nom * DF. Feasibility check for SPD: max sustainable (export + avoided import against baseline) at the demonstration half hour >= CO.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 3.5.1-3.5.6, 3.5B.1, 8.6.2, 13.4.1

#### D-54 · MUST · Model termination and non-completion exposure for a new-build BTM CMU: the Financial Commitment Milestone and Substantial Completion Milestone must be achieved, and Termination Fee rates TF1..TF9 are stated on the Capacity Market Register at the date the Capacity Agreement is issued.

- **Why:** Rule 6.6/6.6A (Financial Commitment Milestone), Rule 6.7 (Substantial Completion Milestone), Rule 7.4(m) (the Capacity Agreement Notice must state, for x = 1 to 9, the TFx rate applying if a Termination Fee or Non-completion Fee becomes payable). NESO publishes Termination Fee 1-5 rates per auction in the Capacity Market Auction Static Data.
- **Error if omitted:** For a BTM project that wins a T-4 agreement four years before delivery, the termination/non-completion fee is a real contingent liability against a construction programme that must clear planning, DNO connection and customer landlord consents. A model that treats a won CM agreement as pure upside with no downside understates the risk of bidding.
- **How to implement:** Contingent liability = TFx_rate * De-rated Capacity, triggered on failure to achieve the Financial Commitment Milestone or Substantial Completion Milestone by the (possibly long-stopped) date. Pull the rates for the relevant auction from the NESO Capacity Market Auction Static Data 'Termination Fee 1-5 Rate' fields. Include the Long Stop / Declared Long Stop options as mitigations.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 6.6, 6.6A, 6.7, 6.10, 7.4(m)
- *Source:* NESO, Capacity Market Auction Static Data (Termination Fee 1-5 Rate fields), https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/capacity-market-register/capacity_market_auction_static_data

#### D-55 · MUST · Record and use the Rule 2.3.4B trigger: a Storage Generating Technology Class is only Duration Limited (and therefore EFC-de-rated) if at least 5% of Loss of Load Occurrences for the delivery year are estimated to last longer than that class's minimum duration; the Auction Guidelines must state which classes are Duration Limited.

- **Why:** Rule 2.3.4B and 2.3.4C. For classes that are NOT Duration Limited, Rule 2.3.4(a) applies the Technology Class Weighted Average Availability instead of the EFC — which is why the historic de-rating table shows generic 'Storage' entries at 0.9611-0.9738 in the earliest auctions and why the curve plateaus at the TCWAA. It also explains the sharp discontinuities: the 2021-22 T-4 shows Storage (Duration 2h) at 0.9611 while Storage (Duration 1h) is 0.4041.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that assumes every duration bin is always EFC-de-rated will mis-handle both the long-duration plateau and any future year in which NESO determines a class is no longer Duration Limited. Longer-duration classes have historically flipped to the TCWAA treatment, which is a step change of several tens of percentage points.
- **How to implement:** Read the per-auction Duration Limited determination from the published Auction Guidelines (required by Rule 2.3.4C). If the declared duration class is not Duration Limited for that auction, DF = TCWAA for the Storage GTC rather than EFC. In practice this is captured automatically by using the published per-bin DF table, so the requirement is: use the table, never a modelled EFC.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 2.3.4(a), 2.3.4(d), 2.3.4A, 2.3.4B, 2.3.4C, Schedule 3B
- *Source:* NESO, Capacity Market De-Rating Factors dataset (2021-22 T-4 rows: Storage 1h 0.4041, Storage 2h 0.9611)

#### D-56 · MUST · For DSR CMUs demonstrating Satisfactory Performance Days, model the CMU Portfolio aggregation option: DSR CMUs with components on two or more different sites, and Distribution/Transmission CMUs whose aggregate generating unit connection capacity is no greater than 50 MW, may demonstrate SPDs in aggregate across a portfolio.

- **Why:** Rules 13.4.1B, 13.4.1C, 13.4.1D. A CMU that has demonstrated SPDs as part of one portfolio must not form part of any other portfolio in that Delivery Year, and the same portfolio must be used for all SPDs.
- **Error if omitted:** For a fleet of small BTM batteries across several C&I sites — the exact deployment shape in scope — portfolio SPD demonstration materially reduces the operational burden and the risk of an individual site failing. A model that requires every site to independently demonstrate three SPDs overstates operating cost and failure probability.
- **How to implement:** If modelling a portfolio: sum(discharge_MW across portfolio CMUs at settlement period t) >= sum(CapacityObligation across those CMUs) on each of three separate Winter days, with at least one in Jan-Apr. Constrain each CMU to exactly one portfolio per Delivery Year. Note this relief applies to SPDs under Rule 13.4, NOT to the Extended Performance Test under Rule 13.4A.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 13.4.1B, 13.4.1C, 13.4.1D, 13.4.2(d)

#### D-57 · MUST · Require true half-hourly site data (import, export, and battery terminal flows separately) for at least a full year before any CM revenue is underwritten, because every CM quantity for a BTM asset is defined per Settlement Period against a baseline built from historic half-hourly demand.

- **Why:** The Schedule 2/2A baseline is constructed from same-weekday and last-10-working-day half-hourly demand samples; Eij under Rule 8.6.2 nets export, baseline and consumption per Settlement Period; the SPD test is per Settlement Period; the EPT is over consecutive Settlement Periods. There is no aggregated or monthly formulation of any of these.
- **Error if omitted:** Synthesising site load from monthly consumption and a generic profile will produce a baseline that is systematically wrong at winter evening peaks — precisely the periods in which stress events, SPDs and EPTs occur. The error is not random: C&I profiles with shift patterns, seasonal process load or on-site generation deviate strongly from standard profile classes exactly at peak. Any CM deliverability conclusion drawn from synthetic data is unfalsifiable and should not be underwritten.
- **How to implement:** Hard data gate: cm_revenue_recognised requires (a) >= 12 months of actual half-hourly import data at the CMU's MPANs, (b) export data or a documented no-export configuration, (c) evidence that the metering configuration can distinguish battery flows from site load. If only synthetic data exists, output the CM line as an explicitly-labelled indicative figure excluded from the bankable case.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Schedule 2 paragraph 2.1, Schedule 2A paragraph 2.1, Rules 8.6.2, 13.4.1, 13.4A.3
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity Regulations 2014 (SI 2014/2043), regulation 4 (net output measured by half-hourly meters)

#### D-58 · MUST · Model six independent products (DCL, DCH, DML, DMH, DRL, DRH) with separate contracted quantities per Response Window, not a single lumped 'FFR' revenue line.

- **Why:** Response Products are defined individually and low and high frequency services are procured separately, each with its own buy curve, clearing price and contracted quantity. Prices differ by more than an order of magnitude and by sign across the six.
- **Error if omitted:** Systematic mis-statement of revenue. In FY26/27 the six mean clearing prices span −£8.84/MW/h (DRH) to +£16.64/MW/h (DRL). Collapsing them to a single average understates DRL revenue by ~5x and turns a DRH liability into an asset.
- **How to implement:** Decision variables q[s,d,w] in MW for s in {DC,DM,DR}, d in {L,H}, w in Response Windows. Revenue_w = sum_s,d q[s,d,w] * price[s,d,w] * hours(w) with price allowed to be negative. Enforce q integer (see integer-bid requirement).
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Procurement Rules', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, Schedule 1 definition of 'Response Products', https://www.neso.energy/document/384601/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', June 2026, 'We procure low and high frequency services separately', https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Enduring Auction Capability (EAC) auction results', accessed 11 August 2026, https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/eac-auction-results

#### D-59 · MUST · Use a four-hour Response Window (EFA block) as the commitment granularity for DC/DM/DR, starting at 23:00, and allow 3-hour and 5-hour blocks on the two clock-change days. Do not model response commitment at half-hourly granularity.

- **Why:** 'Response Window' is defined as each consecutive four-hour period commencing at 23:00 hours. Contract delivery is done across four-hour EFA Blocks. Observed EAC delivery windows are 4 h with min 3 h and max 5 h.
- **Error if omitted:** A half-hourly response commitment variable lets the optimiser cherry-pick the 8 most valuable half-hours of a block. This inflates response revenue and, worse, understates the state-of-energy burden, because the Minimum State of Energy Requirement recursion runs across the whole block whether or not the price was attractive in every half-hour.
- **How to implement:** Index response decisions by w (EFA block); index energy/SoC by t (settlement period); link with q[s,d,w] constant for all t in w. hours(w) = 4 except on the last Sunday in March (3) and October (5).
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Procurement Rules', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, Schedule 1 definition of 'Response Window', https://www.neso.energy/document/384601/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', June 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, EAC auction results (author's analysis of deliveryStart/deliveryEnd, FY25/26 and FY26/27: min 3 h, max 5 h, mean 4.0 h), https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/eac-auction-results

#### D-60 · MUST · Enforce the Reserved Capacity power constraint for energy-limited units: sum over all products in the low direction of (offered quantity + its reserved capacity), plus the same sum for the high direction, must not exceed the unit's Maximum Registered Capacity. Reserved capacity percentages are DC 10 %, DM 20 %, DR 40 % of offered quantity.

- **Why:** This is an explicit pre-condition on the validity of a Sell Order, and it is derived exactly as the power needed to deliver the mandated 20 % Energy Recovery volume within one 30-minute settlement period: %RC = (delivery duration in minutes / 60) x 0.2 x 2 x 100.
- **Error if omitted:** Overstates the sellable MW by up to 40 %. A symmetric DR position is capped at P_rated/2.8 = 0.357 P_rated per direction, not P_rated/2 = 0.5. Omitting this overstates DR revenue by 40 % and produces bids that would be rejected at validation or lead to deemed unavailability under Service Terms paragraph 5.7.
- **How to implement:** sum_{s in {DC,DM,DR}} (1 + rc_s) * q[s,L,w] + sum_s (1 + rc_s) * q[s,H,w] <= P_registered, with rc_DC = 0.10, rc_DM = 0.20, rc_DR = 0.40. Equivalently rc_s = (T_sus_s / 60) * 0.4 where T_sus is 15/30/60 minutes.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Procurement Rules', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, paragraph 8.3.3.2 and Schedule 1 definition of 'Reserved Capacity', https://www.neso.energy/document/384601/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'SOE Monitoring Guidance for Energy Limited DC/DM/DR Providers', V2, March 2025, section 3 'Reserved Capacity at Bid Stage' and 'General equation for Dx Service', https://www.neso.energy/document/347241/download

#### D-61 · MUST · Implement the Minimum State of Energy Requirement (MSoER) as a per-settlement-period recursion within each contracted service period, seeded at the Contracted Response Energy Volume, decremented by delivered response energy, and incremented by an Energy Recovery Adjustment Volume capped at 20 % of REV and lagged by four settlement periods.

- **Why:** The Service Terms make this the operative constraint for energy-limited units, and failing it makes the unit deemed unavailable for that settlement period and all subsequent periods until NESO is satisfied compliance is restored.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that only checks 'SoC >= contracted MW x duration' at the start of the block will overstate availability. The four-settlement-period lag on energy recovery means a unit can be non-compliant for two hours after a heavy delivery even though its physical SoC has recovered. The penalty is loss of the entire availability payment from that period onward in the block.
- **How to implement:** REV_s = q_s * T_sus_s/60 (MWh). ER_s = 0.2 * REV_s. For j = 1: MSoER(1) = sum_s REV_s. For j > 1: MSoER(j) = MSoER(j-1) - LFE(j-1) + adj(j-1), where adj(j) = adj0(j-4) for j > 4 else 0, and adj0(j) = min(LFE(j) + leftover(j), ER). Constraint: SoE_export(j) >= MSoER_LF(j) and SoE_import(j) >= MSoER_HF(j), computed separately per direction (asymmetric when Q_L != Q_H).
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, paragraphs 6.10–6.13 (State of Energy Management Rules) and 5.6–5.7, https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'SOE Monitoring Guidance for Energy Limited DC/DM/DR Providers', V2, March 2025, section 4 and Table 5, https://www.neso.energy/document/347241/download

#### D-62 · MUST · Compute the energy actually delivered by each contracted product from a measured 1 Hz (or finer) system frequency time series applied to the published delivery curve, and charge that energy against the battery's state of charge and its degradation budget. Do not assume response is energy-neutral or de minimis.

- **Why:** The delivery curves are exact piecewise-linear functions of frequency and the throughput they imply differs by a factor of ~20 between DC and DR. GB frequency spends roughly half its time outside the ±0.015 Hz deadband, so delivery is near-continuous even though it is small in magnitude for DC.
- **Error if omitted:** Understates cycling on DR by an enormous margin. Author's calculation from NESO 1-second frequency data: gross MWh per MW contracted per year is DCL 64 / DCH 62 (Jan-2026 basis) and DRL 1,275 / DRH 1,247. For a 1 MW / 2 MWh unit fully contracted on DRL all year that is ~640 equivalent full cycles from the service alone. A model that ignores it will pass a 350-cycle/year warranty test that the real asset fails, and will also double-count the same MWh as available for arbitrage.
- **How to implement:** R_DC(dev) = 0 for dev<=0.015; 0.05*(dev-0.015)/0.185 for 0.015<dev<=0.2; 0.05+0.95*(dev-0.2)/0.3 for 0.2<dev<=0.5; 1 above. R_DM(dev) = 0 for dev<=0.015; 0.05*(dev-0.015)/0.085 for 0.015<dev<=0.1; 0.05+0.95*(dev-0.1)/0.1 for 0.1<dev<=0.2; 1 above. R_DR(dev) = clip((dev-0.015)/0.185, 0, 1). dev = |f-50|, LF services active when f<50, HF when f>50. E_delivered[s,d,t] = q[s,d] * sum over seconds in t of R_s(dev)/3600. Throughput is additive across stacked services because the general stacked curve is the quantity-weighted sum.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, Schedule 2 Table 1 (delivery curve values), Table 2 (general stacked curve) and Table 3, https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, historic system frequency data (1-second), January 2026 and June 2026 files, https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/system-frequency-data
- *Source:* Fan, F., Nwobu, J. and Campos-Gaona, D. (2023), 'Co-located battery energy storage optimisation for dynamic containment under the UK frequency response market reforms', CSEE Journal of Power and Energy Systems, DOI 10.17775/CSEEJPES.2023.01210 — corroborates that DC responses 'usually locate below 5%' of contracted quantity

#### D-63 · MUST · Treat delivered response energy as unremunerated: value low-frequency (export) delivery as a loss of stored energy at its opportunity cost, and high-frequency (import) delivery as free energy received. Do not book a utilisation payment for DC/DM/DR.

- **Why:** DC/DM/DR pay for availability only. Response energy volumes are passed to Elexon as Applicable Balancing Services Volume Data so that imbalance positions are adjusted by reference to them — the provider is neither charged nor paid for the response energy through imbalance.
- **Error if omitted:** Two errors of opposite sign. Booking a utilisation payment invents revenue that does not exist. Ignoring the energy entirely makes DRH look like a loss-making product (it clears negative) when the free charge energy is precisely what makes it rational: at 1,247 MWh/MW/yr of free import, ~0.142 MWh per MW per hour, a £60/MWh energy value is ~£8.5/MW/h — almost exactly the −£8.84/MW/h mean DRH clearing price in FY26/27. Any model that cannot reproduce that identity is mis-specified.
- **How to implement:** Add to the objective: - sum_t lambda_t * E_delivered_LF[t] + sum_t lambda_t * E_delivered_HF[t] * eta_charge, where lambda_t is the marginal energy value used elsewhere in the model (wholesale/imbalance for a merchant party, avoided import tariff for a BTM site — see the BTM settlement requirement). Route both into the SoC balance and the throughput/degradation counter.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', June 2026, 'Payments are provided for availability only', https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, paragraph 16 (ABSVD), https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Stacking Guidance', V1, November 2025, section 2.4 'any BOA will not impact the determination of response energy volume data which is provided to Elexon under the ABSVD submission', https://www.neso.energy/document/372541/download

#### D-64 · MUST · Allow response clearing prices to be negative, and allow the model to choose a negative-priced product when the free-energy benefit exceeds the payment made to NESO.

- **Why:** High-frequency products regularly clear below zero. Negative Bid Prices, Sell Prices and Market Clearing Prices are explicitly contemplated by the Procurement Rules.
- **Error if omitted:** A model with a price >= 0 assumption, or one that floors revenue at zero, will never select DRH or DMH and will therefore miss the free-charging strategy that the observed market is actually pricing. It will also fail to capture the corresponding downside: the settlement formula makes a K=0 failure on a negative-priced product cost more than the price alone.
- **How to implement:** No lower bound on price inputs. Revenue term must be signed. In the penalty term use PF = max(MCP, £1/MW/h) so that a failed DRH contract at MCP = -£10/MW/h costs (-10 - 1) x V x 0.5 = -£5.50 per MW per settlement period rather than -£5.00.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Procurement Rules', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, paragraphs 7.2.5, 8.2.7 and Schedule 1 definitions of 'Maximum Market Price'/'Minimum Market Price', https://www.neso.energy/document/384601/download
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, EAC auction results (author's analysis): DRH mean clearing price FY23/24 −£4.55, FY24/25 −£4.62, FY25/26 −£3.25, FY26/27 −£8.84/MW/h, https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/eac-auction-results

#### D-65 · MUST · Implement the availability payment and penalty exactly: S = round(((P − ((1 − K·F) · PF)) · V · 0.5), 2) per settlement period, with PF = max(MCP, Minimum Adjustment Price of £1/MW/h), F = 0 if unavailability in that settlement period totals at least 0.1 % of its duration, and K taken as the minimum settlement-period k factor across the entire contracted service period.

- **Why:** This is the settlement formula in Schedule 3 of the Service Terms. Two features dominate the risk: the 0.1 % availability cliff (1.8 seconds in a 30-minute period) and the fact that K is a block-level minimum, not a period-level value.
- **Error if omitted:** Modelling penalties as a smooth availability factor understates tail risk severely. A single 2-second dropout zeroes a whole settlement period's payment. A single bad half-hour sets K for all eight half-hours of the four-hour block. Underwriting a P90 without this produces a P90 that is not a P90.
- **How to implement:** Per EFA block w: K_w = min over j in w of k_j, where k_j = 1 if E_j < A; 1 − (E_j − A)/(B − A) if A <= E_j <= B; 0 if E_j > B. Then for each j in w: S_j = (P_w − (1 − K_w · F_j) · max(P_w, 1.0)) · V · 0.5. In a MILP, represent this as a scenario-weighted expected K rather than endogenously, and stress-test K in {1.0, 0.9, 0.5} bands.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, Schedule 3 'Calculation of Settlement Value' and 'K factor calculation', and Schedule 1 definition of 'Minimum Adjustment Price' (£1/MW/h), https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download

#### D-66 · MUST · When stacking two or more Dynamic Response services on the same unit and window, apply the tightest of the stacked services' parameters: minimum max-time-to-full-delivery, minimum lag upper bound, minimum ramp time bound, maximum minimum-ramp-rate, lowest error tolerances A and B, shortest grace periods and shortest rolling minimum window.

- **Why:** Schedule 2 and Schedule 3 of the Service Terms state that stacked parameters are set by the fastest service and that the lowest A and B values apply. DR standalone is judged at A = 0.05 / B = 0.25 with a 10 s time to full delivery and a 2 s rolling window; stacked with DC or DM it is judged at A = 0.03 / B = 0.07 with 1 s delivery and a 0.2 s rolling window.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that stacks DR with DC to maximise £/MW will book the DR revenue without recognising that the whole stack is now held to DC's performance standard, and that DR's own performance-monitoring data rate rises from 2 Hz to 20 Hz. The realistic effect is a lower expected K on the entire stack and higher metering/telemetry cost.
- **How to implement:** Stacked parameter set: TdMAX = min over stacked services; TiMAX = min; tr_max = min; rr_min = max; A = min(A_s); B = min(B_s); grace1 = min; grace2 = min; e_win = min. If any DC or DM contract is held in a window, DR in that window inherits TiMAX = 0.5 s, TdMAX = 1 s, A = 0.03, B = 0.07, grace1 = 0.55 s, grace2 = 2 s, e_win = 0.2 s.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, Schedule 2 service parameter table (DR values '(or 0.5 s when Stacked with DC and/or DM)') and Schedule 3 'Lags and ramp limits', https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download

#### D-67 · MUST · Restrict sell orders to whole MW integers, with a minimum of 1 MW and a maximum of 100 MW per Response Unit per Response Product, and model partial acceptance (the auction may award less than offered).

- **Why:** The Procurement Rules require the Offered Quantity to be an integer not less than 1 MW and not greater than the Maximum Sell Size, which is 100 MW per Response Product unless otherwise notified. Acceptance Ratios for Child and Substitutable Child orders lie between 0 and 1.
- **Error if omitted:** For a 0.1–5 MW BTM asset this is the binding eligibility test. A 0.8 MW battery cannot participate at all on its own — it must be aggregated. A continuous-MW model will produce a 0.6 MW DRL award that is not a legal order. Ignoring partial acceptance overstates realised volume.
- **How to implement:** q[s,d,w] integer, q >= 1 or q = 0 (semi-continuous with a binary), q <= 100. Apply a clearing/acceptance probability or a haircut factor to awarded volume; do not assume Acceptance Ratio = 1.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Procurement Rules', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, paragraphs 8.3.3.1, 9.2 and Schedule 1 definition of 'Maximum Sell Size', https://www.neso.energy/document/384601/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', June 2026, 'A minimum response capacity of 1 MW is required per response unit', 'A maximum response capacity of 100 MW', 'Sell orders may only be submitted in whole MWs', https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download

#### D-68 · MUST · If the asset is below 1 MW, model aggregation explicitly and constrain aggregation to assets within the same GSP Group; carry an aggregator fee and the loss of control that comes with it.

- **Why:** Aggregation is possible when assets sit behind the same GSP Group, subject to technical assurance. The minimum is per Response Unit, not per asset.
- **Error if omitted:** A sub-1 MW BTM asset modelled as a direct market participant is not underwritable — it cannot hold a Response Contract. Ignoring the aggregator's share overstates net revenue; typical aggregator commercial terms are not published, so this must be an explicit, sensitised input.
- **How to implement:** Boolean gate: if P_registered < 1 MW then response revenue = 0 unless aggregation_route = True. If aggregated: revenue_net = revenue_gross x (1 − aggregator_share) and add a constraint that the asset's GSP Group matches the aggregation unit's.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', June 2026, 'Aggregation is possible when assets sit behind same GSP Group (subject to technical assurance being carried out on the risks associated with moving to GSP Group)', https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Procurement Rules', Version 6, Schedule 1 definitions of 'Auction Unit', 'GSP Group', 'Relevant Metering', https://www.neso.energy/document/384601/download

#### D-69 · MUST · Model the ±0.015 Hz deadband as a hard prohibition on baseline deviation, and do not apply the Zero Deadband option to a battery unless the unit is genuinely not Energy Limited.

- **Why:** Response must not be delivered within the deadband, and a Response Unit must not deviate from its Operational Baseline while frequency is inside the deadband, whether to manage state of energy or otherwise. Zero Deadband may only be designated for an Auction Unit which is NOT Energy Limited.
- **Error if omitted:** Two errors. First, a model that lets the battery trade freely at all times while under contract will over-earn from arbitrage inside the deadband, which is ~52 % of the time and is contractually forbidden except through a submitted (and therefore visible) baseline. Second, applying Zero Deadband to a battery raises modelled DR throughput by roughly 18 % (author's calculation: DRL 1,275 to 1,503 MWh/MW/yr, Jan-2026 basis) on an option the battery is not eligible for.
- **How to implement:** Add a state variable for the submitted baseline; the physical power must equal baseline + response, and any change in physical power inside the deadband must be effected by changing the baseline, subject to gate closure at 60 minutes before the settlement period for BM units. Set zero_deadband_eligible = (not energy_limited).
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, paragraphs 6.7(iv), 6.11(vii), 6.13 ('State of Energy shall not be managed by over and/or under delivery including "spilling"') and Schedule 2, https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Procurement Rules', Version 6, paragraph 6.5, https://www.neso.energy/document/384601/download
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 1-second system frequency, January and June 2026 (author's calculation: |f-50| <= 0.015 Hz for ~52 % of seconds)

#### D-70 · MUST · Model state-of-energy recovery as an explicit market transaction executed through the submitted Operational Baseline (a physical notification / OBP submission), priced at the prevailing energy value, and constrained by gate closure.

- **Why:** The Service Terms require SoE to be managed by submission of updated Operational Baselines for charging or discharging, and expressly forbid managing it by over- or under-delivery. The baseline must reflect the natural state of the unit absent response and is subject to gaming checks.
- **Error if omitted:** Free recharging. A model that silently restores SoC after response delivery is assuming energy at zero cost. For a symmetric DR position the net drift over a four-hour block reaches ±1.5 MWh per MW contracted (author's calculation), which for a 1 MW / 2 MWh unit is 75 % of usable capacity and must be bought or sold at market prices, with the associated round-trip losses.
- **How to implement:** Baseline b[t] as a decision variable with cost lambda_t * b[t] * 0.5 (positive = discharge revenue, negative = charge cost); SoC[t+1] = SoC[t] − 0.5*b[t]/eta_dis (or *eta_ch) − E_LF_delivered[t]/eta_dis + E_HF_delivered[t]*eta_ch. Cap |b| by the residual power after Reserved Capacity. Fix b[t] at gate closure (60 minutes ahead) if modelling forecast error.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, paragraphs 6.2, 6.3, 6.11(v), 6.13, https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', June 2026, 'Gate Closure: The time 60 minutes prior to the start of the Settlement Period', https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Guidance on Baseline and Meter Data Approaches for Dynamic Response', December 2025, 'gaming checks' section, https://www.neso.energy/document/375491/download

#### D-71 · MUST · Allow simultaneous Response delivery and Balancing Mechanism participation, but model the protection mechanism: MEL/MIL reduced by the contracted response quantity, and price-out bands (up to £9999/MWh offers, −£9999/MWh bids) where MEL/MIL cannot express the constraint. Performance monitoring is then against the BM-adjusted baseline.

- **Why:** The Response Stacking Guidance sets out the general principles for splitting Dynamic Response with BOAs. MEL protects low-frequency contract capacity; MIL protects high-frequency contract capacity. Where the physical notification is negative, MEL cannot go below zero so price bands must be used.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that assumes full BM access while holding a DC/DM/DR contract will over-earn from the BM. Conversely, a model that forbids BM participation entirely while under response contract will under-earn. For a BTM asset that is a non-BM unit, the BM route is generally unavailable anyway, which is itself the more important modelling fact.
- **How to implement:** MEL[t] <= P_export_max − sum_s q[s,L,w]; MIL[t] >= −(P_import_max − sum_s q[s,H,w]). If PN < 0, add a priced-out offer tranche of |PN| MW at the price cap. Set BM_available = (is_BM_unit); for BTM non-BM units set to False and route flexibility through the OBP instead.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Stacking Guidance', V1, November 2025, section 2.1 'General principles' items 1, 2, 8, 9 and worked Examples 1–3, https://www.neso.energy/document/372541/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, paragraphs 6.3, 12.7(i), https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download

#### D-72 · MUST · For a behind-the-meter battery co-located with C&I site load behind a single boundary meter, model one of NESO's three permitted metering topologies and carry its capital and operating cost. A single combined site baseline must not be used.

- **Why:** NESO's December 2025 guidance approves exactly three configurations: Solution 1 (20 Hz boundary meter plus a meter on the ancillary load, battery active power derived by subtraction), Solution 2 (additional AC or DC meter on the battery, with a NESO-approved loss-adjustment methodology if DC), Solution 3 (DC-coupled battery and load sharing one inverter, DC metering on both plus an agreed loss-adjustment methodology). AC-coupled sites may use Solutions 1 or 2; DC-coupled sites may only use Solution 3, and Solution 3 is incompatible with mixed NBMU/BMU registration.
- **Error if omitted:** This is the single largest omission risk for a BTM model. Assuming the customer's existing supply meter is sufficient makes the project un-prequalifiable. The additional metering, 20 Hz data path and (for DC) a loss-adjustment methodology approved by NESO are real capex and schedule items, and DC-coupled solar-plus-storage on one inverter is materially harder than AC-coupled.
- **How to implement:** Site configuration input: coupling in {AC, DC}; registration in {BMU, NBMU} per asset. Gate: DC-coupled and mixed NBMU/BMU -> response revenue = 0. Add capex line for battery-specific metering, 20 Hz AC boundary meter (required at prequalification in all DC-metering cases), and a one-off loss-adjustment methodology approval. Performance data must report battery baseline as a separate variable from site load.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Guidance on Baseline and Meter Data Approaches for Dynamic Response — Battery and Site Load Co-Located Scenario', December 2025 (published February 2026), sections 2.2–2.4 and 3, https://www.neso.energy/document/375491/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, paragraph 12.9 (excess capacity must be separately Metered), https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download

#### D-73 · MUST · Carry the full telemetry and data obligation as an operating cost and a technical prerequisite: 1 Hz Operational Data with maximum 5-second delay, 20 Hz Performance Data (2 Hz permitted only for unstacked Dynamic Regulation), metered data accurate to 1 %, frequency to 0.001 Hz, hourly submission via a Data Concentrator, three-month retention, plus a Connection Indicator every five minutes for non-BM units via the OBP.

- **Why:** These are explicit obligations in paragraph 15 of the Service Terms, and failure to provide the data entitles NESO to withhold the availability payment and ultimately to deregister the asset.
- **Error if omitted:** Understated opex and, more importantly, understated technical risk. Failure to submit at least two consecutive Connection Indicators, or ten consecutive minutes without EDL communication, causes the unit to be deemed unavailable for the entire settlement period. For a small BTM site the comms path is often the weakest link in the chain.
- **How to implement:** Opex lines: data concentrator / API integration, comms link with SLA, metering maintenance. Availability model: A_service = A_battery x A_comms x A_metering, with A_comms explicitly bounded well below 1 and an outage duration distribution, because the 0.1 %-of-settlement-period rule makes even short dropouts binary.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, paragraphs 6.5, 7.3, 15.1–15.11, https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download

#### D-74 · MUST · Model prequalification and testing as a gating milestone with cost and elapsed time: duration test, frequency sweep test, step test and live system test, signed off by a technical engineer appointed by the provider, plus operational metering, Data Concentrator performance-monitoring and (for non-BM units) OBP testing.

- **Why:** The Provider Guidance sets out four test types and the requirement for a signed test report, and states that tests may be conducted at individual asset or aggregated unit level.
- **Error if omitted:** Revenue recognised from day one of commercial operation that cannot legally be earned. This shifts the whole cash flow profile and the debt service cover ratio in year 1.
- **How to implement:** Add a prequalification delay parameter (months) before response revenue starts, a testing cost line, and a probability-of-first-pass parameter. Re-testing is required if registered capacity changes; registration of Eligible Assets to a Response Unit may not be changed during a subsisting Response Contract.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', June 2026, 'Service Requirements' and 'Asset testing consists of 4 types of tests, Duration, Frequency Sweep, Step and Live system testing', https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, paragraph 5.9, https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download

#### D-75 · MUST · Model the disarming/re-arming regime: NESO may instruct a unit to cease providing any individual product with two minutes to comply, and the availability payment is unaffected by a Disarming Instruction.

- **Why:** Paragraph 6.14–6.19 of the Service Terms establishes per-service disarm codes (XCL/XCH/XML/XMH/XRL/XRH) and re-arm codes, with a two-minute acknowledgement and compliance window, and paragraph 6.19 preserves the availability payment.
- **Error if omitted:** Understates the value of the contract slightly (disarmed periods still pay) but, more importantly, understates the control-system requirement: the asset must accept per-service disarm within two minutes while continuing to deliver the other stacked services unchanged. This is a real controller specification, not a paperwork item.
- **How to implement:** Revenue is unaffected by disarm; SoC/throughput model should set delivered response to zero during disarmed intervals (a small upside to cycling). Add the per-service disarm capability as a technical prerequisite in the capex specification.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, paragraphs 6.14–6.19, https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', June 2026, Tables 15 and 16 (re-arm and disarm reason codes) and stacking disarm example, https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download

#### D-76 · MUST · Model the state-of-energy-depletion safe harbour: if an energy-limited unit exhausts its state of energy having complied with the SoE Management Rules, notified NESO and complied with disarm instructions, it is not deemed unavailable and the availability payment remains payable.

- **Why:** Paragraph 5.6 of the Service Terms creates this carve-out. Paragraph 5.7 removes it where NESO believes the SoE rules were deliberately or recklessly breached, in which case the unit is deemed unavailable for the entire contracted service period including the part before the failure.
- **Error if omitted:** Overstates downside if ignored (the model penalises an outcome the contract forgives), and understates downside if the safe harbour is assumed unconditionally — the penalty for the non-compliant version is retrospective loss of the whole four-hour block, not just the affected period.
- **How to implement:** Binary compliance flag per block. If compliant: payment retained on depletion. If non-compliant: S = 0 for all settlement periods in the block, and NESO may deem unavailability to persist into subsequent periods until satisfied of compliance (paragraph 6.12). Model the non-compliant branch as a low-probability, high-severity scenario in the P90.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, paragraphs 5.6, 5.7, 6.12, https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'State of Energy (SOE) Management Rules FAQs', https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/frequency-response-services/dynamic-services-dcdmdr

#### D-77 · MUST · Carry the non-delivery liability: reimbursement of NESO's additional costs of alternative or replacement service provision, capped per Response Contract at the greater of £250,000 and the aggregate availability payments for that contracted service period.

- **Why:** Paragraphs 12.3 and 12.4 of the Service Terms. For a small BTM asset the £250,000 floor dwarfs the revenue at risk from a single four-hour block.
- **Error if omitted:** The downside tail is missing entirely. For a 1 MW unit earning ~£3/MW/h on DCL, a four-hour block is worth £12; the liability cap is £250,000. This asymmetry is material to an insurance and covenant discussion even if the probability is low.
- **How to implement:** Liability_max = max(250000, sum of availability payments in the contracted service period). Model as a contingent liability with a probability weighting in the P90 case and as an insurance/reserve requirement in the financing structure.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, paragraphs 12.2–12.4, https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download

#### D-78 · MUST · Cap modelled volumes against the published GB-wide requirement for each product, and apply a market-share and clearing-probability haircut rather than assuming the asset always clears.

- **Why:** The requirements are small and published. As at 23 July 2026 the indicative requirements are DML 500–530 MW, DMH 510–570 MW, DRL 480 MW, DRH 480 MW and SFFR 250 MW per EFA block; the DC 4-day forecast for 11–14 August 2026 is 1,495 MW for both DC-H and DC-L; the long-term P50 forecast created 1 April 2026 puts DC-H at 1,100–1,700 MW and DC-L at 1,100 MW through April 2027, with DM and DR flat at 500 MW.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that assumes a fixed price and unlimited volume ignores the fact that GB battery capacity vastly exceeds these requirements, which is exactly why DCL fell from £17.00/MW/h in 2021Q3 to £2.79/MW/h in FY26/27. Any forecast that holds today's prices flat for 15 years is asserting a market equilibrium the data has repeatedly falsified.
- **How to implement:** For each product and window, revenue = q x price x hours x P(clear), with P(clear) estimated from the merit-order position implied by the bid price and the historic distribution of cleared volume versus requirement. Explicitly model the requirement as a hard cap on the fleet-level addressable market and derive the project's share.
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Dynamic Moderation Requirements' (Indicative DM Requirements) and 'Dynamic Regulation Requirements' (Indicative DR Requirements), accessed 11 August 2026
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Dynamic Containment 4 Day Forecast', accessed 11 August 2026
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Long-Term Forecasts for DC, DM and DR Requirements' (DX Future Requirement, forecast created 2026-04-01)
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Static Firm Frequency Response Requirement', accessed 11 August 2026
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, EAC and DC/DM/DR auction results (author's price analysis)

#### D-79 · MUST · Use price vintages no older than the current market design, and treat the 2020–2023 price history as evidence of regime change rather than as a distribution to sample from. In particular, do not use pre-2024 Dynamic Regulation High prices at all.

- **Why:** The observed history is not stationary. DCL cleared at exactly £17.00/MW/h in 2021Q3 and now averages £2.79/MW/h. DRL peaked at a quarterly mean of £29.59/MW/h in 2022Q3 and is £16.64/MW/h in FY26/27. DRH has changed sign, from +£26.17/MW/h in 2022Q2 to −£8.84/MW/h in FY26/27.
- **Error if omitted:** A model calibrated on 2021–2022 will overstate DC revenue by ~5x and will treat DRH as a revenue source rather than a cost. A model calibrated on any single year will understate variance. Both failure modes produce a P50 that is not a P50 and a P90 that is not conservative.
- **How to implement:** Maintain a price table keyed by (product, EFA block, financial year). Fit the revenue distribution on the post-EAC period only (FY25/26 onward for the co-optimised auction). For long-dated cash flows, impose a mean-reversion or decay path toward a saturation price justified by entry economics, and stress the decay rate as a named sensitivity.
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Dynamic Containment, Regulation and Moderation auction results' — DC, DR & DM Results Summary Master Data 2021–2023, https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/dynamic-containment-data
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Enduring Auction Capability (EAC) auction results', FY2023/24 to FY2026/27, https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/eac-auction-results
- *Source:* NESO, 'Annual Balancing Services Spend Report 2024/25', v2.0, 5 September 2025, 'Total volumes were noted as increased during the summer of last regulatory year, while clearing prices were down by up to 50%', https://www.neso.energy/document/371936/download

#### D-80 · MUST · Model prices at EFA-block granularity, not as a flat annual average, and reflect the systematic block shape.
