- **Why:** Clearing prices vary materially and systematically by EFA block. In FY26/27, DRL averages £22.73/MW/h in EFA5 (11:00–15:00) versus £12.74/MW/h in EFA3 (03:00–07:00); DRH averages −£12.35/MW/h in EFA5 versus −£1.10/MW/h in EFA4; DCH averages £4.16/MW/h in EFA4 versus £0.74/MW/h in EFA6.
- **Error if omitted:** Flat-price modelling destroys the co-optimisation signal. The block shape is exactly what determines whether the battery should hold response or do arbitrage in a given block, and a flat price makes that choice arbitrary. It also biases the result, because the high-value response blocks (EFA5, daytime) are also the high-value arbitrage blocks.
- **How to implement:** price[s,d,w] indexed by EFA block 1..6 (EFA1 = 23:00–03:00 through EFA6 = 19:00–23:00). Derive from the EAC results summary, which gives one clearing price per auctionProduct per deliveryStart/deliveryEnd window.
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Enduring Auction Capability (EAC) auction results' (author's pivot of clearing price by auctionProduct and EFA block, FY25/26 and FY26/27), https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/eac-auction-results

#### D-81 · MUST · Model Static Firm Frequency Response as a separate, still-live, low-frequency-only product with a 49.7 Hz trigger, 30-second response and 30-minute sustain, procured by daily day-ahead tender via the Single Market Platform with one bid per EFA block per unit and no multi-block bids.

- **Why:** SFFR remains live in 2026 and, unlike DC/DM/DR, has effectively zero energy throughput because GB frequency almost never reaches 49.7 Hz — 128 seconds in the whole of June 2026 and zero seconds in January 2026 (author's calculation from NESO 1-second frequency data). It cleared at a mean £4.63/MW/h in FY25/26 and £4.45/MW/h in FY26/27, above DCL.
- **Error if omitted:** A BTM model that omits SFFR omits the highest-margin-per-cycle frequency product available to it. SFFR pays more than DC while requiring essentially no energy delivery. It is also the product where demand-side and load response dominate the cleared stack, which is the natural fit for a C&I site.
- **How to implement:** Separate product with q_SFFR[w] integer >= 1 MW, revenue = q x price_SFFR[w] x hours(w). Headroom constraint: q_SFFR must be deliverable within 30 s and sustained 30 min, so reserve 0.5 h x q MWh of discharge energy. Throughput ~0 MWh/MW/yr. Treat as mutually exclusive with DC/DM/DR on the same MW absent a NESO no-conflict determination.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Static Firm Frequency Response (SFFR)' service page, accessed 11 August 2026 (trigger 49.7 Hz, 30 s response, sustained to 30 minutes, minimum 1 MW single or aggregated), https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/frequency-response-services/static-firm-frequency-response-sffr
- *Source:* NESO, 'Static FFR Service Terms', effective 31 July 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/384301/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Annual Balancing Services Spend Report 2024/25', v2.0, section on Static FFR ('Bids are submitted on a £/MW/Hr basis and providers can only submit one bid per EFA block for each unit. Bids spanning multiple EFA blocks are not permitted'), https://www.neso.energy/document/371936/download
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Static Firm Frequency Response auction results' (author's analysis), https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/static-firm-frequency-response-auction-results

#### D-82 · MUST · Remove closed services from the revenue stack: Dynamic Firm Frequency Response (discontinued), Enhanced Frequency Response (no longer actively procured), Short Term Operating Reserve (ended 1 April 2026 at 05:00), and Optional Fast Reserve (replaced by Quick Reserve).

- **Why:** NESO's Annual Balancing Services Spend Report states DFFR 'has now been discontinued' and EFR 'is no longer actively procured'. Slow Reserve went live 31 March 2026 and replaces STOR, which ends 1 April 2026 at 05:00. Quick Reserve replaces the legacy Optional Fast Reserve.
- **Error if omitted:** Any model still carrying an 'FFR' revenue line derived from pre-2021 tenders is booking revenue from a market that does not exist. This is the classic 2019-vintage error: FFR was the dominant battery revenue stream in 2017–2019 and is now either closed (dynamic) or a small, static, diesel-and-load-response-dominated market.
- **How to implement:** Explicit service registry with status and effective dates. Assert at model load: any revenue stream whose status is 'closed' contributes zero after its closure date. Log a warning if any input file references DFFR, EFR, FFR Dynamic, or STOR for a period after their closure.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Annual Balancing Services Spend Report 2024/25', v2.0, 5 September 2025, sections on SFFR ('Previously procured Dynamic Firm Frequency Response (DFFR) service has now been discontinued') and EFR ('This service is no longer actively procured'), https://www.neso.energy/document/371936/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Slow Reserve' service page (go-live 31 March 2026; replaces STOR which ends 1 April 2026 at 05:00), https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/slow-reserve
- *Source:* NESO, 'Quick Reserve Service Summary', Version 5.0, July 2025 ('Quick Reserve will replace the legacy Optional Fast Reserve service'), https://www.neso.energy/document/364336/download

#### D-83 · MUST · Model the auction as a co-optimised, welfare-maximising clearing across Response, Quick Reserve, Balancing Reserve and Slow Reserve, not as six independent single-product auctions. Represent the bidder's order structure (Parent, Child, Substitutable Child, Basket, Looped Family) at least to the extent of all-or-nothing and mutual-exclusivity behaviour.

- **Why:** The Market Clearing Rules maximise Market Welfare defined across Response plus the Quick Reserve, Balancing Reserve and Slow Reserve auction products, subject to acceptance-ratio constraints; the Market Clearing Price for each product and window is then set to minimise Total Auction Procurement Cost. A Basket contains one Parent Order (acceptance 0 or 1) and up to ten Child Orders and ten Substitutable Child Orders; Baskets over Concomitant windows are mutually exclusive; Looped Families force equal acceptance across non-overlapping windows.
- **Error if omitted:** A price-taker model that assumes independent clearing per product cannot represent the substitution NESO performs between products, and will misprice the correlation between DC, DM, DR and reserve outcomes. It will also fail to capture the practical bidding strategy (offer DRL or DCL as substitutable alternatives in the same window), which is how a real operator captures value.
- **How to implement:** Represent the bid as a set of mutually exclusive alternatives per window: at most one Basket accepted per Auction Unit per window; within a Basket, at most one Substitutable Child; Parent acceptance binary. Clearing prices co-move: sample scenarios jointly across all products rather than independently.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Procurement Rules', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, paragraphs 8.4–8.6, 9.1–9.5 and Schedule 1 definitions of 'Market Welfare', 'Basket', 'Looped Family', 'Substitutable Child Order', https://www.neso.energy/document/384601/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Annual Balancing Services Spend Report 2024/25', v2.0 ('Each service is procured via a day ahead auction within NESO's Enduring Auction Capability (EAC)'), https://www.neso.energy/document/371936/download

#### D-84 · MUST · Route the response energy through the battery degradation model on a throughput and depth-of-cycle basis, with the service-specific throughput derived from measured frequency, and check the result against any warranty cycle limit.

- **Why:** The three services impose radically different cycling burdens, and the difference is the single biggest determinant of whether frequency response is compatible with a warranty. DC is ~64 MWh/MW/yr; DR is ~1,275 MWh/MW/yr (Jan-2026 basis, per direction). Academic work on the same problem finds that increased cycle count combined with increased depth of discharge accelerates degradation and reduces project lifespan, and that the optimum energy footroom sits above the bare Minimum Energy Requirement precisely to limit this.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that treats response as availability-only will pass DR through with zero degradation cost and will therefore always prefer DR (the highest-priced product). Correcting for throughput can reverse the ranking entirely: on a 1 MW rated unit, a DRL-only position is capped at 0.714 MW by Reserved Capacity, earning ~£104k/MW-rated/yr at the FY26/27 mean price, but discharges ~910–1,052 MWh/yr that must be bought back — roughly £60–75k/yr at £60–70/MWh before degradation. The apparent £104k is closer to £30–45k.
- **How to implement:** Add E_delivered from the frequency-driven response model into the same throughput accumulator used by arbitrage. Apply a cycle-ageing model with SoC-dependent and DoD-dependent stress terms. Add a hard constraint or a penalty when annual equivalent full cycles exceed the warranty limit. Report EFC attributable to each revenue stream separately so the trade-off is visible.
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 1-second system frequency, January and June 2026 (author's throughput calculation applied to Service Terms Schedule 2 delivery curves)
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Procurement Rules', Version 6, Schedule 1 'Reserved Capacity', https://www.neso.energy/document/384601/download
- *Source:* Fan, F., Nwobu, J. and Campos-Gaona, D. (2023), 'Co-located battery energy storage optimisation for dynamic containment under the UK frequency response market reforms', CSEE Journal of Power and Energy Systems, DOI 10.17775/CSEEJPES.2023.01210 — cycle counting from the SoC time series with temperature, SoC and DoD stress factors; finds increased cycles plus increased DoD accelerate degradation and reduce project lifespan

#### D-85 · MUST · Drive the model with at least one full year of measured system frequency at 1 Hz or finer, and re-run on multiple years. Do not synthesise frequency, and do not use monthly or half-hourly average frequency.

- **Why:** Response delivery is a nonlinear function of instantaneous frequency, and the throughput differs by ~25–55 % between two months of the same year (author's calculation: DRL 1,275 MWh/MW/yr on January 2026 versus 1,474 on June 2026; DML 322 versus 495). Half-hourly averaging destroys the signal entirely because positive and negative excursions cancel.
- **Error if omitted:** Throughput, and therefore degradation and state-of-energy feasibility, is computed on a fiction. Averaging frequency to half-hourly resolution would report near-zero response energy for every service, which is the most dangerous possible error because it makes DR look free.
- **How to implement:** Data requirement gate: assert the frequency series has >= 1 sample/second and covers the modelled period. Compute per-settlement-period delivered energy per product and store it as an input array to the dispatch optimisation, so the MILP sees deterministic per-period response energy rather than a nonlinear function of a decision variable.
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, historic system frequency data (1-second resolution), https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/system-frequency-data
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, paragraph 15.4 (Performance Data at 20 Hz, or 2 Hz for unstacked Dynamic Regulation) and paragraph 15.8 (Frequency Measurement Standard), https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download

#### D-86 · MUST · Do not assume the baseline can be shaped to manufacture value. The Operational and Performance Baselines must accurately represent the natural state of the unit without response delivery, and NESO runs correlation and anomaly-detection checks against gaming.

- **Why:** The Stacking Guidance requires baselines to represent the natural state assuming frequency is at 50 Hz, and reserves NESO's right to investigate differences and suspected unwarranted manipulation. The December 2025 co-location guidance lists the specific gaming checks: correlation between baseline and ideal response, performance-monitoring versus operational-metering active power outside threshold, reported active power versus ideal response outside performance bounds, reported versus expected baseline, unavailability behaviour, and price incentive reactions.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that optimises the baseline jointly with the response to extract additional arbitrage will produce a strategy that is detected and penalised, and in the limit leads to deregistration. It will also overstate returns in a way that will not survive lender technical due diligence.
- **How to implement:** Constrain the baseline to be a function of the site's own intended operation (load, solar, arbitrage schedule) only, and forbid any baseline term that is a function of forecast frequency. Add a validation test that the correlation between the modelled baseline and the ideal response signal is approximately zero.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Stacking Guidance', V1, November 2025, section 2.1 principle 6 and section 2.3 ('Providers should not use pre-processing to artificially increase their apparent performance in delivery of Dynamic Response or mask any underperformance'), https://www.neso.energy/document/372541/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Guidance on Baseline and Meter Data Approaches for Dynamic Response', December 2025, 'Gaming checks calculations', https://www.neso.energy/document/375491/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', June 2026, 'De-registration of Units', https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download

#### D-87 · MUST · Enforce the bi-directional bidding check: the aggregate contracted quantities across opposite directions must not exceed the unit's MW capacity even before state-of-energy management, and NESO actively monitors and penalises breaches with deemed unavailability.

- **Why:** The Provider Guidance describes enhanced monitoring of bi-directional bidding, with the worked example that a unit with 10 MWh badged capacity tested for 10 MW DRL and 10 MW DRH cannot hold both simultaneously; units whose aggregate contracted quantities exceed unit capacity are deemed unavailable under paragraph 5.7.
- **Error if omitted:** Models frequently sell the same MW twice by treating LF and HF as independent because they use headroom and footroom respectively. That is correct for energy but wrong for power and wrong for the contractual test, which is on aggregate registered quantity.
- **How to implement:** This is the same constraint as the Reserved Capacity requirement, but it must be checked even when Reserved Capacity is set to zero: sum over all products in both directions of contracted quantity <= aggregate Maximum Registered Capacity of the constituent Eligible Assets. Add an explicit assertion and a test case.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', June 2026, 'Contracts' section on bi-directional bidding monitoring, https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, paragraphs 5 and 6, https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Procurement Rules', Version 6, paragraph 8.3.3.2, https://www.neso.energy/document/384601/download

#### D-88 · MUST · Model the auction timeline as a decision under uncertainty: sell orders are irrevocable after 14:00 on D-1, results are published at 16:00 on D-1, and the physical notification / operational baseline gate closes 60 minutes before each settlement period.

- **Why:** Auction Opening Time is 08:00 on D-15, Auction Closing Time is 14:00 on the EFA day immediately preceding the Service Day, Auction Results Time is 16:00 on that day, and after the closing time Sell Orders may not be modified or cancelled.
- **Error if omitted:** A perfect-foresight model that commits response volume knowing the realised day-ahead and imbalance prices will systematically overstate value, because the real decision is taken 10–34 hours before delivery with only a forecast. This is the standard perfect-foresight bias, and for a co-optimised battery it is typically worth 10–30 % of gross margin in the literature. The model must at minimum quantify the gap between perfect-foresight and forecast-driven dispatch.
- **How to implement:** Two-stage structure: stage 1 at D-1 14:00 fixes q[s,d,w] using forecast prices; stage 2 per settlement period optimises the baseline and arbitrage subject to the fixed response commitment and to gate closure at T-60 min. Report both the perfect-foresight upper bound and the forecast-driven result, and underwrite on the latter.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Procurement Rules', Version 6, Schedule 1 definitions of 'Auction Opening Time', 'Auction Closing Time', 'Auction Results Time', and paragraphs 8.7, 8.11, https://www.neso.energy/document/384601/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', June 2026, 'Gate Closure: The time 60 minutes prior to the start of the Settlement Period', https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download

#### D-89 · MUST · Model the ancillary-service revenue distribution for P50/P90 from the joint distribution of clearing prices across the six products and six blocks, using at least the post-EAC period, and report the P90 on annual revenue rather than on a single block.

- **Why:** Within-year dispersion is large and asymmetric. In FY26/27 DCL ranges from −£0.42 to £35.00/MW/h with a p10 of £1.10 and p90 of £5.13; DRL ranges from −£2.19 to £89.79 with p10 £7.49 and p90 £23.83. The distributions are right-skewed with occasional scarcity spikes.
- **Error if omitted:** Using a mean price with a symmetric uncertainty band will misstate both tails. Because the products are correlated (they clear in one welfare-maximising auction), independent sampling understates portfolio variance in some cases and overstates it in others.
- **How to implement:** Bootstrap or block-bootstrap whole days from the historic EAC results so that the cross-product and cross-block correlation structure is preserved. Compute the annual revenue distribution and take P50 and P90 from it. Report separately the contribution of the response stack to the DSCR and the sensitivity of the DSCR to a decay in response prices.
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Enduring Auction Capability (EAC) auction results' (author's distributional analysis by product and financial year), https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/eac-auction-results

#### D-90 · MUST · Treat Dynamic Moderation volume and price as event-driven and non-stationary: NESO raises DM requirements ad hoc at day-ahead for planned BM outages and system events.

- **Why:** The published industry notifications show repeated day-ahead uplifts to DML and DMH requirements for named events — a planned BM outage on 5 December 2024, the Euro 2024 final on 14 July 2024, and others — with the requirement raised for specific EFA blocks only.
- **Error if omitted:** Modelling DM as a smooth annual average both understates the upside tail and, more importantly, misrepresents the reliability of the revenue. DM cleared volume roughly tripled between FY24/25 (mean 173 MW DML) and FY26/27 (mean 510 MW DML), which is a step change driven by NESO requirement policy, not by a trend a forecaster could extrapolate.
- **How to implement:** Model DM revenue with an explicit base requirement plus an event-uplift process (frequency, duration in EFA blocks, magnitude). Do not extrapolate the FY25/26–FY26/27 DM growth linearly. Flag DM as the least forecastable of the six products in the underwriting note.
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Ancillary Services Important Industry Notifications' — DC, DM, DR Important Industry Notifications (accessed 11 August 2026)
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, EAC auction results (author's analysis of DML/DMH cleared volume by financial year), https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/eac-auction-results

#### D-91 · MUST · Model round-trip efficiency and auxiliary load consistently across response and arbitrage, and locate the metering point correctly (grid connection point, AC side, after inverter and transformer losses).

- **Why:** Active Power in performance monitoring is defined as the power flow of the unit to or from the grid measured at the grid connection point, and operational metering data and performance monitoring active power must correspond to the same electrical point — the same connection point and the same side of a transformer or inverter. Where DC metering is used, a NESO-approved loss-adjustment methodology must convert to an AC-equivalent value.
- **Error if omitted:** Contracted MW measured at the DC side rather than the AC connection point overstates deliverable response by the inverter and transformer loss, causing persistent negative performance error and K-factor erosion. It also breaks the state-of-energy accounting, because the contracted response energy volume is defined in terms of delivered (AC) energy.
- **How to implement:** Define P_contract and all response quantities at the AC grid connection point. SoC balance: SoC[t+1] = SoC[t] − (E_discharge_AC / eta_dis) + (E_charge_AC x eta_ch) − aux_load x dt. Ensure the Reserved Capacity and Minimum State of Energy tests use AC-referred quantities.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Guidance on Baseline and Meter Data Approaches for Dynamic Response', December 2025, assumptions section and Solutions 2 and 3, https://www.neso.energy/document/375491/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, paragraph 15.5(ii) (metered data to 1 % margin of error), https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download

#### D-92 · MUST · Gate any Balancing Mechanism or Balancing Reserve revenue on the asset actually meeting the eligibility thresholds, including BM Unit registration and the 1 MW minimum contract capacity.

- **Why:** A 0.1-5 MW BTM asset is at or below the participation floor for several GB products. Balancing Reserve requires BM Unit registration in the Single Markets Platform and a minimum contract capacity of 1 MW or above, plus control and/or system telephony during all contracted windows.
- **Error if omitted:** Sub-1 MW sites are credited with revenue streams they are legally unable to access, unless the model explicitly represents aggregation through a Virtual Lead Party. For a 0.1-0.5 MW BTM battery this can be most of the assumed stack.
- **How to implement:** Boolean gates on the revenue lines, evaluated per site: eligible_BR = (registered_BMU AND contracted_MW >= 1.0 AND telephony_available). Where aggregation is assumed, model the route explicitly: BSC Modification P415 (approved by Ofgem October 2023, implemented 7 November 2024) lets independent aggregators register as Virtual Trading Parties and access wholesale markets; the Open Balancing Platform (live 12 December 2023) introduced bulk dispatch of small BM units and batteries. Apply the aggregator's revenue share as a cost line.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Balancing Reserve' — participation requires registration as a Balancing Mechanism unit in the Single Markets Platform, 'a minimum contract capacity of 1MW or above', and control and/or system telephony during all contracted windows, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/balancing-reserve
- *Source:* Elexon, 'Elexon implements landmark rule change to drive growth in flexibility' (BSC Modification P415 implemented 7 November 2024), https://www.elexon.co.uk/2024/11/07/elexon-implements-landmark-rule-change-to-drive-growth-in-flexibility-and-progress-to-net-zero/
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Decision on P415: Facilitating access to wholesale markets for flexibility dispatched by VLPs', October 2023, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/ofgem-decision-p415-facilitating-access-wholesale-markets-flexibility-dispatched-vlps
- *Source:* NESO, 'First stages of Open Balancing Platform go live', 12 December 2023, https://www.neso.energy/news/first-stages-open-balancing-platform-go-live

#### D-93 · MUST · Represent BM acceptance as an explicit probabilistic or derating term applied to in-merit volume, not as deterministic clearing. Never assume that a bid/offer priced inside the marginal price is dispatched.

- **Why:** NESO and LCP Delta define a skip precisely as in-merit volume that was NOT dispatched, and measured battery skip rates have never been below ~35% on a Stage-5 Post System Action basis since publication began in December 2024. A deterministic price-clearing model implicitly assumes a 0% skip rate.
- **Error if omitted:** Overstates BM revenue by roughly 1/(1-s). At the H1 2026 measured battery rate of 38%, a deterministic model overstates BM energy revenue by ~61%. At the 2023 LCP Stage-3 rate of 93% for offers it overstates by >10x. Gale et al. quantify the profitability sensitivity at ~7% loss per 10pp of skip rate.
- **How to implement:** For each half-hour t and direction d ∈ {bid, offer}: E[V^accepted_{d,t}] = (1 − s_{d,t,z,k}) · V^in-merit_{d,t}, where V^in-merit_{d,t} = ρ_{d,t} · V^declared_{d,t} and ρ is the probability the declared price is inside the marginal accepted price. BM revenue_t = Σ_d E[V^accepted_{d,t}] · p^BOD_{d,t} with sign convention: offers paid to the unit, bids paid by the unit (negative bid prices become income). Skip rate index s is indexed by direction d, half-hour-of-day t, GSP zone z and route/size class k.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Defining, measuring, and addressing skip rates', December 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348236/download
- *Source:* LCP Delta for NESO, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* Gale E., Schmidt O., O'Cinneide A., Johnson N., Staffell I., 'Balancing with batteries: The impact of revenue stacking and skip rates on battery energy storage profitability in Great Britain', Journal of Energy Storage vol. 166, 2026, DOI 10.1016/j.est.2026.122328

#### D-94 · MUST · State explicitly which skip-rate metric the model uses — 'All BM Skip Rate' or 'Post System Action Skip Rate' — and which exclusion stage (0 to 5), and never mix vintages or stages in one time series.

- **Why:** NESO defines two distinct categories with materially different values, and LCP Delta's five-stage exclusion process changes the number by 23–33 percentage points between Stage 1 and Stage 5. Phase 1 (compare to marginal price) and Phase 2 (reconstruct merit stack to the actual energy requirement) methodologies also give different answers for the same period.
- **Error if omitted:** Silent order-of-magnitude error. Example: for all technologies in 2023, offer skip rate is 87% on the Phase 1 basis, 71% at Phase 2 Stage 1, and 48% at Phase 2 Stage 5. Picking the wrong one changes modelled BM revenue by a factor of ~4.
- **How to implement:** Model config must carry {metric: 'PSA'|'ALL_BM', stage: 0..5, methodology: 'LCP_Phase2', vintage: 'YYYY-MM'}. Recommended default for underwriting a merchant BTM asset: PSA Stage 5, because Stage 5 is the measure NESO publishes daily by technology and is the closest proxy for 'volume the control room could actually have taken from you'. Validate the loaded parameter against NESO's published Stage 5 PSA by-technology file.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Defining, measuring, and addressing skip rates', December 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348236/download
- *Source:* LCP Delta for NESO, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Skip Rates' dataset, from 15 December 2024, NESO Open Data Licence, https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/skip-rates

#### D-95 · MUST · Model separate skip rates for bids and for offers. Do not use a single symmetric parameter.

- **Why:** Bid and offer skip rates differ materially and the sign of the gap has reversed over time. LCP Delta 2023: battery offers 93%, bids 94% (Stage 3). NESO published data, volume-weighted: 2025 battery bids 50.3% vs offers 40.9%; 2026 YTD bids 40.6% vs offers 38.4%.
- **Error if omitted:** Mis-sizes the charge/discharge asymmetry. Because a BTM battery earns on offers (discharge) and pays or is paid on bids (charge), a symmetric parameter distorts both round-trip economics and state-of-charge feasibility. A ~10pp bid/offer error in 2025 translates to a ~7% profitability error per Gale et al.
- **How to implement:** Two parameter surfaces s^offer and s^bid, each of dimension [half-hour-of-day × month × zone]. Charging economics: cost_t = (1−s^bid_t)·V^bid_t·p^bid_t where p^bid < 0 means the unit is paid to charge.
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Skip Rates', file '5_fuel_type_skip_rate.csv' (Stage 5 PSA skip rate by technology), analysed 10 August 2026, https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/skip-rates
- *Source:* LCP Delta for NESO, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download

#### D-96 · MUST · Build the in-merit test against the reconstructed balancing energy requirement in the period, not against the marginal accepted price, and do it at 5-minute granularity if back-testing against NESO/LCP figures.

- **Why:** LCP Delta's Phase 2 methodology replaced the Phase 1 'anything cheaper than the marginal action is in-merit' test with a reconstructed price-ordered stack sized to the actual energy requirement. The change is large: LCP's worked example gives 60% under Phase 1 versus 50% under Phase 2 for the same data, and all-technology 2023 offer skip rates are 87% (Phase 1) versus 71% (Phase 2 Stage 1).
- **Error if omitted:** Overstates the addressable BM volume, because volume that appears cheaper than the marginal action may never have been needed. Calibrating a model's skip parameter from a Phase 1 statistic and applying it to a Phase 2 style volume estimate double-counts the correction and produces internally inconsistent revenue.
- **How to implement:** Skip Rate = (Total volume of skipped in-merit bid/offer) / (Total volume of in-merit bid/offer). For each 5-minute period: Q_t = Σ accepted volumes (the requirement); construct the stack S_t of cheapest available bid-offer pairs summing to Q_t; in-merit volume = |S_t|; skipped volume = volume in S_t not actually accepted. Monthly/annual rates are the VOLUME-WEIGHTED average of the 5-minute rates, weighted by Q_t, not a simple mean. Tie-breaks: an acceptance beats a potential skip at equal price; two potential skips at the same price share skipped volume on a volume-weighted basis.
- *Source:* LCP Delta for NESO, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* NESO/LCP Delta, 'Enhancing Storage in the Balancing Mechanism' webinar pack, 7 November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/347186/download

#### D-97 · MUST · Reproduce the five staged exclusions when calibrating, and understand that the exclusions can move the rate in either direction.

- **Why:** LCP Delta's stages remove (1) volumes procured outside the BM — BSAD, wind offers, winter coal contingency; (2) infeasible or inaccessible within BM timescales — MZT/MNZT ≥ 12 h, NDZ ≥ 90 min with zero post-BOA output, SEL/SIL capping; (3) volumes not taken for energy balancing only — system-flagged and frequency-response-flagged; (4) unwind actions; (5) volumes inaccessible to the balancing team — MZT/MNZT/NDZ ≥ 31 minutes. LCP explicitly show that excluding a cheap accepted action raises the measured skip rate (50% → 60% in their worked example) while excluding an expensive one lowers it (50% → 33%).
- **Error if omitted:** A home-grown skip metric will not reconcile with NESO's published dataset and cannot be defended to a lender. Directionally, omitting Stage 2/5 feasibility exclusions inflates the measured rate, making the asset look worse than it is; omitting Stage 3 system-flag exclusions deflates it for constrained locations, making the asset look better than it is.
- **How to implement:** Implement the exclusion cascade as an ordered filter over BOD/BOALF/dynamic-parameter data: Stage 1 filter on BSAD + fuel=WIND offers + winter coal contingency BMU list; Stage 2 filter MZT≥720min, MNZT≥720min, NDZ≥90min & post-BOA output = 0 in the prior 5-min block, cap volume at SEL/SIL; Stage 3 filter acceptances with soFlag=true or frequency-response tagging (and cascade the flag down to lower bid-offer pairs of the same BMU); Stage 4 drop unwind pairs; Stage 5 filter MZT/MNZT/NDZ ≥ 31 min sync/desync actions and SEL–MEL / SIL–MIL range violations.
- *Source:* LCP Delta for NESO, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* NESO/LCP Delta, 'Enhancing Storage in the Balancing Mechanism', 7 November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/347186/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Defining, measuring, and addressing skip rates', December 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348236/download

#### D-98 · MUST · Model Physical Notification, MEL and MIL submission explicitly, and derive the BM-available headroom and footroom from the site's net position rather than from the battery nameplate.

- **Why:** The Grid Code requires PNs and Export/Import limits per BM Unit per settlement period, and NESO's Balancing Reserve terms state that units position headroom or footroom via MEL, PN, SEL/SIL and MIL. For a behind-the-meter asset the BM Unit sits behind the customer's supply meter, so the deliverable offer is limited by the site's export capability and the deliverable bid by the site's import capability net of the customer's own load.
- **Error if omitted:** The model sells BM offers the asset physically cannot export (site export limit, or a zero-export G99 condition) and BM bids it cannot import (site import capacity / MIC). Under-delivery against a BOA creates imbalance exposure and, in repeated cases, a Grid Code compliance and dynamic-parameter-accuracy problem.
- **How to implement:** Per half-hour t: MEL_t = min(P_discharge,max · SoH, ExportCapacity_site − Generation_other,t, (SoC_t − SoC_min)·2/Δt); MIL_t = −min(P_charge,max, ImportCapacity_site − Load_t, (SoC_max − SoC_t)·2/Δt). PN_t = the traded position. Offer volume available = MEL_t − PN_t; bid volume available = PN_t − MIL_t. All BM volume variables must be bounded by these, in the MILP, at half-hourly resolution using the site's actual half-hourly load.
- *Source:* NESO, Grid Code Balancing Code No.1 (BC1), Issue 6 Revision 22, 2 April 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/33851/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Balancing Reserve' service page, accessed August 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/balancing-reserve
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Grid Code GC0166: Introducing new Balancing Mechanism Parameters for Limited Duration Assets', decision, 22 October 2025, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/gc0166-grid-code-changes

#### D-99 · MUST · Model Bid-Offer Data as up to 10 price/volume pairs per BM Unit per settlement period, submitted by gate closure one hour ahead, and enforce that no PN or BOD change is possible after gate closure.

- **Why:** Grid Code BC1 Appendix 1 specifies 'Up to 10 Bid-Offer Pairs as defined in the BSC' for each BM Unit for each Settlement Period, and BC1.4.4 states that no changes to Physical Notification or Bid-Offer Data may be submitted after Gate Closure for that Settlement Period. LCP Delta note gate closure of 60 minutes from the end of the current settlement period, meaning the earliest an instruction can be sent is 89 minutes prior to delivery. LCP Delta's Phase 2 change to consider all bid-offer pairs (not just the first) materially changed measured skip rates.
- **Error if omitted:** A single-price-per-period model cannot represent the tranching strategy that real BM participation uses, and will systematically mis-state both accepted volume and realised price. Ignoring gate closure creates a perfect-foresight model that trades on information not available at submission time — the classic source of un-underwritable revenue.
- **How to implement:** Decision variables: for pair j ∈ {1..5} (offers) and j ∈ {−1..−5} (bids), volume V_{j,t} and price p_{j,t}, with Σ_j V_{j,t} bounded by MEL/MIL headroom. Information set for period t must be frozen at t − 60 minutes (gate closure); implement as a rolling-horizon or scenario-based formulation, never as a full-year perfect-foresight LP over BM prices.
- *Source:* NESO, Grid Code BC1 Appendix 1, BC1.A.1.4 Bid-Offer Data, Issue 6 Revision 22, 2 April 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/33851/download
- *Source:* NESO, Grid Code BC1.4.4 Receipt Of BM Unit Data Prior To Gate Closure, https://www.neso.energy/document/33851/download
- *Source:* LCP Delta for NESO, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download

#### D-100 · MUST · Represent the full set of Grid Code dynamic parameters and set them honestly, because they are the direct mechanical cause of Stage 2 and Stage 5 skips.

- **Why:** Grid Code BC1.A.1.5 defines the dynamic parameter set: up to three Run-Up and three Run-Down Rates (MW/minute) with elbows (MW), Notice to Deviate from Zero (NDZ, minutes), Notice to Deliver Offers (NTO) and Notice to Deliver Bids (NTB, minutes, capped at 2 minutes for a Genset), Minimum Zero Time (MZT, minutes), Minimum Non-Zero Time (MNZT, minutes), Stable Export Limit (SEL, MW), Stable Import Limit (SIL, MW), and Maximum Delivery Volume (MDV, MWh) over a Maximum Delivery Period (MDP, minutes). LCP Delta's exclusions are built directly on MZT/MNZT/NDZ/SEL/SIL thresholds. Ofgem has ruled these are technical parameters that must reflect true operating characteristics and must not be used for commercial gain.
- **Error if omitted:** Omitting MNZT/MZT means the model schedules 5-minute in-and-out dispatches that the control room cannot instruct, inflating BM volume. Omitting SEL/SIL means the model schedules operation between zero and the stable limit, which is infeasible. Setting them for commercial advantage is a compliance breach that a lender's technical adviser will flag.
- **How to implement:** Add MILP constraints: minimum up-time (MNZT) and minimum down-time (MZT) via binary commitment variables u_t with Σ_{τ=t}^{t+MNZT−1} u_τ ≥ MNZT·(u_t − u_{t−1}); ramp constraints |P_t − P_{t−1}| ≤ RunUpRate·Δt; operation excluded from (0, SEL) and (0, SIL) via a semi-continuous variable or a big-M binary; delivery lead time via NDZ/NTO/NTB offsets between instruction and delivery; and MDV/MDP as a rolling energy cap Σ_{τ∈MDP window} V_τ ≤ MDV.
- *Source:* NESO, Grid Code BC1.A.1.5 Dynamic Parameters, https://www.neso.energy/document/36456/download
- *Source:* NESO, Grid Code BC1, Issue 6 Revision 22, 2 April 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/33851/download
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Grid Code GC0166' decision, 22 October 2025, citing Ofgem's September 2020 open letter on dynamic parameters, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/gc0166-grid-code-changes
- *Source:* LCP Delta for NESO, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download

#### D-101 · MUST · Model the '30-minute rule' constraint on MEL/MIL for pre-GC0166 periods and its replacement by MDO, MDB and FSoE from 2026, and treat the resulting change in addressable BM volume as a step change, not a smooth trend.

- **Why:** Ofgem's GC0166 decision records that NESO used MEL/MIL plus a '30-minute' rule (previously a '15-minute' rule) as a workaround for communicating storage state of charge: MEL/MIL were set at the maximum power deliverable for 30 minutes, and this limited the control room's visibility of availability beyond 30 minutes. GC0166 introduces Maximum Delivery Offer (MDO), Maximum Delivery Bid (MDB) and Future State of Energy (FSoE). Modo estimated that increasing the maximum dispatch length raises the share of BM volume a battery can compete for from 24% to 69%.
- **Error if omitted:** A model built on pre-2026 GB practice caps the asset's addressable BM volume at roughly a third of what post-GC0166 practice will allow, understating BM revenue for the second half of the asset's life. A model that assumes the post-GC0166 world from day one overstates the first two years. The transition is dated and should be modelled as such.
- **How to implement:** Add a regime switch at the GC0166 compliance date: pre-regime, cap any single BOA's deliverable energy at MEL_t × 0.5 h; post-regime, replace with the MDO/MDB energy caps declared per BM Window and an FSoE trajectory. Model the addressable-volume multiplier as 24% → 69% of total BM volume across the switch. Note MDO/MDB may be redeclared post gate closure only in four defined cases: BMU technical fault, NESO issues a BOA, a frequency event depletes reserved ancillary energy, or the BMU changes its PN in the Settlement Period after the BM Window Period.
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Grid Code (GC) GC0166: Introducing new Balancing Mechanism Parameters for Limited Duration Assets', decision, published 22 October 2025, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/gc0166-grid-code-changes
- *Source:* NESO, 'GC0166: Introducing new Balancing Mechanism Parameters for Limited Duration Assets', implemented 18 December 2025, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/codes/gc/modifications/gc0166-introducing-new-balancing-mechanism-parameters-limited-duration-assets
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism: how could GC0166 enable longer battery dispatches?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/balancing-mechanism-battery-energy-storage-skip-rates-grid-code-bess-open-platform
- *Source:* ESS News, 'Rule change approved to reduce UK battery skipping', 23 October 2025, https://www.ess-news.com/2025/10/23/rule-change-approved-to-reduce-uk-battery-skipping/

#### D-102 · MUST · Track state of energy across periods in the optimisation, and do not inherit the skip-rate methodology's assumption that each period is independent.

- **Why:** LCP Delta explicitly list state of charge as a limitation of the skip-rate methodology: 'Each 5-minute period is assumed to be independent and the state of charge of storage assets is not tracked', because tracking it would require assumptions on cycling limits, round-trip efficiency and state of health. This means the published skip rate is measured against an availability definition that ignores SoC feasibility.
- **Error if omitted:** If the model applies a published skip rate to a volume it computed WITHOUT SoC tracking, it double-counts optimism: the in-merit volume includes energy the battery could not actually have delivered given its charge state. Conversely, applying the published skip rate to a correctly SoC-constrained volume is conservative — this asymmetry must be stated, not left implicit.
- **How to implement:** SoC_{t+1} = SoC_t + η_ch·P^ch_t·Δt − P^dis_t·Δt/η_dis, with SoC_min ≤ SoC_t ≤ SoC_max, and BM offer/bid volumes bounded by the SoC-feasible envelope. Document explicitly that the skip parameter is applied to SoC-feasible in-merit volume, which is a subset of the volume basis on which NESO measures the rate, and quantify the resulting conservatism.
- *Source:* NESO/LCP Delta, 'Enhancing Storage in the Balancing Mechanism', slide 'Limitations of the analysis', 7 November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/347186/download
- *Source:* LCP Delta for NESO, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download

#### D-103 · MUST · Model the interaction between frequency response / reserve contracts and BM availability: energy reserved for an ancillary service is not available to the BM, and this is a documented cause of skips.

- **Why:** LCP Delta list as a limitation that 'Assets held back by ESO in readiness to provide other ancillary services (Reserve, Response) are not excluded' from the skip calculation — meaning some measured skips are actually assets legitimately reserved elsewhere. Modo note that frequency response contracts reduce the MDB/MDO volumes a battery can declare. Ofgem's GC0166 decision allows post-gate-closure MDO/MDB redeclaration specifically if a frequency event depletes energy reserved for an Ancillary Service.
- **Error if omitted:** Double-counting the same MW in both the frequency response stack and the BM stack. For GB batteries this is the single largest revenue-stacking error available, because frequency response was £30k/MW/year in October 2025 against a total of £77k/MW/year — an unconstrained stack can inflate total revenue by 30–40%.
- **How to implement:** A single shared power and energy budget per half-hour: P^FR_t + P^BM,dis_t + P^wholesale,dis_t ≤ P_max and equivalently for charging; plus an energy reservation E^FR_t = P^FR_t · d_FR (service duration) that is subtracted from the SoC envelope available to the BM. Enforce that a MW contracted to a response service cannot simultaneously be offered in the BM at a price that would be accepted.
- *Source:* NESO/LCP Delta, 'Enhancing Storage in the Balancing Mechanism', 'Limitations of the analysis', 7 November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/347186/download
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Grid Code GC0166' decision, 22 October 2025, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/gc0166-grid-code-changes
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'ME BESS GB: Revenues climb to £77k/MW/year in October 2025', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/battery-energy-storage-revenues-gb-october-2025-record-balancing-mechanism-dispatch-rates
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism: how could GC0166 enable longer battery dispatches?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/balancing-mechanism-battery-energy-storage-skip-rates-grid-code-bess-open-platform

#### D-104 · MUST · Enforce the 1 MW minimum BM Unit size and GSP-Group-only aggregation as a hard gate on whether a given BTM site can access the BM at all.

- **Why:** NESO's Wider Access guidance states, for both the Supplier and Virtual Lead Party routes, that 'aggregation is permitted but is limited to Grid Supply Point Groups (Distribution Network Owner areas)' and 'The minimum size for a Balancing Mechanism Unit is 1MW'. A 0.1–1 MW BTM asset therefore cannot access the BM standalone and must be aggregated with other assets in the same GSP Group.
- **Error if omitted:** Awarding BM revenue to a sub-1 MW standalone site that legally cannot earn it. This is a binary, project-killing error for the small end of the 0.1–5 MW range and it is not recoverable by any amount of price optimism.
- **How to implement:** Gate: if site_capacity_MW < 1.0 and no aggregation partner exists within the same GSP Group, set BM revenue = 0 and flag the project as BM-ineligible. If aggregated, model the site's share of a portfolio BMU and apply the aggregator's allocation rule, and require an Aggregator Impact Matrix (per-GSP % impact plus postcodes and MSIDs) as a data prerequisite.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Wider Access to the Balancing Mechanism: Onboarding and Registration Guidance for Suppliers and Virtual Lead Parties', 22 April 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/150276/download
- *Source:* NESO, Grid Code BC1.A.10 Aggregator Impact Matrix, https://www.neso.energy/document/33851/download

#### D-105 · MUST · Model the BM access route explicitly (Traditional / Supplier Additional BMU / VLP Secondary BMU) with its registration cost, lead time and ongoing system obligations as capex, opex and a delay to first BM revenue.

- **Why:** NESO's Wider Access guidance gives concrete numbers: the Supplier Agreement and the Virtual Lead Party Agreement each cost £5,000 plus VAT; Supplier and VLP registration timelines 'can take up to 5 months in total for the end-to-end process'; all new BM Units go into NESO BM System SORT and 'this upload takes place once every 8 weeks'; SMP applications must be submitted one week prior to the SORT cut-off; operational metering testing must be booked 4 weeks prior to the SORT cut-off; EDL, EDT and control/system telephony are mandatory; an EIC code from the Local Issuing Office is required before the BMU enters NESO systems; and new BSC Parties may need Elexon Performance Assurance Board approval.
- **Error if omitted:** Assuming BM revenue from commercial operation date. Realistically the first BM revenue is 5+ months after the decision to register, and the 8-week SORT cadence means a missed cut-off costs a further two months. In a DSCR-constrained first debt-service period this is a direct covenant risk, not a rounding error.
- **How to implement:** Add to the model: one-off cost £5,000 + VAT for the agreement, plus EDL/EDT connectivity (own, third-party or WA API), operational metering installation and testing, EIC registration and Elexon registration costs; a BM revenue start lag of ≥5 months from registration commencement, snapped forward to the next 8-weekly SORT window; and an annual opex line for EDT/EDL, operational metering and MODIS/REMIT reporting.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Wider Access to the Balancing Mechanism: Onboarding and Registration Guidance for Suppliers and Virtual Lead Parties', 22 April 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/150276/download

#### D-106 · MUST · Model operational metering to the BM standard as a cost and a technical prerequisite, distinguishing the BAU standard from the Small Aggregated Asset derogation.

- **Why:** NESO states the BAU standard requires metering for BM assets 'to deliver readings every second with an accuracy of +/- 1%'. Following the Power Responsive trial, the Small Aggregated Asset (SAA) Metering Capacity route relaxes this to ±2.5% accuracy and 60-second reads, but is capped: NESO 'has agreed to admit up to 300MW of small aggregated assets', each with individual capacity below 1 MW.
- **Error if omitted:** Understates capex for a small BTM asset (1-second, ±1% operational metering plus EDL/EDT is a material cost on a 1 MW project) and, if the model assumes the SAA derogation, ignores that the derogation is a capped 300 MW cohort that may be full — a binary availability risk.
- **How to implement:** Metering capex line selected by route: BAU (1-second reads, ±1% accuracy) or SAA derogation (60-second reads, ±2.5%) subject to a portfolio-level availability check against the 300 MW cap. Require a signed Supplier or VLP SAA Metering Capacity Agreement as a condition precedent if the SAA route is assumed.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Wider Access to the Balancing Mechanism: Onboarding and Registration Guidance for Suppliers and Virtual Lead Parties', 22 April 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/150276/download

#### D-107 · MUST · Model P375 asset metering (AMSIDs under COP 11) as the settlement mechanism for a battery behind a C&I customer's boundary meter, and do not assume boundary-point metering can separate battery activity from site load.

- **Why:** Ofgem's P375 decision allows, for independent assets behind the boundary meter, 'for secondary meters to be used for the purpose of settlement of balancing services (bid-offer acceptances), rather than using metering equipment at the site boundary point', because using only boundary metering means 'the independent, uncontrollable activity of other assets within the site boundary' contaminates settlement. P375 created Code of Practice 11 and Asset Metering System Identifiers (AMSIDs) registered with the SVAA, complementing MSIDs. Implementation date 30 June 2022. Elexon's Asset Metering guidance requires Asset Metering Systems to be installed behind a Half Hourly Boundary Point Metering System.
- **Error if omitted:** Without asset metering, a BTM battery's BM delivery is measured net of an uncontrollable C&I load, producing settlement error and delivery-performance risk. A model that assumes clean BM settlement without provisioning AMSID-compliant metering assumes an arrangement that does not exist for that configuration.
- **How to implement:** Condition precedent flag: BM revenue for a BTM asset requires either (a) a dedicated MSID for the battery, or (b) AMSID registration under COP 11 with an Asset Metering VLP (AMVLP) role. Add COP 11-compliant asset metering capex and the AMVLP arrangement to the cost stack. Require a Half Hourly Boundary Point Metering System at the site as a prerequisite.
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'BSC P375: Settlement of Secondary BM Units using metering behind the site Boundary Point', decision published 24 February 2021, implementation date 30 June 2022, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/docs/2021/02/p375_final_authority_decision.pdf
- *Source:* Elexon Digital BSC, 'Asset Metering' guidance note V6.0, effective 14 July 2026, https://bscdocs.elexon.co.uk/guidance-notes/asset-metering
- *Source:* NESO, 'Wider Access to the Balancing Mechanism' guidance, 22 April 2026 (AMVLP is an Elexon role; a VLP process with NESO is also required), https://www.neso.energy/document/150276/download

#### D-108 · MUST · Model imbalance/cash-out as a distinct exposure and, separately, as a distinct revenue line — and calibrate cash-out on the current single-price, PAR 1 regime, not on pre-2015 dual pricing.

- **Why:** Ofgem's Electricity Balancing Significant Code Review moved GB to a single cash-out price paid or received by all parties, and to fully marginal pricing based on 1 MWh (PAR 1), stepping from PAR 250 by early winter 2014/15 to PAR 50 by early winter 2015/16 and PAR 1 by early winter 2018/19; it also introduced Reserve Scarcity Pricing with a Value of Lost Load of £3,000/MWh before winter 2018/19 rising to £6,000/MWh thereafter. Modo's GB BESS revenue index carries 'Imbalance' as a separate revenue stream alongside the BM, wholesale, frequency response and Capacity Market.
- **Error if omitted:** Two errors. Ignoring imbalance as a revenue line understates the stack (it is a reported component of the GB BESS index). Ignoring imbalance as an exposure understates downside: under PAR 1 with VoLL at £6,000/MWh, a single half-hour of non-delivery against a BOA in a tight period can cost more than a month of BM revenue. A model calibrated on pre-2015 dual pricing will understate both the volatility and the tail.
- **How to implement:** Imbalance_t = (Metered_t − ContractPosition_t − Σ AcceptedBOA_t) × SIP_t, where SIP is the single imbalance price. Model SIP as a fat-tailed distribution with an explicit scarcity tail (RSP/VoLL up to £6,000/MWh) rather than a normal around the day-ahead price. Add a delivery-failure probability against accepted BOAs and price the resulting imbalance explicitly in the P90 case.
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Electricity Balancing Significant Code Review – Final Policy Decision Impact Assessment', May 2014, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/docs/2014/05/electricity_balancing_significant_code_review_-_final_policy_decision_impact_assessment_1.pdf
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'ME BESS GB: Revenues fall to £47k/MW/year in December 2025', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/me-bess-gb-battery-energy-storage-revenues-december-2025-low-demand-christmas

#### D-109 · MUST · Ingest and back-test against NESO's published Skip Rates datasets rather than relying on a single assumed parameter, and rebuild the parameter surface on a defined refresh cadence.

- **Why:** NESO publishes daily skip-rate datasets on its Data Portal from 15 December 2024 onward under the NESO Open Data Licence, including a Summary file (30-minute, All-BM and PSA by stage), monthly In Merit All-BM and In Merit PSA files at BM-Unit and 5-minute granularity with prices and volumes, an Exclusion Reasons file, and a Stage 5 PSA Skip Rate by Technology file. NESO states technology-type skip rates can be calculated from December 2024 onward. NESO also corrected the data on 18 November 2025 for a long-notice-unit exclusion bug.
- **Error if omitted:** A hard-coded skip constant goes stale within one or two quarters in a market that has moved from 49% to 38% for batteries in twelve months. Worse, without ingesting the primary data the model cannot produce the by-zone, by-hour, by-size cuts that materially change the answer, nor can it detect vendor data revisions.
- **How to implement:** ETL from https://api.neso.energy/api/3/action/package_show?id=skip-rates; join monthly In Merit PSA files (fields: date, bm_unit, fuel, bid_offer, stage, available_volume_MWh, average_price_per_MWh, pair_id, accepted_volume_MWh, in_merit_volume_MWh, skipped_volume_MWh) to Elexon BMRS https://data.elexon.co.uk/bmrs/api/v1/reference/bmunits/all for gspGroupName, generationCapacity and bmUnitType. Compute s = Σ skipped_volume_MWh / Σ in_merit_volume_MWh at stage 5, grouped by [bid_offer × hour × gspGroupName × size band × bmUnitType]. Refresh at least quarterly and version the parameter set against the NESO revision note.
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Skip Rates' dataset, created 16 December 2024, updated daily, NESO Open Data Licence, https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/skip-rates
- *Source:* NESO, 'Skip rates', industry information page, accessed August 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/skip-rates
- *Source:* Elexon BMRS Insights, BM Units reference endpoint, https://data.elexon.co.uk/bmrs/api/v1/reference/bmunits/all

#### D-110 · MUST · Require genuine half-hourly (or finer) site load data for a BTM asset before modelling BM revenue at all, because BM headroom and footroom are defined against the site's net position.

- **Why:** For a behind-the-meter BM Unit, MEL, MIL, PN, SEL and SIL are all defined at the Grid Entry Point or Grid Supply Point and must reflect the true operating characteristics of the unit — which for a BTM asset means the battery net of the customer's own consumption. NESO's Grid Code requires the Aggregator Impact Matrix to record the effect of a BOA on each Grid Supply Point plus the postcodes and MSIDs comprising the Additional or Secondary BM Unit.
- **Error if omitted:** Modelling BM volume from battery nameplate alone will offer volume that the site's import/export capacity cannot carry when the customer's load is high, and will bid volume the site cannot absorb when load is low. For a C&I site with a peaky profile, the error is largest exactly at the evening peak when offer value is highest — so it is not self-cancelling.
- **How to implement:** Require an HH load series L_t for the site as a hard model input. Compute per half-hour: available offer = min(P_dis,max, ExportLimit_site + L_t) − PN_t; available bid = PN_t − max(−P_ch,max, −(ImportCapacity_site − L_t)). Refuse to produce a BM revenue figure if L_t is synthetic and unvalidated; flag any such run as indicative only.
- *Source:* NESO, Grid Code BC1.A.1.5 Dynamic Parameters (SEL/SIL defined at Grid Entry Point or Grid Supply Point), https://www.neso.energy/document/36456/download
- *Source:* NESO, Grid Code BC1.A.10 Aggregator Impact Matrix, https://www.neso.energy/document/33851/download
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'BSC P375' decision, 24 February 2021, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/docs/2021/02/p375_final_authority_decision.pdf

#### D-111 · MUST · Do not assume P415 Virtual Trading Party status confers BM access, and model wholesale-market access for VLP-dispatched flexibility as a separate, distinct arrangement.

- **Why:** NESO's Wider Access guidance states explicitly: 'A VTP role alone cannot participate in the BM. To be in the BM, you would need to also become a VLP.' P415, approved by Ofgem on 6 October 2023 with an implementation date of 7 November 2024, enables VLPs to trade Deviation Volumes on wholesale markets on behalf of consumers, with supplier compensation calculated using Ofgem's Price Cap Methodology and mutualised across suppliers under the Proposed Solution Ofgem directed.
- **Error if omitted:** Conflating VTP and VLP roles produces a model that assumes BM revenue from a registration that does not permit it, or assumes wholesale revenue from a VLP arrangement that predates P415. It also misses the supplier-compensation cost flow, which under the Alternative Solution (rejected) would have been borne by VLPs directly and remains a live commercial risk if the arrangements are revisited.
- **How to implement:** Model role registration as a set: {VLP → Secondary BMU → BM access} and {VTP → wholesale Deviation Volume trading} as separate flags with separate costs. Gate BM revenue on the VLP flag only. Model P415 wholesale access as available only from 7 November 2024 onward, with Deviation Volumes valued at wholesale less any supplier compensation flow.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Wider Access to the Balancing Mechanism' guidance, 22 April 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/150276/download
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Decision: BSC P415 Facilitating access to wholesale markets for flexibility dispatched by Virtual Lead Parties', published 6 October 2023, implementation date 7 November 2024, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/ofgem-decision-p415-facilitating-access-wholesale-markets-flexibility-dispatched-vlps

#### D-112 · MUST · Treat unwind actions as non-additive volume: an unwind BOA only becomes available after an opposing acceptance and must not be counted as independent revenue.

- **Why:** LCP Delta's Stage 4 exclusion removes 'unaccepted actions to unwind previously accepted bids or offers' because 'these actions only become available upon the acceptance of a bid or offer in the opposing direction'. Applying Stage 4 reduced all-technology offer skip rates from 71%/67% (Stage 3) to 58%/57% in 2023/2024 — i.e. unwinds account for roughly 10–13 percentage points of apparent skipped volume.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that treats every declared bid-offer pair as independently available will inflate addressable BM volume by roughly the unwind share, and will also produce dispatch schedules that violate energy conservation (charging and discharging the same energy twice for revenue).
- **How to implement:** In the MILP, make the unwind pair conditional: V^unwind_{t} ≤ Σ_{τ<t} V^accepted,opposite_{τ} not yet unwound. Exclude unwind volume from the denominator when calibrating skip rate against Stage 4/5 published figures.
- *Source:* LCP Delta for NESO, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', November 2024, Stage 4 exclusion, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* NESO/LCP Delta, 'Enhancing Storage in the Balancing Mechanism', 7 November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/347186/download

#### D-113 · MUST · Flag and reject any model assumption drawn from 2019–2023 GB literature without a vintage check, because the BM has changed materially in identifiable ways.

- **Why:** Between 2019 and 2026 GB has seen: the Open Balancing Platform (first iteration December 2023) with battery utilisation up 290% and daily battery instructions up 940% in its first year; Bulk Dispatch (December 2023 / January 2024); Balancing Reserve and Quick/Slow Reserve (from December 2024); the frequency response product set replaced by Dynamic Containment/Moderation/Regulation, moving into the OBP from January 2026; GC0166 replacing the 30-minute rule (implemented 18 December 2025, full compliance by early November 2026); the National Dispatch Optimiser (parallel run January 2026); and more than 500 new BM Units registered since April 2021, a 55% increase in assets. NESO's own headline metric moved from 93% battery offer skips in 2023 to 38% in H1 2026.
- **Error if omitted:** Specific, identifiable errors: a 2019-vintage model will use a 15- or 30-minute maximum dispatch length (24% of BM volume addressable rather than 69%), will assume manual single-unit dispatch, will omit Balancing Reserve entirely, will use the pre-2021 frequency response product set, and will carry a skip rate roughly 2.5x today's — a combination that can misstate BM revenue by more than an order of magnitude in either direction.
- **How to implement:** Attach a `vintage` field to every market-structure parameter and hard-fail the model run if any parameter vintage predates the most recent structural change affecting it. Maintain a dated change register: OBP Dec-2023, Bulk Dispatch Jan-2024, Quick Reserve Dec-2024, skip-rate dataset Dec-2024, GC0166 Dec-2025/Nov-2026, NDO Jan-2026, DC/DM/DR in OBP Jan-2026, P462 decision 2026.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Defining, measuring, and addressing skip rates', December 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348236/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'The start of the balancing mechanism revolution', 12 December 2023, https://www.neso.energy/news/start-balancing-mechanism-revolution
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Grid Code GC0166' decision, 22 October 2025, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/gc0166-grid-code-changes
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'NESO Roadmap 2026', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-neso-national-energy-system-operator-2026-energy-storage-roadmap-p462-connections-reform-balancing-mechanism
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Skip Rates', https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/skip-rates

#### D-114 · MUST · Gate all DNO flexibility revenue behind an explicit site-postcode → Constraint Management Zone (CMZ) / Flexibility Zone lookup, per DNO, per product, per direction, per season. If the site postcode is not in a live zone for the product and direction the battery can deliver, the DNO revenue line must be exactly zero for that year — not a national average.

- **Why:** DNO flexibility is procured only where a specific network constraint has failed the DNO's cost-benefit test (DNOA/CEM). NGED procured across 99 HV zones and 744 LV zones in 2024/25 out of a 55,000 km2 licence area; UKPN's Summer-2026 tender covers 48 HV zones and 316 LV zones. The overwhelming majority of GB postcodes have no live requirement at all.
- **Error if omitted:** Systematic over-statement of revenue. For a site outside any zone the error is 100% of the modelled DNO line. Because DNO flex is often 5–20% of a BTM stack, this is typically a 5–20% overstatement of gross revenue and a much larger overstatement of equity IRR.
- **How to implement:** A hard boolean gate: `zone_id = lookup(postcode, dno, product, direction, season)`; `if zone_id is None: dno_flex_revenue[t] = 0`. Source the mapping from NGED's Postcode Checker / Connected Data Portal (CMZ code ↔ postcode), UKPN's 'Flexibility Zone Postcodes' sheet in the Competition Data workbook (63,449 postcode rows), and SSEN's postcode lists per licence area. Re-run the gate every tender round — zones open and close annually.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'NGED DSO - Revenue Estimator September 2025 V2.0' (Postcodes sheet), 2025, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1176
- *Source:* UK Power Networks, 'Competition Data – Summer 2026 Tender V2' (Flexibility Zone Postcodes sheet), 2026, https://d1lf1oz5vvdb9r.cloudfront.net/app/uploads/2026/07/Competition-Data-Summer-2026-Tender-V2.xlsx
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'NGED Distribution Flexibility Services Procurement Report 2024-25 V1.0', 2025, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1181

#### D-115 · MUST · Model the CURRENT ENA common product set — Scheduled Utilisation (SU), Scheduled Availability + Operational Utilisation (SAOU), Variable Availability + Operational Utilisation (VAOU), Operational Utilisation (OU), Peak Reduction — and treat Sustain / Secure / Dynamic / Restore as legacy names that map onto them. Do not build the model around the legacy four.

- **Why:** NGED's March 2026 FSP guidance lists exactly three procured products (SU, SAOU, OU) as 'a subset of the industry standard flexibility products defined in Open Network Products Review and Alignment paper'. UKPN states LT-SU replaced 'Peak Reduction' from October 2024 and that its three products 'replaced previous offerings called Sustain, Secure, and Dynamic'. SSEN procures SU, SAOU, VAOU and OU. NGED's own baseline workbook labels Sustain/Secure/Dynamic/Restore as 'Retired Product'.
- **Error if omitted:** The model prices a product that is no longer tendered, with the wrong payment structure (e.g. Sustain was utilisation-only against a fixed £/kW/season ceiling; Secure paid availability). Direction of error is unbounded and product-specific.
- **How to implement:** An enum `Product ∈ {SU_HV, SU_LV, SAOU, VAOU, OU, PEAK_REDUCTION}` with per-product payment flags `(pays_availability: bool, pays_utilisation: bool, dispatch_notice: {at_trade, day_ahead, 15min}, grace_factor, penalisation_multiplier)`. Map legacy names: Sustain→SU (specific periods), Secure→SU (settlement periods)/SAOU, Dynamic→SAOU/OU, Restore→OU.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'Guidance for Electricity Distribution Flexibility Service Providers', March 2026 V1.0, §2.2 and Table 2.1, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1121
- *Source:* UK Power Networks, 'Participation Guidance V1.2', October 2024, Table 1, https://d1lf1oz5vvdb9r.cloudfront.net/app/uploads/2024/10/Participation-Guidance-V1.2.pdf
- *Source:* Scottish and Southern Electricity Networks, 'Flexibility Services' product pages (SU, SAOU, VAOU, OU), 2026, https://www.ssen.co.uk/about-ssen/dso/flexibility/flexibility-services/
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'NGED DSO - Baseline Values August 2025 V1.0' (row 'Retired Product'), 2025, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1122

#### D-116 · MUST · Implement the ENA Open Networks availability payment formula exactly, including the Monthly Utilisation Performance Factor (MPF) clawback, rather than availability_price × MW × hours.

- **Why:** The ENA Standardised DNO Settlement Methodology (Aug 2024, adopted by NGED post-Sept 2024 and reflected in UKPN's Performance Factor) makes the availability payment for a whole month conditional on delivery performance during utilisation events in that month. A single badly-delivered utilisation event can wipe a material fraction of a month's availability revenue.
- **Error if omitted:** Over-statement of availability revenue in any month containing a utilisation event with imperfect delivery. With a 5% Grace Factor and a Performance Multiplier of 3, 80% delivery reduces the payable percentage to 50%; delivery at or below 63% pays zero.
- **How to implement:** AP_sm = Σ_{j=1..t} AC_sj × AVP_sj × CC_sj × SA_sj × MPF_sm, where AC = availability price (£/MW/h), AVP = availability period (minutes/60), CC = contracted capacity (MW), SA ∈ {0,1} (0 if declared or deemed unavailable), and MPF_sm = 1 if there were no dispatch events in the month or delivery is within the Grace Factor, else MPF_sm = (1/p)(1/n) Σ_{e=1..p} Σ_{k=1..n} MAX(MIN((MM_ek − BM_ek)/DC_ek, 1), 0). Signage: demand units negative for BM and MM, generation positive; DC positive for demand-reduction/generation-increase.
- *Source:* Energy Networks Association Open Networks, 'Standardised DNO Settlement Methodology', Version 1.0, August 2024, §4.1, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1124
- *Source:* UK Power Networks, 'Participation Guidance V1.2', October 2024, §6.1 'Availability payment performance adjustments', https://d1lf1oz5vvdb9r.cloudfront.net/app/uploads/2024/10/Participation-Guidance-V1.2.pdf

#### D-117 · MUST · Implement the ENA utilisation payment formula with the Grace Factor, Performance Multiplier and Payable-Over-Delivery (POD) cap, assessed per metered time period (minute-by-minute where the DNO requires it), and with over-delivery unpaid and non-compensating.

- **Why:** Payment is not linear in delivered MWh. Under-delivery below the grace band is penalised at a multiple; over-delivery in one minute cannot offset under-delivery in another. UKPN states explicitly: 'over-delivery in any period is not paid, nor will it be treated as compensating under-delivery in another period'.
- **Error if omitted:** Over-statement of utilisation revenue whenever the battery cannot hold exactly the dispatched MW for the whole event (SoC exhaustion, thermal derate, auxiliary load, site-load interaction). Materially: NGED's own data shows 6% of agreed delivery events fail to respond at all and only 84.9% of events achieve 95–100% of target.
- **How to implement:** UP_sm = Σ_j UF_sj × UVP_sj × UC_sj × PM_sj, where UC_sj = MAX(MIN((MM_sj − BM_sj)/DC_sj, POD_sj), 0) × |DC_sj| and PM_sj = IF(Delivery% ≥ (1 − GraceFactor), 1, MAX(0, (1 − GraceFactor) − ((1 − GraceFactor − Delivery%) × (Multiplier% × 100)))). Use GraceFactor = 0.05, Multiplier = 3, POD = 1.0 for NGED SU and SAOU; GraceFactor = 0, Multiplier = 2 for NGED OU; GraceFactor = 0.05, Multiplier = 3 for UKPN.
- *Source:* Energy Networks Association Open Networks, 'Standardised DNO Settlement Methodology', Version 1.0, August 2024, §4.2 and Table 4, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1124
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'Guidance for Electricity Distribution Flexibility Service Providers', March 2026, Table 6.1, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1121
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'Flexibility Market Insights Report', June 2025, p.6, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1184

#### D-118 · MUST · Model utilisation as a low-probability stochastic call, not as delivery across the whole availability window. Use a utilisation rate no higher than the DNO's own published assumption and sanity-check it against realised dispatch data.

- **Why:** This is the single largest source of DNO flexibility revenue over-statement. NGED's own Revenue Estimator states: 'We have assumed a utilisation rate of 5% within the revenue computations in this tool, for the Scheduled Availability, Operational Utilisation - Day Ahead Notice product.' UKPN's Summer-2026 competition data publishes availability hours and utilisation hours per zone-season; the median ratio is 22%. Realised NGED 2024/25 SLC31E data shows a median of 6 distinct dispatch days per flexible unit per year for SAOU_DA (p75 = 10, max = 71).
- **Error if omitted:** A model that assumes utilisation across the whole contracted window over-states utilisation revenue by roughly 4–20×. On UKPN Summer-2026 medians (270 availability hours, 60 utilisation hours per season), assuming full-window utilisation over-states by 4.5×; on NGED's 5% assumption, by 20×.
- **How to implement:** Two-stage: (a) availability revenue = f(accepted availability windows) — deterministic once awarded; (b) utilisation revenue = utilisation_price × contracted_MW × expected_utilisation_hours, with expected_utilisation_hours = utilisation_rate × availability_hours. Set utilisation_rate = 0.05 for NGED SAOU_DA (P50) and 0.22 for UKPN SAOU (P50), and run P90 at 0.00–0.02. For SU products, awarded delivery periods ARE the utilisation, so utilisation_rate = 1.0 within awarded windows but the award probability applies instead.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'NGED DSO - Revenue Estimator September 2025 V2.0', Overview tab notes, 2025, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1176
- *Source:* UK Power Networks, 'Competition Data – Summer 2026 Tender V2', Competition Data TR14 sheet ('Hours of availability', 'Hours of utilisation'), 2026, https://d1lf1oz5vvdb9r.cloudfront.net/app/uploads/2026/07/Competition-Data-Summer-2026-Tender-V2.xlsx
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'C31E Supporting Data 2024-25', Dispatch tab, 2025, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1180

#### D-119 · MUST · For Variable Availability (VAOU) and any product where availability itself is instructed rather than fixed at trade, model availability HOURS as stochastic too — the DNO only instructs availability when it expects to need it.

- **Why:** SSEN's VAOU product refines contracted capacity closer to real time. Its 2025/26 SLC31E report shows SEPD VAOU with 44.61 MW contracted producing only 641.6 MWh of instructed availability and 382.4 MWh of instructed utilisation over the whole year — approximately 14 hours of instructed availability and 9 hours of utilisation per contracted MW per year.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that pays the availability fee across the whole contracted season under a VAOU contract over-states availability revenue by an order of magnitude or more. At 300 season-hours assumed versus ~14 realised, the error is ~20×.
- **How to implement:** `availability_revenue = availability_price × contracted_MW × instructed_availability_hours`, with `instructed_availability_hours` drawn from a distribution calibrated on the DNO's SLC31E 'Total Availability Instructions' divided by 'Total Contracted' MW. Do not use the tender's nominal window length for VAOU. Flag VAOU contracts as materially riskier than SAOU in the risk register.
- *Source:* Scottish and Southern Electricity Networks, 'SLC31E — SSEN 2025-26 Flexibility Services Procurement Report', April 2026, Table 6, https://www.ssen.co.uk/globalassets/our-services/flexibility-services-document-library/slc31e-reports--statements/slc31e--ssen-2025-26-flexibility-services-procurement-report.pdf
- *Source:* Scottish and Southern Electricity Networks, 'Flexibility Services Contract Register v2.30', 30/07/2026, https://www.ssen.co.uk/globalassets/our-services/flexibility-services-document-library/contract-register/flexibility-services-contract-register-v2.30-v2.xlsx

#### D-120 · MUST · Model tender award probability explicitly. Do not assume a bid submitted is a contract won.

- **Why:** Awards are competitive and partial. NGED's weekly trade results for Sept–Nov 2025 delivery contain 405,710 bid rows of which 182,691 (45.0%) were Rejected. Long-term rounds accept only a fraction of offered capacity, and the DNO may accept only part of a declared availability window. SSEN and UKPN both build a merit-order price stack and cut it at the required volume.
- **Error if omitted:** Over-statement of contracted volume and hence of both availability and utilisation revenue. On NGED's autumn-2025 short-term data the naive error is roughly 1/(1−0.45) ≈ 1.8× on volume.
- **How to implement:** `P(award | bid_price, zone, product)` estimated from published results: NGED Weekly Trade Results (offered vs awarded price and capacity per delivery period), SSEN Contract Register, UKPN Localflex results. Model expected revenue = P(award) × contracted revenue. In the MILP, treat award as a scenario branch, not a certainty; in the underwriting model, apply a distinct award-probability haircut before the utilisation haircut.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'Weekly Trade Results' (405,710 rows, delivery 15/09/2025–02/11/2025), 2025, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1194
- *Source:* UK Power Networks, 'Participation Guidance V1.2', October 2024, 'Long-term Awards', https://d1lf1oz5vvdb9r.cloudfront.net/app/uploads/2024/10/Participation-Guidance-V1.2.pdf
- *Source:* Scottish and Southern Electricity Networks, 'Assessment of Bids and Offers in SSEN', V2.1, April 2026, §2.2, https://www.ssen.co.uk/globalassets/our-services/flexibility-services-document-library/service-documentation/assessment-of-bids-in-ssen-v2.1-apr-2026.pdf

#### D-121 · MUST · Never use the published ceiling / indicative price as the revenue price. Model a clearing price at a documented discount to ceiling, and model that discount widening over time.

- **Why:** NGED reports that in its autumn-2024 long-term round it 'awarded flexibility in HV zones at prices 34% lower than our ceiling prices, and in LV zones at 18% lower'. Its Revenue Estimator is explicitly labelled 'the maximum potential revenue' / 'upper bound'. UKPN publishes 'lower range' and 'higher range' indicative fees, not clears. NGED's fallback fixed price where competition is insufficient is ~£300/MWh, which is not a clearing price either.
- **Error if omitted:** Direct over-statement of revenue by the ceiling-to-clear gap: at least 34% (HV) and 18% (LV) on NGED's own 2024 evidence, and far more where competition has since intensified.
