- *Source:* Energy Networks Association, 'Engineering Recommendation G100 Issue 2 2022 Amendment 2 (2023): Technical Requirements for Customers' Export and Import Limitation Schemes', §§4.2, 4.3.2, 4.3.3, 4.4, 4.5.1.3, 4.5.2.1, https://www.energynetworks.org/publications/ena-erec-g100-issue-2-amendment-2-technical-requirements-customer-export-and-import-limitation-schemes

#### D-166 · MUST · Where the site is HV-connected, or LV-connected without a demonstrably Fail Safe CLS, include the capital and operational cost of overload and/or reverse-power protection at the connection point, set no higher than the State 2 limits and instantaneous (fast acting, no definite-time delay), with a maximum operating time of 10 s and graded with the DNO's protection.

- **Why:** G100 §4.5.3.1 requires that for all HV installations and LV installations with a non-Fail-Safe CLS, the customer shall install overload or reverse power protection at the connection point. §4.5.3.2 sets a default operating margin of 2% where protection substitutes for a CLS, and caps the maximum allowable operating time at 10 s.
- **Error if omitted:** Understates capex and, more importantly, understates schedule risk: the protection design and settings must be agreed with the DNO and recorded in the Connection Agreement, which is a gating item for energisation.
- **How to implement:** Add a capex line item conditional on (voltage == HV) OR (NOT CLS_fail_safe_type_tested). Add a development-schedule gate: connection_agreement_variation_agreed before commercial operation date.
- *Source:* Energy Networks Association, EREC G100 Issue 2 Amd 2 (2023), §§4.5, 4.5.3, 4.5.3.1, 4.5.3.2, 4.9, https://www.energynetworks.org/publications/ena-erec-g100-issue-2-amendment-2-technical-requirements-customer-export-and-import-limitation-schemes

#### D-167 · MUST · Linearise the peak/demand-charge term with an epigraph variable rather than a non-linear max, and include a binary preventing simultaneous charge and discharge unless it can be proven that simultaneous operation cannot pay.

- **Why:** This is the standard and verified MILP construction. Carmona et al. (2024) formulate exactly this: binaries b_h enforce that charging or discharging occurs one at a time, and the demand-charge maximum is replaced by s_i >= L_i,h − PV_h + C·π⁻_h − C·π⁺_h with s_i >= 0 minimised in the objective. Shi et al. (2017) note that in a pure arbitrage/regulation setting the no-simultaneous-operation condition is automatically satisfied, so the binary can sometimes be relaxed — but only after proof.
- **Error if omitted:** Without the binary, a model with round-trip losses and any negative-price or negative-effective-price period can 'burn' energy by charging and discharging simultaneously, manufacturing revenue. Without the epigraph the problem is non-linear and either fails to solve or is solved heuristically with an unquantified optimality gap.
- **How to implement:** Objective adds λ_demand × s where s >= (L[t] − PV[t] + P_ch[t] − P_dis[t]) for all t in the billing period, s >= 0. Charge/discharge exclusivity: 0 <= P_ch[t] <= (1−b[t])·P_rated; 0 <= P_dis[t] <= b[t]·P_rated; b[t] ∈ {0,1}. SOC: E[t] = E[t−1] + η_ch·P_ch[t]·Δt − P_dis[t]·Δt/η_dis, with SOC_min <= E[t] <= SOC_max.
- *Source:* Carmona, R., Yang, X., Bhela, S., Zeng, C., 'Maximizing on-bill savings through battery management optimization', arXiv:2409.03942, September 2024, https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.03942
- *Source:* Shi, Y., Xu, B., Tan, Y., Kirschen, D., Zhang, B., 'Optimal Battery Control Under Cycle Aging Mechanisms', arXiv:1709.05715v2, 2017, https://arxiv.org/abs/1709.05715

#### D-168 · MUST · Model AC-side round-trip efficiency including auxiliaries and transformer losses, and apply charge and discharge efficiencies separately rather than a single round-trip number applied once.

- **Why:** Seward et al. assume a round-trip efficiency of 90%, applied as a charging/discharging efficiency of 94.87% each way (0.9487² ≈ 0.90). Carmona et al. use η = 0.8 round trip, applied as η⁺ = η⁻ = sqrt(0.8). Applying a single RTE at one end of the cycle mis-times energy flows relative to time-varying prices.
- **Error if omitted:** Mis-states the SOC trajectory and therefore both the achievable peak shave and the energy-arbitrage spread required to break even. A 90% RTE means the required price spread is ~11% above the charge price before degradation cost.
- **How to implement:** E[t] = E[t−1] + η_ch·P_ch[t]·Δt − P_dis[t]·Δt/η_dis with η_ch = η_dis = sqrt(RTE_AC). Add a parasitic/auxiliary load term (HVAC, BMS) as a constant kW draw that appears on the site's import meter and therefore in the demand charge.
- *Source:* Seward, W., Qadrdan, M., Jenkins, N., PSCC 2022, §III, https://pscc-central.epfl.ch/repo/papers/2022/19890.pdf
- *Source:* Carmona, R., et al., arXiv:2409.03942, Table 1, https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.03942

#### D-169 · MUST · Impose operational SOC bounds strictly inside 0–100%, and impose an end-of-horizon SOC constraint so the optimiser cannot finance the last day by emptying the battery.

- **Why:** Carmona et al. use preservation constraints SOC ∈ [20%, 96%] with SOC_0 = 20%. Seward et al. constrain the final state of charge on the last day to be equal to or larger than the initial state of charge. Both are standard defences against terminal-condition artefacts.
- **Error if omitted:** Without terminal constraints the optimiser liquidates stored energy at the horizon, inflating revenue by up to one full discharge and distorting the last periods of every rolling-horizon solve. Without operating-band limits the model over-uses depth of discharge that the warranty forbids.
- **How to implement:** SOC_min <= E[t]/E_max <= SOC_max for all t (e.g. 0.10–0.95, set from the OEM warranty, not from first principles); E[T] >= E[0]. For rolling horizons, carry a shadow value on terminal energy rather than a hard equality.
- *Source:* Carmona, R., et al., arXiv:2409.03942, §3.1, https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.03942
- *Source:* Seward, W., Qadrdan, M., Jenkins, N., PSCC 2022, §III, https://pscc-central.epfl.ch/repo/papers/2022/19890.pdf

#### D-170 · MUST · If Balancing Mechanism participation is credited, enforce the BM energy-limited-provider rules: MEL/MIL must be sustainable for at least 15 minutes, redeclaration takes at least 17 minutes from the point of instruction (15 minutes duration plus 1-minute ramps), and where DFR is also contracted, the capacity above the reserved headroom must be priced at ±£99,999 so it is not accepted.

- **Why:** NESO's stacking guidance sets these as the enduring principles for storage in the BM: battery BMUs declare MEL/MIL open-ended to reflect the capacity to follow a BOA that ramps from PN to MEL/MIL and remains there for 15 minutes; redeclaration is 'a minimum of 17 minutes from the first point of instruction'. In the approved (Scenario 3a) stacking mode, the first tranche (the headroom/footroom) is priced competitively and the remainder to MEL/MIL is priced at ±£99,999 to indicate that any acceptance there would erode response.
- **Error if omitted:** Models that let the battery bid its full rating into the BM while also holding a DFR contract are describing a position the operator cannot legally take under NESO's currently-active guidance (Scenario 3b, which allows erosion priced at DC opportunity cost, is explicitly marked INACTIVE pending NESO satisfaction with stacking performance). Overstates BM revenue.
- **How to implement:** BM_offerable_MW[t] = P_rated − V_DFR_contracted − SoE_management_headroom, where the headroom 'will vary per asset and contract quantity'. Require E[t] >= BM_offerable_MW × 0.25 h + V_DFR × T_service. Model a minimum 17-minute lockout after each BOA before MEL/MIL can be improved.
- *Source:* National Grid ESO, 'Unlocking Stacking of BOAs with Frequency Response Services', §§2–3, https://www.neso.energy/document/184466/download

#### D-171 · MUST · If wholesale-market trading of BTM flexibility is credited, model it through the P415 Virtual Lead Party route with Deviation Volumes and supplier compensation — do not assume the site can simply trade its own flexibility at day-ahead prices.

- **Why:** Before P415, the BSC assigned all flexibility delivered by a consumer to their supplier, so consumers could only access the wholesale market through their supplier. Ofgem directed P415 be made on 6 October 2023 with an implementation date of 7 November 2024. It introduces 'Deviation Volumes' and requires supplier compensation calculated at a price representing average supplier sourcing costs using Ofgem's Price Cap Methodology; under the approved Proposed Solution those costs are mutualised amongst suppliers.
- **Error if omitted:** Overstates wholesale arbitrage value and understates commercial friction. A model that books the full day-ahead spread for a BTM asset ignores both the VLP's share and the settlement machinery that has only existed since November 2024; pre-2024 literature assuming supplier-mediated access is now out of date in the opposite direction.
- **How to implement:** Split wholesale revenue: gross_spread × (1 − VLP_share). Flag whether the site's supply contract is pass-through or fixed — under a fixed contract the site captures the supplier's avoided-cost, not the day-ahead spread. Record the counterfactual explicitly.
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Decision on BSC modification P415: Facilitating access to wholesale markets for flexibility dispatched by Virtual Lead Parties', 6 October 2023, implementation 7 November 2024, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/decision-bsc-modification-p415
- *Source:* Elexon, 'P415 Facilitating access to wholesale markets for flexibility dispatched by Virtual Lead Parties', https://www.elexon.co.uk/bsc/mod-proposal/p415/

#### D-172 · MUST · Model Capacity Market revenue on DE-RATED capacity using duration-specific de-rating factors, not nameplate, and gate it on the CM's minimum unit size, metering and demonstration requirements.

- **Why:** Battery de-rating factors are duration-dependent and low. Modo Energy reports 13.64% for 1-hour and 27.15% for 2-hour batteries in the T-1 auction for delivery year 2025/26, and 10.47% (1h) and 20.94% (2h) for the T-4 for 2028/29. The T-1 auction for 2025/26 cleared at £20.00/kW/year on 5 March 2025, procuring 7,936.289 MW across 246 CMUs. DSR CMUs must demonstrate capacity across three separate days in winter, and government has consulted on a £5,000/MW termination fee for failure to provide a DSR Test certificate.
- **Error if omitted:** Using nameplate capacity overstates CM revenue by a factor of ~4–7 for 1–2 hour batteries. Modo's GB benchmark shows Capacity Market contributing only about £2.7k/MW/year of incremental revenue when batteries entered new 2025/26 contracts — a small line, easily inflated to a decisive one by a de-rating error.
- **How to implement:** CM_revenue = P_rated_MW × derating_factor(duration_h, auction) × clearing_price_£/kW/yr × 1000. Gate on CMU minimum size and on the metering/demonstration obligations; add the cost of CM metering compliance and the risk of termination fees. Model stress-event obligation as a dispatch constraint that competes with peak shaving on winter weekday evenings — the same hours as the DUoS red band.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Capacity Market 2024/25: Increased derating factors for BESS confirmed', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-capacity-market-2025-bess-derating-factors-confirmed-target-capacity
- *Source:* Solar Power Portal, 'NESO T-1 and T-4 capacity market results roundup' (T-1 2025/26: £20.00/kW/year, 7,936.289 MW, 246 CMUs, 5 March 2025), https://www.solarpowerportal.co.uk/solar-projects/neso-t-1-and-t-4-capacity-market-results-roundup
- *Source:* Drax, 'Capacity Market DSR documents published / Government looks at Capacity Market reform for DSR', https://energy.drax.com/intelligence/government-looks-at-capacity-market-reform-for-dsr/
- *Source:* DESNZ, 'Capacity Market Rules' (informal consolidation), https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/capacity-market-rules

#### D-173 · MUST · Recognise the direct temporal conflict between Capacity Market stress-event obligation, DUoS red-band shaving, TNUoS locational triad avoidance and evening wholesale peak — all of which fall in roughly the same winter weekday 16:00–19:30 window — and resolve it explicitly in the optimisation rather than crediting all of them.

- **Why:** DUoS red bands are weekday late-afternoon/early-evening (examples 16:00–19:30, 16:30–19:00, 16:00–19:00). Fourteen HH locational TNUoS tariffs are still charged in £/kW on consumption over the Triad, and 14 NHH locational tariffs on 4–7pm consumption. Capacity Market stress events and the highest wholesale prices occur in the same window. This is the concrete GB instance of the 'one asset cannot be in two places' constraint.
- **Error if omitted:** This is the single largest source of stacked-value overstatement for a BTM C&I battery: four or five separate value lines are computed against the same three or four hours of the same winter weekdays, and a 2-hour battery can serve only one pass through that window. Direction: always overstates, potentially by several multiples of the true evening value.
- **How to implement:** Do not compute per-service values in isolation. Solve a single MILP over the year where all these prices/obligations appear as terms in one objective sharing one P_dis[t] and one E[t]. Then report a DECOMPOSITION by shadow price or by leave-one-out re-solve, and report the stacking discount = 1 − (stacked value / Σ standalone values). Publish that discount as a headline number.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Transmission Demand Residual (TDR) Guidance Document', Figure 2 (14 HH locational tariffs on Triad; 14 NHH locational tariffs on 4–7pm consumption; 18 nationwide residual tariffs), https://www.neso.energy/document/288186/download
- *Source:* NUS Consulting Group, 'DUoS Charges (UK Mainland) 2025' (red band window examples), https://www.nusconsulting.com/energy-news/uk-duos-charges-2025/
- *Source:* National Grid ESO, 'Unlocking Stacking of BOAs with Frequency Response Services', https://www.neso.energy/document/184466/download

#### D-174 · MUST · Model solar co-location by first netting PV against load at half-hourly resolution to derive net site load, then optimising the battery against the NET profile — and separately value self-consumed energy at the retail import price (including all avoidable levies) versus exported energy at the export price, which are materially different.

- **Why:** Self-consumed PV avoids the full delivered price of imported electricity (commodity + DUoS unit + BSUoS + CCL + supplier margin + VAT treatment), whereas exported PV earns only an export tariff. Under the Smart Export Guarantee, SEG Licensees set the rate, contract length and terms, subject only to the rate being above zero — there is no floor price to rely on. Export also requires export meter readings.
- **Error if omitted:** Valuing all PV at one price is wrong by the whole retail-versus-wholesale wedge. Since a battery's main PV-related job is to convert export into self-consumption, mis-pricing this wedge directly mis-sizes the battery.
- **How to implement:** NetLoad[t] = Load[t] − PV[t]. Self_consumed[t] = min(PV[t], Load[t] + P_ch[t]). Export[t] = max(PV[t] + P_dis[t] − Load[t] − P_ch[t], 0), subject to Export[t] <= MEC × (1 − G100 margin). Value: self_consumption_value = Σ Self_consumed[t] × (p_commodity[t] + p_DUoS_band(t) + p_BSUoS + p_CCL + p_other_levies); export_value = Σ Export[t] × p_export. State p_export as a contracted assumption, not a market price.
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Smart Export Guarantee (SEG)', https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/environmental-and-social-schemes/smart-export-guarantee-seg
- *Source:* Energy Networks Association, EREC G100 Issue 2 Amd 2 (2023), https://www.energynetworks.org/publications/ena-erec-g100-issue-2-amendment-2-technical-requirements-customer-export-and-import-limitation-schemes
- *Source:* Gridcog, 'The economics of behind-the-meter battery storage. Part 1: Reducing your energy bill', 28 January 2025, https://www.gridcog.com/blog/the-economics-of-behind-the-meter-battery-storage-part-1-reducing-your-energy-bill

#### D-175 · MUST · Do not credit the battery with avoiding levies that are not avoided by reducing metered import at the relevant time, and enumerate the bill stack line by line with an explicit avoidable/non-avoidable flag for each.

- **Why:** GB non-commodity costs behave differently: BSUoS is a volumetric £/MWh on demand and IS avoided by reducing import; CCL is a p/kWh on supplied electricity and IS avoided by self-supply; DUoS unit charges ARE avoided; DUoS and TNUoS residuals are FIXED £/site/day and are NOT avoided; DUoS capacity charges are levied on contracted MIC and are NOT avoided within the year. The Climate Change Levy main rate for electricity was £0.00775/kWh for both 2024-25 and 2025-26, rising to £0.00801/kWh for 2026-27 and £0.00827/kWh for 2027-28, with a 92% discount for holders of a Climate Change Agreement.
- **Error if omitted:** Mixing avoidable and non-avoidable components into a single blended p/kWh import price is the most common way BTM savings are overstated. The residual and capacity components are precisely the ones that have GROWN post-TCR, so the error is getting worse over time, not better.
- **How to implement:** Build the import price as a vector of components with flags: {commodity: avoidable_volumetric, DUoS_unit: avoidable_volumetric_by_band, BSUoS: avoidable_volumetric, CCL: avoidable_volumetric (0 if CCA at 92% relief), RO/FiT/CfD levies: avoidable_volumetric if passed through volumetrically — verify against the supply contract, DUoS_fixed_residual: NOT avoidable, TNUoS_TDR: NOT avoidable, DUoS_capacity: NOT avoidable in-year}. Only sum the avoidable components into the dispatch price signal.
- *Source:* HMRC/GOV.UK, 'Climate Change Levy rates', https://www.gov.uk/guidance/climate-change-levy-rates
- *Source:* Drax, 'BSUoS fixed tariffs to fall through 2026', https://energy.drax.com/intelligence/bsuos-fixed-tariffs-to-fall-through-2026/
- *Source:* NESO, 'Transmission Demand Residual (TDR) Guidance Document', https://www.neso.energy/document/288186/download
- *Source:* London Power Networks plc, 'Use of System Charging Statement', effective 1 April 2026, v1.2, https://media.umbraco.io/ukpn-cms/h5ahq0jp/london-power-networks-lc14-statement-2026-v1-2.pdf

#### D-176 · MUST · Use the fixed, forward-published BSUoS tariff for the relevant six-month period rather than a historic average or a forecast, and carry it as a known input over the tariff horizon with an explicit forecast beyond.

- **Why:** BSUoS is now set as a fixed £/MWh tariff for six-month periods and published in advance. Published values: Tariff 5 £10.74/MWh; Tariff 6 (October 2025 – March 2026) £15.69/MWh; Tariff 7 (April – September 2026) £13.74/MWh; Tariff 8 (October 2026 – March 2027) £12.49/MWh.
- **Error if omitted:** BSUoS has moved by nearly 50% between adjacent published tariffs. Using a stale value mis-prices every avoided MWh; using a forecast where a published fixed tariff exists imports avoidable uncertainty into the near term.
- **How to implement:** p_BSUoS[t] = published_fixed_tariff(period(t)) for periods with a published tariff; a stated forecast thereafter. Include BSUoS in the avoidable volumetric price seen by the optimiser.
- *Source:* Drax, 'BSUoS fixed tariffs to fall through 2026', https://energy.drax.com/intelligence/bsuos-fixed-tariffs-to-fall-through-2026/
- *Source:* NESO, 'December 2025 Final BSUoS Tariffs for 2026/27', https://www.neso.energy/document/375086/download

#### D-177 · MUST · Define the measurement and verification method for savings BEFORE the model is used commercially, choose an IPMVP option, and specify the adjusted baseline and the treatment of non-routine adjustments (production changes, site closure, new load, tariff changes).

- **Why:** IPMVP (EVO, Core Concepts 2022) states that 'energy savings in a facility cannot be directly measured because savings represent the absence of consumption', so savings must be determined by comparing measured consumption before and after, with adjustments for changed conditions. The four options are A and B (retrofit isolation, boundary around the affected equipment), C (whole facility, using the meters that measure total supply — the natural choice where the payment is a share of the site's bill), and D (calibrated simulation, where baseline data are unreliable or unavailable).
- **Error if omitted:** In a savings-share structure the absence of an agreed M&V method is a guaranteed dispute and, for a lender, an unbankable revenue definition. It also creates a moral hazard: without non-routine adjustment rules, a host that increases production sees 'savings' evaporate and the developer's revenue with them.
- **How to implement:** Specify: option (C is usual for bill-share on a single-meter C&I site); baseline period (>= 12 months of HH data); routine adjustment variables (HDD/CDD, production units, occupancy) fitted by regression on the baseline; non-routine adjustment triggers and process; and the tariff basis (fix the tariff at contract signature or re-price annually — state which, since a DUoS re-basing of +108% on capacity charges materially changes measured savings without any change in physical operation).
- *Source:* Efficiency Valuation Organization, 'International Performance Measurement and Verification Protocol (IPMVP) Core Concepts 2022', https://evo-world.org/en/products-services-mainmenu-en/protocols/ipmvp
- *Source:* Drax, 'DUoS charges: 2025's box of frogs' (magnitude of year-on-year tariff re-basing), https://energy.drax.com/intelligence/2025-duos-charges/

#### D-178 · MUST · For frequency response, distinguish availability revenue from response-energy revenue and apply GB's current position: DC, DM and DR are paid for AVAILABILITY ONLY. Do not credit response energy for these services.

- **Why:** NESO's Provider Guidance states plainly for all three Dynamic Services: 'Payments are provided for availability only.' Seward et al. record the same for DC ('the DC response energy payment received is 0'), in contrast to the legacy FFR service, which paid an availability fee plus a response energy payment settled at 125% of the balancing price for export and 75% for import (a 25% benefit either way).
- **Error if omitted:** Crediting response energy on DC/DM/DR invents revenue. Conversely, models built on legacy FFR economics carry a response-energy line that no longer exists for the current product set — an identifiable way in which a 2019-vintage model is wrong.
- **How to implement:** FR_revenue = availability_payment only. If a legacy FFR-style product is modelled for a historical period, use φ = Σ 0.25 × K_balancing[t] × E_dispatched[t] and state the vintage. Also account for the energy the battery actually moves while responding — it degrades the cell and shifts SOC even though it is unpaid.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026, slide 6, https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download
- *Source:* Seward, W., Qadrdan, M., Jenkins, N., PSCC 2022, §II.B, eqns (11)–(12), https://pscc-central.epfl.ch/repo/papers/2022/19890.pdf

#### D-179 · MUST · Model the state-of-energy management obligation for energy-limited assets providing Dynamic Services, and hold back enough SOC in both directions for the full delivery duration of the contracted service in every settlement period of every contracted EFA block.

- **Why:** NESO states that 'Energy limited assets have to comply with the "state of energy" management rules' and that low and high frequency services are procured separately. Delivery durations that must be sustainable are 15 minutes (DC), 30 minutes (DM) and 60 minutes (DR). NESO's stacking guidance notes that 'some headroom/footroom is required for the unit to manage SoE. The amount will vary per asset and contract quantity.'
- **Error if omitted:** A 1-hour battery contracted for DR-low for a whole 4-hour EFA block must hold a full hour of discharge energy for all 8 settlement periods — leaving essentially nothing for peak shaving in that block. Ignoring the SOC hold makes the two look compatible when they are not. Direction: overstates.
- **How to implement:** For every t in a contracted EFA block e: E[t] − E_min >= V_low[e] × T_service(service) and E_max − E[t] >= V_high[e] × T_service(service). Add a separate SoE-management headroom band as a parameter (state its value and source). Note DR (60 minutes) is effectively incompatible with peak shaving for a 1-hour asset.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026, slides 6–7, https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download
- *Source:* National Grid ESO, 'Unlocking Stacking of BOAs with Frequency Response Services', Scenario 3a, https://www.neso.energy/document/184466/download

#### D-180 · MUST · Include the cost and feasibility of the metering, telemetry and IT obligations that each revenue stream imposes, and treat them as gating conditions rather than as a small opex line.

- **Why:** Dynamic Services require performance data at 20 Hz (36,000 rows per settlement period for a Low service), operational metering, performance monitoring through the Data Concentrator, and Open Balancing Programme testing for non-BM units. BM participation requires working EDL and EDT connections (or wider access API), PN and bid-offer data submitted to gate closure, dynamic data (MEL, MIL, SEL), working telephone contact, and a 24/7 Grid Code obligation to phone the NESO Control Engineer to explain any BOA rejection. Capacity Market participation requires compliant metering and, for DSR, demonstration across three separate winter days.
- **Error if omitted:** Understates cost and, more seriously, overstates feasibility for a small BTM asset. A 500 kW battery at a factory cannot economically carry BM-grade telemetry and a 24/7 control-room obligation on its own; it must be aggregated, and the aggregator's share must appear in the model.
- **How to implement:** Attach to each revenue stream a boolean prerequisite set and a cost: {stream: [metering_class, telemetry_rate, control_room_obligation, prequalification_test, annual_cost_£]}. If a prerequisite is unmet, set the stream's revenue to zero rather than discounting it.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026, slides 6, 13 and 19, https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download
- *Source:* National Grid ESO, 'Unlocking Stacking of BOAs with Frequency Response Services', §1, https://www.neso.energy/document/184466/download
- *Source:* EMR Settlement Ltd, 'G1 – Capacity Market Metering', https://www.emrsettlement.co.uk/document/guidance/g1-capacity-market-metering/

#### D-181 · MUST · Do not carry pre-2023 GB revenue assumptions forward. Audit the model for these specific dead or changed items: triad avoidance of the transmission residual (dead from April 2023); Firm Frequency Response as a live product (replaced by DC/DM/DR); BSUoS payable by generation (removed); DC as the only stackable frequency product; and 'embedded benefits' framed as they were pre-TCR.

- **Why:** The last Triad period was February 2023 and from April 2023 the TDR became a fixed £/site/day; only a small locational element remains, floored at zero. NESO states that Dynamic Firm Frequency Response 'has been replaced with these dynamic services and has now been phased out'. Ofgem's CMP308 removed BSUoS charges from generation. NESO's TCR reform also set the TNUoS Generation Residual to £0.
- **Error if omitted:** A model built on 2019 assumptions is wrong in specific, identifiable, always-optimistic ways: it credits triad avoidance that no longer exists, an FFR product that no longer exists, and embedded benefits that TCR removed.
- **How to implement:** Add an assumption-vintage audit: every price and mechanism carries a source and an effective-date field; refuse to run if any mechanism's effective date has lapsed. Explicitly assert triad_residual_avoidance_value = 0 for periods on or after 1 April 2023.
- *Source:* Drax, 'Triads: How TNUoS charges are changing', https://energy.drax.com/insights/end-of-triads-tnuos-charges-changing/
- *Source:* NESO, 'Transmission Demand Residual (TDR) Guidance Document', https://www.neso.energy/document/288186/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026, slide 4, https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'CMP308: Removal of BSUoS charges from Generation', https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/cmp308-removal-bsuos-charges-generation

#### D-182 · MUST · Flag DUoS reform as an open regulatory risk with no fixed date, and stress-test the case against a redesign of DUoS time bands and capacity charging.

- **Why:** Ofgem's DUoS Significant Code Review update (22 April 2025) states that Ofgem is deferring substantive reform pending REMA, TNUoS charging reform and the Energy Cost Allocation and Recovery review, will address 'urgent issues as they arise' through interim measures, and defers 'design-led enduring solutions for later work'. No implementation dates are given. Meanwhile the year-on-year tariff re-basing is already large: 2025/26 versus 2024/25 saw capacity charges rise on average about 108% and standing charges fall about 72% for an LV band-2 site-specific user.
- **Error if omitted:** A ten-year BTM savings case rests on a charging methodology that the regulator has publicly stated it intends to redesign. Presenting a flat escalation of today's red-band rate as a P50 is not defensible.
- **How to implement:** Sensitivity: (a) red-band rate ±50%; (b) red-band window narrowed to 2 hours; (c) volumetric DUoS replaced in part by capacity charging; (d) capacity charges +100%. Report the DSCR breach point for each. State the regulatory risk explicitly in the underwriting memo.
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Distribution Use of System Charges: Significant Code Review update', 22 April 2025, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/policy/distribution-use-system-charges-significant-code-review-update
- *Source:* NUS Consulting Group, 'DUoS Charges (UK Mainland) 2025', https://www.nusconsulting.com/energy-news/uk-duos-charges-2025/

#### D-183 · MUST · For an EHV-connected or HV-substation-metered site, use the EDCM rather than the CDCM, and model the super-red unit charge as an export CREDIT as well as an import charge.

- **Why:** The EDCM applies to customers connected at EHV or connected at HV and metered at an HV substation. Its components are a fixed charge (p/MPAN/day), a capacity charge (p/kVA/day levied on MIC, MEC and exceeded capacity, incorporating the LRIC component and the National Grid transmission cost), and a super-red unit charge (p/kWh) that 'is positive for import and negative for export which means you can either reduce your charges by minimising consumption or increasing export at those times'. Charges are site-specific and locational.
- **Error if omitted:** Applying CDCM red/amber/green rates to an EDCM site produces the wrong number entirely, and misses the export credit, which for a battery able to export in the super-red window is a genuine additional revenue line unavailable to CDCM sites.
- **How to implement:** Branch on charging methodology by voltage and metering point. For EDCM: cost = fixed_p_per_day + (MIC + MEC + exceeded) × capacity_rate + Σ_t super_red_rate × (import[t] − export[t]) over the super-red window defined in the DNO's Annex 2. Note the DNO warns that 'future charge rates may be affected by consumption during the Super-red period', so reducing super-red consumption has a compounding benefit.
- *Source:* London Power Networks plc, 'Use of System Charging Statement', effective 1 April 2026, v1.2, Appendix 2 paras 1.22–1.26, https://media.umbraco.io/ukpn-cms/h5ahq0jp/london-power-networks-lc14-statement-2026-v1-2.pdf

#### D-184 · MUST · Where multiple MPANs sit behind one connection point, model the aggregation rules for fixed charges and for exceeded-capacity/reactive calculations, because they change which meter the battery should sit behind.

- **Why:** DNOs state that where two or more HH MPANs/MSIDs are at the same point of connection with the same LLFC/DUoS Tariff ID and registered to the same supplier, only one daily fixed charge applies; and that where data for two or more MPANs is aggregated for billing, the half-hourly consumption values are summed BEFORE the exceeded-capacity and reactive calculations. Conversely, where a site has more than one point of connection, separate charges apply to each.
- **Error if omitted:** Mis-places the battery. A battery behind the wrong MPAN cannot shave the peak that drives the charge, and the model will not detect this if it treats the site as a single node.
- **How to implement:** Represent the site as a graph of connection points and MPANs with the battery attached at a specified node. Compute charges at the level at which they are actually levied (per connection point / per aggregated MPAN group). Test alternative battery locations as a design variable.
- *Source:* London Power Networks plc, 'Use of System Charging Statement', effective 1 April 2026, v1.2, paras 2.21, 2.24, 2.42, 2.51, https://media.umbraco.io/ukpn-cms/h5ahq0jp/london-power-networks-lc14-statement-2026-v1-2.pdf

#### D-185 · MUST · Include a degradation penalty term in the dispatch objective so that the optimiser trades revenue against wear in every period, rather than optimising revenue and then applying degradation ex post.

- **Why:** Carmona et al. include an explicit daily battery degradation penalty EDP = λ_deg × Σ_h |π⁻_h − π⁺_h − π⁻_{h−1} + π⁺_{h−1}|, linearised in the MILP with auxiliary variables u_h, v_h >= 0. Shi et al. show the rainflow degradation cost is convex and can therefore be embedded directly in the optimisation with solution-quality guarantees; the whole point of their paper is that treating degradation inside the control problem, rather than after it, changes the optimal policy.
- **Error if omitted:** Ex-post degradation accounting produces a dispatch that is optimal for a battery that does not wear out, then charges it for wear it would not have incurred under a wear-aware policy. It over-cycles, and both revenue and life are wrong.
- **How to implement:** Add to the objective: λ_deg × Σ_h (u_h + v_h) subject to π⁻_h − π⁺_h − π⁻_{h−1} + π⁺_{h−1} = u_h − v_h, u_h >= 0, v_h >= 0. Set λ_deg from the marginal cost of life loss (cell cost × dΦ/du at the operating depth), not from an arbitrary throughput figure.
- *Source:* Carmona, R., et al., arXiv:2409.03942, §3.2.4 and eqn (1), https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.03942
- *Source:* Shi, Y., Xu, B., Tan, Y., Kirschen, D., Zhang, B., arXiv:1709.05715v2, 2017, https://arxiv.org/abs/1709.05715

#### D-186 · MUST · Model the demand-charge state as a running maximum within the billing period, so the controller's decision to shave depends on the peak already set that month — and do not let the model reset that state at a horizon boundary.

- **Why:** Demand charges are levied on the maximum net import in the billing period, so the marginal value of shaving on any given day depends on the running maximum to date. Carmona et al. handle this explicitly, generating probabilistic estimates of whether a given day will set a new running maximum ('the probability that day d sees a new running maximum'), because the non-coincident-peak reward depends on it.
- **Error if omitted:** Naive daily optimisation shaves every day equally, spending cycles (and life) on days that cannot possibly set the month's peak, and under-shaves on the days that do. Overstates cost, understates achievable savings, and over-cycles the asset — a triple error.
- **How to implement:** Carry a state variable M[d] = max over half hours up to day d of net import in the billing period. The demand-charge saving on day d is λ_demand × max(0, M_baseline[end of month] − M_actual[end of month]), so the controller must reason over the remaining days. Implement either as a full-month MILP (perfect foresight bound) or as a rolling-horizon controller with a probability estimate p_new_max[d] (realistic case) and report both.
- *Source:* Carmona, R., et al., arXiv:2409.03942, §§2.3.3 and 3.2.3, https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.03942

#### D-187 · MUST · Where load is not fully controllable, state the assumption. Do not allow the model to implicitly curtail or shift the host's process load unless a contractual demand-response arrangement exists.

- **Why:** Carmona et al. assume explicitly that 'the microgrid's load is assumed to be non-controllable: it has to be satisfied at all times and cannot be modified'. This is the correct default for a C&I host: production is not the developer's to interrupt. G100 makes the converse point on the network side — a CLS 'is not intended to interfere with any load or generation flexibility that Customers wish to make use of', but it MUST be able to override any market-driven control system to keep flows within State 1 limits.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that quietly shifts host load creates savings that the host will not permit and that no M&V method will recognise. It also inverts the sizing conclusion: a battery sized against a shiftable load is smaller than one sized against a rigid load.
- **How to implement:** Load[t] is exogenous unless an explicit DSR contract object exists with defined curtailable kW, notice period, maximum events per year and compensation. Add a constraint that the CLS override takes precedence over the economic optimiser: if the G100 limit binds, the optimiser's schedule is superseded.
- *Source:* Carmona, R., et al., arXiv:2409.03942, §2.1, https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.03942
- *Source:* Energy Networks Association, EREC G100 Issue 2 Amd 2 (2023), §§1 and 4.3.1, https://www.energynetworks.org/publications/ena-erec-g100-issue-2-amendment-2-technical-requirements-customer-export-and-import-limitation-schemes

#### D-188 · MUST · Rebuild the revenue stack to the current GB market structure, not to a frequency-response-led 2019-2022 structure, and state the stack composition as a model output.

- **Why:** The GB stack has inverted. In 2020-2021 frequency response (FFR, EFR, then Dynamic Containment at a £17/MW/h cap) carried the revenue; by 2024-2026 the Balancing Mechanism and wholesale dominate, with the Open Balancing Platform, bulk dispatch in merit order and Grid Code GC0166 (real-time state of charge visibility) changing BM access materially. Gresham House notes NESO has historically assumed batteries have a maximum of 30 minutes of charge, 'which significantly reduces their usefulness to NESO', and that GC0166 implementation will change this.
- **Error if omitted:** A model calibrated on a frequency-response-led stack will misprice duration (short duration was optimal for DC; longer duration is optimal for BM and wholesale), misprice the value of state-of-charge management, and produce revenue that has no relationship to what current assets earn.
- **How to implement:** Model each stream separately with its own price series, availability window, and co-optimisation rules: wholesale day-ahead, intraday, Balancing Mechanism (bid and offer, with skip-rate and dispatch-probability assumptions), the current dynamic response product set, reserve products, and Capacity Market. Report the modelled stack shares by year and compare to the observed GB index composition. Flag any assumption inherited from the DC era (e.g. a fixed £/MW/h frequency price, or an assumption that response is always available) as stale.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'The evolution of the GB battery energy storage revenue stack' — FFR/EFR era, DC at a £17/MW/h price cap, subsequent diversification, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/5023
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB BESS revenue forecast v3.4: Model enhancements and revenue outlook', April 2025 — Balancing Mechanism optimisation fully integrated into the core linear optimisation, replacing a fixed allocation, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-battery-revenue-energy-storage-gb-april-2025-forecast-revenue-changes-new-balancing-mechanism
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — bulk dispatch in merit order; Grid Code GC0166 approved, real-time state of charge visibility, implementation scheduled Q2; NESO's 30-minute charge assumption, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf

#### D-189 · MUST · Model the grid or site connection as a hard constraint and as a bankability gate: firm versus non-firm, import and export limits, and the connection agreement date.

- **Why:** DNV identifies grid connection firmness as critically affecting operational certainty and revenue generation, and distinguishes firm from non-firm connections as a bankability question. For a BTM asset the constraint is usually the host's existing capacity, and any export requires a separate agreement. Gresham House records a connections reform process reviewing over six thousand grid connection offers and delaying project dates.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that lets the battery charge and discharge at rated power ignoring the site's agreed capacity will produce dispatch that cannot physically or contractually occur, and will overstate both arbitrage volume and any capacity-market or ancillary participation. Ignoring non-firm curtailment overstates availability.
- **How to implement:** Constrain -ImportLimit_kW <= NetSiteFlow_hh <= ExportLimit_kW where NetSiteFlow = HostLoad + BatteryCharge - BatteryDischarge - OnsiteGeneration. For export-limited or zero-export sites set ExportLimit = 0 and confirm that all revenue streams modelled are achievable without export. For non-firm connections apply a curtailment profile. Gate the case on: connection agreement in place, capacity sufficient, and expiry/reassessment date beyond the debt tenor.
- *Source:* DNV, 'Financing the energy transition: building bankable battery projects' — firm vs non-firm connection distinction; network tariffs and curtailment restrictions materially impact project economics, https://www.dnv.com/article/financing-energy-transition-building-bankable-battery-projects/
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — connections reform, Gate 2 offers, over six thousand connection offers reviewed, delays to connection dates, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf

#### D-190 · SHOULD · Represent skip as a stochastic, direction-specific probability of non-acceptance applied to volume that is already in merit — not as a flat revenue haircut and not as a price adjustment. Sample it (Monte Carlo) rather than applying the mean.

- **Why:** The only peer-reviewed treatment (Gale et al. 2026) uses 'stochastic allocation of skip rates (bid non-acceptance) using a Monte Carlo approach'. LCP Delta's own construction is volumetric: skip rate is 'the ratio of volume in-merit but not utilised to the overall balancing requirement for that time-period'. Skips are volumetric events, and they are correlated with exactly the periods a battery most wants to be dispatched (system stress, constraints).
- **Error if omitted:** Applying the mean skip rate to mean revenue understates variance and therefore understates P90 downside and DSCR breach probability, because BM revenue is convex in acceptance during high-price periods. A flat haircut also destroys the ability to test a P90 case, which is the whole point of underwriting.
- **How to implement:** For each period t, direction d, band p: draw A(t,d,p) ~ Bernoulli(1 − s(t,d,·)) where s is the skip rate conditional on stage/technology/region/period. Realised BM volume V*(t,d,p) = A(t,d,p) · V_inmerit(t,d,p). Run N ≥ 1,000 paths; report P50 and P90 of annual BM revenue. Preserve serial and cross-sectional correlation of s with price and with constraint state (do not draw i.i.d. across periods).
- *Source:* Gale, E., Schmidt, O., O'Cinneide, A., Johnson, N. & Staffell, I., 'Balancing with batteries: The impact of revenue stacking and skip rates on battery energy storage profitability in Great Britain', Journal of Energy Storage, Vol. 166, 20 July 2026, DOI 10.1016/j.est.2026.122328, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2352152X26019924
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, p.3, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download

#### D-191 · SHOULD · Apply an explicit market-depth (volume) cap on how much BM volume the asset can win, derived from the total BM energy requirement, the share addressable given the asset's dynamic parameters, and the size of the competing fleet.

- **Why:** Modo measured the control room dispatching ~71 GWh/day across both directions (~1.5 GW continuous each way); 39% is system-flagged; of the non-system-flagged volume only 24% was accessible under the 15-minute rule, i.e. ~440 MW continuous / ~11 GWh/day, against a GB battery fleet that has since grown from 2.9 GW to 7.2 GW / 11.8 GWh (Q1 2026). Modo also measures ~0.4 MWh dispatched per MW offered per day.
- **Error if omitted:** An unconstrained optimiser will let a single site win unlimited BM volume at attractive prices. As the GB fleet grows from 2.9 GW to a projected 22.8 GW by 2029, per-MW BM revenue must be modelled as competing for a finite and only slowly-growing pool. Omitting this produces per-MW BM revenues that cannot be earned by the fleet in aggregate.
- **How to implement:** Cap: Σ_sites V_BM(t) ≤ AddressableDepth(t) = R_energy(t) × f_param, and allocate to the modelled asset a share ≤ (P_asset / P_fleet_addressable). Sanity gate: implied annual BM throughput per MW should not exceed the empirical benchmark of ~0.4 MWh/MW/day (≈146 MWh/MW/yr) without an explicit justification; flag if it does.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism: how deep is the market for battery energy storage?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/10374
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism availability: how much are batteries getting dispatched?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/balancing-mechanism-availability-dispatch-battery-energy-storage
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB BESS buildout Q1 2026: fleet reaches 7.2 GW', 2026, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-bess-buildout-q1-2026-great-britain-battery-energy-storage-connections-reform

#### D-192 · SHOULD · Do not model skip rate as a pure function of the asset's own bid/offer price. Price-competitiveness is the entry condition (being 'in merit'); skip is what happens after that condition is met.

- **Why:** By construction, NESO's skip rate is 'a measurement of out of cost merit actions ... taken by NESO against the total volume of actions available' — the skipped volume is by definition already cheaper than what was taken. LCP Delta attributes residual skips to asset dynamics, transmission limitations, ENCC IT and process limitations, desk allocation ('Zonal Management'), and operator behaviour — not to price.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that assumes cutting bid prices buys acceptance will recommend value-destroying aggressive pricing: the asset gives up margin on the small volume it does win without materially improving acceptance. LCP Delta's stakeholder evidence is that operators responded to skips by pivoting away from the BM entirely, and that expectation of skipping 'alters pricing'.
- **How to implement:** Two-step: (i) merit test — include volume in the in-merit stack only if π(u,p) < π_marginal(t) for offers (> for bids); (ii) acceptance — apply s(·) that is independent of π given merit. If any price-elasticity of acceptance is asserted, it must be evidenced separately and labelled as an assumption, not as literature.
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, pp.7-8, 47, 55-56, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download

#### D-193 · SHOULD · Model the aggregator/optimiser (route-to-market) commercial share as an explicit, separately disclosed line in the cashflow, and model floor structures where present.

- **Why:** The GB BESS market 'heavily relies on optimisers'; there is no standard contract. Reported traditional revenue-share models give the optimiser 3–10% of revenues; above-floor revenue is sometimes shared 50/50; floor agreements trade a higher fee for a minimum guaranteed return that is typically set at a level sufficient to secure debt.
- **Error if omitted:** Omitting the optimiser share overstates net revenue by 3–10% of gross across every stream, and the DSCR is computed on the wrong cashflow. For a BTM asset the aggregator is not optional — it is the only route to the BM — so the fee is unavoidable, not a modelling choice.
- **How to implement:** Net_revenue(t) = Gross_revenue(t) × (1 − φ) − Fixed_fee, with φ ∈ [0.03, 0.10] for revenue-share, or Net_revenue = max(Floor, Gross×(1−φ_high)) with the floor sized to cover debt service and φ_high the elevated share. Run DSCR on the floor case for the downside and on the merchant case for the base.
- *Source:* Bryan Cave Leighton Paisner, 'Battery storage revenues and routes to market', https://www.bclplaw.com/en-US/events-insights-news/battery-storage-revenues-and-routes-to-market.html
- *Source:* enspired, 'BESS revenue models: tolling, floor & fully merchant', https://www.enspired-trading.com/blog/bess-revenue-models-toll-floor-fully-merchant
- *Source:* Pexapark, 'What Are BESS Offtake and Optimization Agreements?', https://pexapark.com/what-are-bess-offtake-and-optimization-agreements/

#### D-194 · SHOULD · Calibrate total revenue and the BM share against published GB benchmarks, and flag any model output that materially exceeds them. Do not present a BM revenue per MW that the GB fleet has never achieved.

- **Why:** Published benchmarks: GB BESS revenue averaged ~£51k/MW/yr in 2024 and ~£72k/MW/yr in 2025; two-hour BESS averaged £73,145/MW/yr over the twelve months to April 2026 with wholesale + BM ~60% of the stack; monthly range across 2025–26 spans £47k (Dec 2025) to £77k (Oct 2025). BM alone hit a record £27k/MW/yr in February 2025 and £46k/MW/yr in March 2026.
- **Error if omitted:** Underwriting models that produce £120k+/MW/yr merchant revenue for a BTM asset are not lender-credible. The published range of £55,000–£120,000/MW across 2023–2025 is for front-of-meter, BM-active, ≥6 MW assets — a BTM 1 MW asset behind an aggregator should be at or below the bottom of that range after the optimiser share.
- **How to implement:** Add a validation gate: if modelled gross revenue/MW/yr > 1.25 × the trailing 12-month published benchmark for the equivalent duration class, halt and require explicit justification. Report the modelled BM share of the stack alongside the published ~60% wholesale+BM benchmark and the ~33% gross frequency response share.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'How does a battery energy storage system make money?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/how-does-battery-energy-storage-make-money
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'ME BESS GB' monthly revenue series (Sep 2025 – Mar 2026), e.g. https://modoenergy.com/research/en/me-bess-gb-revenues-rise-march-2026-balancing-mechanism-record-gas-prices-
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB battery energy storage markets: 2024 year in review', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-battery-energy-storage-markets-2024-year-in-review-great-britain-wholesale-balancing-mechanism-frequency-response-reserve

#### D-195 · SHOULD · Model locational value explicitly at GSP-group granularity, not as a national average, for any revenue arising from constraint or system-flagged actions.

- **Why:** Peer-reviewed transmission-system modelling of the GB BM (Nosratabadi et al., Oxford/Bocconi/Brunel, 1,982-node model) finds the marginal BM cost benefit of a 1 MW 2-hour Li-ion battery varies from ~£0 to ~£4,000 per month depending on GSP group and season, and that for 100 MW units the January monthly BM cost reduction ranges from ~£15,500 to ~£372,000 by GSP group. LCP Delta's stakeholders separately noted that the ENCC's split of dispatch between geographical North and South desks itself generates 'Zonal Management' skips.
- **Error if omitted:** A single national skip rate and a single national BM price ignores that BM value in the constrained north can be an order of magnitude different from the south. For a BTM site the location is fixed and known — there is no excuse for a national average.
- **How to implement:** Parameterise by GSP group g: BM_value(g,t) = f(R_energy(t), R_sys(g,t), s_energy(t), s_sys(g,t), π_BM(g,t)). Where GSP-level skip data is unavailable, use the NESO technology dataset as the national prior and apply a documented regional adjustment, flagging it as an assumption. Note North Scotland is reported as having the highest battery skip rates.
- *Source:* Nosratabadi, S.M., Savelli, I., Kumtepeli, V., Grunewald, P., Aunedi, M., Howey, D.A. & Morstyn, T., 'The Impact of Grid Storage on Balancing Costs and Carbon Emissions in Great Britain', arXiv:2410.07740, 2024, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2410.07740
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, pp.46, 55-56, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Skip rates: How have these improved for BESS in 2024?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/bess-skip-rates-balancing-mechanism-2024-update-system-energy-actions

#### D-196 · SHOULD · Model round-trip efficiency and directional efficiencies explicitly in the BM volume calculation, and state the metering point at which they apply (the connection point / settlement metering, not the DC bus).

- **Why:** NESO's GC0166 FSoE model requires that BOAs and PNs be modelled 'at the point where these are measured as defined in the BSC (normally settlement metering)'; a 'very simple model may just have export and import efficiencies'. Modo's published index methodology applies an import efficiency of 85% and an export efficiency of 99% when modelling non-BMU behaviour. Nosratabadi et al. use 85% round-trip for Li-ion and 79% for pumped storage in their BM model.
- **Error if omitted:** Applying efficiency at the wrong boundary double-counts or omits auxiliary and transformer losses. For BTM assets the settlement boundary is the asset meter (AMSID) or the site boundary point, and losses between the two matter for verified BM delivery.
- **How to implement:** SoC(t+1) = SoC(t) + η_ch·P_ch(t)·Δt/E_cap − P_dis(t)·Δt/(η_dis·E_cap), with η_ch and η_dis referenced to the settlement metering point. Default η_ch = 0.85, η_dis = 0.99 per Modo's non-BMU convention, or an explicit AC-AC round-trip of ~85% per the peer-reviewed GB BM model, and state which convention is used.
- *Source:* NESO, 'GC0166 Final Modification Report', 8 July 2025, p.11, https://www.neso.energy/document/364346/download
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB Methodology — Indices', https://modoenergy.com/methodology/gb
- *Source:* Nosratabadi et al., arXiv:2410.07740, 2024, p.6, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2410.07740

#### D-197 · SHOULD · Model BSUoS on the Final-Demand-only basis effective from 1 April 2023 (CMP308) with an ex-ante fixed volumetric tariff set with advance notice (CMP361 WACM3), and do not charge BSUoS on the battery's charging energy where that energy is consumed for the purpose of generation or export rather than as final demand.

- **Why:** From April 2023 CMP308 moved BSUoS from generation and demand to Final Demand only, where Final Demand is 'electricity consumed other than for the purposes of generation or export onto the electricity network'. CMP361 WACM3 introduced a fixed volumetric BSUoS tariff published for six months with nine months' notice.
- **Error if omitted:** For a BTM battery the split between energy consumed as site final demand and energy consumed for export/generation is the difference between paying and not paying BSUoS on charging volume — material for a high-cycling BM strategy. Using a pre-2023 volumetric BSUoS on generation also mis-states the cost of BM offers.
- **How to implement:** BSUoS_cost(t) = BSUoS_tariff(t) × FinalDemand_volume(t), where FinalDemand_volume excludes battery charging attributable to subsequent export/BM delivery. Use the published fixed tariff for the relevant six-month window rather than a forecast volumetric rate. Flag the final-demand apportionment as a site-specific determination requiring supplier/DNO confirmation.
- *Source:* Ofgem/NGESO, CMP308 and CMP361 decisions as summarised in NESO, 'BSUoS fixed tariff model methodology', https://www.neso.energy/document/262041/download
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'CMP361 and CMP362 — Minded-to decision', September 2022, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2022-09/CMP361%20Minded-to%20final%20PDF%20-%20Publication.pdf
- *Source:* EDF Energy, 'BSUoS reform: why and how charges are changing', https://www.edfenergy.com/large-business/talk-power/blogs/BSUoS-charging-reform

#### D-198 · SHOULD · Derive P50 and P90 revenue from the joint distribution of price and acceptance, and report DSCR under a downside that holds skip rates at their current observed level rather than assuming the reform trajectory continues.

- **Why:** LCP Delta's stakeholder evidence is unambiguous that this is the binding underwriting issue: investors reported dispatch rates 'a country mile' below project-development-stage projections, one battery 'sitting idle for up to two-years', and stakeholders 'now incorporating higher levels of skip rates in their modelling forecasts', with some holding back GB investment altogether. Gale et al. quantify the elasticity at ~7% of profit per 10 percentage points of skip.
- **Error if omitted:** A P90 built only on price uncertainty misses the dominant risk. The historical realisation of skip-rate risk (90%+ against development-stage expectations) is precisely the failure mode this survey exists to catch.
- **How to implement:** P90_revenue = 10th percentile of the Monte Carlo distribution over jointly sampled (price path, skip path), not the product of separate P90s. Downside case: s frozen at latest observed value, no further reform benefit, constraint bid-down banned (Option 3A). Sizing case: DSCR ≥ lender threshold must hold in the downside, not the base.
- *Source:* LCP Delta, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', 2024, pp.46-47, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* Gale et al., 'Balancing with batteries', J. Energy Storage 166, 2026, DOI 10.1016/j.est.2026.122328, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2352152X26019924

#### D-199 · SHOULD · Do not stack CM revenue with a Triad-avoidance revenue line for a delivery year after 2021/22 without explicit justification; treat post-TCR embedded benefits as (a) avoided volumetric DUoS unit rates, (b) any remaining locational transmission demand charge exposure, and (c) BSUoS treatment, each sourced from the current charging statements.

- **Why:** Ofgem's Targeted Charging Review decision (21 November 2019) moved residual network charge recovery to fixed charges for all households and businesses, removed Transmission Generation Residual liability for generators, and reformed balancing services charges for smaller distributed generators; NESO records implementation at both transmission and distribution level in April 2022. The Triad-avoidance embedded benefit that dominated pre-2018 BTM battery business cases was progressively removed.
- **Error if omitted:** A BTM battery business case built on a 2017-era stack (Triad avoidance + CM + DUoS red-band avoidance) will overstate revenue by the entire Triad line, historically a very large component for a C&I site. This is the single clearest way in which a model built on pre-2020 GB literature is wrong. Conversely, assuming ALL network charge avoidance has gone will understate the volumetric DUoS red-band saving, which remains real.
- **How to implement:** Network charge avoidance must be computed from the DNO's current Use of System charging statement (volumetric red/amber/green p/kWh bands applied to the site's half-hourly import) and the current TNUoS demand tariff structure for the site's charging category, not from a legacy Triad model. Assert: `delivery_year >= 2022 implies triad_avoidance_revenue == 0` unless a specifically-sourced exception is documented. Note this is a different lens; obtain the tariff numbers from the network-charging workstream rather than from the CM lens.
- *Source:* Ofgem, Targeted Charging Review: Decision and Impact Assessment, 21 November 2019, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/decision/targeted-charging-review-decision-and-impact-assessment
- *Source:* NESO, Targeted Charging Review, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/charging/charging-futures/targeted-charging-review

#### D-200 · SHOULD · Treat the storage de-rating factor for future auctions as a forecast with a downward central trend and wide uncertainty, not a constant, when modelling post-agreement re-contracting.

- **Why:** Modo Energy's July 2026 methodology documentation states BESS de-rating factors 'fell sharply for CM years 2026/27 and 2027/28' following NESO methodology changes, partially recovered in 2028/29 and 2029/30, and that the long-term trajectory is 'expected to be downward'. The published record confirms the volatility: 4h storage 0.9552 (2019/20 T-1) -> 0.3078 (2027/28 T-4) -> 0.4396 (2029/30 T-4). The mechanism is structural: as more storage enters the system, the marginal EFC of an additional duration-limited unit falls.
- **Error if omitted:** For a 15-year agreement the re-contracting tail matters less; for a 3-year or 1-year route it is the dominant driver. A model that assumes today's de-rating factor persists for 15+ years overstates merchant-tail CM revenue. A model that assumes a linear decline may equally overstate the decline given the 2028/29-2029/30 recovery.
- **How to implement:** Scenario the per-hour de-rating slope k. Base: hold the latest T-4 slope flat (0.1099/h for 2029/30). Downside: decline k by 3-7%/yr toward saturation. Upside: hold at the 2024/25 T-4 level (k = 0.2477/h). Apply to CM revenue only in delivery years after the contracted agreement ends.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, Capacity Market (July 2026 forecast documentation), https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jul-2026/uk-europe/gb/capacity-market/
- *Source:* NESO, Capacity Market De-Rating Factors dataset (full history 2016-17 to 2029-30)
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rule 2.3.4B (a Storage GTC is Duration Limited if at least 5% of Loss of Load Occurrences are estimated to last longer than the class's minimum duration)

#### D-201 · SHOULD · Do not allow a Response contract and a Reserve contract (Quick Reserve, Balancing Reserve, Slow Reserve) to be held on the same MW of the same unit in the same window in the base case. Treat any such co-delivery as a modelled upside only, flagged as not yet permitted.

- **Why:** The Response Service Terms provide that where a Service Provider is required to provide any other Balancing Service that conflicts, the two cannot be provided simultaneously and the Response Unit is deemed unavailable for the other Balancing Service. The QR Service Terms contain the mirror clause with QR prevailing. The Quick Reserve design states splitting with Response is not possible in the initial phases, with an intention to broaden it in future subject to an EBR Article 18 consultation.
- **Error if omitted:** Double-counting the same MW across two markets. Given FY26/27 mean prices of £2.79/MW/h (DCL) and £3.98/MW/h (PQR), naive stacking would inflate modelled ancillary revenue by roughly 100 % on that capacity. It would also expose the project to withheld availability payments, since availability payments are withheld for units holding more than one reserve service contract in the same direction for a given window.
- **How to implement:** Constraint per unit and per overlapping window: sum_s (1+rc_s) q_response[s,d,w] + q_reserve[all products, w] <= P_registered, with a hard binary forbidding simultaneous non-zero response and reserve on the same registered capacity unless a flag 'neso_no_conflict_determination = True' is set. Model the upside as a separate sensitivity.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, effective 31 July 2026, paragraphs 12.5–12.7, https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Quick Reserve Service Terms', paragraph 17.4–17.5, https://www.neso.energy/document/364276/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Quick Reserve Service Summary', Version 5.0, July 2025, 'Revenue Stacking' section: 'For a given QR Window, splitting is allowed between Positive and Negative Quick Reserve only... Availability payments will be withheld for any units holding >1 Reserve service contract in the same direction'; 'Whilst not possible for the initial phases of Quick Reserve, NESO intend to broaden splitting capabilities with other services including Response, Balancing Reserve and Slow Reserve in the future', https://www.neso.energy/document/364336/download

#### D-202 · SHOULD · If the asset is behind a distribution connection subject to an Active Network Management scheme or flexibility connection, test eligibility explicitly before booking reserve revenue, and check the equivalent position for Response.

- **Why:** For Quick Reserve, NESO states that eligible assets will not normally be registered if they have a DNO connection agreement condition signing them up to an ANM scheme or flexibility connection, though it may permit participation case by case with evidence of high forecast availability. The Response Procurement Rules define 'Active Network Management Scheme' and give NESO discretion to refuse a Sell Order where an Eligible Asset's location would compromise operational security.
- **Error if omitted:** Many small BTM and distribution-connected sites are on flexible or ANM connections precisely because that is how they got connected quickly. Booking full ancillary revenue on such a site can be simply wrong, and the failure is binary rather than marginal.
- **How to implement:** Site input flag: anm_or_flexible_connection (bool). If true, set reserve revenue to zero in the base case and response revenue subject to a named NESO-discretion probability; require documented evidence (e.g. DNO Curtailment Assessment Report) to relax it.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Quick Reserve Service Summary', Version 5.0, July 2025, 'Active Network Management Schemes' section, https://www.neso.energy/document/364336/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Procurement Rules', Version 6, Schedule 1 definition of 'Active Network Management Scheme' and paragraph 6.6, https://www.neso.energy/document/384601/download

#### D-203 · SHOULD · Apply a locational skip-rate adder driven by the asset's GSP Group / constraint zone, and do not assume a national average applies to a Scottish or otherwise constrained site.

- **Why:** NESO explicitly lists 'the location of the asset on the transmission system' and 'transmission constraints' among the operational constraints causing skips, and system-flagged (constraint) acceptances are excluded from the PSA measure by construction. Joining NESO's BMU-level Stage-5 PSA data to Elexon BM Unit reference data shows North Scotland 60.3% and South Scotland 56.6% in January 2025 versus Eastern 47.2% and Northern 43.2%; by June 2026 the gap had widened to North Scotland 53.5% / South Scotland 54.8% versus Eastern 35.2%.
- **Error if omitted:** Understates skip rate by ~10 percentage points (Jan 2025) to ~17 percentage points (Jun 2026) for Scottish sites, overstating their BM revenue by roughly 25–40%. Conversely, the model misses that constrained-zone assets can earn large negative-priced bid revenue that the PSA metric excludes as system-flagged — so it can also badly understate constraint revenue if only the energy-balancing merit stack is modelled.
- **How to implement:** s_{d,h,z} = s_{d,h} + Δ_z, with Δ_z calibrated per GSP Group from NESO's monthly '3_in_merit_data_post_system_actions_skips_MM_YYYY.csv' joined to Elexon '/reference/bmunits/all' on nationalGridBmUnit → gspGroupName. Model constraint/system-flagged revenue as a separate, explicitly locational stream rather than folding it into the energy-balancing merit stack.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Defining, measuring, and addressing skip rates', December 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348236/download
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Skip Rates', monthly In Merit Post System Action files, joined to Elexon BMRS Insights reference data https://data.elexon.co.uk/bmrs/api/v1/reference/bmunits/all, analysed 10 August 2026
- *Source:* Timera Energy, 'Key changes are impacting BESS constraint revenue capture', https://timera-energy.com/blog/key-changes-are-impacting-bess-constraint-revenue-capture/ (consultancy blog)
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Four batteries 20 miles apart beat the ME BESS GB Index in January', 2025, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/battery-energy-storage-revenues-operations-january-2025-scotland-balancing-mechanism

#### D-204 · SHOULD · Handle deeply negative bid prices as a distinct, locational, constraint-driven revenue mode with its own (much higher) skip rate, not as part of the national energy-balancing merit stack.

- **Why:** NESO's Stage-5 PSA data for June 2026 shows battery bids priced below −£50/MWh were skipped 76.1% of the time and bids in the −£50 to −£20 band 49.4%, versus 28.0% for bids in the £0–20 band. These deeply negative bids are predominantly behind-constraint charging offers, and the corresponding acceptances are system-flagged and therefore excluded from the PSA measure entirely.
- **Error if omitted:** Two symmetric failures. A model that applies the average skip rate to deeply negative bids will overstate constraint charging revenue by ~2x. A model that ignores the constraint mode altogether will miss the dominant BM revenue driver for Scottish and other constrained sites — Modo observed a Scottish cluster running losses of >£30k/MW/year in the BM while earning >£120k/MW/year in wholesale, i.e. paying for BM charging in order to sell energy.
- **How to implement:** Split BM into (a) national energy-balancing acceptances, priced against the merit stack with s from the PSA dataset, and (b) constraint/system-flagged acceptances, modelled as a locational volume × price process calibrated per constraint boundary. Flag both against Elexon's BOALF 'soFlag' / NESO Dispatch Transparency reason codes when back-testing.
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Skip Rates', '3_in_merit_data_post_system_actions_skips_06_2026.csv', BATTERY bids at stage 5 by price band, analysed 10 August 2026
- *Source:* LCP Delta for NESO, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', November 2024 (Stage 3 excludes system-flagged acceptances taken to alleviate transmission constraints), https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Four batteries 20 miles apart beat the ME BESS GB Index in January', 2025, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/battery-energy-storage-revenues-operations-january-2025-scotland-balancing-mechanism

#### D-205 · SHOULD · Model the dispatch rate (share of available energy actually dispatched) as a separate, explicitly bounded quantity, and check it against battery cycling limits before accepting the revenue it implies.

- **Why:** Modo Energy defines dispatch rate as the percentage of total available energy dispatched by the control room, measured 1.7% on average in 2023 with a central forecast of 6% by 2027 (high 8%, low 2%). It separately reports an 'in-merit dispatch rate' of 18% in October 2025, up from 11% in September 2025. These are the physical throughput constraints on BM revenue, and they bind against warranty cycling limits: a one-hour battery at an 8% dispatch rate cycles twice daily on BM alone, while two-hour assets can reach ~17% before cycling constraints bind.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that lets the BM absorb unlimited volume will produce BM revenue that requires more cycles than the warranty permits, invalidating both the degradation schedule and the O&M/warranty assumptions the lender is underwriting. For a 1-hour BTM asset this is the binding error.
- **How to implement:** Add a MILP constraint linking BM throughput to cycling: Σ_t (V^BM,charge_t + V^BM,offer_t + V^wholesale_t)/(2·E_nominal) ≤ N_cycles,max per day (typically 1–3 per warranty), and report the implied dispatch rate D = Σ_t V^dispatched_t / Σ_t V^available_t as a model output that must be checked against the 1.7%–8% published range for the relevant vintage. If D exceeds the plausible range, the revenue is not achievable.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism: how the Open Balancing Platform could improve battery revenues', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/balancing-mechanism-battery-energy-storage-revenues-forecast-bess-dispatch-skip-rates-open-platform
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'ME BESS GB: Revenues climb to £77k/MW/year in October 2025', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/battery-energy-storage-revenues-gb-october-2025-record-balancing-mechanism-dispatch-rates
- *Source:* Modo Energy forecast documentation, 'Balancing Mechanism', January 2026 edition, https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jan-2026/uk-europe/gb/bm/

#### D-206 · SHOULD · Model Balancing Reserve (and Quick/Slow Reserve) as a day-ahead availability commitment that reserves headroom/footroom and is delivered through the BM, with both an availability payment (£/MW/h, single clearing price) and a utilisation payment via BOA.

- **Why:** NESO's Balancing Reserve procures firm day-ahead regulating reserve; participants must be a BM Unit with a minimum 1 MW contracted capacity, must position headroom or footroom via MEL, PN, SEL/SIL and MIL, and are paid a single clearing availability price (£/MW/h) plus utilisation when instructed via BOA. NESO explicitly increased Balancing Reserve volumes and introduced Quick Reserve (phase 1 from 3 December 2024, phase 2 Q2 2025, Slow Reserve Q3 2025) as part of its response to skip rates. From October 2025, afternoon auctions co-optimise Balancing Reserve with Quick Reserve and Dynamic Response services.
- **Error if omitted:** Missing a revenue stream that is deliberately designed to be additive to the BM, and — worse — mis-modelling the BM itself, because a Balancing Reserve commitment constrains PN/MEL/MIL and so changes the BM volume the model can offer. Co-optimisation from October 2025 means the streams cannot be modelled independently without bias.
- **How to implement:** Add a day-ahead decision variable R_t (MW of reserve committed, ≥1 MW) with revenue = R_t·p^avail_t·0.5 h + accepted BM utilisation. Constrain headroom: MEL_t − PN_t ≥ R^up_t and PN_t − MIL_t ≥ R^down_t, and reserve the corresponding energy in the SoC envelope. Co-optimise Balancing Reserve, Quick Reserve and Dynamic Response in a single stage for periods after October 2025, not sequentially.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Balancing Reserve' service page, accessed August 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/balancing-reserve
- *Source:* NESO, 'Defining, measuring, and addressing skip rates', December 2024 (commitment to increase Balancing Reserve volume; Quick Reserve phase 1 from 3 December 2024), https://www.neso.energy/document/348236/download

#### D-207 · SHOULD · Model the Open Balancing Platform / Bulk Dispatch effect as a change in instruction granularity, not simply as more volume: more, smaller instructions to more units simultaneously.

- **Why:** Bulk dispatch launched 12 December 2023, was suspended after three days due to errors dispatching actions priced up to £99,000/MWh, and relaunched 8 January 2024. In the first relaunch week, BOAs doubled from a 260 to a 540 daily average and grouped instructions rose from 94 to 126 per day, but dispatched volume rose only 8% and average volume per instruction fell 48%. Maximum simultaneous BOAs doubled from 15 to 30 units. NESO reports battery utilisation up 290% and daily battery instructions up 940% in the first year of OBP.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that converts 'more instructions' into 'more MWh' overstates BM volume by roughly an order of magnitude. It also misses that shorter, smaller instructions raise the cycle count per MWh earned, worsening degradation per unit of revenue, and increase the share of dispatch that falls foul of MNZT/MZT.
- **How to implement:** Parameterise BM dispatch as (instruction arrival rate λ_t, mean instruction duration d, mean instruction MW m) rather than a single MWh figure. Cycle count contribution = Σ instructions × (m·d)/(2·E_nominal). Calibrate d against the post-bulk-dispatch average and re-check MNZT feasibility for each instruction.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism: Bulk dispatch launches for batteries', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/balancing-mechanism-bulk-dispatch-battery-energy-storage-open-platform-volume-skip-rates
- *Source:* NESO, 'Defining, measuring, and addressing skip rates', December 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348236/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'The start of the balancing mechanism revolution', 12 December 2023, https://www.neso.energy/news/start-balancing-mechanism-revolution

#### D-208 · SHOULD · Model pay-as-bid price capture explicitly: the unit is paid its own submitted BOD price, not a market clearing price, so realised price capture is strictly below the marginal price.

- **Why:** The BM settles accepted bid-offer volume at the submitted BOD price (pay-as-bid), and Gale et al. find that 'imperfect price capture under pay-as-bid settlement proportionally erodes financial returns'. Modo's own BM model assumes 'a minimum spread of £50/MWh between bid and offer prices, based on historical data', reflecting that a participant must price inside the margin to be selected and therefore cannot capture the full marginal price.
- **Error if omitted:** Overstates BM revenue per accepted MWh by the gap between the marginal price and the submitted price. Because a participant must price below the marginal offer to be in-merit, this error is systematic and one-directional, and it compounds with the skip-rate error rather than offsetting it.
- **How to implement:** Revenue = Σ_t Σ_j V^accepted_{j,t} · p^submitted_{j,t}, with p^submitted ≤ p^marginal for offers and ≥ p^marginal for bids. Introduce a price-capture ratio κ = Σ V·p^submitted / Σ V·p^marginal, report it as a model output, and constrain the modelled bid-offer spread to be no tighter than the observed floor (Modo use £50/MWh).
- *Source:* Gale E. et al., 'Balancing with batteries: The impact of revenue stacking and skip rates on battery energy storage profitability in Great Britain', Journal of Energy Storage vol. 166, 2026, DOI 10.1016/j.est.2026.122328
- *Source:* Modo Energy forecast documentation, 'Balancing Mechanism', January 2026, https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jan-2026/uk-europe/gb/bm/

#### D-209 · SHOULD · Model BM participation as a revenue-diversification benefit as well as a revenue-level benefit, and reflect the reduced volatility in the cost of capital or DSCR sizing rather than only in the mean.

- **Why:** Gale et al. find that participation in the GB balancing mechanism increases returns by 'up to 250% more than wholesale arbitrage alone' while reducing income volatility. NESO records that 'some batteries now earn 61% of their revenue from the Balancing Mechanism'. Modo's monthly GB BESS index shows the volatility being managed: £86k/MW/year (December 2024), £77k (October 2025), £59k (November 2025), £47k (December 2025).
