- **Error if omitted:** Underwriting BM revenue purely on its mean misses the correlation structure that matters to a lender. Conversely, a model that treats BM as an independent uncorrelated stream will understate P90 downside, because BM, wholesale and imbalance revenues all fell together in December 2025.
- **How to implement:** Build the P50/P90 from a joint distribution across revenue streams with an estimated correlation matrix, not from independent per-stream percentiles. Report BM share of total revenue as a concentration metric (benchmark: up to 61% for some GB batteries) and stress DSCR at a BM revenue haircut consistent with a 10pp adverse skip-rate move (≈7% profitability impact per Gale et al.).
- *Source:* Gale E. et al., 'Balancing with batteries: The impact of revenue stacking and skip rates on battery energy storage profitability in Great Britain', Journal of Energy Storage vol. 166, 2026, DOI 10.1016/j.est.2026.122328
- *Source:* NESO, 'Defining, measuring, and addressing skip rates', December 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348236/download
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'ME BESS GB: Revenues fall to £47k/MW/year in December 2025', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/me-bess-gb-battery-energy-storage-revenues-december-2025-low-demand-christmas

#### D-210 · SHOULD · Count BM dispatch cycles in the degradation model, using the actual accepted (not offered) volume and the actual instruction durations.

- **Why:** BM instructions became far more numerous and far shorter after Bulk Dispatch — average volume per individual instruction fell 48% while instruction count doubled — and NESO reports BM battery dispatch averaging 2.3 GWh per day across the fleet. Modo's cycling analysis shows a one-hour battery at an 8% dispatch rate would cycle twice daily on BM alone, and that warranty constraints typically permit between one and three daily cycles.
- **Error if omitted:** Understates throughput-driven capacity fade and can breach the warranty. Because BM revenue and cycling scale together, an unconstrained model produces a revenue case whose degradation schedule and warranty position are both invalid — the two errors compound rather than offset.
- **How to implement:** Equivalent full cycles from BM = Σ_t (V^BM,accepted,charge_t + V^BM,accepted,discharge_t) / (2 · E_nominal). Feed into the calendar-plus-cycle fade model, and add a hard MILP constraint on daily/annual equivalent full cycles at the warranty limit (1–3/day typical). Attribute the resulting capacity fade cost against BM revenue when comparing revenue streams.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism: how the Open Balancing Platform could improve battery revenues', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/balancing-mechanism-battery-energy-storage-revenues-forecast-bess-dispatch-skip-rates-open-platform
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism: Bulk dispatch launches for batteries', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/balancing-mechanism-bulk-dispatch-battery-energy-storage-open-platform-volume-skip-rates
- *Source:* NESO, 'Defining, measuring, and addressing skip rates', December 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348236/download

#### D-211 · SHOULD · Include the consumer-cost/materiality framing as a model output so the underwriting case can be sanity-checked against published aggregate figures.

- **Why:** Modo Energy quantified the cost of skipping batteries: £24 million of additional balancing costs in 2023, £8 million January–April 2024, a peak monthly cost of £3.9 million in October 2023, and a finding that using skipped battery volume over January 2023–April 2024 would have cost £47 million versus £79 million actual — a 40% (£32 million) saving. Skipped battery volume peaked at 132 GWh in December 2023, more than three times the January 2023 level, as available battery capacity grew from 750 MW to 2.4 GW.
- **Error if omitted:** Without an aggregate cross-check, a model can produce a per-MW BM revenue number that, scaled to the fleet, implies a BM cost far larger than the market actually spends. Fleet-level reconciliation is the cheapest available detector of a per-unit revenue error.
- **How to implement:** Report modelled BM revenue per MW × assumed GB battery fleet MW and compare against published aggregate BM battery spend and skipped-volume statistics for the same period. Flag the case if the implied fleet BM cost is inconsistent with the published total (e.g. £551 million total BM cost January–April 2024, of which battery skips represented 1.5%).
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism: The cost of skipping battery energy storage', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-benchmark-balancing-mechanism-battery-energy-storage-skip-rates-cost

#### D-212 · SHOULD · Explicitly separate 'why a unit was not accepted' into the taxonomy NESO publishes, and do not attribute all non-acceptance to control-room inefficiency.

- **Why:** NESO lists the operational constraints that legitimately cause out-of-merit dispatch: the location of the asset on the transmission system; transmission constraints; the length of time the asset can produce energy; maintaining network frequency; ramp up and ramp down times of the asset; and ancillary services required to meet operational requirements. NESO states that 'these do not necessarily represent inefficient dispatch'. NESO's Root Cause Analysis programme with machine-learning support, and the publication of findings and recommendations, is scheduled for April 2026.
- **Error if omitted:** Assuming all residual skips are fixable inefficiency leads to an over-optimistic improvement trajectory. Assuming none are fixable ignores NESO's regulatory commitment. Either way, without the taxonomy the model cannot say which portion of today's skip rate is expected to fall and when.
- **How to implement:** Decompose s = s_locational + s_dynamic-parameter + s_ancillary-reserved + s_tooling + s_residual, calibrate each from the NESO Exclusion Reasons dataset and the stage-by-stage funnel, and apply a different improvement trajectory to each component (tooling fastest, locational slowest and dependent on network investment).
- *Source:* NESO, 'Defining, measuring, and addressing skip rates', December 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348236/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Skip rates' industry information page (Root Cause Analysis programme, Independent Dispatch Strategic Review), accessed August 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/skip-rates
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Skip Rates' — 'Skip Rate - Exclusion Reasons' monthly files, https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/skip-rates

#### D-213 · SHOULD · If the BTM battery is to provide balancing services or participate in the Balancing Mechanism, model it as a secondary BM unit under BSC P375 with metering behind the site boundary point, and model the settlement interaction with the host site's supplier.

- **Why:** P375 'Settlement of Secondary BM Units using metering behind the site boundary point' was implemented on 1 April 2024 and is the mechanism by which a behind-the-meter asset can be settled separately for balancing services while sitting downstream of the site's main metering point. Without it, the asset's output is simply netted into the host supplier's position and the value accrues to the supplier.
- **Error if omitted:** Either (a) assuming BM/ancillary revenues accrue to the project when contractually they accrue to the host supplier, or (b) assuming they are unavailable when P375 makes them available. Also determines whether the asset's energy is 'traded away' from the supply contract, which changes the network-charge base.
- **How to implement:** Model two settlement regimes: (1) netted-into-supply (default) — battery output only reduces metered import, all value is the avoided delivered cost; (2) P375 secondary BMU — battery output is separately settled, earning market prices, but the host site's import is grossed up correspondingly and network charges/levies apply to the gross import. Make the regime a required input; the two regimes give materially different network-charge outcomes and must not be blended.
- *Source:* Ofgem, P375 Authority Decision Letter, 2021, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/docs/2021/02/p375_final_authority_decision.pdf
- *Source:* Elexon, P375 'Settlement of Secondary BM Units using metering behind the site boundary point' (implemented 1 April 2024), https://www.elexon.co.uk/bsc/mod-proposal/p375/

#### D-214 · SHOULD · Apply a real price-decline curve to DNO flexibility prices across the asset life. Do not hold today's clear flat or escalate it with CPI.

- **Why:** Awarded prices are falling sharply as domestic aggregators enter. SSEN's contract register shows awarded VAOU (week-ahead) availability price medians of £150/MW/h for 25/26, £145 for 26/27, £18.71 for 27/28, £10.70 for 28/29 and £8.54 for 29/30 delivery — a ~94% decline over five delivery years, already contracted. NGED's SU_SEP dispatch utilisation price median was £958/MWh in 2024/25 against an SU-HV awarded median of £91.93/MWh for autumn-2025 delivery.
- **Error if omitted:** Severe over-statement of years 3–15 of the revenue stack. A flat or escalating price assumption applied to SSEN's own forward contracts would over-state year-5 availability revenue by roughly 17×.
- **How to implement:** `price[y] = price[0] × decay(y)` with decay calibrated on the SSEN contract register forward curve (or, more conservatively, on a merchant-flexibility price floor). Expose the decay as an explicit, sensitivity-tested assumption; run a scenario where DNO flexibility prices converge on the wholesale/BM opportunity cost by year 5.
- *Source:* Scottish and Southern Electricity Networks, 'Flexibility Services Contract Register v2.30' (Call Off Contracts sheet, 1,537 awarded contracts), 30/07/2026, https://www.ssen.co.uk/globalassets/our-services/flexibility-services-document-library/contract-register/flexibility-services-contract-register-v2.30-v2.xlsx
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'C31E Supporting Data 2024-25', Dispatch tab, 2025, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1180
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'Weekly Trade Results', 2025, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1194

#### D-215 · SHOULD · Count DNO utilisation cycles against the battery's degradation and warranty budget, and model the SoC pre-positioning cost of holding availability.

- **Why:** Realised dispatch durations are long relative to a 1–4 hour BTM battery: NGED's 2024/25 dispatch data shows modal durations of 1–2 hours for Dynamic/SAOU_DA but up to 14 hours for Secure, and 4-hour blocks for Sustain and SU_SEP. Holding availability across a 3–4 hour evening window requires the battery to arrive at the window with sufficient SoC and to forgo the wholesale trade that would otherwise have used it.
- **Error if omitted:** Understated degradation (throughput and calendar) and understated opportunity cost. Where the utilisation rate is low, the dominant cost of an SAOU contract is not cycling — it is the SoC reservation that cannot be traded, which a model without the reservation constraint books as free.
- **How to implement:** Add utilisation-event throughput to the annual cycle count feeding the degradation model, and add the reservation constraint from the service-window requirement (SoC headroom during accepted windows). Test whether the availability fee exceeds the value of the reserved energy; if not, the contract should not be bid.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'C31E Supporting Data 2024-25', Dispatch tab ('Actual Duration of Dispatched Utility'), 2025, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1180
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'Guidance for Electricity Distribution Flexibility Service Providers', March 2026, Table 2.1, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1121

#### D-216 · SHOULD · Never underwrite on a perfect-foresight, single-shot annual MILP. Run a rolling-horizon re-optimisation with a realistic information set and calibrate the output against a published capture benchmark.

- **Why:** The published GB benchmark methodology (Modo v3.3) explicitly re-optimises across the delivery day in 2-hour increments and injects demand, wind and solar forecast error plus unplanned outages, precisely because a single perfect-foresight pass is not achievable revenue.
- **Error if omitted:** Systematic overstatement of wholesale revenue. The literature does not publish a single GB haircut percentage, so the correct treatment is calibration to the TB benchmark ratios below rather than an assumed derate. Direction: revenue overstated; magnitude unquantified in public sources.
- **How to implement:** Solve on a rolling window (e.g. 24-48h horizon, re-solved every 2h), fixing the first block of decisions each pass and carrying SoC forward. Then compute realised_revenue / TB_benchmark and reject the run if it falls outside the published band (see the TB capture constants).
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB BESS revenue forecast v3.3: hyper-real revenue operations', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-bess-revenue-forecast-33-hyper-real-revenue-operations
- *Source:* Modo Energy forecast documentation, 'Historic performance', https://forecastdocs.modoenergy.com/docs/historic-performance

#### D-217 · SHOULD · Validate modelled wholesale revenue against the top-bottom spread benchmark for the asset's duration, and require the ratio to sit inside the published range.

- **Why:** Modo publishes the realised relationship between GB battery revenue and the TBx spread: in 2025 one-hour assets earned around twice TB1 and two-hour assets around 42% more than TB2. This is the only public, duration-specific capture yardstick for GB.
- **Error if omitted:** No means of detecting a model that is either fantasising spread or ignoring the multi-venue/churn uplift. Both errors are large and neither is visible from the MILP objective alone.
- **How to implement:** TB1_d = max_h(p_d,h) - min_h(p_d,h); TB2_d = sum of 2 highest hourly prices minus sum of 2 lowest, per day d. Assert 1.5 <= R_wholesale_1h / sum_d(TB1_d) <= 2.5 and 1.2 <= R_wholesale_2h / sum_d(TB2_d) <= 1.7 as a model-acceptance gate, and log the ratio in the underwriting pack.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Top-bottom spread revenue benchmark for BESS: GB, Europe, Spain, Germany 2025', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/top-bottom-spread-revenue-benchmark-battery-energy-storage-sytems-gb-europe-spain-germany-solar-2025
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'How does battery energy storage make money?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/how-does-battery-energy-storage-make-money

#### D-218 · SHOULD · Model intraday trading and re-trading (churn) as a distinct revenue source, or explicitly assume day-ahead only and label the resulting revenue as a floor.

- **Why:** Modo measured intraday-only revenue 37% above day-ahead-only for a 1-hour, 1-cycle GB asset, and day-ahead plus intraday up to 96% above day-ahead-only for short-duration assets. Intraday average daily spread in 2023 was £103/MWh vs £71/MWh day-ahead, with intraday spreads larger on 90% of days.
- **Error if omitted:** Understates wholesale revenue by up to roughly a factor of two for a 1-hour asset. This is the opposite-sign error to perfect foresight and the two must not be allowed to silently cancel.
- **How to implement:** Add a second trading stage after the day-ahead clear: variables e_ID_t (buy/sell) settled at the intraday index, with a cumulative position constraint sum_t(e_DA_t + e_ID_t) matching physical delivery, and a churn cap (traded volume / delivered volume <= 5) reflecting observed GB churn.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Intraday and day-ahead power trading: revenue, spread and churn for battery energy storage', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/intraday-day-ahead-power-trading-revenue-spread-churn-battery-energy-storage
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Trading in the continuous intraday market: how does it work?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/continuous-intraday-trading-wholesale-epex-n2ex-volume-battery-energy-storage-prices

#### D-219 · SHOULD · Cap the value attributed to continuous intraday by its liquidity. Do not let a small BTM asset assume unlimited intraday execution.

- **Why:** GB continuous intraday averaged 47 GWh/day in 2023, 56% below EPEX day-ahead hourly and 83% below N2EX day-ahead. The effective trading window is roughly the 3 hours before delivery and average trade size was 1.3 MWh, with under 1% of trades exceeding 10 MWh. Modo separately notes that intraday trading overhead deters small-site participation.
- **Error if omitted:** A 0.5 MW BTM asset modelled as freely re-trading every half hour in continuous intraday is assuming an execution capability its optimiser will not sell it. Direction: revenue overstated.
- **How to implement:** Constrain |e_ID_t| <= liquidity_cap_t, with liquidity_cap_t non-zero only in the last N settlement periods before delivery, and add a per-trade minimum lot and a fixed transaction cost so that sub-MWh opportunities are not harvested.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Trading in the continuous intraday market: how does it work?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/continuous-intraday-trading-wholesale-epex-n2ex-volume-battery-energy-storage-prices
- *Source:* Modo Energy documentation, 'Small BESS sites', https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/apr-2026/uk-europe/gb/small-bess-sites/

#### D-220 · SHOULD · Price the cost of charging behind the customer's meter using the full delivered import stack, not the wholesale price, unless a specific exemption is evidenced.

- **Why:** Modo's tabulation for 2023/24 shows a battery pays BSUoS £13.41-14.03/MWh, CfD ~£13/MWh, Renewables Obligation ~£27/MWh, FiT ~£8/MWh, Capacity Market supplier charge plus settlement costs levy ~£11/MWh, Climate Change Levy £7.80/MWh and AAHEDC £0.42/MWh, plus DUoS capacity and unit charges and TNUoS triad exposure at £2.2/kW. Grid-scale storage escapes most of these via a generation licence and a Non-Final Demand declaration; Modo states behind-the-meter batteries typically do not qualify.
- **Error if omitted:** Charging at wholesale price understates the cost of every charged MWh by roughly £60-80/MWh at 2023/24 rates. On a 1-hour, 1-cycle asset that is on the order of tens of thousands of pounds per MW per year — enough to invert the sign of the arbitrage line.
- **How to implement:** charge_cost_t = c_t * (p_wholesale_t + BSUoS_t + DUoS_unit_t + CCL + RO + CfD + FiT + CM_levy + AAHEDC + supplier_margin) / RTE_charge_leg. Gate each levy term on an evidenced exemption flag (generation licence held; Non-Final Demand form submitted; Class A exemption claimed).
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Electricity import charges for battery energy storage', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/electricity-import-charges-battery-energy-storage
- *Source:* Modo Energy documentation, 'Small BESS sites', https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/apr-2026/uk-europe/gb/small-bess-sites/

#### D-221 · SHOULD · Model the route-to-market agreement as an explicit cashflow line with the correct structure (revenue share, fixed fee, floor with upside share, or toll), applied to GROSS trading revenue before it reaches the project.

- **Why:** BCLP notes there is no standard form of GB optimisation agreement and that optimisers charge a service fee and/or a share of revenues. Sympower publishes typical revenue shares of 3-10%. Enspired publishes worked structures: merchant with a 90:10 owner:optimiser split, floor without cap at 80:20, floor with cap at 85:15 plus a ceiling. Gridcog notes tolling terms typically span four to seven years while floors can support up to fifteen-year tenors.
- **Error if omitted:** Booking gross trading revenue to the project overstates equity cashflow by the fee. At a 10% share on £45k/MW/yr of wholesale plus BM revenue that is £4.5k/MW/yr straight off the equity line, before any DSCR test.
- **How to implement:** net_trading_revenue = max(floor, gross_trading_revenue * (1 - share)) - fixed_fee, with the floor and share as scenario inputs. Report contracted revenue and merchant revenue as separate lines so the lender can size debt to the contracted line only.
- *Source:* Bryan Cave Leighton Paisner, 'Battery storage revenues and routes to market', https://www.bclplaw.com/en-US/events-insights-news/battery-storage-revenues-and-routes-to-market.html
- *Source:* Sympower, 'BESS revenue models', https://sympower.net/articles/bess-revenue-models
- *Source:* enspired, 'BESS revenue models: toll, floor & fully merchant', https://www.enspired-trading.com/blog/bess-revenue-models-toll-floor-fully-merchant
- *Source:* Gridcog, 'Battery storage offtake and bankability', https://www.gridcog.com/blog/battery-storage-offtake-bankability

#### D-222 · SHOULD · Model a day-ahead financial swap or hedge as a variance-reduction instrument with a quantified effect on the revenue distribution, not as a level change.

- **Why:** Gridcog models a 100 MW four-hour battery trading wholesale and balancing with a financial day-ahead swap on its exports at a fixed £100/MWh: the monthly revenue range narrows from £13,100-£22,500/MW to £14,551-£17,304/MW. The swap compresses both tails.
- **Error if omitted:** P90 revenue is understated for a hedged asset (the hedge lifts the floor) and P50/P10 is overstated (the hedge caps the upside). A model that applies a hedge as a flat uplift gets both wrong.
- **How to implement:** hedged_revenue_t = merchant_revenue_t + hedge_volume_t * (strike - p_DA_t). Recompute the full P10/P50/P90 distribution post-hedge rather than adjusting the mean.
- *Source:* Gridcog, 'Battery storage offtake and bankability', https://www.gridcog.com/blog/battery-storage-offtake-bankability
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB battery energy storage: de-risking returns — offtake, LDES and P462, Q3 2025', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-battery-energy-storage-derisking-returns-revenues-great-britain-offtake-ldes-p462-q3-2025

#### D-223 · SHOULD · Read published ancillary-services shares carefully: distinguish NET (after Dynamic Regulation High import costs) from GROSS, and do not mix the two in one stack.

- **Why:** Modo's published stack reports ancillary on a NET basis, with Dynamic Regulation High imports counted as negative because batteries pay to provide the service. On that basis the ancillary band went below zero in five months of 2024 and averaged only 5% of the 2024 stack, while gross frequency response in 2024 was roughly 21% of the stack. Modo's separate explainer quotes ancillary at roughly 33% of the stack on a gross basis, down from 87% in 2020-2022.
- **Error if omitted:** Silently mixing a gross ancillary share with a net wholesale share double-counts the DRH charging energy, which sits in the wholesale line. The 2024 stack is internally incoherent unless the basis is stated.
- **How to implement:** Carry an explicit basis flag on every revenue benchmark ('net_of_service_import_cost' vs 'gross') and refuse to sum lines with different bases. Record the DRH import cost as its own line so it can be moved between the two conventions.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB BESS revenue stack evolution' chart methodology note, https://modoenergy.github.io/modo-charts/charts/europe/gb/geo-foundation-bess-revenue/chart-e-gb-revenue-stack-evolution.html
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'How does battery energy storage make money?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/how-does-battery-energy-storage-make-money

#### D-224 · SHOULD · Do not apply the published GB fleet benchmark to a 0.1-5 MW behind-the-meter asset without an explicit small-site derate.

- **Why:** Modo's forecast documentation states its GB model targets 10-100 MW utility-scale assets and is calibrated on operational data from sites of 7 MW and above. It sets out specific small-site penalties: integer-MW granularity in frequency response means a 5 MW site reserving 10% for state-of-charge management has 4.5 MW available but can only sell 4 MW, giving roughly a 20% revenue reduction versus roughly 10% for a 50 MW site; smaller sites may lack direct hourly wholesale price exposure due to hedging or fixed-price PPAs; and sites without generation licences incur additional charges.
- **Error if omitted:** Applying an £80k/MW/yr fleet number to a 1 MW BTM asset imports the economics of a transmission-connected, BMU-registered, generation-licensed, ancillary-qualified asset into a project that is none of those things. Direction: revenue overstated, potentially by tens of percent.
- **How to implement:** Apply a size derate chain: granularity_loss(MW) = 1 - floor(MW * (1 - soc_reserve)) / MW with soc_reserve = 0.10; plus a licence/charging derate from the import-charge stack; plus an intraday-access derate reflecting liquidity. Document each factor separately so a reviewer can challenge them individually.
- *Source:* Modo Energy documentation, 'Small BESS sites', https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/apr-2026/uk-europe/gb/small-bess-sites/
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB BESS revenue stack evolution' chart, https://modoenergy.github.io/modo-charts/charts/europe/gb/geo-foundation-bess-revenue/chart-e-gb-revenue-stack-evolution.html

#### D-225 · SHOULD · Derive P50/P90 from a resampled distribution of monthly GB outcomes rather than scaling a single representative year, and disclose the dispersion.

- **Why:** The published monthly GB series is extremely dispersed: £36.6k/MW/yr in January 2024 versus £83.7k/MW/yr in December 2024 versus £88k/MW/yr in January 2025 — a factor of 2.4 within thirteen months. Modo separately quotes low-revenue scenarios around £40k/MW/yr and strong-market scenarios around £70k/MW/yr for the same asset class.
- **Error if omitted:** A P90 built by applying a flat haircut to a P50 point estimate will not reproduce the observed left tail and will not survive lender diligence. Direction: P90 overstated, DSCR overstated, gearing overstated.
- **How to implement:** Block-bootstrap the monthly stack (preserving within-year autocorrelation and the wholesale/balancing anticorrelation visible in the series), simulate N annual paths, and report P10/P50/P90 of annual revenue and of minimum annual DSCR.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB battery energy storage markets: 2024 year in review', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-battery-energy-storage-markets-2024-year-in-review-great-britain-wholesale-balancing-mechanism-frequency-response-reserve
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB battery energy storage: de-risking returns, Q3 2025', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-battery-energy-storage-derisking-returns-revenues-great-britain-offtake-ldes-p462-q3-2025
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB BESS revenue stack evolution' chart, https://modoenergy.github.io/modo-charts/charts/europe/gb/geo-foundation-bess-revenue/chart-e-gb-revenue-stack-evolution.html

#### D-226 · SHOULD · Constrain cycling to the range that actually pays and stop crediting marginal cycles.

- **Why:** Modo measured that for GB assets a second daily cycle raised revenues by 23% for 1-hour and 20% for 2-hour assets, but a third daily cycle added only about 1% to revenues on average. Montel's worked model targets 0.5-1.5 cycles per day.
- **Error if omitted:** An unconstrained MILP will cycle to the physical limit, harvesting spreads that do not exist net of degradation and that the historical record shows do not materialise. Direction: revenue overstated and degradation understated simultaneously.
- **How to implement:** Add sum_t (c_t + d_t) / (2 * E_nom) <= cycles_max_per_day with cycles_max_per_day parameterised at 2 for the base case, and run a sensitivity at 1 and 3 to confirm the 3-cycle case adds ~1%, matching the published elasticity. If it adds materially more, the price series is too volatile.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Intraday and day-ahead power trading: revenue, spread and churn', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/intraday-day-ahead-power-trading-revenue-spread-churn-battery-energy-storage
- *Source:* Montel, 'Battery storage revenues explained', https://montel.energy/resources/blog/battery-storage-revenues-explained

#### D-227 · SHOULD · Apply an availability derate to all market revenue lines, sourced to the observed fleet, and state it separately from technical degradation.

- **Why:** Modo reports GB fleet average availability of 95% from September 2024 onwards, down from 97-98% in the prior year.
- **Error if omitted:** Modelling 100% availability overstates every revenue line by 2-5% and, more importantly, hides the trend. Direction: revenue overstated.
- **How to implement:** Multiply all market revenue by availability_factor (base 0.95), applied after the MILP rather than inside it so the derate is visible and auditable. Do not fold it into round-trip efficiency or degradation.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB battery energy storage: de-risking returns, Q3 2025', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-battery-energy-storage-derisking-returns-revenues-great-britain-offtake-ldes-p462-q3-2025

#### D-228 · SHOULD · Model the VLP's own imbalance exposure on Deviation Volumes as a cost line separate from the site's imbalance.

- **Why:** Axle Energy notes that if a VTP's traded volumes do not match delivered volumes it faces the System Imbalance Price for under- or over-delivery, exactly as any market participant does. Because the delivered volume depends on a baseline that is itself an estimate of counterfactual site demand, the VLP's delivery error is structurally larger than a conventional generator's.
- **Error if omitted:** Booking the full wholesale price on every traded Deviation Volume MWh ignores the settlement risk that arises from the baseline being wrong, which is precisely the risk Ofgem's CBA identified as inherent to baselining heterogeneous consumers.
- **How to implement:** vlp_imbalance_cost = sum_t |traded_DV_t - realised_DV_t| * E[SIP_short - SIP_long], with realised_DV_t derived from the actual metered outturn versus the BL01 baseline, not from the optimiser's intent.
- *Source:* Axle Energy, 'P415: how to wholesale', https://www.axle.energy/blog/p415-how-to-wholesale
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Decision on BSC P415', 6 October 2023, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2023-10/Ofgem%20decision%20P415%20'Facilitating%20Access%20to%20Wholesale%20Markets%20for%20Flexibility%20Dispatched%20by%20VLPs_0.pdf
- *Source:* Elexon, 'System Prices Analysis Report: March 2025', https://assets.elexon.co.uk/wp-content/uploads/sites/11/2025/05/16114739/System-Prices-Analysis-Report-March-2025-Elexon-BSC.pdf

#### D-229 · SHOULD · Do not credit DNO flexibility revenue and avoided-import revenue in the same half-hour without checking the dispatch is compatible, and use published payment ranges rather than assumed ones.

- **Why:** Gridcog reports GB DNOs offering between 3p and 25p per kWh (£30-250/MWh) for demand reduction services, with a modelled 500 kWh/250 kW supermarket battery earning between £475 and £2,300 a year. That is a wide range and a small absolute number relative to the wholesale line.
- **Error if omitted:** Overstating DNO flex as a reliable annuity for a small BTM asset, and double-counting the same discharge half-hour across flex, avoided import and P415 wholesale.
- **How to implement:** Single allocation constraint per half-hour: each MWh of discharge is assigned to exactly one of {onsite_avoided_import, export_wholesale, p415_deviation, dno_flex}, with revenue computed from the assigned stream only.
- *Source:* Gridcog, 'The economics of behind-the-meter battery storage part 2: earning value in energy markets', https://www.gridcog.com/blog/the-economics-of-behind-the-meter-battery-storage-part-2-earning-value-in-energy-markets

#### D-230 · SHOULD · Run the dispatch optimisation at half-hourly resolution matched to GB settlement, and validate the peak-shaving result against the actual metering resolution used for billing. Do not optimise at hourly or daily resolution and scale.

- **Why:** Liu (2021) shows that peak-shaving outcomes differ systematically between 15-minute and 1-hour resolution, and that the sign of the error depends on where the battery sits in the power-energy plane relative to a 'critical power' and 'critical energy' derived from the load profile: in the power-constrained region the difference between the fine-resolution peak and the coarse average peak persists in the optimised net load; in the energy-constrained region averaging changes the apparent peak duration, and the sequence effect causes energy-constrained batteries to UNDERESTIMATE peak shaving. GB DUoS/settlement is half-hourly and MHHS has now migrated over 50% of industry MPANs to site-specific half-hourly reconciliation.
- **Error if omitted:** Sign-ambiguous but always material. Coarse resolution overstates achievable shaving for power-constrained (short-duration) batteries and understates it for energy-constrained ones; either way the sizing recommendation is wrong.
- **How to implement:** Time index t over 17,520 half hours per year. Demand-charge term uses the maximum over half-hourly net import within the billing period, exactly as the DNO does. Report critical power CP and critical energy CE for the site's load and state which region the chosen rating sits in.
- *Source:* Liu, S., 'Battery Operating Regimes: Effect of Temporal Resolution on Peak Shaving by Battery Energy Storage System', MSc thesis, University of California San Diego, 2021, https://escholarship.org/uc/item/2gp2v64w
- *Source:* MHHS Programme, https://www.mhhsprogramme.co.uk/

#### D-231 · SHOULD · Underwrite on a realistically-controlled dispatch, not perfect foresight. Produce at least two dispatch runs — a perfect-foresight upper bound and a forecast-driven / rolling-horizon run — and report the gap explicitly as the control-quality risk.

- **Why:** Carmona et al. (2024) compare a scenario-based stochastic MILP against a rational rule-based benchmark on a real anonymised microgrid and find monthly demand charges differ by approximately 45%; total final cost over one summer was $17,952.05 for the benchmark versus $6,654.59 for the optimised solution, a difference of $11,294.49, of which the coincident-peak component alone was $5,029.93. The value of a BTM battery is therefore substantially a function of control quality and forecast skill, not of the hardware.
- **Error if omitted:** A perfect-foresight model is an upper bound with no probability attached. Presenting it as P50 systematically overstates revenue; the literature's implied magnitude of the control-quality gap is of the same order as the entire equity return.
- **How to implement:** Run (a) full-year deterministic MILP with perfect foresight → 'technical potential'; (b) rolling-horizon MPC with day-ahead load/PV/price forecasts of stated skill, re-solved each day with realised outturn → 'expected'. Report realised/perfect ratio. For demand charges specifically, model the peak-tracking state (running monthly maximum to date) as a state variable so the controller cannot cheat by knowing the month's peak in advance.
- *Source:* Carmona, R., Yang, X., Bhela, S., Zeng, C., arXiv:2409.03942, 2024, Table 3, https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.03942

#### D-232 · SHOULD · Take degradation as the MAXIMUM of calendar and cyclic ageing per day, not the sum, and re-solve dispatch each year against the degraded capacity until end-of-life (typically 80% of original energy capacity) rather than assuming a fixed capacity across the appraisal period.

- **Why:** Seward et al. set daily degradation as D_day = max(D_calendar, D_cyclic) and iterate the operational optimisation each year with the updated E_max, terminating when E_max_y / E_max_0 < 0.8. They also show revenue stacking CHANGES lifetime: their best-NPV scenario (arbitrage + Dynamic Containment) achieved an 11-year lifetime, longer than arbitrage-only scenarios, because DC availability requires little energy throughput.
- **Error if omitted:** Fixed-capacity models overstate late-life revenue and miss the interaction between service mix and lifetime — which is itself a first-order finding: an availability-heavy stack can extend life while raising revenue.
- **How to implement:** Outer loop over years y: solve dispatch with E_max[y]; compute D_total[y] = Σ_days max(D_cal, D_cyc); E_max[y+1] = E_max[y] × (1 − D_total[y]); stop when E_max[y]/E_max[0] < 0.8. Add throughput attributable to frequency-response energy delivery: D_total = D_arbitrage × (1 + dispatch_energy_throughput / arbitrage_energy_throughput).
- *Source:* Seward, W., Qadrdan, M., Jenkins, N., PSCC 2022, §II.C, Fig. 2, Fig. 10, https://pscc-central.epfl.ch/repo/papers/2022/19890.pdf

#### D-233 · SHOULD · Require genuine half-hourly site consumption data for at least 12 months before producing an underwritable number. Where synthetic or profile-scaled data is used, mark the output as indicative and widen the confidence band; do not produce a DSCR or debt sizing from synthetic load.

- **Why:** Peak-shaving and demand-charge value is a function of the peakiness and coincidence of the specific load shape, not of annual consumption. Liu (2021) shows the achievable shave depends on where the asset sits relative to the site's critical power and critical energy, both derived from the observed profile. DUoS red-band value depends on the fraction of consumption falling in a 2.5–3.5 hour weekday window. The Carmona et al. formulation requires the joint distribution of site load with system/utility peaks, which cannot be recovered from an annual total.
- **Error if omitted:** Any number produced from a scaled standard profile is a statement about the profile, not the site. Errors of a factor of two in peak-shaving value are entirely possible because standard profiles are smoothed averages across many sites and systematically understate individual-site peakiness.
- **How to implement:** Hard gate: if hh_data_coverage < 12 months OR data_source == 'synthetic', set output_class = 'indicative' and refuse to emit DSCR/P90/debt-sizing outputs. Compute and report load factor (annual kWh / (peak kW × 8760)), red-band consumption share, and the ratio of monthly peak to monthly mean for each month.
- *Source:* Liu, S., UCSD MSc thesis, 2021, abstract and Chapter 1, https://escholarship.org/uc/item/2gp2v64w
- *Source:* Carmona, R., et al., arXiv:2409.03942, §2.2, https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.03942
- *Source:* MHHS Programme (settlement is moving to site-specific half-hourly reconciliation), https://www.mhhsprogramme.co.uk/

#### D-234 · SHOULD · Model the counterparty structure explicitly and attribute each cash flow to the party that actually receives it. A savings-share, a fixed £/kWh, a capacity fee and an outright purchase allocate volume risk, price risk, performance risk and residual value differently, and produce different project and equity IRRs from identical physical dispatch.

- **Why:** Billimoria and Simshauser (2023) classify storage contracts along four axes — derivative index (net periodic revenue vs a yardstick/spread), contractual form (swap; options: put floor, call cap, collar; grants/service contracts/availability-scaled), periodicity (daily/monthly/quarterly/yearly), and tenor (bilateral and negotiated contracts 'tend to be structured over a longer periods (i.e. 7 to 15 years) to underwrite project financings') — and find that 'preferences for structural simplicity in contract design could introduce incentive incompatibility without careful consideration of the interactions between storage operations and investment'. Third-party shared-savings structures in practice are 'long-term agreement[s], typically ten or more years' in which the provider finances and owns the asset and takes a percentage of savings and revenue.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that computes 'site savings' and calls it project revenue conflates two different cash flows with two different risk profiles. Under a savings share the developer bears the site's load-shape risk (including plant closure and load growth); under a capacity fee the host does. Getting this wrong makes the DSCR meaningless.
- **How to implement:** Parameterise the contract as a cashflow transform applied to the physical result: revenue_developer[t] = f(savings[t], market_revenue[t]) where f is one of: share_pct × (savings + market_revenue); fixed £/kWh_discharged × kWh_dis[t]; fixed capacity fee £/kW/month × P_rated regardless of dispatch; or tolling (fixed fee, offtaker directs dispatch). Run each and report project IRR, equity IRR and DSCR under each. Model the incentive-compatibility test: does the developer's optimal dispatch under f equal the socially/host-optimal dispatch?
- *Source:* Billimoria, F., Simshauser, P., 'Contract design for storage in hybrid electricity markets', EPRG WP 2304 / CWPE 2322, February 2023, §2.1 and Figure 2, https://www.jbs.cam.ac.uk/wp-content/uploads/2023/12/eprg-wp2304.pdf
- *Source:* Peak Power, 'Energy Storage Shared Savings Agreement', https://peakpowerenergy.com/2023/11/15/energy-storage-shared-savings-agreement/
- *Source:* Davis Wright Tremaine LLP, 'Structuring Battery Energy Storage Agreements', 19 February 2026, https://www.dwt.com/blogs/energy--environmental-law-blog/2026/02/energy-store-contractual-regulatory-considerations

#### D-235 · SHOULD · Separate P50 and P90 revenue by running the dispatch over multiple stochastic realisations of the uncertain inputs (site load, PV, prices, frequency-response clearing prices, availability), not by applying a flat percentage haircut to a single deterministic run.

- **Why:** Lender practice sizes debt on a P90 one-year cash flow at a stated DSCR, and merchant revenue is either floored/contracted or subjected to a haircut with lower gearing. The uncertainty in a BTM stack is not a single scalar: it comprises load-shape uncertainty (which drives demand-charge value), price uncertainty (arbitrage), clearing-price uncertainty (frequency response), and the binary availability factor (which can zero a whole settlement period's response payment). These do not combine linearly and a flat haircut cannot represent them.
- **Error if omitted:** A flat haircut produces a P90 that is not a percentile of anything. It will be rejected by a credit committee and, worse, it hides which risk actually drives the tail — which is the information needed to structure a floor or a contract.
- **How to implement:** Monte Carlo over N scenarios of {load, PV, wholesale price, DFR clearing price, availability flag}. For each scenario run the rolling-horizon dispatch and compute annual net revenue. Report the empirical 50th and 10th percentiles of the one-year distribution as P50 and P90. Size debt to min over the schedule of CFADS/(debt service) >= target DSCR on the P90 case, and report LLCR and PLCR alongside. Decompose the P50–P90 gap by variance contribution per input.
- *Source:* Billimoria, F., Simshauser, P., EPRG WP 2304, 2023 (on the 'manifestly random nature and variability of spot revenue streams' making long-tenor financing difficult), https://www.jbs.cam.ac.uk/wp-content/uploads/2023/12/eprg-wp2304.pdf
- *Source:* Carmona, R., et al., arXiv:2409.03942, 2024 (scenario-based stochastic formulation with N_s = 1000 Monte Carlo scenarios), https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.03942
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Response Services – Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026 (binary availability factor), https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download

#### D-236 · SHOULD · Treat merchant frequency-response and wholesale revenue as unbankable at full value: either contract/floor it, or apply lower gearing, and never size senior debt against uncontracted BTM revenue at the same DSCR as contracted savings.

- **Why:** Billimoria and Simshauser (2023) note that ancillary-service markets 'are frequently small and illiquid' and 'ultimately subject to a form of saturation risk', and that 'the manifestly random nature and variability of spot revenue from such markets makes it challenging to secure long-tenor financing'. GB BESS benchmark revenues moved from about £57k/MW/year in July 2025 to £77k/MW/year in October 2025 to £59k in November and £47k in December 2025 — a range of more than 1.6× within six months.
- **Error if omitted:** Over-gearing. A DSCR computed on a single year's merchant revenue is not a covenant a project can hold through a 40% swing in benchmark revenue.
- **How to implement:** Split CFADS into contracted (host savings under an agreed M&V method; Capacity Market agreement; any floor/tolling fee) and merchant (frequency response, BM, wholesale). Size debt on contracted CFADS at the base DSCR; either exclude merchant entirely or include it at a haircut with a separate, higher DSCR. Report both.
- *Source:* Billimoria, F., Simshauser, P., EPRG WP 2304, 2023, §2, https://www.jbs.cam.ac.uk/wp-content/uploads/2023/12/eprg-wp2304.pdf
- *Source:* Modo Energy, GB BESS revenue benchmark series, July–December 2025, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/me-bess-gb-battery-energy-storage-revenues-december-2025-low-demand-christmas

#### D-237 · SHOULD · Model the interaction between service mix and asset lifetime, and re-run the investment case for each candidate mix — do not assume lifetime is independent of dispatch.

- **Why:** Seward et al. found the scenario with the largest NPV and the LONGEST battery lifetime was arbitrage + Dynamic Containment, with an NPV of £7,255.42 and a lifetime of 11 years, versus shorter lifetimes for arbitrage-heavy scenarios. The mechanism is that DC is paid for availability with minimal energy throughput: in their results essentially all DC income was availability payment, since DC has no response energy payment.
- **Error if omitted:** Models that fix lifetime at a nameplate figure cannot see that an availability-heavy stack both raises revenue and extends life, and so systematically prefer the wrong dispatch strategy. Direction: mis-ranks strategies, not simply mis-levels them.
- **How to implement:** For each candidate service mix, run the year-by-year degradation loop to end of life and compute NPV over the resulting (variable) lifetime rather than over a fixed appraisal period. Report lifetime as an OUTPUT of the optimisation, not an input.
- *Source:* Seward, W., Qadrdan, M., Jenkins, N., PSCC 2022, §IV.A.2 and Fig. 10, https://pscc-central.epfl.ch/repo/papers/2022/19890.pdf

#### D-238 · SHOULD · Model site load growth, load decline and site-exit risk over the contract term as an explicit scenario, not as a static profile, because the entire BTM savings case is contingent on the host's load continuing to exist in its current shape.

- **Why:** This is the structural difference between BTM and front-of-meter storage: front-of-meter revenue depends on a market, BTM savings depend on one counterparty's operations. Shared-savings agreements run 'typically ten or more years', over which a C&I host's production, shift patterns, electrification (EV fleets, heat pumps) and even site tenure can change materially. Under a savings-share the developer holds this risk; under a capacity fee the host does.
- **Error if omitted:** The single largest uninsured risk in a BTM project is invisible. A model with a static load profile cannot show that a 20% load reduction can eliminate the demand-charge saving entirely while leaving the debt intact.
- **How to implement:** Scenario set over load: {flat, +2%/yr, −2%/yr, step change at year 5, site closure at year 7}. Also model shape change: electrification adds evening/overnight load that can either help (flattens peaks) or hurt (raises MIC requirement). Report DSCR under each. Add a contractual mitigant flag: minimum-payment floor, change-of-control, or step-in rights.
- *Source:* Peak Power, 'Energy Storage Shared Savings Agreement' (typical ten-year-plus tenor, third-party ownership), https://peakpowerenergy.com/2023/11/15/energy-storage-shared-savings-agreement/
- *Source:* Billimoria, F., Simshauser, P., EPRG WP 2304, 2023 (contract tenor 7–15 years to underwrite project financings), https://www.jbs.cam.ac.uk/wp-content/uploads/2023/12/eprg-wp2304.pdf

#### D-239 · SHOULD · Calibrate the capital cost per kWh to the small-scale C&I segment, not to utility-scale benchmarks, and test whether the case survives realistic small-system pricing.

- **Why:** Seward et al. found that at a capital cost of $177/kWh (approximately £128/kWh), the 2020 average for stationary BSS, several stacked scenarios were NPV-positive; but small-scale domestic/commercial BSS prices ranged from £445/kWh to £1,315/kWh, and their case remained NPV-positive only up to a capital cost of approximately £490/kWh. They concluded that 'despite revenue stacking improving investment viability of BSSs, the cost of small-scale BSSs will limit their mass adoption'.
- **Error if omitted:** Using a utility-scale £/kWh for a 0.1–1 MW BTM system can understate capex by a factor of 3–4 and turn a negative-NPV project positive. This is the most direct route to an unbuildable recommendation.
- **How to implement:** Segment capex by system size band and source the £/kWh from actual quotations for that band. Run a breakeven capex analysis: report the maximum £/kWh at which the project clears the target IRR, and compare it with observed market pricing for the relevant size band. If breakeven capex is below observed pricing, say so explicitly.
- *Source:* Seward, W., Qadrdan, M., Jenkins, N., PSCC 2022, §IV.A.2, https://pscc-central.epfl.ch/repo/papers/2022/19890.pdf

#### D-240 · CONSIDER · Treat the skip rate as a policy-dependent, time-varying scenario input under active regulatory intervention, not a fixed constant. Model at minimum a declining base case, a flat pessimistic case and an Ofgem-parity optimistic case, and re-derive the number at each vintage.

- **Why:** Ofgem's determination directs NESO to achieve 'technological parity' by end of the financial year and to set absolute numerical dispatch targets; NESO committed to a skip-rate reduction target decision in February 2026, 'optimisation within a constraint' from April 2026, and full GC0166 implementation by early November 2026. Measured battery skip rates fell from >90% (2023) to 76% (Aug 2024) to 49% (H1 2025) to 38% (H1 2026), but rose to 35% in June 2026 from a 31% Jan–Jun 2026 average.
- **Error if omitted:** Freezing a 2023 or 2024 skip rate into a 15–25 year underwriting model produces a systematically pessimistic (or, if you freeze a 2026 number, systematically optimistic) revenue curve. The literature shows a ~55 percentage-point move in three years, i.e. roughly £66k/MW/yr of profit swing on the Gale et al. elasticity.
- **How to implement:** s(y) = s_terminal + (s_0 − s_terminal)·exp(−k(y−y_0)), fitted to the published series {2023: 0.93 offer, 2024ytd: 0.83, H1-2025: 0.49, H1-2026: 0.38}. Optimistic terminal: parity with CCGT (Stage-3 CCGT offer skip 55% in 2024 ytd). Pessimistic: hold s at the current observed value flat. Report IRR/DSCR under all three.
- *Source:* Hypercube, 'Ofgem's skip rate determination: a shift for battery storage economics?', 2026, https://wearehypercube.com/ofgem-skip-rate-bess/
- *Source:* NESO, 'Our commitment to improve battery dispatch rates in the Balancing Mechanism', 2024, https://www.neso.energy/news/our-commitment-improve-battery-dispatch-rates-balancing-mechanism
- *Source:* ESS News, 'Lower skip rates for UK battery storage but NESO plans further reform', 14 July 2026, https://www.ess-news.com/2026/07/14/lower-skip-rates-for-uk-battery-storage-but-neso-plans-further-reform/
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'NESO Roadmap 2026', 2026, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-neso-national-energy-system-operator-2026-energy-storage-roadmap-p462-connections-reform-balancing-mechanism

#### D-241 · CONSIDER · Model the risk that NESO restricts storage bid-down inside active transmission constraints, which would remove a material BM bid revenue line for assets in or behind constrained regions.

- **Why:** As of 16 July 2026 NESO's leading option (3A) 'would stop storage from being bid down in the Balancing Mechanism during a constraint', leaving units idle. Modo quantifies the affected flows: £180m of replacement energy between December 2024 and April 2026 (78% attributable to pumped hydro), £30m of net consumer savings from Scottish batteries over the same window, and a £68m constraint-solving value gap between Options 3A and 3AA.
- **Error if omitted:** For any asset whose modelled BM bid revenue depends on charging during constraint periods, this is an existential downside scenario, not a sensitivity. It is live regulatory risk with a 2026 decision point.
- **How to implement:** Scenario switch: constraint_bid_allowed ∈ {yes, 3A_no, 3AA_conditional, 3C_capped}. Under 3A, set Bid_volume = 0 in any period flagged as an active constraint in the asset's zone. Under 3C, cap cumulative daily constraint-window bid energy (Modo's illustrative cap: 100 MWh/day for a 50 MW 2-hour battery, i.e. 1 cycle/day). Report IRR under each.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Constraints retrading: banning storage would cost consumers millions', July 2026, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/banning-storage-balancing-mechanism-cost-consumers-constraints-repetitive-retrading-neso-july-2026

#### D-242 · CONSIDER · Make the CMU registration route (Generating CMU in a Storage Generating Technology Class vs Proven/Unproven DSR CMU) an explicit modelled choice, and apply the correct de-rating factor to each — because for a one-year agreement the DSR route can be de-rated at ~86% while the storage route is de-rated at ~14%.

- **Why:** Rule 2.3.4(b) applies the Average Availability of Non-BSC Balancing Services (AABS) to DSR CMUs, EXCEPT those described in Rule 2.3.4(e). Rule 2.3.4(e) applies the Storage EFC only to Unproven DSR CMUs bidding for or awarded agreements exceeding one Delivery Year with a declared Storage Facility component (Rule 3.10.1(aa)(iv)). Rule 3.9.3(a)(i)(bb) expressly names 'Import reduction achieved by behind-the-meter Storage' as a valid DSR type. So a Proven DSR CMU, or a one-year Unproven DSR CMU, containing a BTM battery, falls under the DSR de-rating factor.
- **Error if omitted:** Published DSR de-rating factors: 0.8558 (2026/27 T-1 and 2029/30 T-4), 0.7987 (2028/29 T-4), 0.7902 (2027/28 T-4). Against a 1h storage EFC of 0.1361 (2026/27 T-1) that is a 6.3x difference in CM revenue for the same physical asset. A model that hard-codes one route silently either understates BTM CM revenue by ~6x or overstates it by ~6x, depending on which route it chose.
- **How to implement:** Enumerate both routes as scenarios. Route A (Generating CMU, Storage GTC): DF = EFC[duration], multi-year agreements available (up to 15 years), Extended Performance Test applies (Rule 13.4A). Route B (Proven DSR CMU): DF = AABS (DSR factor), ONE-YEAR agreements only in practice (T-1 awards are single-year; multi-year triggers Rule 2.3.4(e)), DSR Test required (Rule 13.2), Rule 13.4A EPT does not apply. Underwrite Route B with a heavy haircut and an explicit reperformance/regulatory-change risk flag; do not put Route B revenue into a P50 base case without legal sign-off.
- *Source:* DESNZ/NESO, Capacity Market Rules consolidation 24 July 2026, Rules 2.3.4(b), 2.3.4(e), 3.9.3(a)(i)(bb), 3.10.1(aa)(iv), 13.4A.1
- *Source:* NESO, Capacity Market De-Rating Factors dataset, DSR rows by auction (2026-27 T-1 = 0.8558; 2029-30 T-4 = 0.8558; 2027-28 T-4 = 0.7902)
- *Source:* DESNZ, Capacity Market: Government response - 2023 Phase 2 Consultation and 2024 Rule amendments, July 2024, https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/669a1e7e0808eaf43b50d28e/capacity-market-government-response__1_.pdf

#### D-243 · CONSIDER · Include a downside sensitivity for the announced move of Dynamic Response procurement from four-hour EFA blocks to 30-minute blocks, and for the introduction of an instructed real-time Dynamic Response market alongside the day-ahead auction.

- **Why:** NESO's Future Frequency Response programme states it is aligning Dynamic Regulation's technical requirements with the other Dynamic Response services and moving toward 30-minute procurement blocks. A detailed service design for a real-time, instructed Dx market (limits and prices submitted half-hourly to gate closure, start/cease instructions, settlement on the integral of the time-varying contracted quantity) was published in June 2026.
- **Error if omitted:** Two structural risks are unmodelled. Finer granularity increases competition and typically compresses prices, because assets can now offer only their genuinely spare hours. The real-time market converts part of the availability payment into an option NESO exercises, shifting revenue from firm to contingent — which materially affects the DSCR profile a lender will accept.
- **How to implement:** Scenario switch: procurement_granularity in {4h, 30min} with an associated price-compression factor; realtime_share parameter moving x % of the day-ahead volume into an instructed market with an exercise probability. Also model the settlement formula difference: real-time pays S = (P − (1−K·F)·PF) x integral of Q(t) dt, i.e. pro-rated by actual instructed duration.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Future Frequency Response' page, accessed 11 August 2026 ('Moving toward 30-minute procurement blocks (rather than EFA blocks)'; 'Aligning technical requirements of Dynamic Regulation with other Dynamic Response Services'), https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/frequency-response-services/future-frequency-response
- *Source:* NESO, 'Real-time Dynamic Response Detailed Service Design', v2, June 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/382806/download

#### D-244 · CONSIDER · For a behind-the-meter asset, resolve explicitly how the physical response energy at the customer's supply meter is settled, and do not assume the ABSVD adjustment neutralises the customer's retail exposure.

- **Why:** ABSVD adjusts the imbalance position of the relevant BSC party by reference to the response energy volumes. A BTM battery behind a C&I customer's supply meter changes the site's metered import or export, which is settled under the customer's supply contract with its licensed supplier, not directly under the BSC party's imbalance account. Public NESO documentation does not address this interaction.
- **Error if omitted:** Either a large phantom benefit (assuming low-frequency discharge avoids retail import at the full delivered rate, which would make DCL/DRL far more valuable BTM than in front of the meter) or a large phantom cost. For a C&I site paying £150–250/MWh all-in, mis-assigning ~1,275 MWh/MW/yr of DRL discharge is a six-figure per-MW error in either direction.
- **How to implement:** Make the settlement route an explicit, named model input with at least three cases: (a) asset registered as a separate MPAN / BSC-accredited half-hourly metered site, response energy settled at the BSC party level and not through the customer's tariff; (b) asset inside the customer's MPAN with a supplier-side sleeving arrangement at a stated price; (c) unresolved — response revenue set to zero. Do not let the model default silently.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, paragraph 16 (ABSVD, BSC Section Q), https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Procurement Rules', Version 6, Schedule 1 definition of 'Relevant Metering' ('the BSC accredited half hourly metering registered to each Eligible Asset') and paragraph 6.7 (all energy volumes must be included within Applicable Balancing Services Volume Data as a pre-condition to participation), https://www.neso.energy/document/384601/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Guidance on Baseline and Meter Data Approaches for Dynamic Response', December 2025, https://www.neso.energy/document/375491/download

#### D-245 · CONSIDER · Do not assume Capacity Market and Dynamic Response co-delivery without checking; the explicit carve-out exists for Quick Reserve but not, on the face of the current Response Service Terms, for Response.

- **Why:** QR Service Terms paragraph 17.6 expressly provides that nothing prevents a QR Unit providing Quick Reserve in a window overlapping a Capacity Market contract, and Quick Reserve has been added to the list of Relevant Balancing Services. The Response Service Terms paragraph 12.1 instead lists performance of any third-party agreement 'pursuant to the Capacity Market Rules or otherwise' among the things that must not impair response availability, with no equivalent express carve-out in the text reviewed.
- **Error if omitted:** If the model stacks a Capacity Market agreement on the same MW as a DC/DM/DR contract and the carve-out does not exist, the response contract can be terminated and the £250,000-floor liability engaged during exactly the stress event when the Capacity Market obligation bites. This is a correlated tail risk, not an independent one.
- **How to implement:** Model CM and Response as co-deliverable only under an explicit flag defaulting to false, with a note to verify against the current Response Service Terms and the Capacity Market Rules list of Relevant Balancing Services. Model the stress-event overlap explicitly rather than assuming independence.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Quick Reserve Service Terms', paragraph 17.6, https://www.neso.energy/document/364276/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Quick Reserve Service Summary', Version 5.0, July 2025, 'Co-delivery' section, https://www.neso.energy/document/364336/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services Service Terms', Version 6, paragraphs 12.1–12.2, https://www.neso.energy/document/384606/download

#### D-246 · CONSIDER · Align ancillary decision variables to NESO's 30-minute service windows with linked-window minimum durations, not to 4-hour EFA blocks.

- **Why:** GB service window granularity changed. NESO's Enduring Auction Capability operates on 30-minute service windows, with a 'Linked Service Windows' feature (default 2-hour minimum) allowing sell orders across consecutive 30-minute periods with a consistent volume. A model that assumes 4-hour EFA-block commitment either over-constrains (losing real optionality) or, if it assumes free half-hourly switching without linkage, over-optimises.
- **Error if omitted:** Modelling free half-hourly ancillary switching overstates the ability to interleave frequency response with arbitrage; modelling rigid 4-hour EFA blocks understates it. Either way the ancillary/wholesale split is wrong, and it is the split that drives duration selection in the build decision.
- **How to implement:** Define r_{s,w} on service windows w of 30 minutes; add linkage constraints r_{s,w} = r_{s,w+1} for all w within a linked block of default minimum 2 hours (4 windows). Map each 30-minute window one-to-one onto the settlement period index so the energy-headroom constraints bind at the same granularity.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Enduring Auction Capability (EAC)': 30-minute service windows; Linked Service Windows allowing sell orders across multiple consecutive 30-minute periods with a default minimum duration of 2 hours and consistent volumes; UI redesign and Flexible Service Windows deployed January 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/enduring-auction-capability-eac

#### D-247 · CONSIDER · Model the GB day-ahead ancillary auction as a single co-optimised, pay-as-clear clearing across Dynamic Containment, Dynamic Moderation, Dynamic Regulation, Quick Reserve and Balancing Reserve (plus Slow Reserve from March 2026), settled at 14:00 D-1 — and commit those volumes BEFORE the intraday energy optimisation.

- **Why:** Since 29 October 2025 NESO clears these products together in one simultaneous day-ahead auction, and the Balancing Reserve auction moved from 08:15 to 14:00 to align with it. Any model built on the pre-2025 structure of independent per-product auctions, or that co-optimises ancillary and intraday energy with common hindsight, has the wrong information structure.
- **Error if omitted:** Two distinct errors. (1) Modelling separate auctions ignores substitution between products and will misallocate capacity across the stack. (2) Letting a single MILP choose ancillary volumes and intraday energy simultaneously with full-year price foresight gives the asset information it cannot have at 14:00 D-1 — a pure perfect-foresight artefact that inflates the stacked revenue above anything achievable.
- **How to implement:** Two-stage structure: Stage 1 fixes r_{s,w} for the whole of day D using information available at 14:00 D-1 (forecast prices); Stage 2 optimises P^ch_t, P^dis_t within the residual power and energy envelope left by Stage 1, using intraday/within-day prices. Do not merge the stages. Pay-as-clear implies the asset is a price taker on availability revenue: revenue_s = r_{s,w} * clearing_price_{s,w} * 0.5 h.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Balancing Reserve': changes approved by Ofgem in October 2025; final morning auction 28 October 2025, new co-optimised afternoon auction from 29 October 2025; the Balancing Reserve auction is now co-optimised with the Dynamic Response and Quick Reserve auction, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/balancing-reserve
- *Source:* NESO, 'Enduring Auction Capability (EAC)': 'Response & Reserve @ 14:00' daily auction covering DC, DM, DR, Balancing Reserve and Quick Reserve; pay-as-clear via the N-SIDE matching algorithm; Slow Reserve live March 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/enduring-auction-capability-eac
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Decision to approve amendments to the Terms and Conditions relating to balancing for new Slow Reserve', October 2025 — Slow Reserve as 'a co-optimised service alongside Quick Reserve, Dynamic Response and Balancing Reserve via a single, simultaneous, day-ahead, pay-as-clear auction', https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2025-10/Decision_to_approve_amendments_to_the_Terms_and_Conditions_relating_to_balancing_for_new_Slow_Reserve.pdf

#### D-248 · CONSIDER · Make the skip rate time-of-day dependent, using at minimum a half-hourly or hourly profile, because skip rates are anti-correlated with offer value.

- **Why:** Analysis of NESO's Stage-5 PSA by-technology dataset for 2026 YTD shows battery OFFER skip rates of 26.2% at 15:00 and 27.0% at 14:00 but 49.6% at 20:00 and 49.0% at 21:00 — batteries are most likely to be skipped precisely during the evening peak when offer prices are highest. Bid skip rates show the mirror pattern (50.6% at 16:00 vs 34.9% at 20:00).
- **Error if omitted:** A flat annual skip rate applied to a peak-weighted dispatch profile systematically overstates revenue, because the model captures high evening offer prices at the low daytime skip rate. Directionally this inflates BM revenue; the size of the error scales with how peak-concentrated the modelled dispatch is, and for an evening-peak-only strategy the offer skip rate is understated by up to 23 percentage points.
- **How to implement:** s^offer_{h} and s^bid_{h} for h = 0..47 half-hours, applied as E[V^accepted_{d,h}] = (1 − s_{d,h})·V^in-merit_{d,h}. Seed with the 2026 hourly profile: offers 43.1, 39.9, 43.5, 40.0, 39.8, 39.2, 38.4, 37.6, 34.7, 34.4, 40.3, 39.8, 36.5, 31.2, 27.0, 26.2, 27.7, 30.2, 38.2, 46.9, 49.6, 49.0, 46.1, 45.2 (%, hours 00–23); bids 39.9, 39.4, 40.5, 44.4, 43.6, 38.3, 38.6, 39.2, 43.2, 41.1, 40.7, 38.5, 38.4, 45.7, 46.0, 48.1, 50.6, 44.7, 35.8, 36.8, 34.9, 37.2, 35.7, 36.3.
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Skip Rates', file '5_fuel_type_skip_rate.csv', volume-weighted analysis of BATTERY rows for 2026-01 to 2026-08, retrieved 10 August 2026, https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/skip-rates

#### D-249 · CONSIDER · Apply a route-to-market and unit-size skip penalty appropriate to a small BTM asset reaching the BM as a Secondary BMU (VLP) or Additional Supplier BMU, and treat that penalty as a decaying, not permanent, parameter.

- **Why:** NESO states its systems 'are not well suited to efficiently utilising many smaller BMUs'. Analysis of NESO's Stage-5 PSA data joined to Elexon BM Unit types shows VLP Secondary BMUs (type V) skipped 60.5% in January 2025 versus 46.3% (embedded), 48.0% (transmission) and 45.0% (supplier additional); units below 10 MW registered capacity were skipped 57.2% versus 44.8–47.9% for larger bands. By June 2026 both gaps had largely closed (V 41.2% vs 38.6–40.0%; <10 MW 40.6% vs 35.1–41.6%).
- **Error if omitted:** Using an all-battery-fleet average skip rate for a small VLP-routed BTM asset understates its skip rate by up to 14 percentage points in the early years of the asset's life, overstating early-year BM revenue by roughly 25–35% — precisely the years that dominate DSCR in the first debt-service window.
- **How to implement:** s_{d,h,z,k} = s_{d,h,z} + Δ_k(vintage), where k ∈ {VLP_SBMU, SUPPLIER_ABMU, EMBEDDED, TRANSMISSION} and a size adder for registered capacity < 10 MW. Calibrate Δ_k by year and decay it to zero over the modelled convergence horizon; treat the decay rate itself as a scenario variable (P90 = no convergence).
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Skip Rates', monthly In Merit Post System Action files (January 2025 and June 2026), joined to Elexon BMRS '/reference/bmunits/all' bmUnitType and generationCapacity, analysed 10 August 2026
- *Source:* LCP Delta for NESO, 'BM Skip Rates Phase 2 Report', November 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348241/download

#### D-250 · CONSIDER · Do not model skip probability as decreasing in how deeply in-merit the price is. Conditional on being in-merit, treat offer acceptance probability as approximately price-independent.

- **Why:** Analysis of NESO's Stage-5 PSA data by price band shows battery offer skip rates of 49.2% (£20–50/MWh), 53.0% (£50–80), 45.1% (£80–120), 43.2% (£120–200) and 40.0% (£200–500) in January 2025; and 30.5%, 40.9%, 40.2%, 30.2% across comparable bands in June 2026. There is no monotone improvement from bidding more aggressively once inside the merit stack.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that lets the optimiser 'buy' acceptance by shaving its offer price will converge on a low-price, high-volume strategy that does not exist in reality. It will simultaneously overstate accepted volume and understate realised price, and will produce a dispatch schedule (and hence a cycling and degradation profile) that cannot be delivered.
- **How to implement:** Separate the two stages: P(in-merit | price p) is a decreasing function of p for offers, estimable from the BOD price distribution against the marginal accepted price; P(accepted | in-merit) ≈ s-complement and is treated as independent of p. E[V^accepted] = P(in-merit | p) · (1 − s) · V^declared. Deeply negative bids are the exception and must be handled separately (see the constraint-bid requirement).
- *Source:* NESO Data Portal, 'Skip Rates', file '3_in_merit_data_post_system_actions_skips_01_2025.csv' and '..._06_2026.csv', BATTERY rows at stage 5, banded on average_price_per_MWh, analysed 10 August 2026, https://www.neso.energy/data-portal/skip-rates

#### D-251 · CONSIDER · Model a forward trajectory for skip rate improvement as an explicit, dated, scenario-driven assumption, and stress it to 'no further improvement' for the P90 case.

- **Why:** NESO has a public, dated reform pipeline: OBP national optimisation and constraint optimisation (summer 2025), OBP–NCMS interface (early 2026), National Dispatch Optimiser parallel run (January 2026), GC0166 MDO/MDB/FSoE full implementation by early November 2026, and a stated goal of substantial reduction in avoidable skips by the end of Business Plan 3 (March 2026). Realised improvement so far: battery Stage-5 PSA 49.1% (H1 2025) → 38.0% (H1 2026). But NESO has also stated that 'the low hanging fruit has already been picked'.
- **Error if omitted:** Two-sided. Freezing today's skip rate for 15 years understates lifetime BM revenue if reform lands — Modo estimated OBP improvements alone could raise lifetime battery revenues by up to 20% for two-hour systems in a high case. Assuming continued linear improvement to a low terminal rate overstates it, and does so in a way that concentrates the error in the later years that determine terminal value.
- **How to implement:** s_{d,h,z}(y) = s^terminal + (s^today − s^terminal)·exp(−λ·(y − y0)), with three cases: P10 s^terminal ≈ 20% by 2030; P50 s^terminal ≈ 30%; P90 s^terminal = s^today (no improvement, λ=0). Tie the P10 case to delivery of named milestones (GC0166 full compliance, NDO go-live, constraint optimisation) and make delivery slippage a documented model risk.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Defining, measuring, and addressing skip rates', December 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/348236/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Skip rates', industry information page, accessed August 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/skip-rates
- *Source:* ESS News, 'Lower skip rates for UK battery storage but NESO plans further reform', 14 July 2026, https://www.ess-news.com/2026/07/14/lower-skip-rates-for-uk-battery-storage-but-neso-plans-further-reform/
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Balancing Mechanism: how the Open Balancing Platform could improve battery revenues', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/balancing-mechanism-battery-energy-storage-revenues-forecast-bess-dispatch-skip-rates-open-platform
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'NESO Roadmap 2026: Key dates for battery energy storage stakeholders', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-neso-national-energy-system-operator-2026-energy-storage-roadmap-p462-connections-reform-balancing-mechanism

#### D-252 · CONSIDER · Model the aggregator / optimiser / route-to-market commercial share as an explicit line item between gross BM revenue and project revenue, and disclose the assumption because no reliable public benchmark exists.

- **Why:** A BTM asset cannot access the BM without a VLP or Supplier route, which in practice means an aggregator or optimiser. Legal and market commentary confirms the structures — 'a revenue optimisation service in return for a service fee and/or a portion of revenues generated by the BESS' (BCLP), 'a share of revenue plus a floor or performance guarantee', and tolling. But no consulted source discloses percentage revenue shares or £/MW/year fees for GB. Disclosed GB deals in 2025 were dominated by floors (80% of disclosed volumes) with tolling tenors of 10 and 15 years.
- **Error if omitted:** Gross-to-net revenue error applied to the entire merchant stack. If the model reports gross BM revenue as project revenue, both project IRR and DSCR are overstated by the full share, with no offsetting conservatism anywhere else in the model. Because the number is unsourced, it must be a named, sensitised assumption, not a hard-coded constant.
- **How to implement:** Revenue_project = Revenue_gross × (1 − α) − F, with α = optimiser revenue share and F = fixed fee (£/MW/year). Mark α and F as UNSOURCED, sensitise across a stated range, and require the actual executed optimisation agreement terms as a condition of the final underwriting case. Model floor and tolling alternatives explicitly, since a floor changes the P90 case materially more than the P50.
- *Source:* Bryan Cave Leighton Paisner, 'Battery Storage Revenues and Routes to Market', https://www.bclplaw.com/print/v2/content/1547603/battery-storage-revenues-and-routes-to-market.pdf
- *Source:* Pexapark, 'GB BESS Shift to Tolling as Competition Intensifies', https://pexapark.com/blog/gb-bess-shift-to-tolling-as-competition-intensifies/ (industry blog)
- *Source:* Energy-Storage.news, 'Battery storage optimisation: UK firms look beyond merchant model', https://www.energy-storage.news/uk-bess-battery-storage-optimisation-merchant-contracting-model/ (trade press)

#### D-253 · CONSIDER · Model P462 (removal of subsidies from Bid Prices in the BM) as a dated downside scenario for constraint-driven charging revenue.

- **Why:** P462, raised by NESO in October 2023, would remove the cost of losing subsidies from how subsidised wind prices its bids. Modo report the claimed consumer saving as £518 million by 2030, and that current subsidised wind bids 'sometimes reach -£100/MWh or lower'. Timera describe the mechanism as charging prices rising 'from deeply negative levels to around 0 £/MWh'. NESO's 2026 roadmap places the P462 Assessment Report at February 2026 and the Final Modification Report at April 2026.
- **Error if omitted:** For a constrained-location asset, the model would carry deeply negative bid revenue (being paid to charge) into perpetuity when a live code modification could remove most of it. This is a single-modification, step-change risk that is directly relevant to the locational premium the model may be assuming.
- **How to implement:** Scenario switch from the P462 implementation date: floor the modelled bid price distribution at approximately £0/MWh (removing the −£60 to −£100/MWh tail), and re-run the P50/P90. Treat the pre-P462 negative-bid revenue as non-contracted and non-bankable in the base case if the asset's economics depend on it.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'P462: Could fixing a market distortion cause another?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/p462-gb-balancing-mechanism-reform-wind-battery-energy-storage-cfd-subsidy-pricing-distortion
- *Source:* Timera Energy, 'Key changes are impacting BESS constraint revenue capture', https://timera-energy.com/blog/key-changes-are-impacting-bess-constraint-revenue-capture/
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'NESO Roadmap 2026', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-neso-national-energy-system-operator-2026-energy-storage-roadmap-p462-connections-reform-balancing-mechanism

#### D-254 · CONSIDER · Implement the specific DNO baseline that applies to an I&C battery at the specific metering point, and treat the baseline as a first-class input rather than assuming metered output equals delivered MWh. Confirm the mapping with the DNO in writing before underwriting.

- **Why:** Delivered MW = Metered MW − Baseline MW. DNOs have moved from historic baselines to static technology-based baselines aligned to network planning assumptions. UKPN applies a Zero baseline to Storage across SAOU, LT-SU and DA-SU. NGED maps I&C 'Stored energy' to BL_AsCap (Asset Capacity) for the default DTD/GTU direction and Zero for DTU/GTD; Commercial DSR at UKPN uses Recent History (Y=10 workdays, Y=4 non-workdays); NGED I&C demand uses a Self-Nominated baseline from a 4-week history window.
- **Error if omitted:** Under a Zero baseline, delivered MWh = discharge MWh and revenue is as naively modelled. Under an Asset Capacity baseline the deliverable volume and its sign change entirely; under a Recent History or Self-Nominated baseline, prior utilisation and prior site behaviour reduce the baseline and therefore the payable delta, creating a decaying-revenue effect a static model will miss.
- **How to implement:** `delivered_MW[t] = metered_MW[t] − baseline_MW[t]`, with `baseline_MW` selected by `(dno, technology, metering_point ∈ {DER_level, point_of_connection}, product, direction, season)`. Encode: UKPN Storage → 0. NGED I&C Stored energy → Asset Capacity (DTD/GTU) / Zero (DTU/GTD). NGED I&C demand → Self-Nominated = single-value mean of minute or HH data over the previous 4 weeks, window 15:00–20:00 weekdays for DTD/GTU and 10:00–15:00 for DTU/GTD, submitted by the second-last Tuesday of each month, with prior utilisation events removable. UKPN Commercial DSR → mean of the Y most recent eligible same-day-type days with no utilisation events, resolved to half-hourly periods.
