- *Source:* UK Power Networks, 'Participation Guidance V1.2', October 2024, §6.2 Table 3 and 'Operational Baselines', https://d1lf1oz5vvdb9r.cloudfront.net/app/uploads/2024/10/Participation-Guidance-V1.2.pdf
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'NGED DSO - Baseline Values August 2025 V1.0' (Technology Categories and Baseline Values sheets), 2025, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1122
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'Guidance for Electricity Distribution Flexibility Service Providers', March 2026, Appendix 1 §8.2–8.3, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1121
- *Source:* Scottish and Southern Electricity Networks, 'SSEN's Fixed Reference Baseline', 2025, https://www.ssen.co.uk/globalassets/our-services/flexibility-services-document-library/service-documentation/ssens-baselining-methodology.pdf

#### D-255 · CONSIDER · Model the Capacity Market interaction correctly: a DNO flexibility service is NOT (as of the last published assessment) a Relevant Balancing Service, so simultaneous DSO delivery during a CM Stress Event exposes the CMU to non-delivery penalties.

- **Why:** Cornwall Insight for NGED: 'simultaneous provision of DSO services where the FSP was not exporting at its derated capacity would incur penalties'; 'providing a service that is not covered by Relevant Balancing Services could expose a CM provider to penalties should a CM Stress Event occur'. The report also notes the RBS list is periodically reviewed with a lag, so this must be re-checked against the current CM Rules rather than assumed.
- **Error if omitted:** Under-statement of downside risk. The joint probability is low (CM stress events have been rare) but the loss is the CM termination/penalty exposure, which is a tail event a lender will price. Conversely, assuming DSO services are simply prohibited under a CM agreement would under-state revenue.
- **How to implement:** Add a conflict indicator: `cm_conflict[t] = cm_stress_event[t] AND dso_utilisation[t] AND (export < derated_capacity)`. Model expected CM penalty = P(stress event) × P(DSO call in same period) × penalty exposure, and surface it as a named risk in the underwriting pack. Re-verify against the current Capacity Market Rules RBS list before every underwriting run.
- *Source:* Cornwall Insight for National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'Revenue Stacking for Flexibility', December 2023, §9.3 and Figure 9, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1150
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'Unlocking Value Through Revenue Stacking: National Grid DSO's Approach', March 2026, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1198

#### D-256 · CONSIDER · Represent the stochastic-availability character of the market in the procurement/bidding module rather than assuming deterministic delivery, in line with the academic treatment of local flexibility markets.

- **Why:** The academic literature on local flexibility markets treats FSP availability as inherently uncertain and models DSO procurement as a chance-constrained selection problem: 'distribution system operators will need new mechanisms to structure flexibility procurement, enabling them to manage the risks associated with uncertain resources effectively.' The critical review of European LFMs identifies lack of standardised products, regulatory fragmentation and inadequate automation as the dominant barriers — all of which manifest as availability risk to the provider.
- **Error if omitted:** The model treats the DNO as a passive price-taker for whatever volume is offered, missing that the DNO deliberately over-procures or rejects on reliability grounds, and missing the confidence-level trade-off that determines how much of a zone's requirement a single provider can win.
- **How to implement:** Model the DSO's selection as a chance-constrained knapsack over offers with uncertain delivery: maximise procured reliability subject to `P(Σ delivered ≥ requirement) ≥ 1 − ε`; from the provider's side, this means the probability of award increases with demonstrated reliability and decreases with the DSO's confidence target. Use it to justify the performance-score feedback loop above.
- *Source:* C. Essayeh, I. Savelli, T. Morstyn, 'Optimising flexibility procurement in local flexibility markets under stochastic availability of participants', Sustainable Energy, Grids and Networks, 2025, https://doi.org/10.1016/j.segan.2025.102061
- *Source:* A. Scrocca, E. Daccò, D. Andreotti, G. Rancilio, F. Bovera, D. Falabretti, M. Delfanti, 'Local flexibility markets in Europe: A critical review of market designs, operational maturity and stakeholder perspectives', Renewable and Sustainable Energy Reviews, 2025, https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rser.2025.116434
- *Source:* 'How to Procure Flexibility Services within the Electricity Distribution System: Lessons from an International Review of Innovation Projects', Energies 14(15):4475, 2021, https://doi.org/10.3390/en14154475

#### D-257 · CONSIDER · Model generation-constrained (curtailment-avoidance) services as a separate, growing opportunity where the site has co-located solar, distinct from demand-constraint services.

- **Why:** NGED has launched a 'FlexUp' service 'aimed at reducing curtailment during periods of high generation output', procured using an existing standard product with no exclusivity clause. UKPN's day-ahead launch zones include 'Gen↓; Dem↑' zones with maximum utilisation prices up to £871/MWh. SSEN's largest single dispatch category in 2025/26 was 'Logie Pert Sustain generation curtailment'.
- **Error if omitted:** A co-located solar + battery site in a generation-constrained zone has a genuine DTU revenue line (paid to charge) that a demand-only model books at zero, and a curtailment risk that a model without generation constraints ignores.
- **How to implement:** Add `zone_constraint_type ∈ {demand, generation, mixed}` from the DNO data (NGED 'Constraint Trigger' field; UKPN 'Zone direction'). For generation-constrained zones, the battery's revenue-earning action is charging during high-generation periods; settle against the DTU/GTD baseline. Note that a postcode may sit in more than one zone with different constraint types (NGED flags this as 'Mixed').
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'Unlocking Value Through Revenue Stacking', March 2026, item 5 (FlexUp), https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1198
- *Source:* UK Power Networks, 'Appendix 3 – Day Ahead – Competition Data v1.1', April 2024, 'DA Launch Zones_Revenue Ranges', https://d1lf1oz5vvdb9r.cloudfront.net/app/uploads/2024/04/Appendix-3-_-Day-Ahead-_-Competition-Data-v1.1-1.xlsx
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, 'NGED DSO - Revenue Estimator September 2025 V2.0', Overview notes ('Mixed'), 2025, https://www.flexiblepower.co.uk/downloads/1176
- *Source:* Scottish and Southern Electricity Networks, 'SLC31E — SSEN 2025-26 Flexibility Services Procurement Report', April 2026, §2.3, https://www.ssen.co.uk/globalassets/our-services/flexibility-services-document-library/slc31e-reports--statements/slc31e--ssen-2025-26-flexibility-services-procurement-report.pdf

#### D-258 · CONSIDER · Treat imbalance as an asymmetric COST by default, using the published long/short price split, and only model it as revenue if the underwriting explicitly sanctions a NIV-chasing strategy — which must then carry a regulatory-change reserve.

- **Why:** Elexon's System Prices Analysis Report for March 2025 shows the price a party receives when the system is long (mean £63.29/MWh) is far below what it pays when short (mean £119.83/MWh) — a £56.54/MWh systematic penalty for landing on the wrong side. The system was long in 56% of settlement periods in March 2025 and 53% per month over the prior 12 months. Separately, DESNZ's REMA Summer Update names NIV chasing explicitly as behaviour the balancing and settlement reforms are intended to suppress.
- **Error if omitted:** Two failure modes. Modelling imbalance as symmetric understates the cost of forecast error at a BTM site (whose load is the largest forecast error source). Modelling imbalance as a deliberate revenue line capitalises a strategy that the regulator has said it intends to close.
- **How to implement:** Split the cash-out into two indexed series, SIP_long_t and SIP_short_t, drawn from the empirical distributions above, and apply the applicable one to the sign of the position. Add an imbalance cost line = sum_t |forecast_error_t| * E[SIP_short - SIP_long] as a haircut on gross trading revenue.
- *Source:* Elexon, 'System Prices Analysis Report: March 2025', https://assets.elexon.co.uk/wp-content/uploads/sites/11/2025/05/16114739/System-Prices-Analysis-Report-March-2025-Elexon-BSC.pdf
- *Source:* DESNZ, 'Review of Electricity Market Arrangements (REMA) Summer Update', 10 July 2025, https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/686f71412557debd867cbeff/review-of-electricity-market-arrangements-rema-summer-update-2025.pdf

#### D-259 · CONSIDER · Model the gate closure structure: BM gate closure is one hour before the settlement period, while wholesale trading currently continues right up to delivery. Flag the REMA proposal to re-align them as a modelled downside scenario.

- **Why:** DESNZ states the two deadlines used to be the same but a 2017 rule change allowed trading to continue until delivery while gate closure remained an hour earlier, and that the government is considering realigning them, requiring Physical Notifications to match traded positions, and lowering the mandatory BM participation threshold.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that assumes post-gate-closure wholesale optimisation persists for the whole asset life is underwriting a rule that a government policy document has said it intends to change. For a BTM asset, the compensating upside — a lower mandatory BM threshold bringing small batteries into the BM — should be modelled as a separate scenario, not netted off.
- **How to implement:** Parameterise trading_deadline_offset (currently 0 minutes before delivery, downside scenario 60 minutes) and pn_must_match_trades (boolean). In the downside case, remove intraday trades in the final hour and force the PN variable equal to the net traded position.
- *Source:* DESNZ, 'Review of Electricity Market Arrangements (REMA) Summer Update', 10 July 2025, pp.13-14, https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/686f71412557debd867cbeff/review-of-electricity-market-arrangements-rema-summer-update-2025.pdf
- *Source:* Elexon, 'Gate Closure' glossary entry, https://www.elexon.co.uk/bsc/glossary/gate-closure/

#### D-260 · CONSIDER · Do not assume P415 adds material value at every site. Model its incremental value explicitly against the counterfactual tariff, and expect it to shrink where the site already faces pass-through/half-hourly price signals.

- **Why:** Gridcog/Orkestra's published case study found P415 added roughly 3% to overall project value and roughly 21% to the battery component's value, and explicitly notes that where sites are on pass-through tariffs exposing demand charges, P415's marginal value DECREASES because the battery is already optimising against those signals, leaving less deviation to trade.
- **Error if omitted:** Uniformly crediting a P415 uplift across a portfolio double-counts value at pass-through sites, where the same half-hours are already being monetised through avoided import cost.
- **How to implement:** Compute NPV_with_P415 - NPV_counterfactual where the counterfactual is the site's actual supply contract. Assert the uplift is not simultaneously claimed in the avoided-import-cost line for the same settlement period (mutual exclusivity constraint on each half-hour's discharge allocation).
- *Source:* Orkestra, 'P415 and batteries', https://www.orkestra.energy/post/p415-and-batteries
- *Source:* Gridcog, 'The economics of behind-the-meter battery storage part 2: earning value in energy markets', https://www.gridcog.com/blog/the-economics-of-behind-the-meter-battery-storage-part-2-earning-value-in-energy-markets

#### D-261 · CONSIDER · Record and carry the disagreement between forecasters rather than picking one curve. Run at least two independent revenue curves through the model.

- **Why:** Cornwall Insight forecast (28 October 2024) £96/kW for a 2-hour GB battery in 2025 and £108/kW in 2026. Modo's realised ME BESS GB 2-hour index averaged approximately £80k/MW/year across 2025, and £70k/MW/year annualised over January-April 2026. That is a roughly 17-35% forecast overshoot against outturn on the very next year.
- **Error if omitted:** An underwriting that runs a single third-party curve inherits that forecaster's bias with no visibility. Given the observed gap, the choice of curve alone can move equity IRR by several percentage points.
- **How to implement:** Structure the revenue input as a named, dated, versioned curve object; run the DSCR and IRR outputs under at least two curves plus a historical-resample case; report the spread of outcomes, not a single IRR.
- *Source:* Cornwall Insight via Solar Power Portal, 'Cornwall Insight forecasts battery revenue rebound in 2026', 28 October 2024, https://www.solarpowerportal.co.uk/battery-storage/cornwall-insight-forecasts-battery-revenue-rebound-in-2026
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB BESS revenue stack evolution' chart, https://modoenergy.github.io/modo-charts/charts/europe/gb/geo-foundation-bess-revenue/chart-e-gb-revenue-stack-evolution.html
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'How does battery energy storage make money?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/how-does-battery-energy-storage-make-money

#### D-262 · CONSIDER · Maintain an explicit regulatory-change register covering the REMA balancing and settlement package and P462, and run the material items as downside scenarios on the BM and imbalance lines.

- **Why:** DESNZ's REMA Summer Update (10 July 2025) confirms a single national GB wholesale market and rejects zonal pricing, but sets out an active reform list that directly targets the revenue lines a battery relies on: lowering the mandatory BM participation threshold; realigning the market trading deadline with gate closure; requiring Physical Notifications to match traded positions; unit-level bidding; and continued consideration of shortening the imbalance settlement period to 15 or 5 minutes. It also confirms support for P462 (removing subsidy costs from BM bid prices), whose cost-benefit analysis started February 2025 with Ofgem receiving a final report in 2026. Dual-imbalance pricing and quasi-pay-as-clear BM were discounted.
- **Error if omitted:** A 15-year underwriting on the current rule set is an unhedged regulatory position. Several of these changes are directionally negative for post-gate-closure trading and NIV chasing, and one (lower BM threshold) is materially positive for a sub-5 MW BTM asset. Netting them off without modelling is not analysis.
- **How to implement:** Scenario flags: {trading_deadline_realigned, pn_matches_trades, bm_threshold_lowered_to_X_MW, settlement_period_minutes in {30,15,5}, p462_implemented}. Report IRR and minimum DSCR under each, with a probability-weighted case, and put the register in the underwriting pack.
- *Source:* DESNZ, 'Review of Electricity Market Arrangements (REMA) Summer Update', 10 July 2025, https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/686f71412557debd867cbeff/review-of-electricity-market-arrangements-rema-summer-update-2025.pdf
- *Source:* Norton Rose Fulbright, 'REMA summer update: no to zonal pricing, yes to reformed national pricing', https://www.nortonrosefulbright.com/en/knowledge/publications/4399413b/rema-summer-update-no-to-zonal-pricing-yes-to-reformed-national-pricing
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB battery energy storage: de-risking returns, Q3 2025', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-battery-energy-storage-derisking-returns-revenues-great-britain-offtake-ldes-p462-q3-2025

#### D-263 · CONSIDER · Model battery degradation with a cycle-depth-dependent formulation (rainflow or a piecewise-linear approximation of it), not a flat £/MWh throughput cost — or, if a throughput cost is used, calibrate it to the depth distribution the optimiser actually produces and state that as a limitation.

- **Why:** Shi, Xu, Tan, Kirschen and Zhang (2017) show cycle aging is a cumulative fatigue process where life loss ΔL = Σ Φ(v_i)/2 + Σ Φ(w_i)/2 over rainflow-extracted half cycles, with Φ(u) = α·u^β convex, and prove the resulting cost is convex and hence tractable in optimisation. They note an NMC battery has ten times more degradation when operated near 100% cycle depth of discharge compared with 10% DoD for the SAME amount of charged energy or energy throughput, and that a common simplifying assumption in the literature is a constant 2 $/MWh based on energy throughput.
- **Error if omitted:** Throughput-only degradation systematically UNDER-prices deep cycles. Peak shaving in GB is inherently deep and infrequent (one long red-band discharge per weekday), so a throughput model will over-dispatch, over-state life, and understate replacement/augmentation cost. Direction: overstates NPV.
- **How to implement:** f(c,d) = ΔL(c,d) × E × B where B is cell cost in £/kWh and E is capacity in kWh; ΔL from rainflow on the SoC trajectory. A tractable MILP alternative is Seward et al.'s cycle-count polynomial: N_t = 12198·DoD³ + 34954·DoD² − 97517·DoD + 52895, D_cyc = 0.5·|1/N_t − 1/N_{t−1}|, D_day = max(D_calendar, D_cyclic), D_calendar = 20%/(365×20) = 0.00274%/day for a 20-year shelf life to 80% capacity.
- *Source:* Shi, Y., Xu, B., Tan, Y., Kirschen, D., Zhang, B., arXiv:1709.05715v2, 2017, §§II–III, https://arxiv.org/abs/1709.05715
- *Source:* Seward, W., Qadrdan, M., Jenkins, N., PSCC 2022, eqns (13)–(19), https://pscc-central.epfl.ch/repo/papers/2022/19890.pdf

#### D-264 · CONSIDER · Report a stacking discount explicitly: the ratio of jointly-optimised value to the sum of independently-optimised standalone values, for the actual site and asset. Do not import a generic percentage from the literature.

- **Why:** The peer-reviewed GB literature demonstrates the mechanism but does not publish a transferable discount. Seward et al. evaluate five mutually-exclusive operating strategies (arbitrage only; arbitrage + FFR-high; arbitrage + FFR-low; arbitrage + FFR high and low; arbitrage + DC) rather than decomposing a joint stack, precisely because DC could not be stacked with other frequency response. Billimoria and Simshauser (2023) state that 'given the complexity of storage operations and multiple streams of value, standard contract forms are yet to emerge' and characterise value stacking as 'the simultaneous or sequential participation in multiple ancillary service markets as well as the arbitrage in spot electricity markets' — a definition that makes the discount site- and asset-specific.
- **Error if omitted:** Either an unjustified generic haircut (which lenders will challenge) or no haircut at all (which is indefensible). Both fail underwriting scrutiny.
- **How to implement:** For each service s: solve max value with only s enabled → V_s. Solve with all enabled → V_joint. Report D = 1 − V_joint / Σ_s V_s, and the marginal contribution of each service M_s = V_joint − V_joint(without s). Note Σ_s M_s <= V_joint for substitutes; use Shapley or leave-one-out and state which.
- *Source:* Seward, W., Qadrdan, M., Jenkins, N., Electric Power Systems Research 211 (2022) 108292, https://doi.org/10.1016/j.epsr.2022.108292
- *Source:* Billimoria, F., Simshauser, P., 'Contract design for storage in hybrid electricity markets', EPRG Working Paper 2304 / Cambridge Working Paper in Economics 2322, February 2023, https://www.jbs.cam.ac.uk/wp-content/uploads/2023/12/eprg-wp2304.pdf


### E. Revenue stacking, conflicts and mutual exclusivity

#### E-01 · MUST · Couple ancillary/reserve commitment to BOTH a power headroom constraint and a stored-energy sustainability constraint, per direction and per settlement period.

- **Why:** A battery that has sold 1 MW of Dynamic Containment high and low must physically hold both the power headroom and the energy to deliver for the product's sustain duration. This is the constraint most often omitted, and its omission is the classic mechanism by which stacked-revenue models double-sell the same MW.
- **Error if omitted:** Contracted ancillary volumes and energy-market dispatch are added together without a shared capacity budget, so the model sells the same megawatt twice. Xu et al. give the worked example: a 36 MW / 3 MWh BES unconstrained can dispatch 36 MW into the next 5-minute market, but if it is scheduled for 1 MW of reserve under a one-hour sustainability requirement it can only provide 2 MW of baseline generation. Omitting this inflates stacked revenue by a multiple, not a percentage.
- **How to implement:** POWER headroom (Engels et al. eq. 1e): P_min + r <= P_ctrl_t <= P_max - r, where r is contracted reserve capacity and P_ctrl_t the baseline energy dispatch. ENERGY sustainability (Xu et al. eq. 23 form): S * (g^q_t + q_t - d^q_t) <= sum_j ebar_j, where S is the sustainability duration. For GB, apply per product with the NESO durations: Dynamic Containment 15 minutes, Dynamic Moderation 30 minutes, Dynamic Regulation up to 60 minutes, Static FFR 30 minutes, Quick Reserve activation <= 5 minutes with recovery <= 3 minutes. So: E_t - E_min >= sum_s (D_s/60) * r^down_{s,t} (footroom energy to discharge) and E_max - E_t >= sum_s (D_s/60) * r^up_{s,t} (headroom energy to absorb), in MWh, with D_s the sustain duration in minutes.
- *Source:* Engels, J., Claessens, B., Deconinck, G., IEEE Transactions on Smart Grid 10(2):1971-1981, 2019, eq. (1e), arXiv:1710.08331
- *Source:* Xu, B. et al., 2018, eqs (17)-(23) and the worked 36 MW / 3 MWh example, IEEE TPWRS 33(2):2248-2259
- *Source:* NESO, 'Frequency Response Services' product table (Dynamic Regulation 10 s / up to 60 min; Dynamic Moderation 1 s / up to 30 min; Dynamic Containment 1 s / up to 15 min; Static FFR 30 s / 30 min), accessed 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/frequency-response-services
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Containment' — 0.5 s maximum between frequency change and response initiation, full delivery within 1 s, 15 minutes sustained delivery for energy-limited providers, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/frequency-response-services/dynamic-containment
- *Source:* NESO, 'Quick Reserve' — full delivery 1 minute from instruction, activation period not exceeding 5 minutes, recovery period maximum 3 minutes, Phase 1 live 19 November 2024, Phase 2 (non-BM units) 2 September 2025, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/quick-reserve

#### E-02 · MUST · Impose one shared rated-power budget across all simultaneously contracted services and the energy dispatch, in each direction, in every settlement period.

- **Why:** Revenue stacking is only legitimate where products are time-separated or capacity-separated. A single inverter cannot deliver more than its rating.
- **Error if omitted:** Straight-line double counting of the asset's power capacity across DC, DM, DR, Quick Reserve, Balancing Reserve and wholesale. This is the single most expensive omission in BESS underwriting models because it compounds across every stream.
- **How to implement:** For every t: P^dis_t + sum_s r^up_{s,t} <= Pbar_dis  and  P^ch_t + sum_s r^down_{s,t} <= Pbar_ch, where r^up/r^down are the contracted MW in each service that would require discharge/charge respectively. Do NOT allow a service contracted in one direction to consume the other direction's budget.
- *Source:* Xu, B. et al., 2018, eqs (17)-(21), IEEE TPWRS 33(2):2248-2259
- *Source:* Engels, J. et al., 2019, eq. (1e) and Section VI-B (the combined self-consumption + frequency control case explicitly divides the battery into two 'virtual batteries' with separate energy and power capacities), arXiv:1710.08331

#### E-03 · MUST · Separate 'charging from surplus on-site PV' from 'charging from the grid' in the energy balance — only the former avoids the full delivered stack.

- **Why:** PV consumed on site never crosses the supply meter, so it attracts no DUoS unit charge, no BSUoS, no CCL, no RO/FiT/CfD/RAB/AAHEDC. Storing otherwise-exported PV and discharging it later therefore avoids the FULL import stack, not just the DUoS differential — the one case in BTM where the headline retail-price saving is real. Conversely, exported PV earns only an export tariff (SEG or PPA), and SEG covers generation up to 5 MW from qualifying technologies with rates set by each supplier and required only to be above zero.
- **Error if omitted:** Merging the two charging sources into one variable either (a) applies the full import stack to PV-charged energy, understating the co-located case by up to ~7–8 p/kWh, or (b) applies zero cost to grid-charged energy, the far more dangerous error. It also prevents correct treatment of the export/import mutual exclusion.
- **How to implement:** Split charging: c[t] = c_grid[t] + c_pv[t], with c_pv[t] ≤ max(pv[t] − L[t], 0) and the import balance imp[t] = max(L[t] + c_grid[t] − d[t] − min(pv[t], L[t]), 0). Cost c_pv at the foregone export price (SEG/PPA p/kWh), cost c_grid at the full delivered stack p_imp[t]. Enforce imp[t] · exp[t] = 0 with a binary where an export MPAN exists.
- *Source:* Ofgem, Smart Export Guarantee (SEG): all licensed suppliers must declare SEG status annually; eligible technologies up to 5 MW (50 kW micro-CHP); 'SEG tariff rates must always be above zero'; payments calculated from export meter readings, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/environmental-and-social-schemes/smart-export-guarantee-seg
- *Source:* HMRC, Climate Change Levy rates (CCL applies to supplies of taxable commodities; self-consumed on-site generation is not such a supply), https://www.gov.uk/guidance/climate-change-levy-rates

#### E-04 · MUST · If the asset exports, model the distribution-connected export benefits explicitly and separately: DUoS generation credits, the TNUoS Embedded Export Tariff, and transmission loss multipliers — and do not include benefits that were abolished.

- **Why:** The surviving embedded benefits for a distribution-connected exporting asset in 2026/27 are: (i) DUoS generation unit credits (NGED East Midlands: LV Generation Site Specific −7.711/−0.998/−0.060 p/kWh red/amber/green; HV Generation Site Specific −3.958/−0.431/−0.021 p/kWh, with a 61.51 p/day fixed charge); (ii) the TNUoS Embedded Export Tariff, paid to embedded generators under 100 MW on metered Triad export volumes, floored at £0/kW, ranging in 2026/27 from £0.153/kW (N Wales & Mersey) to £17.274/kW (South Western), average £3.05/kW on a forecast 6.71 GW embedded export volume and £20.44m total credit; (iii) avoided transmission losses. The abolished ones are the TNUoS demand residual embedded benefit (gone April 2022) and the BSUoS embedded benefit (gone April 2023).
- **Error if omitted:** Omitting DUoS generation credits understates an exporting HV asset by up to 3.958 p/kWh in red periods. Including the pre-2022 'avoided TNUoS demand' embedded benefit at ~£45/kW, or a BSUoS embedded benefit, adds tens of thousands of pounds per MW per year that no longer exist. Also note that most BTM sites have no export MPAN at all, in which case ALL of these are zero.
- **How to implement:** export_credit[t] = duos_gen_rate[band(t)] (negative p/kWh, i.e. a credit) × exp[t] + seg_or_ppa_price[t] × exp[t]; annual EET = eet_gbp_per_kW[zone] × mean(exported_kW over the 3 Triads), floored at 0. Gate the entire block on `has_export_mpan`. Assert tnuos_demand_residual_embedded_benefit == 0 and bsuos_embedded_benefit == 0.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, Annex 1 (generation tariffs), https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677
- *Source:* NESO, January 2026 Final TNUoS Tariffs for 2026/27, Table 13 and Appendix A ('The final zonal EET is floored at £0/kW ... applied to the metered Triad volumes of embedded exports'; '<100 MW CVA registered'), https://www.neso.energy/document/376336/download
- *Source:* NESO, BSUoS charges (CMP308: BSUoS recovered solely from final demand from 1 April 2023), https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/charging/balancing-services-use-system-bsuos-charges

#### E-05 · MUST · Model Capacity Market agreements with correct derating, term, indexation and termination-fee exposure, and treat CM revenue as contracted only to the extent the agreement is actually secured.

- **Why:** CM is the most reliably contracted GB storage revenue line and DNV notes it 'directly supports debt structuring'. Gresham House had 901 MW under T-4 agreements at the end of 2025, CPI-linked, with the vast majority having at least ten years remaining. In the Econergy Immingham financing, CM accounted for approximately 11% of contracted revenues.
- **Error if omitted:** Including unsecured future CM revenue in the contracted bucket inflates the contracted-DSCR test and therefore the sizeable debt. Conversely, omitting CPI indexation understates the real value of a 15-year agreement by roughly 15-20% over its life.
- **How to implement:** CMRevenue_y = ClearingPrice x DeratedCapacity x CPIIndex_y, where DeratedCapacity = P_MW x DeratingFactor(duration, year). Only classify CM revenue as contracted for periods covered by an awarded agreement; model future auctions as merchant with a probability of clearing. Include termination fees and the obligation to deliver in stress events. Note that derating factors for storage are duration-dependent and have been revised over time, so the factor must be a dated, sourced input rather than a constant.
- *Source:* DNV, 'Financing the energy transition: building bankable battery projects' — CM contracts provide long-term availability-based income that directly supports debt structuring, https://www.dnv.com/article/financing-energy-transition-building-bankable-battery-projects/
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — 901MW under T-4 agreements, CPI-linked, majority with 10+ years remaining, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf
- *Source:* Solar Power Portal, 'Santander provides debt finance for Econergy, Brockwell BESS', 2026 — CM approximately 11% of contracted revenues, https://www.solarpowerportal.co.uk/battery-storage/santander-provides-debt-finance-for-econergy-brockwell-energy-bess

#### E-06 · MUST · Add the NESO Demand Flexibility Service (DFS) as a first-class revenue line available at and above 0.1 MW, with its own registration gate, zonal allocation, baselining choice, submission mechanics and settlement formula — and encode its exclusivity rules correctly. DFS is the only direct NESO revenue route open to a sub-1 MW BTM asset. Model: Registered Unit Capacity in [0.1, 100] MW to one decimal place; allocation of meter points to exactly one DFS Zone (12 zones); Upwards Flexibility (Demand Turn Up / Generation Turn Down) and Downwards Flexibility (Demand Turn Down / Generation Turn Up) as separate directional products; the choice between the P376 BL01 historic baseline and the Self-Nominated Baselining Methodology (available only to Industrial & Commercial and Generation Asset meter points, never Domestic); non-curtailable submissions; and the piecewise performance factor K. Do NOT model 'Primacy' as a general stacking-conflict resolver — it is a DNO conflict-exclusion process.

- **Why:** Every other NESO route is gated at 1 MW: DC/DM/DR require 'a minimum response capacity of 1 MW ... per response unit' with 'whole MWs' orders, and BM participation requires a BM Unit. A checklist without DFS therefore concludes that a 0.1-1 MW BTM battery earns zero NESO revenue, which is false as of the 9 April 2026 service expansion. Equally important is what the rules actually say about stacking, because getting this wrong in the other direction is just as expensive: DFS Procurement Rules 4.7.6 requires that a DFS Unit Meter Point 'shall not form part of a BM Unit except a Supplier Base BM Unit', and 4.7.9 bars registration to provide 'any Balancing Service to NESO or any DNO Flexibility Service ... including participation in an Active Network Management Scheme where so prohibited by the DFS Stacking List'. Capacity Market co-existence is permitted only 'unless otherwise stated ... in the DFS Stacking List' (4.7.8(a)). So DFS is conditionally mutually exclusive with the rest of the stack, and the governing document is the DFS Stacking List, not Primacy.
- **Error if omitted:** Omitting DFS entirely zeroes the only NESO revenue line for sub-1 MW assets and forces the model to conclude that small BTM batteries must sell through an aggregator at an undisclosed margin — a conclusion that is now wrong and that will misprice the entire small-asset segment. Conversely, adding DFS while assuming it stacks freely with DC/DM/DR or BM double-counts capacity that the rules forbid being registered twice, and will fail at registration. Misreading Primacy as a stacking rule leads the model to invent a conflict-resolution mechanism while missing the real one (the Stacking List) and the real DNO exclusion risk (Primacy Exclusion Reports, which can remove a meter point from participation for defined periods at NESO's sole discretion).
- **How to implement:** Registration layer (gates, evaluated before any DFS revenue is booked): (1) Registered Unit Capacity R in [0.1, 100] MW, rounded to 1 dp (para 4.2); (2) all Unit Meter Points in a DFS Unit must be in the same DFS Zone (4.3.2(k)); (3) I&C Boundary Meters must be Half-Hourly Settled unless Profile Class 3 or 4 (4.7.2); (4) metering must meet Code of Practice 11 accuracy class (4.7.7); (5) the meter point must not form part of a BM Unit other than a Supplier Base BMU (4.7.6) — encode as a binary that is mutually exclusive with BM participation; (6) check the current DFS Stacking List for CM and Balancing Service co-existence (4.7.8, 4.7.9); (7) apply a Pre-Participation Waiting Period during which the meter point is deemed Primacy Excluded (4.10). Dispatch layer: for each Service Requirement (Event ID, DFS Service Window, Zonal Cap per zone which 'may be zero (0) MW', direction, DFS Submission Time), submit per Settlement Period a Utilisation Price in integer pounds and pence (£/MWh) and an offered Service Volume in MW to 1 dp, >= 0.1 MW and <= min(Maximum Submission Size, Registered Unit Capacity), excluding any Primacy Excluded meter points (11.5.6, 11.5.7). Submissions are NOT curtailable (12.3) — model acceptance as binary per submission. Clearing: rank by price low to high, accept until the zonal requirement is met without exceeding Zonal Caps (12.2); ties broken by closest volume then by a last-resort rank factor (12.4, 12.5). Settlement: S_ie = 0.5 * P_ie * Q_ie * K_ie, with RV_ie = 100 * V_ie / (Q_ie * 0.5) and K_ie = 1.2 if RV>=120; 0.01*RV if 50<=RV<120; 0.01*(2*RV - 50) if 25<=RV<50; 0 if 0<=RV<25; 0.01*RV if -100<=RV<0; -1 if RV<-100. Note K can be NEGATIVE — model the penalty branch, do not clip at zero. Baseline: for an I&C site choose between BL01 (average of up to ten most recent like Working Days from a 60-day window, excluding Event Days and clock-change days, no in-day adjustment) and the Self-Nominated Baseline (submit a Consolidated MPAN Forecast per meter point per settlement period, no earlier than the start of the preceding Business Day, updated on material change). For a battery behind a variable C&I load, model both and pick the one with the higher expected K — the historic baseline is contaminated by the battery's own prior dispatch, which is exactly why the self-nominated option exists.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service Procurement Rules', v5.0, effective 18 May 2026, published 18 May 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/379391/download — paras 4.2 (0.1-100 MW, 1 dp), 4.3.2 (zone allocation), 4.7.2/4.7.6/4.7.7/4.7.8/4.7.9 (HH settlement, BMU exclusion, CoP11, Stacking List), 4.10 (Pre-Participation Waiting Period), 8 (Primacy: Risk of Conflict File, Primacy Exclusion Report), 10.3 (Service Requirement, Zonal Cap), 11.5 (submission format), 12 (assessment and clearing), Schedule 1 (Minimum Submission Size 0.1MW, Maximum 100MW, Guaranteed Acceptance Price), Schedule 3 Parts 2-4 (BL01 and Self-Nominated Baselining)
- *Source:* NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service Service Terms', v4.0, effective 9 April 2026, published 25 March 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/379396/download — Schedule 1 Utilisation Payments: S_ie = 0.5 * P_ie * Q_ie * K_ie and the full piecewise K schedule including the negative branch
- *Source:* NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service' service page, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/demand-flexibility-service — year-round in-merit margin tool from 27 November 2024; 9 April 2026 expansion adding bi-directional flexibility, zonal procurement, Self-Nominated Baseline, Primacy and the 0.1 MW threshold
- *Source:* NESO, 'DFS 12 Zones GeoJson Map', https://www.neso.energy/document/376656/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'DFS Participation Guidance', v18, 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/346011/download
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services (DC/DM/DR) Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026 — 1 MW minimum per response unit, whole-MW sell orders (establishes the gap DFS fills)


### F. Network charges, levies and avoided cost

#### F-01 · MUST · Linearise capacity/maximum-demand charges with an auxiliary peak variable per billing period, and treat time-of-use bands as time-indexed price coefficients (which need no binaries).

- **Why:** Time-varying tariffs (DUoS red/amber/green, day/night supply rates) are linear once the price is indexed by settlement period — a common over-engineering error is to introduce binaries for them. Capacity/max-demand charges are genuinely non-linear in the dispatch and DO need an auxiliary variable.
- **Error if omitted:** If the peak variable is missing, the model cannot value peak shaving against agreed-capacity or maximum-demand charges at all, which for many C&I sites is a primary BTM revenue line. If binaries are added for TOU bands, the model is needlessly slow with no gain.
- **How to implement:** TOU: cost += sum_t c_band(t) * P^imp_t * dt, where c_band(t) is a lookup on the settlement period and calendar day. Peak/capacity charge for billing period m: introduce Pk_m >= 0, constrain Pk_m >= P^imp_t for all t in m (and, if the tariff uses an average of several highest half-hours, model each separately), then cost += kappa_m * Pk_m. If the tariff ratchets, add Pk_m >= rho * max_{m' < m} Pk_{m'}.
- *Source:* Mariaud, A., Acha, S., Ekins-Daukes, N., Shah, N., Markides, C.N., 'Integrated optimisation of photovoltaic and battery storage systems for UK commercial buildings', Applied Energy 199:466-478, 2017, DOI 10.1016/j.apenergy.2017.04.067
- *Source:* Hassan, A.S., Cipcigan, L., Jenkins, N., 'Optimal battery storage operation for PV systems with tariff incentives', Applied Energy 203:422-441, 2017, DOI 10.1016/j.apenergy.2017.06.043

#### F-02 · MUST · Do not model triad avoidance or any residual-charge avoidance from peak shaving; only the volumetric (red-band) DUoS unit rate and any agreed-capacity charge remain avoidable by dispatch.

- **Why:** Ofgem's Targeted Charging Review decision (December 2019) moved residual network charges to fixed charges by band for all households and businesses, removed transmission generation residual liability, and reduced embedded benefits for distributed generation. This ended triad avoidance as a dispatchable revenue stream.
- **Error if omitted:** A model carrying pre-TCR triad avoidance credits a BTM battery with a revenue line that no longer exists. Historically this was one of the largest single BTM value drivers in GB, so its inclusion is a first-order overstatement, not a rounding error.
- **How to implement:** Split network charges in the objective into (i) fixed, dispatch-invariant residual terms, entered as a constant in the cashflow and NOT in the MILP objective, and (ii) volumetric terms with a time-varying unit rate entered as c_band(t) on P^imp_t. Set the triad revenue line to zero. Retain only the agreed/authorised capacity charge, which is avoidable only if the connection agreement is renegotiated downward — a build decision, not a dispatch decision.
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Targeted Charging Review: decision and impact assessment', December 2019 — 'Residual charges will be levied in the form of fixed charges for all households and businesses'; removal of transmission generation residual liability; reduction of embedded benefits, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/targeted-charging-review-decision-and-impact-assessment

#### F-03 · MUST · Compute a full half-hourly delivered import price stack and apply the IDENTICAL stack to the battery's charging import. Never model charging at wholesale-only and discharging at retail.

- **Why:** A BTM battery behind the customer's supply meter does not import at wholesale. Its charging energy passes the same meter as site load and attracts every volumetric component of the delivered tariff — DUoS unit rate for the band it charges in, BSUoS, CCL, RO, FiT, CfD, AAHEDC, Nuclear RAB, EII levy and the supplier's own shape/margin adders.
- **Error if omitted:** Systematically overstates arbitrage margin by the full flat-levy stack, roughly 6–8 p/kWh in 2026/27, plus the green-band DUoS unit rate. On a 2 MW/4 MWh asset cycling once daily this is an overstatement of order £150k–£250k/yr — enough to turn a negative-NPV project into a bankable one.
- **How to implement:** p_imp[t] = w[t] + duos_unit[band(dno, t)] + bsuos[period(t)] + ccl[y] + ro[y] + fit[y] + cfd_ilr[q(t)] + aahedc[y] + rab[q(t)] + eii[y] + supplier_adder. Objective: min Σ_t Δt · p_imp[t] · imp[t] − Σ_t Δt · p_exp[t] · exp[t] + fixed_charges, with imp[t] − exp[t] = L[t] + c[t] − d[t] − pv[t], imp[t] ≥ 0, exp[t] ≥ 0, imp[t] ≤ MIC/1000 · Δt, and imp[t]·exp[t] = 0 enforced by a binary if export is permitted.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement — Notice of Charges Effective from 1 April 2026, Annex 1, December 2024, https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677
- *Source:* NESO, BSUoS Fixed Tariff 6 October 2025–March 2026, v1.0, December 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/350411/download
- *Source:* HMRC, Climate Change Levy rates, updated 2026, https://www.gov.uk/guidance/climate-change-levy-rates

#### F-04 · MUST · Model flat (time-invariant) per-kWh levies as a NET COST proportional to round-trip losses, not as an avoided cost. Add an explicit line item: levy_leakage = λ_flat × E_discharged × (1/η_RT − 1).

- **Why:** Levies with no half-hourly variation cancel between charge and discharge on a 1:1 kWh basis. What does not cancel is the extra energy the battery must import to cover its own losses. Since 1 April 2023 BSUoS is a flat £/MWh with no HH profile, so BSUoS joins CCL/RO/FiT/CfD/AAHEDC/RAB in this category.
- **Error if omitted:** Understates operating cost by λ_flat × (1/η − 1) per kWh discharged. With λ_flat ≈ 7.5 p/kWh (2026/27: CCL 0.801 + BSUoS 1.374 + RO ~3.27 + CfD ~1.0 + FiT ~0.5 + RAB ~0.44 + AAHEDC 0.044 + EII ~0.04) and η_RT = 0.85, that is ~1.3 p/kWh discharged — comparable in size to the entire NGED HV red-to-green DUoS spread of 2.828 p/kWh. Omitting it can flip the sign of the DUoS optimisation case.
- **How to implement:** For each flat levy λ_i with no time index: contribution to objective = λ_i × Σ_t (c[t] − d[t]) Δt = λ_i × (E_ch − E_dis) = λ_i × E_dis × (1/η_RT − 1). Implement as a post-solve reconciliation check: assert |Σ_t λ_i·(c[t]−d[t])Δt − λ_i·E_dis·(1/η_RT − 1)| < tol. Do NOT credit λ_i against discharged kWh.
- *Source:* NESO, BSUoS Fixed Tariff 6 October 2025–March 2026, December 2024, https://www.neso.energy/document/350411/download
- *Source:* Drax, BSUoS fixed tariffs to fall through 2026, 2026, https://energy.drax.com/intelligence/bsuos-fixed-tariffs-to-fall-through-2026/
- *Source:* HMRC, Climate Change Levy rates, https://www.gov.uk/guidance/climate-change-levy-rates
- *Source:* DESNZ, Calculating the level of the Renewables Obligation for 2026 to 2027, 29 September 2025, https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/renewables-obligation-level-calculations-2026-to-2027/calculating-the-level-of-the-renewables-obligation-for-2026-to-2027

#### F-05 · MUST · Load the specific DNO's published red/amber/green time bands from its Use of System Charging Statement, in UK CLOCK time, and honour that DNO's bank-holiday and seasonal treatment. Do not use a generic 16:00–19:00 weekday rule.

- **Why:** Bands are set per DNO and per tariff class. NGED East Midlands 2026/27 sets Red as Monday–Friday INCLUDING BANK HOLIDAYS 16:00–19:00 ALL YEAR for LV/HV Designated Properties, Amber 07:30–16:00 and 19:00–21:00, Green 00:00–07:30, 21:00–24:00 and all day Saturday/Sunday. Its EHV (EDCM) Super Red band is a different definition: Monday–Friday 16:00–19:00 November–February only. Its unmetered band is Nov–Feb only. Other DNOs use different clock times and seasonal splits.
- **Error if omitted:** Two classes of error. (a) Using UTC instead of UK clock time misaligns every red half-hour by one settlement period for 7 months of the year, mis-pricing ~30% of red periods. (b) Assuming bank holidays are green under NGED removes ~8 red days/yr of correctly-priced dispatch and, worse, causes the optimiser to charge at red prices on those days. (c) Assuming a Nov–Feb-only red band at HV under NGED discards ~8 months of red-band value.
- **How to implement:** band(t) = f(dno_id, llfc/duos_tariff_id, local_clock_time(t, Europe/London), weekday(t), is_bank_holiday(t), month(t)). Store as a per-DNO calendar table with an explicit `bank_holidays_are_red` flag and `red_months` set. Validate by asserting Σ_t 1[band=red] matches the DNO's own stated red-hours count for the year.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, Annex 1 (LV/HV time bands) and Annex 2 (EHV Super Red), December 2024, https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677
- *Source:* Selectra, DUoS Charges Explained (industry explainer, last resort): 'the exact times and rates are set by each DNO and published in its annual charging statement, so they differ by region', https://selectra.co.uk/energy/network/guides/duos-charges-explained

#### F-06 · MUST · Use the site's actual connection voltage and LLFC/DUoS tariff ID to pick unit rates. Do not apply LV rates to an HV-connected site or vice versa.

- **Why:** The red-band unit rate differs by a factor of ~2.7 between LV Site Specific and HV Site Specific on the same DNO (NGED East Midlands 2026/27: LV red 7.777 p/kWh, LV Sub red 5.147 p/kWh, HV red 2.837 p/kWh). The entire economic case for DUoS optimisation depends on this one parameter.
- **Error if omitted:** Applying LV rates to an HV site overstates the annual DUoS red-band saving by ~4.9 p/kWh × red-band discharged energy. For a 2 MW asset discharging 3 h/day × 250 weekdays that is ~1.5 GWh × 4.9 p = ~£73k/yr of fabricated saving.
- **How to implement:** Key the tariff lookup on (dno_licence_area, llfc, duos_tariff_id, tcr_band). Require llfc as a mandatory model input; refuse to run on a guessed voltage. Rates for NGED East Midlands 2026/27 (p/kWh): LV Site Specific 7.777/0.958/0.054; LV Sub Site Specific 5.147/0.560/0.027; HV Site Specific 2.837/0.257/0.009.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, Annex 1, https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677

#### F-07 · MUST · Treat the DUoS capacity charge (p/kVA/day on MIC) as a fixed cost that dispatch cannot reduce. Only an agreed MIC reduction reduces it, and that is contractually rate-limited.

- **Why:** The chargeable capacity is the agreed MIC/MEC, not the metered peak. NGED states the MIC 'will be agreed with us at the time of connection or pursuant to a later change in requirements. Following such an agreement ... no reduction in MIC/MEC will be allowed for a 12 month period' and 'Reductions to the MIC/MEC may only be permitted once in a 12 month period', applied from the start of the next billing period. Once surrendered, the capacity may not be available again without reinforcement charges.
- **Error if omitted:** Modelling peak-shaving as reducing the capacity charge in-year invents a saving that cannot be realised. Conversely, ignoring that a BTM battery may REQUIRE an MIC increase (to charge at full power on top of site load) omits a real, permanent cost increase of capacity_rate × ΔMIC × 365.
- **How to implement:** capacity_cost = MIC_kVA × rate_p_per_kVA_per_day × days. Make MIC a decision variable only at the investment stage with a step cost and a 12-month lock; in the dispatch MILP hold it constant. Add a constraint: imp[t]·(1/Δt) ≤ MIC × pf for all t, and if the optimum violates it, either curtail charging or price an MIC uplift (NGED 2026/27: LV 7.68, LV Sub 7.40, HV 8.57 p/kVA/day).
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, paras 2.37–2.40 and Annex 1, https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677

#### F-08 · MUST · Implement the DCP161 exceeded-capacity charge exactly as the DNO defines it: computed every half hour from active and reactive energy, maximum taken over the billing period, then charged on the excess kVA for the FULL billing period.

- **Why:** A BTM battery charging at full power on top of an existing site peak is the single most likely cause of an MIC breach, and the penalty is not marginal — one half-hour of exceedance is billed as if it persisted all month. The DNO-published rate is a per-DNO parameter: NGED 2026/27 sets it equal to the capacity rate; 2017-era industry commentary quotes 'up to four times higher than the standard agreed rate' at other DNOs.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that ignores exceeded capacity will happily schedule charging into the site's own peak. One breach of 500 kVA at NGED HV costs 500 × 8.57 p × ~30 days ≈ £1,286 for that month; at a DNO charging 4× it is ~£5,100 per month per breach event. Repeated monthly this can exceed the entire DUoS red-band saving.
- **How to implement:** Demand exceeded capacity = max(2 × sqrt(AI² + max(RI,RE)²) − MIC, 0), evaluated for every half hour (AI in kWh, RI/RE in kVArh, MIC in kVA; the factor 2 converts half-hourly energy to average kVA), using only reactive values coincident with active import; take the maximum over the billing period and charge exceeded_kVA × exceeded_rate × days_in_billing_period. In the MILP, either (a) hard-constrain 2·sqrt(AI[t]²+Q[t]²) ≤ MIC, or (b) add a per-month continuous variable X_m ≥ 2·sqrt(AI[t]²+Q[t]²) − MIC, X_m ≥ 0, with cost X_m × rate × days_m (a linearised second-order-cone or piecewise-linear approximation of the kVA norm).
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, paras 2.41–2.43, https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677
- *Source:* Catalyst Commercial, DCP161 – Changes To Excess kVA Charges, 29 January 2017 (industry blog, dated — labelled low-tier), https://www.catalyst-commercial.co.uk/dcp161-excess-kva-charges/

#### F-09 · MUST · Model excess reactive power charges and the battery inverter's reactive behaviour, using the DNO's 0.95 power-factor threshold formula.

- **Why:** DUoS levies a charge on reactive units above a 33% threshold of active energy in each half hour (equivalent to power factor 0.95). A battery inverter operating at non-unity power factor, or an inverter that trips out the site's existing PF correction, changes this. NGED 2026/27 rates: LV 0.244, LV Sub 0.155, HV 0.076 p/kVArh.
- **Error if omitted:** Either a missed cost (if the inverter imports/exports VArs) or a missed saving (if the inverter is used for on-site PF correction, which is a real, cheap co-benefit of a BTM BESS that most models ignore).
- **How to implement:** Demand chargeable kVArh[t] = max( max(RI[t],RE[t]) − sqrt(1/0.95² − 1) × AI[t], 0 ), i.e. threshold coefficient = 0.328684, evaluated per half hour with only reactive values coincident with active import, then summed over the billing period and multiplied by the p/kVArh rate. Expose inverter Q[t] as a decision variable bounded by the inverter apparent-power limit: P[t]² + Q[t]² ≤ S_inv².
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, paras 2.48–2.53 and Annex 1, https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677

#### F-10 · MUST · Treat the TCR-banded DUoS residual (fixed p/MPAN/day) and TNUoS demand residual (fixed £/site/day) as strictly non-avoidable by dispatch, and check whether the project pushes the site into a higher band.

- **Why:** Since 1 April 2022 residual charges are recovered through fixed charges for all Final Demand Sites, applied on a banded basis with four non-domestic bands for each of LV-no-MIC, LV-with-MIC, HV and EHV. Band boundaries are calculated nationally under DCUSA Schedule 32 and, for TNUoS, NESO states 'the residual band thresholds will remain the same for the duration of the RIIO-ET3 price control period'. The band depends on the site's capacity/consumption segment, not on half-hourly behaviour.
- **Error if omitted:** (a) Crediting residual avoidance to the battery fabricates the single largest line in the pre-2022 BTM business case. (b) Missing a band step-up is a real omitted cost: NGED East Midlands 2026/27 HV fixed charges are 360.65, 866.35, 1543.02 and 3736.24 p/MPAN/day for bands 1–4 — a Band 2→3 step is ~£2,470/yr; TNUoS HV2→HV3 is £117.15→£185.42/day, ~£24,900/yr.
- **How to implement:** fixed_residual_cost = duos_fixed_p_per_day[band] × days / 100 + tnuos_residual_gbp_per_day[band] × days. Make `tcr_band` a mandatory input, and add an investment-stage check: if the project increases MIC across a band boundary, apply the band delta as an incremental project cost for the whole life. Never index these to dispatch variables.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, paras 2.26–2.32 and Annex 1, https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677
- *Source:* NESO, January 2026 Final TNUoS Tariffs for 2026/27, Tables 8 and 10, January 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/376336/download
- *Source:* NESO, Targeted charging review: 'reforms at transmission level being introduced in April 2022 and at the distribution level in April 2022', https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/charging/charging-futures/targeted-charging-review

#### F-11 · MUST · State explicitly that triad avoidance of the transmission DEMAND RESIDUAL ceased on 1 April 2022, and model only the surviving TNUoS locational HH demand tariff, which is still charged on Triad demand and is zero in demand zones 1–7.

- **Why:** Ofgem's TCR decision (18 December 2019) removed the demand residual from volumetric/triad recovery; NESO confirms implementation in April 2022 at both transmission and distribution level. What remains is the locational element: NESO states 'HH demand and embedded export is charged at the GSP' on gross HH demand at Triads, and 'Zones 1 to 7 are subject to the zero floor on demand tariffs'. 2026/27 final HH demand tariffs (£/kW): zones 1–7 nil, Midlands 2.634, Eastern 0.063, South Wales 5.970, South East 4.368, London 6.453, Southern 7.354, South Western 14.130.
- **Error if omitted:** Modelling pre-TCR triad values (~£45–60/kW) overstates annual revenue by roughly £45–60 per kW of dependable triad reduction. For a 2 MW BTM asset that is ~£90k–£120k/yr of pure fiction. Conversely, assuming triads are worth nothing everywhere understates a South Western or Southern site by £7–14/kW/yr.
- **How to implement:** tnuos_locational_saving = zonal_tariff_gbp_per_kW[zone, year] × mean over the 3 Triad half-hours of (baseline_gross_HH_demand_kW − optimised_gross_HH_demand_kW). Because Triads are ex-post (three settlement periods ≥10 days apart in Nov–Feb), model as expected value: E[saving] = tariff × Σ_h p_triad(h) × ΔkW(h) over candidate half-hours, with p_triad calibrated to a stated triad-prediction hit rate; run P50/P90 on that hit rate. Set tariff = 0 for zones 1–7.
- *Source:* Ofgem, Targeted Charging Review: Decision and Impact Assessment, 18 December 2019, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/decision/targeted-charging-review-decision-and-impact-assessment
- *Source:* NESO, Targeted charging review, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/charging/charging-futures/targeted-charging-review
- *Source:* NESO, January 2026 Final TNUoS Tariffs for 2026/27, Table 9/11 and Appendix A, January 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/376336/download

#### F-12 · MUST · Do not model any BSUoS embedded benefit or any BSUoS time-of-use signal. Model BSUoS as a flat £/MWh on final demand only, changing every six months on 1 April and 1 October.

- **Why:** CMP308 defined that BSUoS is recovered solely from final demand, and CMP361 introduced an ex-ante fixed tariff, both effective 1 April 2023. Generators and embedded generation neither pay nor benefit. Under the earlier regime BSUoS was half-hourly and volatile, and embedded export earned a BSUoS 'embedded benefit' — pre-2023 literature and any model built on it will be wrong on both counts.
- **Error if omitted:** (a) A phantom embedded-benefit revenue line of order £2–4/MWh exported. (b) A phantom intra-day BSUoS arbitrage signal that no longer exists. (c) Wrong period boundaries: the tariff steps on 1 April and 1 October, not on the financial year, so an annual average mis-prices winter dispatch (Oct 2025–Mar 2026 was £15.69/MWh vs £10.74/MWh in the preceding summer, a 46% step).
- **How to implement:** bsuos[t] = tariff_gbp_per_MWh[semiannual_period(t)] / 10 in p/kWh, applied to net metered import only. Tariff schedule: FT5 Apr–Sep 2025 £10.74; FT6 Oct 2025–Mar 2026 £15.69; FT7 Apr–Sep 2026 £13.74; FT8 Oct 2026–Mar 2027 £12.49. Assert bsuos_export_credit == 0.
- *Source:* NESO, Balancing Services Use of System (BSUoS) charges: 'CMP308 defined that BSUoS charges are recovered solely from final demand'; 'CMP361 introduced an ex-ante fixed BSUoS tariff', effective 1 April 2023, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/charging/balancing-services-use-system-bsuos-charges
- *Source:* NESO, BSUoS Fixed Tariff 6 October 2025–March 2026, December 2024 (FT6 = £15.69/MWh), https://www.neso.energy/document/350411/download
- *Source:* Drax, BSUoS fixed tariffs to fall through 2026, https://energy.drax.com/intelligence/bsuos-fixed-tariffs-to-fall-through-2026/

#### F-13 · MUST · Assert that a BTM battery on a site with load does NOT qualify for the DUoS 'No Residual' (Non-Final Demand Site) tariff, and do not apply grid-scale storage charging exemptions to it.

- **Why:** The DCUSA Non-Final Demand Site Certificate requires that 'The property is a Single Site at which either or both Electricity Storage and/or Electricity Generation occurs' and 'has an export MPAN and an import MPAN with associated metering equipment which only measures export from Electricity Storage and/or Electricity Generation and import for or directly relating to Electricity Storage and/or Electricity Generation (and not export from another source and/or import for another activity)'. A commercial site with process/building load fails this on its face.
- **Error if omitted:** Importing the grid-scale BESS charging economics (no residual charge, no final consumption levies on charging) into a BTM model removes roughly £1,300–£13,600/MPAN/yr of DUoS fixed charge and 6–8 p/kWh of levies from the charging cost. This is the single largest category error available in GB BTM modelling.
- **How to implement:** Add a boolean `site_is_non_final_demand`, defaulted FALSE and only settable TRUE when the model is given evidence of a dedicated storage-only MPAN pair. Guard the tariff lookup: if site has any non-storage load MPAN, force tcr_band ∈ {1,2,3,4} and forbid selection of the *_00/No-Residual LLFCs (NGED: L00/LST, S00/SST, H00/HST).
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, section 10 and Appendix 3 (Non-Final Demand Site Certificate of Compliance), https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677

#### F-14 · MUST · Do not apply the Final Consumption Levy (FCL) storage exemption to a battery behind a customer's supply meter unless separately-metered non-final demand is evidenced and declared.

- **Why:** Ofgem's 2 October 2020 decision on the regulatory framework for electricity storage introduced generation licence condition E1 requiring storage operators to 'provide accurate information regarding their electricity storage facility to their relevant suppliers', explicitly to 'address current issues storage providers face surrounding final consumption levies (where some providers currently face double-charging of such levies)'. Elexon operates a declaration process for 'Exemption from Final Consumption Levies' for non-final demand; FCLs 'are charges which recover the costs associated with schemes such as Contracts for Difference (CfD) and the Capacity Market'. The exemption is metering- and declaration-dependent.
- **Error if omitted:** Removes ~4–5 p/kWh (RO + CfD + FiT + CM) from charging cost that will in fact be billed. On a 4 MWh asset cycling daily (~1.7 GWh/yr charged) that is ~£70k–£85k/yr of fabricated saving.
- **How to implement:** Boolean `fcl_exempt_charging` defaulted FALSE; require a declared non-final-demand MPAN and a supplier confirmation to set TRUE. Where TRUE, zero out RO, FiT, CfD ILR and CM from the charging leg only; where FALSE (the BTM default), apply them in full to all metered import including charging.
- *Source:* Ofgem, Decision on clarifying the regulatory framework for electricity storage: changes to the electricity generation licence, 2 October 2020, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/decision-clarifying-regulatory-framework-electricity-storage-changes-electricity-generation-licence
- *Source:* Elexon, Exemption from Final Consumption Levies Declarations, Guidance Note v2.0, effective 11 July 2025, https://bscdocs.elexon.co.uk/guidance-notes/submission-of-declarations-for-exemption-of-non-final-demand-from-final-consumption-levies
- *Source:* Ofgem, Clarifying the regulatory framework for electricity storage: licensing (consultation) — storage faces charges 'at the time of both importing from and exporting electricity to the grid', https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/consultation/clarifying-regulatory-framework-electricity-storage-licensing

#### F-15 · MUST · Apply the Climate Change Levy at the statutory rate to all business import including battery charging, with the correct annual step, and apply the CCA reduced rate only where a Climate Change Agreement is evidenced.

- **Why:** CCL main rate for electricity is £0.00775/kWh for 1 April 2024–31 March 2026, £0.00801/kWh from 1 April 2026, £0.00827/kWh from 1 April 2027. CCA holders receive a 92% discount on the electricity main rate. Self-consumed on-site generation is not a taxable supply, so PV consumed on site attracts no CCL — but a battery charged from the grid does.
- **Error if omitted:** Modelling CCL as avoided on discharge (rather than paid on charge) creates a phantom 0.78–0.83 p/kWh saving. Applying the full rate to a CCA-holding manufacturer overstates the same line by 92%. Missing the 1 April step misprices the whole forward curve of a 10–15 year underwriting case by ~3.3%/yr on this line.
- **How to implement:** ccl[t] = ccl_main_rate[fy(t)] × (1 − 0.92·has_CCA) p/kWh, applied to metered grid import only; zero on PV self-consumption and on battery discharge. Rate table: 0.775 (FY2024-25, FY2025-26), 0.801 (FY2026-27), 0.827 (FY2027-28 onwards) p/kWh.
- *Source:* HMRC, Climate Change Levy rates, https://www.gov.uk/guidance/climate-change-levy-rates

#### F-16 · MUST · Model the Renewables Obligation as a per-MWh cost on supplied volume at obligation level × ROC price, and switch the indexation basis to CPI from 1 April 2026.

- **Why:** Suppliers 'present to Ofgem a specified number of Renewables Obligation Certificates (ROCs) per megawatt hour of electricity supplied'. The GB obligation for 2026/27 is 0.472 ROCs/MWh; the buy-out price is £69.34/ROC for 2026/27 and £67.06 for 2025/26. From 1 April 2026 indexation moved from RPI to CPI following a government decision of 28 January 2026. Energy Intensive Industries are 100% exempt from indirect RO costs in GB.
- **Error if omitted:** RO is the single largest volumetric levy — 0.472 × £69.34 = £32.73/MWh (3.27 p/kWh) in 2026/27 — larger than BSUoS, CCL and CfD combined. Omitting it from the CHARGING leg understates the round-trip levy leakage by more than half. Escalating it at RPI post-April-2026 overstates the long-run cost by roughly 1 percentage point per year.
- **How to implement:** ro_cost_p_per_kWh[y] = obligation_ROCs_per_MWh[y] × roc_price_gbp[y] / 10, with roc_price ≈ buy_out[y] × (1 + recycle_factor). Escalate buy_out by CPI from FY2026-27 onwards. Zero the line where the site holds a valid EII certificate. 2026/27: 0.472 ROCs/MWh (GB), 0.184 (NI), buy-out £69.34.
- *Source:* DESNZ, Calculating the level of the Renewables Obligation for 2026 to 2027, 29 September 2025, https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/renewables-obligation-level-calculations-2026-to-2027/calculating-the-level-of-the-renewables-obligation-for-2026-to-2027
- *Source:* Ofgem, Renewables Obligation: buy-out price and mutualisation threshold and ceilings 2026/27, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/data/renewables-obligation-buy-out-price-and-mutualisation-threshold-and-ceilings-2026-2027

#### F-17 · MUST · Include the AAHEDC levy and the EII Support Levy as small but real volumetric adders on import.

- **Why:** AAHEDC is recovered from all authorised suppliers on a p/kWh basis under Condition F8 of the NESO licence; the forecast 2026/27 Total Scheme tariff is 0.044201 p/kWh (Shetland component 0.012766, AAHEDC ex-Shetland 0.031435), a 7.8% rise, on a forecast demand base of 273.7 TWh and a Total Scheme Amount of £121m. Separately, the EII Support Levy funds the British Industry Supercharger Network Charging Compensation scheme, whose relief rises from 60% to 90% on 1 April 2026, adding an estimated £0.40/MWh for non-domestic consumers.
- **Error if omitted:** Individually ~0.04 p/kWh each, but they are part of the flat levy stack that drives the round-trip loss term, and the EII levy is on a rising trajectory (the government has stated ESL will increase again from 1 April 2027). Omitting them also means the model's bill reconciliation against an actual invoice will not tie out.
- **How to implement:** aahedc[y] = 0.044201 p/kWh (2026/27 forecast; final tariff published mid-July, effective retrospectively from 1 April, invoiced quarterly in arrears). eii_levy[y] added as p/kWh on non-EII supply. Both applied to all metered import including charging. Note the retrospective true-up: model a reconciliation accrual, not just the forecast rate.
- *Source:* NESO, Assistance for Areas with High Electricity Distribution Costs forecast tariff 2026/27, 27 March 2026, https://www.neso.energy/sites/neso.energy/files/documents/Draft%202026-27%20AAHEDC%20Tariff.pdf
- *Source:* NESO, AAHEDC scheme page, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/charging/assistance-areas-high-electricity-distribution-costs-aahedc
- *Source:* DESNZ, Proposed uplift to the Network Charging Compensation scheme for EIIs: government consultation response (60%→90% from 1 April 2026; ~£0.40/MWh impact on non-domestic consumers), https://www.gov.uk/government/consultations/network-charging-compensation-scheme-uplift-for-energy-intensive-industries/outcome/proposed-uplift-to-the-network-charging-compensation-scheme-for-energy-intensive-industries-eiis-government-consultation-response

#### F-18 · MUST · Model the supplier fixed/standing charge and DUoS fixed charge per MPAN per day, and apply the rule that two HH MPANs at the same connection point with the same LLFC and supplier attract only one daily fixed charge.

- **Why:** NGED states 'Fixed charges are generally levied on a pence per MPAN/MSID per day basis. Where two or more HH MPANs/MSIDs are located at the same point of connection ... with the same LLFC/DUoS Tariff ID, and registered to the same Supplier, only one daily fixed charge will be applied.' This matters directly to whether adding a separate storage MPAN doubles the fixed charge.
- **Error if omitted:** Either double-counting a second fixed charge (overstating cost by up to £13,600/yr at HV Band 4) or missing a genuinely additional charge where the LLFC or supplier differs. It also determines whether splitting the battery onto its own MPAN is cost-effective.
- **How to implement:** fixed_charge_count = number of distinct (connection_point, llfc, supplier) tuples, not number of MPANs. Cost = Σ over tuples of fixed_p_per_day[band] × days / 100. Evaluate 'separate storage MPAN' as an explicit investment option with its own band allocation and its own residual liability.
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, para 2.21, https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677

#### F-19 · MUST · Represent the metering topology as an explicit binary build decision with its own capex, not as an open question: does the battery sit on its own import/export MPAN pair (storage-only site) or inside the host's existing MPAN? Evaluate both branches end-to-end — each with its own final-consumption-levy exposure, TCR band and DUoS fixed residual, standing charges, connection agreement, BSUoS treatment, Capacity Market baseline, and whether discharge is 'avoided import' or 'metered export sold at a different price' — and report the NPV difference as a named line in the build case.

- **Why:** This is quantitatively the largest single lever available to the model, and the numbers are public. A GB battery that holds a Generation Licence and files a Non-Final Demand declaration avoids the Contracts for Difference levy (~£13/MWh), the Renewables Obligation (~£27/MWh), the Feed-in Tariff levy (~£8/MWh), the Climate Change Levy (£7.80/MWh, 2023/24), and Capacity Market supplier plus settlement charges (~£11/MWh), and is exempt from BSUoS fixed charges (£13.41/MWh summer and £14.03/MWh winter, 2023/24) and from TNUoS and DUoS fixed residuals, retaining only AAHEDC at £0.42/MWh. That is of the order of £80/MWh, i.e. ~8 p/kWh on every charged unit. And the crucial finding is that this does NOT come for free behind the meter: industry analysis states plainly that the exemptions do 'not apply to storage located Behind-the-Meter. These batteries are currently unlikely to qualify for these exemptions.' So the topology choice is the difference between paying ~8 p/kWh on charging and paying almost none of it.
- **Error if omitted:** The model silently assumes the worse of two branches and never prices the better one. At ~£80/MWh on charged energy, for a 2 MW / 4 MWh asset cycling once per day at ~90% round-trip, annual charged energy is roughly 1,620 MWh, so the levy stack is of the order of £130k per year — plausibly larger than the entire DUoS red-band arbitrage line the model does compute, and larger than most single ancillary lines. Over a 15-year underwriting horizon that is the difference between a bankable and an unbankable project. Omitting it also leaves every downstream question unanswered: the P375 asset-metering treatment, the NESO co-location metering topology, the Capacity Market baseline, and whether discharge value is set by the host's retail import tariff or by a wholesale/export price.
- **How to implement:** Binary decision variable topology in {inside_host_MPAN, separate_storage_MPAN}. Branch A (inside host MPAN): charging energy priced at the host's full delivered retail rate including all levies and DUoS unit rates; discharge valued as avoided import at the same rate; single site TCR band and one DUoS fixed residual (note the single-daily-fixed-charge rule where multiple MPANs sit behind one connection point); Capacity Market and DFS baselines are the host's net site profile; no Non-Final Demand exemption. Branch B (separate storage MPAN): add capex for the additional MPAN, metering, switchgear, protection and any DNO connection works, plus opex for a second supply contract and, if pursued, the cost and obligations of a Generation Licence or licence exemption; charging energy priced at wholesale plus only the charges that survive the Non-Final Demand and generation-licence exemptions; discharge is metered export at wholesale/PPA price OR private-wire supply to the host at a negotiated rate; the storage MPAN gets its own TCR band and its own £/site/day residual; the CM and DFS baselines are the storage MPAN's own. Solve both branches to optimality with the full year of half-hourly data and report NPV_B - NPV_A, the payback on the incremental capex, and the sensitivity to the levy stack being reformed. Hard input gates: DNO written cost and lead time for an additional MPAN at the existing connection; supplier quote for the storage supply contract; confirmation of which TCR band the new MPAN falls into and the £/site/day residual; and a written view on whether the Non-Final Demand / Final Consumption Levy exemption is actually obtainable for the configuration, because the industry position is that BTM configurations usually are not.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Electricity import charges: battery energy storage', https://www.modoenergy.com/research/en/electricity-import-charges-battery-energy-storage — CfD ~£13/MWh, RO ~£27/MWh, FiT ~£8/MWh, CCL £7.80/MWh (2023/24), CM supplier plus settlement costs ~£11/MWh, BSUoS £13.41/MWh summer and £14.03/MWh winter (2023/24), AAHEDC £0.42/MWh; TNUoS Triad import ~£2.2/kW; DUoS capacity charge unavoidable; and explicitly: exemptions do not apply to storage 'Behind-the-Meter', which is 'currently unlikely to qualify' [reputable industry analytics]
- *Source:* Elexon, 'Submission of Declarations for Exemption of Non-Final Demand from Final Consumption Levies', v2.0, effective 11 July 2025, https://bscdocs.elexon.co.uk/guidance-notes/submission-of-declarations-for-exemption-of-non-final-demand-from-final-consumption-levies — the declaration mechanism; 'Final Consumption Levies (FCL) are charges which recover the costs associated with schemes such as Contracts for Difference (CfD) and the Capacity Market (CM)'
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'BSC Modification P395 — Decision', October 2022, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2022-10/P395%20-%20Decision%20.pdf — calculation of Final Consumption Levies by EMRS from BM Unit Demand volumes
- *Source:* DCUSA Schedule 18 (Non-Final Demand Site certificate and Single Site test) and the CUSC/DCUSA TCR banding rules — primary code documents; obtain the current versions and the applicable band for the specific site [not independently retrieved this session]

#### F-20 · SHOULD · Model the Capacity Market supplier charge on its statutory basis — 4 p.m. to 7 p.m. on working days in November, December, January and February — and gate any claimed saving on whether the supply contract actually passes it through on that basis.

- **Why:** The Electricity Capacity (Supplier Payment etc.) Regulations 2014, reg. 2(1) defines 'periods of high demand' as '4 p.m. to 7 p.m. on any working day in November, December, January or February', and 'relevant demand' as 'the amount of electricity (expressed in MWh) supplied by S to premises in Great Britain'. From delivery year 2024/25 the charge is apportioned by share of Total Relevant Demand in the peak period (previously Total Gross Demand). Reducing net import in those half-hours therefore genuinely reduces the supplier's liability — but only a pass-through contract that mirrors that basis passes the saving to the customer.
- **Error if omitted:** Two opposite errors. Omitting it entirely loses a real avoidable cost on the order of tens of £/kW-yr of peak reduction. Including it on a fixed-price or flat-p/kWh contract fabricates a saving the customer will never see, because the supplier has already priced CM into the unit rate regardless of shape.
- **How to implement:** cm_saving = cm_rate_gbp_per_kW × mean over all peak half-hours (t ∈ working weekdays Nov–Feb, 16:00–19:00) of (baseline_import_kW[t] − optimised_import_kW[t]), gated by a boolean contract flag `cm_passthrough_is_peak_based`. If false, cm contribution = 0. Note this is ~186 half-hours per winter, far more than the 3 Triad half-hours, so it is a much more reliable target than triad chasing.
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity (Supplier Payment etc.) Regulations 2014, SI 2014/3354, reg. 2(1), https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2014/3354/regulation/2
- *Source:* LCCC Data Portal, Winter Peak Demand dataset: 'From Winter 24/25 onward ... apportioned across Suppliers by their share of the Total Relevant Demand for the Peak Period within a Delivery Year', https://dp.lowcarboncontracts.uk/dataset/winter-peak-demand
- *Source:* DESNZ, Contracts for Difference and Capacity Market scheme update 2025 (T-1 for 2025/26 cleared at £20/kW/yr; T-4 for 2028/29 at £60/kW/yr), 17 December 2025, https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/contracts-for-difference-and-capacity-market-scheme-update-2025

#### F-21 · SHOULD · Include the CfD supplier obligation (quarterly Interim Levy Rate plus Total Reserve Amount) as a volumetric £/MWh cost on import, refreshed quarterly, and model its volatility as a risk driver.

- **Why:** LCCC sets an Interim Levy Rate quarterly in advance: 'electricity suppliers make pre-payments consisting of a unit cost fixed Interim Levy Rate, charged at a daily £/MWh rate to fund the cost of CfD generation payments'. The rate is highly volatile because it is inversely driven by wholesale prices — industry reporting puts Q1 2026 at around £10/MWh with forecasts of up to £20.67/MWh by Q3 2027.
- **Error if omitted:** Understates charging cost by ~1.0 p/kWh today and potentially ~2.1 p/kWh by 2027. Because CfD ILR moves inversely to wholesale, treating it as a constant also destroys the correlation structure of the P50/P90 revenue distribution: high-wholesale years (good for arbitrage) are low-ILR years, and vice versa.
- **How to implement:** cfd_ilr[t] = ilr_gbp_per_MWh[quarter(t)] / 10 p/kWh on metered import, plus TRA where the supplier passes it through. In the stochastic layer, model ILR as negatively correlated with the wholesale price path rather than as an independent draw. EII sites are exempt.
- *Source:* LCCC Data Portal, Forecast ILR TRA dataset, https://dp.lowcarboncontracts.uk/dataset/forecast-ilr-tra
- *Source:* Energy Advice Hub, A guide to non-commodity cost rises on your electricity bill 2026 and 2027 (industry explainer — labelled second-tier), https://energyadvicehub.org/a-guide-to-non-commodity-cost-rises-on-your-electricity-bill-2026-and-2027/
