#### F-22 · SHOULD · Include the Feed-in Tariff levelisation cost as a volumetric levy on supplied electricity, apportioned by supply market share, with EII exclusion.

- **Why:** Ofgem states the FIT scheme cost 'is allocated between suppliers in proportion to their share of the electricity supply market of Great Britain', with quarterly and annual levelisation. Two categories are excluded: Exempt Supply (verified EU renewable imports) and EII Excluded Electricity. Suppliers pass it through per kWh, so it applies to battery charging import.
- **Error if omitted:** Understates the flat levy stack by roughly 0.4–0.6 p/kWh. Small individually, but it compounds with the other flat levies in the round-trip loss term.
- **How to implement:** fit_cost_p_per_kWh[y] = total_FIT_scheme_cost[y] / total_GB_supply_volume[y] / 10, applied to metered import; zero for EII-certified supply. Because Ofgem does not publish a single headline p/kWh rate, take the supplier's contracted FiT pass-through rate as the primary input and only fall back to the derived figure, flagging it as derived.
- *Source:* Ofgem, Feed-in Tariffs: Levelisation, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/feed-tariffs-fit/tariffs-and-payments/levelisation

#### F-23 · SHOULD · Benchmark the modelled DUoS optimisation value against published third-party estimates and refuse to accept a result that implies DUoS avoidance is broadly profitable across GB.

- **Why:** Modo Energy's 2026/27 analysis finds that a battery optimising around DUoS charges and connecting at high voltage 'would be net positive in four of the fourteen distribution network regions', with the net position ranging from +£9.8k/MW/year in South Eastern to a cost of over £58k/MW/year in North of Scotland. This is the most direct available empirical check on whether a GB DUoS-arbitrage model is producing plausible numbers.
- **Error if omitted:** Models that treat DUoS red-band avoidance as a headline BTM revenue line typically produce £20k–£60k/MW/yr across all regions. The published evidence says the correct answer is negative in 10 of 14 regions once the charging-side DUoS and capacity exposure are counted. Without this benchmark there is no external check on the single most-overstated line in the stack.
- **How to implement:** Add an automated plausibility gate: compute net_duos_position_gbp_per_MW_yr = (DUoS charges under optimised dispatch) − (DUoS charges under baseline dispatch) and assert it lies within [−£60k, +£12k]/MW/yr for an HV-connected asset in the relevant DNO region, raising a hard validation error otherwise. Report the region-by-region sign.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, DUoS charges 2026/27: what is the value for battery energy storage?, 2026 (industry analytics; article partially paywalled, figures from the public summary), https://modoenergy.com/research/en/gb-great-britain-duos-charges-battery-energy-storage-value-2026-27-distribution-use-charge
- *Source:* Drax, 2026/27 distribution figures vary across regions, 2026, https://energy.drax.com/intelligence/duos-figures-published-2026-2027/

#### F-24 · SHOULD · For a behind-the-meter battery, charge the full retail import stack on charging energy unless a levy exemption is evidenced; do not apply grid-scale storage exemptions by default.

- **Why:** GB storage exemptions from final consumption levies (BSUoS, CfD, RO, FiT, Capacity Market supplier charges, CCL) are conditional on generation-licence status and on the import being declared as non-final demand. Modo's analysis of GB import charges states directly that 'behind-the-meter batteries do not currently qualify for these exemptions'.
- **Error if omitted:** This is the single largest BTM-specific modelling error available. Applying grid-scale exemptions to a BTM asset removes roughly £30-40/MWh of charging cost (2023/24 indicative: BSUoS £13.41-14.03/MWh, CCL £7.80/MWh, Capacity Market levies ~£11/MWh combined, plus embedded CfD/RO/FiT costs in the host tariff). At a 1.5-2 cycle/day duty and 85-90% round-trip efficiency that is tens of thousands of pounds per MW per year of phantom margin, and it will make an unbankable project appear bankable.
- **How to implement:** Model charging cost as ImportPrice_hh = WholesaleOrTariff_hh + DUoS_variable(band, hh) + TNUoS_import(if triad-exposed) + BSUoS + CCL + CfD/RO/FiT levy pass-through + CM supplier levies + AAHEDC, with a boolean ExemptionEvidenced flag that zeroes only the specific levies for which a declaration is actually held. Default the flag to FALSE for BTM and require documentary evidence (generation licence, separate MPAN, half-hourly settlement registration, supplier non-final-demand declaration) to set it TRUE. Run the sensitivity both ways and report the difference as a named risk.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Electricity import charges: which do battery energy storage pay?' (2023/24 tariff vintage) — BSUoS £13.41/MWh summer and £14.03/MWh winter; CCL £7.80/MWh; CMSC+SCL ~£11/MWh; AAHEDC £0.42/MWh; TNUoS triad import average £2.2/kW; 'behind-the-meter batteries do not currently qualify for these exemptions', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/electricity-import-charges-battery-energy-storage
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Decision on clarifying the regulatory framework for electricity storage: changes to the electricity generation licence', 2 October 2020, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/decision-clarifying-regulatory-framework-electricity-storage-changes-electricity-generation-licence
- *Source:* Elexon, 'Submission of Declarations for Exemption of Non-Final Demand from Final Consumption Levies', BSC guidance note v2.0, effective 11 July 2025, https://bscdocs.elexon.co.uk/guidance-notes/submission-of-declarations-for-exemption-of-non-final-demand-from-final-consumption-levies

#### F-25 · SHOULD · Model regulatory decay of avoided-cost revenue: assume network and policy cost savings available to a BTM asset erode over the asset life, and stress the case where they go to zero.

- **Why:** The GB legal literature on private-wire and behind-the-meter projects records that 'savings available from avoided network and policy costs will reduce further in the future' as a result of Ofgem's network charging review and potential government action on supplier levy avoidance. The Targeted Charging Review already moved residual network charges to fixed bands, removing much of the behind-the-meter avoidance that pre-2019 models relied on.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that holds today's DUoS red-band and triad avoidance value flat for 15 years is underwriting a regulatory position that regulators have publicly signalled they intend to remove. The error is one-directional and largest in the later years, which is exactly where the debt tail and terminal value sit.
- **How to implement:** Split BTM revenue into (a) market-derived value (wholesale spread, BM, ancillary) and (b) charge-avoidance value (DUoS red-band avoidance, TNUoS triad/demand residual avoidance, levy avoidance, capacity-charge reduction). Apply a decay function to (b): e.g. linear decay to a floor of 0-30% of today's value by 2035, with a stress case setting it to zero from the next charging review. Never allow charge-avoidance value to underpin contracted-revenue or debt-service coverage tests.
- *Source:* Walker Morris, 'Private wire power purchase agreements', https://www.walkermorris.co.uk/comment-opinion/private-wire-power-purchase-agreements/
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Electricity import charges: which do battery energy storage pay?', https://modoenergy.com/research/en/electricity-import-charges-battery-energy-storage

#### F-26 · SHOULD · Model an Active Network Management or flexible (non-firm) connection as a stochastic, time-correlated constraint on export and, where the DNO applies it, on import — not as a one-line eligibility test. Capture (a) the curtailment signal as a time series correlated with system stress, (b) the curtailment principle and queue position (Last In, First Out is the prevailing GB ANM allocation rule, so a new connectee bears the most curtailment), (c) the absence of compensation for curtailment under non-firm access, and (d) the firm-versus-flexible connection decision as an explicit capex-versus-curtailment-risk build variable. Require a DNO site-specific curtailment assessment as a hard input gate before booking any ancillary, CM or DFS revenue.

- **Why:** A curtailment instruction inside a contracted ancillary window is a non-delivery event, not merely lost energy. On NESO's own performance rules the availability factor F is zero for the whole settlement period if there is 'any period or periods of unavailability within Settlement Period j', and the EFA-block performance factor K is the minimum k across the block, so one curtailment instruction destroys the payment for the block. Curtailment is driven by local network conditions that correlate with system-wide stress, so it bites hardest exactly when revenue is highest. Ofgem's Access SCR decision (3 May 2022) committed to 'ensure a standardised non-firm access option is available for larger network users' and to 'introduce clear curtailment limits and end-dates for non-firm access arrangements', which means the curtailment exposure is a defined, contractible quantity that can and should be obtained rather than assumed. DFS makes the collision explicit and machine-readable: DNOs return a Risk of Conflict File flagging conflicts with 'any DNO Flexibility Service, Active Network Management Scheme or DNO Operational Security Requirement', and affected meter points are made Primacy Excluded and cannot participate.
- **Error if omitted:** A mean curtailment percentage applied uniformly to annual energy understates the revenue loss in two compounding ways. First, it misses the correlation: curtailed hours are disproportionately high-price hours. Second, and larger, it converts a binary availability failure into a pro-rata energy loss — but F goes to zero for the settlement period and K is a block minimum, so a 1% curtailment rate concentrated in contracted windows can destroy far more than 1% of the ancillary line, and can trigger non-delivery liability. It also silently assumes away the firm-connection option, which may pay for itself purely by removing that exposure.
- **How to implement:** Obtain from the DNO: the ANM scheme's curtailment principle and the asset's queue position; the historic curtailment time series for the relevant ANM zone (half-hourly, minimum 2 years); and the connection agreement's curtailment limit and end-date if one exists post-Access SCR. Model as a binary avail_net(t) in {0,1} (or a continuous cap MW_allowed(t)) sampled as part of the contemporaneous multivariate block (see the sampling requirement) so the correlation with price and system stress is preserved. Constraints: P_export(t) <= MW_allowed(t); P_import(t) <= MW_allowed_import(t) where the DNO applies import ANM. Ancillary layer: forbid contracting a service in any EFA block whose curtailment probability exceeds a stated threshold, or price the expected F=0 loss plus the expected non-delivery liability into the bid. Build layer: solve the model twice — firm connection (higher capex, no curtailment) and flexible connection (lower capex, curtailment series) — and compare NPV and P90, reporting the implied value of firmness in £/MW. Underwriting gate: refuse to book any ancillary, CM or DFS revenue for a flexible-connection site without a written DNO site-specific curtailment assessment on file.
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Access and Forward-Looking Charges Significant Code Review: Decision and Direction', 3 May 2022, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/decision/access-and-forward-looking-charges-significant-code-review-decision-and-direction — standardised non-firm access option; 'clear curtailment limits and end-dates for non-firm access arrangements'; changes effective 1 April 2023
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (DSO), 'Curtailment Analysis', https://dso.nationalgrid.co.uk/planning-our-future-network/curtailment-analysis — ANM allocates capacity on a Last In, First Out basis by application date
- *Source:* UK Power Networks, 'Curtailment Data Dashboard', https://ukpowernetworks.opendatasoft.com/pages/curtailment-data-dashboard/ — real-time constraint meter readings and historic curtailment data by site (data source)
- *Source:* SSEN, 'Flexible Connections Document Library', https://www.ssen.co.uk/about-ssen/dso/flexibility/access-products/flexible-connections-document-library/
- *Source:* NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service Procurement Rules', v5.0, 18 May 2026, para 8.4 — DNO 'Risk of Conflict File' covering 'any DNO Flexibility Service, Active Network Management Scheme or DNO Operational Security Requirement'; para 8.7 Primacy Excluded meter points cannot participate
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services (DC/DM/DR) Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026 — availability factor F is zero where there is 'any period or periods of unavailability within Settlement Period j'; K_e = min_j k_j across the EFA block

#### F-27 · CONSIDER · Add the Nuclear RAB (Sizewell C) levy as a new volumetric line from 1 December 2025 — most pre-2026 GB cost stacks omit it entirely.

- **Why:** The Nuclear RAB levy is collected by suppliers under quarterly rates set by LCCC, with an ILR-style structure (Interim Levy Rate plus Total Reserve Amount plus an operational costs levy). Reported rates: £3.494/MWh in December 2025, £3.663/MWh in Q1 2026, £4.683/MWh in Q2 2026. The statutory Nuclear RAB operational costs levy is set separately at £0.0063/MWh for 2026-27, £0.0068/MWh for 2027-28 and £0.0073/MWh from 2028. EII sites with a valid DBT certificate are exempt.
- **Error if omitted:** Understates charging cost by 0.35–0.47 p/kWh and rising, and understates the escalation of the flat levy stack over the underwriting horizon. A model calibrated on a pre-December-2025 bill will silently omit it.
- **How to implement:** rab[t] = rab_ilr_gbp_per_MWh[quarter(t)]/10 + rab_op_levy_gbp_per_MWh[fy(t)]/10 p/kWh on metered import; zero for EII-certified supply. Treat as a rising escalator in the long-run cost curve.
- *Source:* The Electricity Supplier Payments (Amendment) Regulations 2026 (Nuclear RAB operational costs levy: £0.0063/MWh 2026-27, £0.0068/MWh 2027-28), https://www.legislationtracker.co.uk/article/the-electricity-supplier-payments-amendment-regulations-2026-25-03-26
- *Source:* Utility Bidder, Nuclear RAB Levy and UK business energy bills, 15 April 2026 (industry explainer — labelled second-tier; rates £3.494/MWh Dec 2025, £3.663/MWh Q1 2026, £4.683/MWh Q2 2026), https://www.utilitybidder.co.uk/business-electricity/nuclear-rab-levy-uk-business-energy-bills/


### G. Contracts, route to market and eligibility

#### G-01 · MUST · Make the supply contract's pass-through structure an explicit, mandatory, first-class model input, and compute savings against the CONTRACT, not against the published tariff.

- **Why:** Every avoidance claim in this lens is conditional on the customer's contract exposing the underlying charge. A fully-fixed (all-inclusive) C&I contract converts DUoS red-band, TNUoS triad and CM peak charges into a flat p/kWh that dispatch cannot touch. A pass-through contract exposes them but usually also passes through the fixed residual charges (which cannot be avoided anyway).
- **Error if omitted:** The most common and most expensive failure mode in BTM underwriting: a full network-charge savings stack modelled for a customer on a fixed contract, where realised saving is the wholesale/shape component only. Overstatement of 50–100% of claimed network savings is routine.
- **How to implement:** Contract object with per-component flags: {duos_unit: passthrough|fixed, duos_fixed: passthrough|fixed, duos_capacity: passthrough|fixed, tnuos_locational: passthrough|fixed, tnuos_residual: passthrough|fixed, bsuos: passthrough|fixed, cm: peak_based|flat|fixed, ccl: passthrough, levies: passthrough|fixed, wholesale: index|fixed_shape}. Multiply each saving term by its flag. Add a hard validation gate: refuse to produce a P50 without a populated contract object, and report the saving decomposition by contract component.
- *Source:* Ofgem, Targeted Charging Review: Decision and Impact Assessment, 2019 (rationale: fixed residuals are non-avoidable by consumption, so only pass-through of forward-looking elements can be signalled to customers), https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/decision/targeted-charging-review-decision-and-impact-assessment
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, section 2 (charges are levied on the Supplier/User, not the end customer), https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677

#### G-02 · MUST · Test that contracted (floor/toll/Capacity Market) revenue alone covers full debt service — a contracted-only DSCR of at least 1.0x — and report the contracted share of total revenue as a first-class output.

- **Why:** This is the explicit GB market test. Gresham House states that its 789 MW floor package means 'future debt servicing (interest and principal repayments) will be fully covered from cashflow derived from contracted revenues'. Econergy's Immingham floor covers c.50% of expected total revenues over ten years with EDF. DNV records that financiers seek contracted revenue for bankability while developers rely on merchant optimisation, and that Capacity Market contracts provide long-term availability-based income that 'directly supports debt structuring'.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that reports only a blended DSCR cannot answer the first question a credit committee asks. It will present as bankable a structure with 1.5x blended DSCR where 80% of the cashflow is merchant, which no GB lender is currently financing on a non-recourse basis at that leverage.
- **How to implement:** Add outputs: ContractedDSCR_n = ContractedCFADS_n / DebtService_n, where ContractedCFADS excludes all merchant wholesale, BM and non-contracted ancillary revenue and excludes merchant upside above a floor; and ContractedShare_n = ContractedRevenue_n / TotalRevenue_n. Gate the debt case on min_n(ContractedDSCR_n) >= 1.0x, or state explicitly that the structure is a merchant financing and reduce leverage accordingly. GB reference points: Gresham House contracted revenue was 39.4% of total in 2025 (£27,000/MW/yr of £68,600/MW/yr), up from 24.7% in 2024 and 22.5% in 2023.
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Long-term floor agreements agreed on 789MW of battery projects', 2025 — 789MW of a 1,072MW portfolio securing minimum annual contracted revenues of £35m over ten years, https://greshamhouse.com/news-media/long-term-floor-agreements-agreed-on-789mw-of-battery-projects/
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf
- *Source:* Solar Power Portal, 'Santander provides debt finance for Econergy, Brockwell BESS', 2026, https://www.solarpowerportal.co.uk/battery-storage/santander-provides-debt-finance-for-econergy-brockwell-energy-bess
- *Source:* DNV, 'Financing the energy transition: building bankable battery projects', https://www.dnv.com/article/financing-energy-transition-building-bankable-battery-projects/

#### G-03 · MUST · Assess the warranty and performance-guarantee package as part of the security analysis: manufacturer covenant strength, direct recourse for the lender, collateral warranties, and whether the guarantee survives the intended duty cycle.

- **Why:** The legal literature sets out the test explicitly: reputation and survival of the manufacturer over the warranty term, whether the lender has a direct claim (a collateral warranty may be required), and whether the manufacturer will confirm the warranty applies to the intended non-standard use. A warranty from a supplier that will not exist in year 10 is not security.
- **Error if omitted:** The model will value a capacity-retention guarantee at face value when it may be worth nothing, and will fail to reserve for the augmentation that a failed warranty would require the project to self-fund.
- **How to implement:** Add a diligence gate with a boolean/score per item: {manufacturer credit rating or parent guarantee, warranty term vs debt tenor, collateral warranty in favour of lender, cycle and DoD envelope vs modelled duty, capacity retention guarantee level and test method, availability guarantee and liquidated damages, LTSA term and step-in}. Where the score fails, either add an augmentation reserve sized to self-fund the guaranteed capacity shortfall or reduce the SoH assumption to an unwarranted level.
- *Source:* Osborne Clarke / Practical Law, 'Structuring a bankable project: energy storage', 2022 — 'Value of warranties' checklist, https://www.osborneclarke.com/system/files/documents/22/06/15/Structuring%20a%20bankable%20project%20energy%20storage%20(w-025-5057)%202022.pdf
- *Source:* Morgan Lewis, 'Project Financing Trends for Global Energy Storage Projects in 2026 and Beyond', 2026 — performance guarantees and equipment warranties must be assigned to the project company; software access and IP rights transferable or in escrow, https://www.morganlewis.com/pubs/2026/03/project-financing-trends-for-global-energy-storage-projects-in-2026-and-beyond
- *Source:* DNV, 'Financing the energy transition: building bankable battery projects', https://www.dnv.com/article/financing-energy-transition-building-bankable-battery-projects/

#### G-04 · MUST · Model floor and tolling structures explicitly, including the upside-sharing mechanics, the counterparty's credit standing and the contract term relative to the debt tenor.

- **Why:** These are now the dominant GB de-risking instruments. Gresham House had 528 MW under two-year tolls with Octopus and 939 MW under ten-year floors with investment-grade counterparties (Statkraft Markets GmbH, Markel Bermuda, and EDF), plus floors on 637 MW of new pipeline 'to underpin their financing'. Under a toll the toller takes operational control for a fixed periodic fee per MW; under a floor the project keeps merchant upside above the floor with revenue shared.
- **Error if omitted:** A model with only 'merchant' and 'contracted' as flat categories cannot value the structure that actually gets the deal financed, and cannot answer the central BTM question of how much upside to give away to reach a bankable floor.
- **How to implement:** Toll: Revenue_t = TollFee_per_MW x P_MW x Availability_t, with the optimiser's dispatch decisions removed (the toller controls dispatch), subject to availability KPIs and liquidated damages for underperformance. Floor: Revenue_t = max(FloorLevel_t, MerchantRevenue_t) with a share mechanism above the floor, i.e. Revenue_t = FloorLevel_t + ShareRate x max(0, MerchantRevenue_t - FloorLevel_t). Indexation: state whether the floor is nominal or CPI-linked. GB reference: 789 MW securing minimum annual contracted revenues of £35m, i.e. approximately £44,355/MW/yr over ten years. Apply a counterparty credit adjustment (or a lower discount rate for investment-grade, as Gresham House does at 7.5% 'reflecting the investment-grade nature of the counterparties and the length of contracts').
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Long-term floor agreements agreed on 789MW of battery projects', 2025 — Statkraft Markets GmbH and Markel Bermuda Limited, both investment grade, ten-year term, https://greshamhouse.com/news-media/long-term-floor-agreements-agreed-on-789mw-of-battery-projects/
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — toll definition, floors on 939MW operational plus 637MW pipeline, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf
- *Source:* EDF, 'EDF signs landmark long-term floor pricing agreements with Gresham House Energy Storage Fund', 2025, https://www.edfenergy.com/media-centre/edf-signs-landmark-long-term-floor-pricing-agreements-gresham-house-energy-storage-fund
- *Source:* Solar Power Portal, 'Santander provides debt finance for Econergy, Brockwell BESS', 2026 — ten-year EDF floor, revenue-sharing above the floor, https://www.solarpowerportal.co.uk/battery-storage/santander-provides-debt-finance-for-econergy-brockwell-energy-bess

#### G-05 · MUST · Model the optimiser / route-to-market agreement explicitly: fee basis, term, termination rights, availability and performance guarantees, and any floor embedded in it.

- **Why:** Route to market is not free. Gresham House notes that third-party optimiser contracts increasingly include floors (minimum revenue per MW guarantees) 'which underpin our debt funding for building new assets, whilst in the case of floors, giving away only a small share with optimisers'. Gore Street internalised optimisation and attributes a c.£9,500/MW/yr revenue advantage to doing so (c.£81,900 vs the c.£72,400/MW/yr GB market average).
- **Error if omitted:** Omitting the optimiser fee overstates net revenue by a several-percent share of gross. Omitting the term mismatch (a 2-3 year optimiser contract against a 7-15 year debt) hides a repricing risk that lenders will require to be addressed.
- **How to implement:** Deduct OptimiserFee_t = FixedFee_per_MW + SharePct x (Revenue_t - Floor_t) before CFADS. Model the contract term and an assumed repricing at expiry. If the optimiser provides a floor, treat that floor as contracted revenue only to the extent the optimiser's credit supports it. Report gross revenue, optimiser fee and net revenue as separate lines.
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf
- *Source:* Gore Street Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Audited Full Year Results to 31 March 2025', 2025 — GSET manages 68% of the GB portfolio; c.£81,900/MW/yr vs GB market c.£72,400/MW/yr, https://www.gsenergystoragefund.com/docs/librariesprovider22/archive/results/prelims-2025.pdf

#### G-06 · MUST · For any solar-co-located site, test BEFORE financial close whether adding the battery jeopardises the EXISTING PV's own FiT, RO, REGO or SEG accreditation, and encode the resulting metering topology as a hard constraint on the dispatch feasible set. Capture the PV's accreditation status, Total Installed Capacity, generation and export meter positions, and whether the battery can be charged from the grid. Where grid charging is possible, require either (a) the battery to sit after the generation meter / be chargeable only from the PV, or (b) a single approved bi-directional meter that itself computes and displays a net value. Obtain written confirmation from the FiT licensee and notify the change to the licensee and, for ROO-FIT, on the Register.

- **Why:** Ofgem's co-location guidance is explicit that support may only be claimed for eligible renewable electricity and that 'there must be no risk that support is claimed for, and issued upon, electricity that was not generated by the accredited generating station or installation', which expressly includes 'electricity imported from the grid'. The metering rule is unusually strict and is the thing that catches projects: 'The generation meter reading cannot be calculated using separate input and output meters to calculate a net output value' (para 3.8) and 'this measurement must be undertaken by a single meter that can perform a net calculation. Using multiple meters to calculate a net output value is not permitted' (para 3.14), with the same rule repeated for export (paras 3.19, 3.25). If the licensee cannot be satisfied, 'the generator will not be entitled to receive generation payments' (3.16) and 'will not be entitled to export payments' (3.29). Ofgem also warns that a compliant bi-directional set-up computes month-on-month differences, so 'If the difference between import and export in one month is negative, zero FITs payments could be claimed' (3.27) and that round-trip losses mean 'FIT payments are likely to be reduced' (3.28). A large share of GB C&I rooftop PV was accredited under FiT before the scheme closed to new applicants and is still receiving index-linked generation payments.
- **Error if omitted:** The host loses FiT generation and/or export income for the remainder of a 20-year index-linked term — a loss that lands entirely on the host, is not in the SPV's model, and poisons the deal irrespective of the battery's own economics. Ofgem may also withdraw accreditation and recover overpayments. Operationally, the compliant configurations impose a hard dispatch constraint: if the only compliant topology is 'battery chargeable only from the PV', overnight grid-charged arbitrage is eliminated from the feasible set entirely, which removes the single largest wholesale revenue line in most BTM cases. NOTE the panel's deemed-export concern is materially overstated for C&I: deemed export at 50% (solar) or 75% (hydro) is available only to installations 'with a TIC of 30kW or less' (paras 3.17, 3.4), so a typical C&I rooftop array is on metered export and the relevant risk is loss of METERED export payments, not distortion of a deemed percentage.
- **How to implement:** Add a pre-optimisation gate with four inputs: (1) PV accreditation scheme and status (FiT / RO / REGO / SEG / none) and accreditation date; (2) PV Total Installed Capacity in kW (drives whether deemed export is even in play at the 30 kW threshold); (3) the generation and export meter positions relative to the proposed battery; (4) whether the battery is physically capable of importing from the grid. Decision logic: if scheme in {FiT, RO} AND battery can grid-charge AND battery is upstream of the generation meter AND there is no single approved bi-directional net-calculating meter, then set grid_charge_allowed = FALSE and add the constraint P_grid_charge(t) = 0 for all t, OR add the capex of a compliant bi-directional meter (or of relocating the battery downstream of the generation meter) to the build case and re-solve. Model both branches and report the delta — this is the cost of FiT compliance. Also model the round-trip-loss reduction in claimable generation under a bi-directional set-up as a direct deduction from the host's FiT income, and the monthly-netting risk that a net-negative month yields zero FiT. Documentation gates before financial close: written FiT licensee confirmation; notification recorded per the Statement of FIT Terms; ROO-FIT accreditation amended on the Register; wiring diagram and battery details supplied (Ofgem Appendix 1 declarations). Separately, feed the PV's accreditation status into the Capacity Market Low Carbon Exclusion test — the accreditation status is the input that triggers it.
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Guidance for generators: Co-location of electricity storage and hydrogen production under the RO, FIT, REGO and SEG', Version 6.2, May 2025, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2025-06/co_location_guidance_for_generators_v6.2_20250528135013.pdf — paras 1.15 (no support for grid-imported electricity), 1.17 (storage forms part of a FIT installation's geographical site), 3.8-3.16 (generation meter rules), 3.17-3.18 (deemed export, TIC 30kW or less, 50% solar / 75% hydro), 3.19-3.25 (metered export rules), 3.26-3.30 (bi-directional meters; most smart meters do not qualify), 3.31-3.33 (notification to FIT Licensee and ROO-FIT Register), 1.7 (withdrawal of accreditation), Appendix 1 (Battery Storage Installation Declarations), Appendix 3 (SEG)
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Feed-in Tariffs: guidance for generators', https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/feed-tariffs-guidance-fit-generators
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Renewables Obligation: Guidance for Generators', https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications-and-updates/renewables-obligation-guidance-generators

#### G-07 · MUST · Convert the regulatory-change register into a change-in-law ALLOCATION MATRIX: for every identified change, name the bearing party across the host agreement, the floor/toll, the optimiser agreement and the O&M contract, and only then decide whether it reaches the SPV's cashflow. Additionally require the model to capture the equity-side terms that a revenue distribution cannot show — host buy-out options at a formula price, termination-for-convenience rights, benchmarking and price-review clauses, and any guaranteed-savings undertaking given to the host — and to stop treating Capacity Market revenue as firm.

- **Why:** A register that lists a regulatory change tells you the risk exists but not who pays for it, and only the latter determines whether it hits the SPV. Three of these terms are invisible to a P50/P90 by construction: a host buy-out at a formula price is a hard cap on equity upside (the distribution's right tail is truncated at the formula, so the mean of the distribution is not the mean of the equity return); a termination-for-convenience right is an option written by the SPV to the host; and a guaranteed-savings undertaking is an unhedged written put on the developer, because if the model over-forecasts, the developer pays the difference in cash — model error becomes a balance-sheet liability rather than a forecast miss. On the Capacity Market, the precedent is documented and recent: the General Court annulled the Commission's State aid approval in Tempus Energy Ltd and Tempus Energy Technology Ltd v European Commission (Case T-793/14) on 15 November 2018; the UK operated the scheme without payments to agreement holders during the ensuing standstill; the Commission re-approved the scheme on 24 October 2019 (SA.35980, Commission Decision (EU) 2020/348); the Court of Justice later set aside the General Court judgment on 2 September 2021 (C-57/19 P). A revenue line that stopped for roughly eleven months within living memory is not an annuity.
- **Error if omitted:** Three separate mispricings. (1) Change-in-law risk is either double-counted (assumed to hit the SPV when a pass-through clause protects it) or ignored (assumed passed through when it is not) — and without the matrix you cannot tell which. (2) Equity IRR is overstated whenever a buy-out formula caps the upside, because the model reports the mean of an untruncated distribution. (3) A guaranteed-savings undertaking converts the model's own error into a cash liability with no offsetting asset; the more aggressive the revenue forecast, the larger the written put, which is a perverse incentive that no revenue distribution will reveal. (4) Treating CM as firm removes a documented ~11-month total-suspension event from the tail; a DSCR sized without it has no cover for the repeat.
- **How to implement:** Build an explicit matrix: rows are identified regulatory changes (levy reform, DUoS band redefinition, TCR band changes, ancillary product redesign, CM rule changes, environmental permitting for BESS, SSES/NIS scope, market-wide half-hourly settlement, wider market reform); columns are the four contracts; each cell records bearer (SPV / host / floor provider / optimiser / O&M contractor), the clause reference, and whether the pass-through is automatic, subject to negotiation, or absent. Only changes whose bearer is the SPV enter the cashflow model. Model the equity-side options directly: a host buy-out at a formula price is a call written to the host — cap the equity distribution at the formula value and report the truncated mean; a termination-for-convenience right is a further truncation with its own notice period and any break fee; a guaranteed-savings undertaking is modelled as max(0, guaranteed - realised) paid in cash each period, with its expected value and its 95th percentile both reported as a liability line, not netted into revenue. Capacity Market: add a suspension scenario in which capacity payments stop entirely for 12 months at some point in the agreement term, calibrated to the 15 November 2018 to 24 October 2019 precedent, and report the minimum DSCR under it; note that post-Brexit the scheme sits under the Subsidy Control Act 2022 with a challenge route to the Competition Appeal Tribunal, so the mechanism of a repeat differs but the exposure persists. Documentation gate: no bankable output without the executed host agreement, floor/toll, optimisation agreement and O&M contract read and their change-in-law, benchmarking, buy-out, termination-for-convenience and guaranteed-savings clauses mapped into the matrix.
- *Source:* Judgment of the General Court (Third Chamber, Extended Composition) of 15 November 2018, Tempus Energy Ltd and Tempus Energy Technology Ltd v European Commission, Case T-793/14, ECLI:EU:T:2018:790, https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=CELEX:62014TJ0793
- *Source:* European Commission, State aid SA.35980 — GB Capacity Market, positive decision of 24 October 2019; Commission Decision (EU) 2020/348, https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/dec/2020/348/oj/eng; announcement https://ec.europa.eu/newsroom/comp/items/661082/
- *Source:* Court of Justice, Case C-57/19 P, judgment of 2 September 2021 setting aside the General Court's judgment in T-793/14
- *Source:* Florence School of Regulation (EUI), 'The GB capacity market standstill: what is happening and why', https://fsr.eui.eu/the-gb-capacity-market-standstill-what-is-happening-and-why-2/ — UK confirmed in December 2018 it would 'operate the capacity market as normal, but without payments being made to agreement holders'; Commission opened in-depth investigation 21 February 2019 [academic/regulatory commentary]
- *Source:* Subsidy Control Act 2022, https://www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/2022/23 — domestic subsidy control regime and the Competition Appeal Tribunal review route
- *Source:* The executed host agreement, floor/toll, optimisation agreement and O&M contract — the only authoritative sources for allocation; must be read per project [project-specific]

#### G-08 · SHOULD · For a BTM asset, model the host/site agreement as a bankability gate: lease or licence term at least equal to debt tenor plus tail, security of tenure, step-in rights, and what happens on host insolvency, site closure or change of occupier.

- **Why:** In a BTM structure the site host is simultaneously landlord, offtaker of the avoided-cost value, and operational counterparty. The private-wire literature identifies 'suitable sites with planning, grid connection and a creditworthy offtaker' as the key requirements, and the general project finance framework requires a lease of comparable length to the financing with appropriate consents. Osborne Clarke notes leases of around 20-30 years for storage projects, with re-energisation (cell replacement) needed within that period.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that assumes a 15-year revenue stream on a 5-year licence with no step-in is underwriting a cashflow that can be terminated by the host's landlord, administrator or a change of occupier. This is a binary, un-hedged risk that no DSCR captures, and it is unique to BTM.
- **How to implement:** Add hard gates: (a) LeaseTerm >= DebtTenor + Tail; (b) lease registered and with landlord consent/waiver where the host is itself a tenant; (c) direct agreement with the host's landlord and financier granting lender step-in; (d) rights of access and egress for O&M and augmentation. Model host risk explicitly: HostSurvival_t probability, and a scenario where the host ceases operation at year k, after which BTM avoided-cost revenue falls to zero and only export-to-grid revenue remains (which requires an export MPAN and DNO connection agreement that may not exist). Report the NPV under host-exit at years 5, 10 and 15.
- *Source:* Osborne Clarke / Practical Law, 'Structuring a bankable project: energy storage', 2022 — lease of around 20 to 30 years with re-energisations during the period; lenders' concerns on project agreement framework and counterparty balance sheets, https://www.osborneclarke.com/system/files/documents/22/06/15/Structuring%20a%20bankable%20project%20energy%20storage%20(w-025-5057)%202022.pdf
- *Source:* Walker Morris, 'Private wire power purchase agreements' — creditworthy offtaker, site, planning and grid connection as the key requirements, https://www.walkermorris.co.uk/comment-opinion/private-wire-power-purchase-agreements/

#### G-09 · SHOULD · Model the host's electricity SUPPLY CONTRACT as a contract, not as a rate card. Require the executed contract and extract: the volume tolerance band and the change-of-consumption / change-of-load clause; the reconciliation or mark-to-market formula applied when actual offtake leaves the band; the change-in-law clause; and the contract end date. Then run a mandatory scenario in which the pass-through structure disappears at first renewal (typically 1-3 years into a 10-15 year model) and the site moves onto a fully fixed all-in p/kWh, so that DUoS red-band, Triad and Capacity Market supplier-charge savings all become unavailable.

- **Why:** A battery is precisely a device for changing a site's volume and shape, and fixed-price C&I supply contracts price a forecast volume and levy a reconciliation charge when actual consumption moves outside a tolerance band. Industry sources report that 'Most suppliers have an arbitrary 80/120 threshold set', i.e. plus or minus 20% of forecast consumption. A peak-shaving and arbitrage duty cycle breaches the band in both directions simultaneously — less volume in red-band hours, more volume overnight — and the resulting change-of-consumption charge is a direct deduction from the savings the model claims. The second point is structural rather than numerical: the model correctly makes the pass-through structure a first-class input, then implicitly holds it constant for 10-15 years, when in reality it is re-tendered every 1-3 years and the host is free to buy a flat all-in price.
- **Error if omitted:** Two unmodelled deductions, both one-directional. (1) Change-of-consumption charges are a pure subtraction from the savings line that the model never books; they can be triggered by the battery's own successful operation, which makes them perverse — the better the optimiser performs, the more likely the breach. (2) The renewal risk is existential for the avoided-cost case: if the host renews onto a fixed all-in p/kWh with no pass-through, the DUoS red-band arbitrage, Triad avoidance and CM supplier-charge savings are no longer visible to the host and cannot be shared, so the entire avoided-cost case reprices toward zero from year 2 or 3 of a 15-year model. A P50/P90 built on the current contract's structure is not a distribution over that outcome — it does not contain it at all.
- **How to implement:** Extract from the executed contract: tolerance band as lower and upper multipliers of forecast annual volume (default 0.80 and 1.20 where the contract is silent, flagged as an assumption); the reconciliation formula (typically the difference between contracted and market price applied to the out-of-band volume, so it is a mark-to-market and can be positive or negative); shape/profile charges; the change-in-law clause; and the termination date. Add a constraint or a penalty to the dispatch objective: annual site volume V must satisfy L*V_forecast <= V <= U*V_forecast, implemented either as a hard constraint (which is what a risk-averse host will actually require) or as a penalty at the contractual reconciliation rate — model both and report the difference, because the hard constraint may bind on the optimal duty cycle. Scenario layer (mandatory, not optional): at the contract end date, branch into (a) renewal on an equivalent pass-through structure, (b) renewal on a fully fixed all-in tariff with all pass-through savings set to zero, (c) renewal on a partial pass-through. Weight the branches, and report the P50 and P90 under the weighted set. Underwriting gate: refuse to book pass-through-dependent savings beyond the current contract's end date without either a contractual commitment from the host to maintain a pass-through structure, or an explicit haircut. Also confirm whether the site is Half-Hourly Settled and on Profile Class 3/4 or not, since DFS registration for an I&C Boundary Meter requires HH settlement.
- *Source:* Cost Advice, 'Volume tolerance clauses', https://www.costadvice.co.uk/latest-news/volume-tolerance-clauses/ — 'Most suppliers have an arbitrary 80/120 threshold set' [energy consultancy, secondary]
- *Source:* Meet George, 'Volume tolerance: the cheapest contract trap for SMEs', https://meetgeorge.co.uk/blog/volume-tolerance-cheapest-contract-trap-sme — consumption 'typically within an 80-120% band' [broker commentary, secondary]
- *Source:* EDF Energy (Large Business), 'No crying over spilt milk: how volume tolerance works in electricity contracts', https://www.edfenergy.com/large-business/talk-power/blogs/no-crying-over-spilt-milk-how-volume-tolerance-works-electricity-contracts [supplier commentary, secondary]
- *Source:* Purely Energy, 'Volume tolerance for business energy and key factors to review', https://www.purelyenergy.co.uk/energy-hub/volume-tolerance-for-business-energy-and-key-factors-to-review [broker, secondary]
- *Source:* The host's executed supply contract — the only authoritative source for the actual band and formula; must be obtained per project [project-specific]
- *Source:* NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service Procurement Rules', v5.0, para 4.7.2 — I&C Boundary Meters must be Half-Hourly Settled unless Profile Class 3 or 4 (relevant to which supply arrangements permit DFS participation)

#### G-10 · CONSIDER · Do not assume the availability price is earned in full: model utilisation, performance/availability derating and any penalty regime for contracted ancillary services.

- **Why:** NESO products pay availability plus, in some cases, utilisation, and are subject to performance monitoring. NESO submitted an EBR Article 18 consultation to Ofgem on 1 May 2026 proposing changes to the Dynamic Response Services aimed at improving 'operational effectiveness and performance monitoring', with a decision expected July 2026 — the penalty regime is actively changing.
- **Error if omitted:** Availability revenue is booked gross of non-delivery penalties and derating, and the model is blind to a live regulatory change that could alter that regime within the underwriting horizon.
- **How to implement:** revenue_s = r_{s,w} * price_{s,w} * dt * availability_factor_s - penalty_s(non_delivery). Model availability_factor from the site's own forced-outage assumption and from the chance-constrained violation probability epsilon derived above, so the two are consistent. Flag the pending EBR Article 18 decision as an underwriting risk with a defined sensitivity.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Dynamic Containment' — NESO submitted an EBR Article 18 consultation to Ofgem on 1 May 2026 proposing changes to the Dynamic Response Services to improve operational effectiveness and performance monitoring, decision expected July 2026, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/frequency-response-services/dynamic-containment
- *Source:* NESO, 'Balancing Reserve' — providers compensated through both availability and utilisation payments, dispatched via Bid Offer Acceptance in the Balancing Mechanism, https://www.neso.energy/industry-information/balancing-services/reserve-services/balancing-reserve


### H. Risk quantification, P50/P90 and bankability

#### H-01 · MUST · Exclude VAT entirely from the savings calculation for a VAT-registered business.

- **Why:** Business supplies of electricity are standard-rated at 20% but input VAT is recoverable by a VAT-registered business making taxable supplies. The 5% reduced rate applies only to qualifying use (domestic, charity non-business) or de minimis supplies of 'not more than an average rate of 33 kilowatt hours per day, 1,000 kilowatt hours per month' — thresholds far below a 0.1–5 MW C&I site.
- **Error if omitted:** Including 20% VAT in avoided cost overstates the entire savings stack by 20%. Including it in capex without netting the recoverable input tax overstates project cost by 20%. Both errors are common and both are material to IRR.
- **How to implement:** vat_multiplier = 1.0 for a VAT-registered taxable trader (savings and capex modelled net of VAT); only set 1.20 (or 1.05) where the counterparty is a charity, partially-exempt body, or de minimis consumer. Surface as an explicit flag in the underwriting pack.
- *Source:* HMRC, VAT on fuel and power (Notice 701/19), https://www.gov.uk/guidance/vat-on-fuel-and-power-notice-70119

#### H-02 · MUST · Report the savings decomposition line by line with each line tagged 'avoided by reducing import in a specific half-hour', 'avoided only by reducing peak kVA', or 'not avoidable', and require the not-avoidable lines to sum to zero benefit.

- **Why:** This is the audit control that catches every error in this lens at once. The correct 2026/27 classification is: DUoS unit rate — avoidable per half-hour; DUoS capacity charge — avoidable only by reducing agreed MIC (not by dispatch); DUoS exceeded capacity — avoidable by not breaching MIC; DUoS fixed/residual — not avoidable; TNUoS locational HH — avoidable only in the three Triad half-hours, and only in zones 8–14; TNUoS residual — not avoidable; BSUoS — flat, so only the round-trip loss matters; CM supplier charge — avoidable in 16:00–19:00 working weekdays Nov–Feb if and only if the contract passes it through on that basis; CCL/RO/FiT/CfD/AAHEDC/RAB/EII — flat, net cost via losses; VAT — not a cost to a VAT-registered business; standing charges — not avoidable.
- **Error if omitted:** Without this classification, non-avoidable charges leak into the savings stack. Given that the fixed residuals now make up the majority of the TNUoS demand charge (NESO: 'The demand residual standing charges make up the majority of the TNUoS demand charge') and are rising at ~60%/yr, this leakage is the dominant overstatement risk in GB BTM underwriting in 2026.
- **How to implement:** Emit a table with columns [component, 2026/27 rate, unit, basis, avoidable_by_HH_import_reduction (bool), avoidable_by_kVA_reduction (bool), charged_on_battery_charging (bool), modelled_annual_saving_£]. Add assertions: for every row with avoidable_by_HH_import_reduction == False and avoidable_by_kVA_reduction == False, assert modelled_annual_saving == 0. Publish this table in the lender pack.
- *Source:* NESO, January 2026 Final TNUoS Tariffs for 2026/27, Demand Tariffs Summary, https://www.neso.energy/document/376336/download
- *Source:* National Grid Electricity Distribution (East Midlands) plc, Use of System Charging Statement effective 1 April 2026, Annex 1, https://commercial.nationalgrid.co.uk/downloads-view-reciteme/684677
- *Source:* Ofgem, Targeted Charging Review: Decision and Impact Assessment, 2019, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/decision/targeted-charging-review-decision-and-impact-assessment

#### H-03 · MUST · Size senior debt by sculpting against CFADS at a target DSCR, and take the binding minimum of the DSCR-sized amount and any gearing cap. Never assert gearing as the primary sizing input.

- **Why:** Every project-finance methodology source describes debt capacity as a function of cashflow, not of capital cost. Lenders lend against CFADS; a gearing percentage is a secondary constraint, and for merchant storage it is the DSCR that binds.
- **Error if omitted:** Asserting e.g. 60% gearing on a merchant BTM asset will overstate debt capacity, understate equity requirement and flatter equity IRR. Where merchant DSCR requirements are 1.40x-2.00x, the DSCR-implied gearing on an uncontracted GB BESS is typically far below the asserted number, so the error is one-directional: equity IRR is overstated.
- **How to implement:** For each debt period n: MaxDebtService_n = CFADS_n / TargetDSCR. DebtCapacity = sum_{n=1..T} MaxDebtService_n / (1+i)^n, where i is the period debt interest rate and T is the debt tenor in periods. Then Debt = min(DebtCapacity, GearingCap x TotalProjectCost). Principal_n = DebtService_n - Interest_n, solved iteratively because Interest_n depends on the opening balance (resolve the circularity by iteration or by an explicit sculpting algorithm).
- *Source:* GreenBridge Infrastructure, 'Debt Sizing', https://greenbridgeinfra.com/resources/project-finance/debt-sizing
- *Source:* Financial Modelling Handbook, 'Debt sizing and debt sculpting', https://www.financialmodellinghandbook.org/debt-sizing-and-debt-sculpting/
- *Source:* Corporate Finance Institute, 'Cash Flow Available for Debt Service (CFADS)', https://corporatefinanceinstitute.com/resources/commercial-lending/cash-flow-available-for-debt-service-cfads/

#### H-04 · MUST · Define CFADS explicitly and completely for a storage asset, and expose the definition as an auditable line-by-line build rather than an EBITDA proxy.

- **Why:** Storage CFADS differs from a generator's: charging cost is a cash operating cost that scales with dispatch, optimiser/route-to-market fees are revenue-share, augmentation is periodic maintenance capex that must sit above the debt-service line, and imbalance/settlement cash lags trading cash.
- **Error if omitted:** Using EBITDA as a CFADS proxy omits maintenance/augmentation capex, tax, working-capital swings and reserve movements. For storage the augmentation omission alone is material: augmenting 129.8 MW from 1h to 2h was budgeted at £18-22m, i.e. roughly £139-169/kWh of incremental energy capacity, which is a real cash call inside the debt term.
- **How to implement:** CFADS_n = (Gross revenue by stream) - (charging energy cost incl. all levies and network charges) - (optimiser/trading fee, incl. revenue share) - (fixed O&M, LTSA) - (insurance) - (business rates) - (land/lease rent) - (metering, comms, DNO/NESO fees) - (SPV admin) - (corporation tax paid) -/+ (change in working capital) - (maintenance and augmentation capex) -/+ (movements in reserve accounts, excluding the DSRA which sits below the line). Exclude interest and principal. State whether CFADS is pre- or post-tax and be consistent with the DSCR definition in the term sheet.
- *Source:* Corporate Finance Institute, 'Cash Flow Available for Debt Service (CFADS)', https://corporatefinanceinstitute.com/resources/commercial-lending/cash-flow-available-for-debt-service-cfads/
- *Source:* GreenBridge Infrastructure, 'Debt Sizing', https://greenbridgeinfra.com/resources/project-finance/debt-sizing
- *Source:* Gore Street Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Audited Full Year Results to 31 March 2025', 2025 (augmentation budget £18-22m for Stony 79.9MW and Ferrymuir 49.9MW, 1h to 2h), https://www.gsenergystoragefund.com/docs/librariesprovider22/archive/results/prelims-2025.pdf

#### H-05 · MUST · Model DSCR twice: once as the sizing ratio and once as a tested covenant with distinct lock-up and event-of-default thresholds, computed on the lender's calculation periods (typically semi-annual, backward-looking and forward-looking).

- **Why:** Sizing DSCR and covenant DSCR are different numbers with different consequences. Gresham House's facility requires semi-annual covenant compliance certificates and its covenant package explicitly includes debt service cover.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that reports only an average or minimum sizing DSCR cannot tell the investor when distributions are trapped. Distribution lock-up is the mechanism by which equity IRR actually degrades in a downside, and omitting it makes the equity case look far more resilient than it is.
- **How to implement:** Compute DSCR_period = CFADS_period / (Interest_period + Scheduled principal_period) on each lender test date. Add: (a) a distribution lock-up test (block dividends if DSCR < lockup, typically sizing DSCR minus 0.10-0.15x, or if forward-looking DSCR fails); (b) an event-of-default threshold (typically ~1.05-1.10x); (c) backward-looking historic DSCR and forward-looking projected DSCR as separate tests. Report the minimum, average and the first breach period.
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — Holdco 1 facility, semi-annual covenant compliance certificates, covenants include debt service cover, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf
- *Source:* Osborne Clarke / Practical Law, 'Structuring a bankable project: energy storage', 2022, https://www.osborneclarke.com/system/files/documents/22/06/15/Structuring%20a%20bankable%20project%20energy%20storage%20(w-025-5057)%202022.pdf

#### H-06 · MUST · Include a cash sweep on merchant cashflow above the base case, and treat windfall revenue as debt prepayment rather than as equity distribution.

- **Why:** This is the specific structural enhancement lenders demand for storage. Both the GB legal literature and the 2026 US storage financing literature list additional cash sweep mechanisms as a condition of financing merchant cashflow.
- **Error if omitted:** Modelling upside revenue as immediately distributable to equity inflates equity IRR and understates the effective cost of debt. It also mis-states the debt balance profile, which feeds back into interest, DSCR and refinancing exposure.
- **How to implement:** Sweep_n = SweepPct x max(0, CFADS_n - CFADS_basecase_n) applied after scheduled debt service and before distributions; reduce the outstanding principal and re-solve the amortisation schedule. Model SweepPct as a term-sheet parameter (commonly 50-100% for merchant tranches) and allow it to step up if DSCR or leverage tests are missed. Cap the merchant component of base-case revenue that is credited to CFADS for sizing purposes, with the excess flowing only to the sweep.
- *Source:* Osborne Clarke / Practical Law, 'Structuring a bankable project: energy storage', 2022, https://www.osborneclarke.com/system/files/documents/22/06/15/Structuring%20a%20bankable%20project%20energy%20storage%20(w-025-5057)%202022.pdf
- *Source:* Morgan Lewis, 'Project Financing Trends for Global Energy Storage Projects in 2026 and Beyond', 2026, https://www.morganlewis.com/pubs/2026/03/project-financing-trends-for-global-energy-storage-projects-in-2026-and-beyond

#### H-07 · MUST · Use a debt tenor materially shorter than the asset life, with a front-loaded (sculpted or annuity-plus-sweep) repayment profile, and enforce an explicit debt tail.

- **Why:** The GB legal literature specifically records that lenders require 'shorter tenors than seen in a typical renewables project' and 'more front-loaded repayment profiles' for storage. Gresham House's 2025 facility has a seven-year term against a fourteen-year amortisation profile — i.e. a bullet/refinancing structure, not full amortisation.
- **Error if omitted:** Modelling a 15-20 year fully amortising facility against a 15-year asset overstates debt capacity dramatically and hides refinancing risk. It also produces a debt tail of zero, which no lender will accept on a merchant asset.
- **How to implement:** Set Tenor <= AssetLife - Tail, with Tail typically 3-5 years for merchant infrastructure. Model a mini-perm explicitly: scheduled amortisation on a longer notional profile (e.g. 14 years) with a balloon at the term date (e.g. year 7), a refinancing assumption at the balloon with its own margin and fee, and a cash-sweep step-up if refinancing is not achieved. Report the balloon as a percentage of original debt and stress the refinancing rate by +200bps.
- *Source:* Osborne Clarke / Practical Law, 'Structuring a bankable project: energy storage', 2022 — shorter tenors, front-loaded repayment, additional cash sweeps, high equity share of development costs, https://www.osborneclarke.com/system/files/documents/22/06/15/Structuring%20a%20bankable%20project%20energy%20storage%20(w-025-5057)%202022.pdf
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — £240m facility, £210m drawn, seven-year term and fourteen-year amortisation profile, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf
- *Source:* KBRA, 'Battery Storage Funding Critical to Europe's Energy Transition', 28 June 2022 — short tenors, narrow pool of specialised funders, https://www.ipfa.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/Battery-Storage-Funding.pdf

#### H-08 · MUST · Do not treat a forecast vendor's 'low case' as P90. If the model reports a P90, it must construct a probability distribution and state what the percentile is over (annual revenue, DSCR-period CFADS, or lifetime NPV).

- **Why:** Modo Energy's own documentation states that its central/low/high scenarios 'represent different market outlooks rather than probabilistic percentiles' and are built from specific driver perturbations — low case is gas prices -10%, battery buildout +10%, higher Balancing Mechanism competition, and a 75% (rather than 80%) calibration factor. None of that is a 10th percentile of anything.
- **Error if omitted:** Relabelling a scenario as P90 fabricates a probability statement that no source supports, and it will systematically understate downside because the vendor's low case is a coherent narrative, not a tail. A DSCR sized off a fake P90 is a DSCR sized off nothing.
- **How to implement:** Either (a) build the distribution: simulate revenue over N paths by sampling the joint uncertainty in the price/spread driver set, run the dispatch optimiser (or a calibrated reduced-form revenue function) per path, and read P10/P50/P90/P99 off the resulting empirical CDF of the chosen metric; or (b) state honestly that the model reports a low/central/high scenario band with no probability attached, and size debt off the low case with an explicitly conservative ratio. Whichever is chosen, the label in the output must match the construction. Also define the percentile horizon: an annual-revenue P90 is not a lifetime-NPV P90 and the two differ by roughly the square root of the number of independent periods.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Scenarios' (GB/UK-Europe forecast documentation, Apr 2026 vintage), https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/apr-2026/uk-europe/scenarios/
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Introduction to our forecasting' v3.0, https://forecastdocs.modoenergy.com/v3.0/docs/introduction

#### H-09 · MUST · Apply the percentile to net margin or CFADS, not to gross revenue, because opex is near-deterministic and therefore amplifies revenue percentiles into cashflow percentiles.

- **Why:** For a GB grid-scale BESS, EBITDA margin is roughly 62-64% of revenue (Gresham House: 64.2% in 2025, 62.5% in 2024), so a 20% fall in revenue is a ~31% fall in EBITDA. Charging cost partially self-hedges (it falls when spreads fall) but fixed O&M, insurance, rates and rent do not.
- **Error if omitted:** Applying a P90 revenue haircut and then a fixed margin percentage understates the downside CFADS by the operational gearing factor. On a 64% margin the error is roughly 1.6x on the cashflow shortfall, which is the difference between a passing and a failing DSCR.
- **How to implement:** Run the full P&L and cashflow at each percentile: CFADS_P90 = Revenue_P90 - ChargingCost(Revenue_P90 dispatch) - FixedOpex - VariableOpex(throughput) - Tax(...). Do not compute CFADS_P90 = CFADS_P50 x (Revenue_P90/Revenue_P50). Report the implied operational gearing (dCFADS/CFADS divided by dRevenue/Revenue) as a diagnostic.
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — EBITDA £38.8m on revenue £60.4m, margin 64.2% (2024: 62.5%); revenue £68,600/MW/yr, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf

#### H-10 · MUST · Calibrate modelled (optimiser) revenue down before it enters the base case, and disclose the calibration factor and its decomposition.

- **Why:** A MILP with perfect foresight over a full year is not achievable revenue. Modo Energy calibrates modelled revenues to 80% (central), 85% (high) and 75% (low), benchmarked to the 75th percentile of its BESS Index of more than 190 operational GB sites (~7 GW), and decomposes the gap as approximately 5% availability, 3% capacity degradation, ~10% imperfect foresight, plus competition and BM timing effects.
- **Error if omitted:** Feeding raw perfect-foresight optimiser output into the bank case overstates revenue by roughly 20-25%, which on a 64% EBITDA margin is a ~31-39% overstatement of CFADS. This single omission is large enough to flip an entire investment decision and is the most commonly made error in storage models.
- **How to implement:** AchievableRevenue = CalibrationFactor x ModelledOptimiserRevenue, with CalibrationFactor decomposed as (1 - availability_loss) x (1 - degradation_effect) x (1 - foresight_loss) x (1 - competition_loss), and each factor separately parameterised and sourced. Default GB central: 0.80. Do not double-count: if the MILP already imposes a rolling-horizon or imperfect-foresight formulation, remove the foresight component from the calibration and say so. Benchmark the calibrated output against the observed GB index (e.g. £72,400/MW/yr GB market average for the year to March 2025, against £81,900/MW/yr for a top-quartile internally optimised portfolio).
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Model Calibration' (GB forecast documentation, Jan 2026 vintage) — central 80%, high 85%, low 75%; availability ~5%, degradation ~3%, imperfect foresight ~10%; benchmark is the 75th percentile of fleet revenues across >190 sites / ~7 GW, https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jan-2026/uk-europe/gb/model-calibration/
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'FAQ: Backtest and Calibration' v3.0 (backcast Jan 2021 - Mar 2024), https://forecastdocs.modoenergy.com/v3.0/docs/faq-backtest-and-calibration
- *Source:* Gore Street Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Audited Full Year Results to 31 March 2025', 2025 — GB market average c.£72,400/MW/yr vs GSET-managed c.£81,900/MW/yr, https://www.gsenergystoragefund.com/docs/librariesprovider22/archive/results/prelims-2025.pdf

#### H-11 · MUST · Backtest the revenue model against outturn before it is used to underwrite, and report the error, not just the fit.

- **Why:** Forecast providers publish backtests as a condition of credibility. Modo backcasts January 2021 to March 2024 against outturn prices and compares each forecast vintage against its BESS Index; it reports that its forecasts 'performed significantly better than the curves used by the publicly listed funds' over 2023 and early 2024 — which is simultaneously evidence that listed-fund curves over-forecast in that period.
- **Error if omitted:** Without a backtest there is no basis for any confidence band at all, and no way to distinguish a model that is conservative from one that is merely wrong. It also removes the only empirical anchor for the curve-level uncertainty term required above.
- **How to implement:** Hold out at least 24 months of outturn. For each held-out period compute modelled revenue with only information available at the forecast date, then report: mean absolute percentage error, bias (signed mean error), and the distribution of (outturn / forecast) by year. Use the empirical distribution of that ratio as the calibration for the curve-level uncertainty factor L. Refuse to publish a P90 whose implied dispersion is narrower than the backtest error.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Historic performance' (forecast documentation) — comparison of each forecast vintage over the last year against the Modo Benchmark for 1h BESS across wholesale day-ahead, intraday, BM, frequency response, capacity market and reserve, https://forecastdocs.modoenergy.com/docs/historic-performance
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'FAQ: Backtest and Calibration' v3.0, https://forecastdocs.modoenergy.com/v3.0/docs/faq-backtest-and-calibration

#### H-12 · MUST · Anchor the equity hurdle rate to published GB evidence for lithium BESS and state it consistently as pre-tax or post-tax, real or nominal.

- **Why:** CEPA's 2025 study for DESNZ is the authoritative GB source: lithium BESS is rated 'medium risk' with a pre-tax real CPI hurdle rate of 8.9% mid (range 7.3-10.6%) at 50% notional gearing over a 15-year term, comprising a 5.1% real return on debt and 12.6% real return on equity, with an asset beta of 0.75. Critically, CEPA records that most investor survey responses sat above its mid-point, averaging ~1.5 percentage points higher on the overall hurdle rate.
- **Error if omitted:** Using an unsourced hurdle rate (a round 10% or 20% IRR target) makes every go/no-go decision arbitrary. Mixing real and nominal is worse: at CEPA's 15-year expected CPI of 1.95%, an 8.9% pre-tax real hurdle is ~11.0% nominal — a 210bps error if conflated, which on a 15-year asset moves NPV by roughly 15-20%.
- **How to implement:** Store the hurdle rate as {value, basis: pre-tax|post-tax, deflator: real-CPI|real-GDP|nominal, gearing, vintage, source}. Convert with (1+nominal) = (1+real_CPI) x (1+CPI), CPI = 1.95% (OBR 15-year average per CEPA). GB base case: 8.9% pre-tax real CPI at 50% gearing; sensitivity 7.3%-10.6%; investor-survey-adjusted case ~10.4%. For a BTM asset with host counterparty risk, justify any downward adjustment explicitly — the risk is higher, not lower, than a grid-scale merchant asset.
- *Source:* CEPA for DESNZ, 'Hurdle rate estimates for electricity sector technologies', 2025 (data as at 31 December 2024), https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/68cd1d818c44a661b4995d9f/cepa-desnz-hurdle-rates-electricity.pdf
- *Source:* DESNZ, 'Electricity Generation Costs 2025', 2025, https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/69ba657326909a14239612e3/electricity-generation-costs-report-2025.pdf

#### H-13 · MUST · Price debt from a sourced GB benchmark: a floating base rate plus a credit-risk-consistent margin, and require an interest rate hedge for the debt term.

- **Why:** CEPA's 15-year nominal cost of debt by credit rating (BBB+ 5.97% through B+ 8.25%) is the GB-official benchmark, and CEPA records that ~50% gearing is broadly consistent with a BB to BBB- rating — i.e. a nominal cost of debt of 6.57-7.48% for a 50%-geared storage project. Ofgem's LDES decision independently uses an iBoxx GBP Non-Financials 15+ BBB benchmark and derives 5.34-5.58% nominal for 1-3 and 5-7 year tenors. Listed funds borrow at SONIA plus a margin with the majority hedged.
- **Error if omitted:** An assumed flat cost of debt with no link to leverage produces a model where increasing gearing always increases equity IRR, which is false. Ignoring hedging leaves the DSCR exposed to rate moves that lenders will require to be hedged as a condition precedent, and understates the all-in cost.
- **How to implement:** CostOfDebt = BaseRate(SONIA or swap) + Margin(rating implied by gearing and contracted share) + Fees amortised. Cross-check against: CEPA nominal 15-year BBB 6.11%, BBB- 6.57%, BB+ 7.03%, BB 7.48%; Ofgem BBB 5.34-5.58% at 1-3 / 5-7 year tenor. Require HedgeRatio (typically 75-100% of drawn debt) and model the hedge as a fixed leg. Include commitment fees on undrawn amounts and an arrangement fee in the sources and uses.
- *Source:* CEPA for DESNZ, 'Hurdle rate estimates for electricity sector technologies', 2025 — Table 4.5 nominal cost of debt by credit rating, 15-year tenor; ~50% gearing consistent with BB to BBB-, https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/68cd1d818c44a661b4995d9f/cepa-desnz-hurdle-rates-electricity.pdf
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Decision - Financial Framework: LDES Window 1 Cap and Floor regime', September 2025 — iBoxx GBP Non-Financials 15+ BBB benchmark, 20-day average pre-FID, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2025-09/LDES%20Window%201%20Financial%20Framework%20Decision.pdf
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — borrowings referenced to SONIA with the majority hedged, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf

#### H-14 · MUST · Report project IRR and equity IRR on distinct cashflow definitions, and state the tax and inflation basis of each.

- **Why:** Project IRR is the return on total capital before financing; equity IRR is the return on sponsor cash after debt service, sweeps, lock-ups and reserve movements. Confusing them, or comparing a post-tax nominal equity IRR to a pre-tax real hurdle rate, is a category error that CEPA's framework makes explicit by publishing pre-tax, post-tax, real and nominal variants separately.
- **Error if omitted:** Comparing a nominal post-tax equity IRR to CEPA's 8.9% pre-tax real hurdle understates the required return by roughly 400-500bps once inflation and tax are accounted for, which will pass projects that do not clear the market hurdle.
- **How to implement:** ProjectCF_t = CFADS_t - Capex_t (unlevered, and state whether pre- or post-tax; if post-tax, use notional tax with no interest shield). EquityCF_t = -EquityDrawn_t + Distributions_t (after debt service, sweeps, lock-ups, reserve funding and releases, and actual tax including the interest deduction). Report each with an explicit {real|nominal, pre-tax|post-tax} tag and compare only like to like. Also report equity payback, minimum DSCR, average DSCR, LLCR and the loan life / project life ratio.
- *Source:* CEPA for DESNZ, 'Hurdle rate estimates for electricity sector technologies', 2025 — publishes pre-/post-tax and real/nominal variants under all gearing assumptions, https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/68cd1d818c44a661b4995d9f/cepa-desnz-hurdle-rates-electricity.pdf
- *Source:* Corporate Finance Institute, 'Cash Flow Available for Debt Service (CFADS)', https://corporatefinanceinstitute.com/resources/commercial-lending/cash-flow-available-for-debt-service-cfads/

#### H-15 · MUST · Handle inflation explicitly: index opex, business rates, insurance, land rent and CPI-linked contracted revenue, and deflate consistently with the discount rate basis.

- **Why:** GB storage cashflows are a mix of nominal-merchant and CPI-linked contracted lines. Gresham House notes its T-4 Capacity Market contracts are CPI-linked with the vast majority having at least ten years remaining. Ofgem indexes LDES cap and floor values to inflation and deflates the cost of capital using a 2% Bank of England target. CEPA uses OBR 15-year averages of CPI 1.95%, RPI 2.22%, GDP deflator 2.13%.
- **Error if omitted:** Running nominal revenue against real opex (or vice versa) produces a margin drift of ~2% per year compounding, which over a 15-year life is a ~35% error in terminal-year margin. This is a silent, systematic error that no single-year check will catch.
- **How to implement:** Tag every cashflow line with an indexation rule {none, CPI, RPI, CPIH, contractual %}. Escalate at the tagged index. If discounting at a real rate, deflate all nominal lines by the same index used to define the real rate (CEPA uses CPI; Ofgem uses CPIH deflated at the BoE 2% target). Reference values: CPI 1.95% (15-yr OBR average), RPI 2.22%, GDP deflator 2.13%.
- *Source:* CEPA for DESNZ, 'Hurdle rate estimates for electricity sector technologies', 2025 — Table 4.9 estimates of expected inflation from OBR long-term determinants, https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/68cd1d818c44a661b4995d9f/cepa-desnz-hurdle-rates-electricity.pdf
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Decision - Financial Framework: LDES Window 1 Cap and Floor regime', September 2025 — CPIH indexation, cost of capital deflated at the BoE 2% target, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2025-09/LDES%20Window%201%20Financial%20Framework%20Decision.pdf
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — 901MW under T-4 Capacity Market contracts, CPI-linked, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf

#### H-16 · MUST · Model the construction phase separately: interest during construction, contingency, delay liquidated damages, a commissioning/proving period at reduced revenue, and a discount-rate premium until practical completion.

- **Why:** GB valuation practice treats pre-construction and energised assets at materially different discount rates — Gore Street uses 10.75-12.00% pre-construction against 7.25-9.50% energised, and Gresham House applies a 50bps premium to base discount rates during the 30-day proving period after practical completion, removed thereafter.
- **Error if omitted:** Valuing a development-stage BTM project at operating-asset discount rates overstates value by roughly 20-30% and ignores the largest single de-risking event in the project's life. Omitting IDC and contingency understates the funding requirement.
- **How to implement:** Two-phase model: construction phase with capex drawdown profile, IDC accrued at the construction margin (Ofgem indicative 6.03-6.11%), contingency as a percentage of capex, and a commissioning ramp; then operations. Apply DiscountRate_preconstruction (10.75-12.00%) until financial close/notice to proceed, DiscountRate_construction until practical completion, DiscountRate_operational + 50bps during the proving period (30 days), and DiscountRate_operational thereafter. Model delay: each month of delay costs a month of revenue plus IDC; check EPC liquidated damages cover debt service during delay.
- *Source:* Gore Street Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Audited Full Year Results to 31 March 2025', 2025 — Discount Rate Matrix, pre-construction 10.75-12.00%, energised 7.25-9.50%, https://www.gsenergystoragefund.com/docs/librariesprovider22/archive/results/prelims-2025.pdf
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — proving period of 30 days after PAC valued with a 50bps premium to base discount rates, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Decision - Financial Framework: LDES Window 1 Cap and Floor regime', September 2025 — indicative IDC rates, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2025-09/LDES%20Window%201%20Financial%20Framework%20Decision.pdf

#### H-17 · MUST · Model availability explicitly, including planned outages, augmentation downtime and unplanned failures, as a reduction to revenue and to contracted availability payments.

- **Why:** Availability is roughly 5% of the modelled-to-achieved revenue gap in the GB calibration. Gresham House reports that 2025 improvements were 'offset by temporary reductions in portfolio availability because of the augmentation programme'. Toll and floor agreements pay against availability KPIs, so an availability shortfall hits contracted revenue too, not just merchant.
- **Error if omitted:** A 100%-available model overstates revenue by roughly 5% before any other error, and cannot represent the augmentation trade-off (spend capex and lose revenue now to raise revenue later), which is the key mid-life decision.
- **How to implement:** Revenue_hh = Availability_hh x DispatchRevenue_hh, with Availability decomposed into planned maintenance windows, augmentation outage windows (site-specific duration), and a stochastic forced-outage rate. Under a toll or floor, apply the contractual availability test: if Availability < MAT (minimum availability target), reduce or forfeit the availability payment. Ofgem's LDES regime uses exactly this construct — performance-linked floor access via a Minimum Availability Target.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Model Calibration' (GB, Jan 2026 vintage) — availability ~5% impact from grid outages and maintenance, https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jan-2026/uk-europe/gb/model-calibration/
