- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Decision - Financial Framework: LDES Window 1 Cap and Floor regime', September 2025 — performance-linked floor access via Minimum Availability Target (MAT), https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2025-09/LDES%20Window%201%20Financial%20Framework%20Decision.pdf

#### H-18 · MUST · Run a defined stress suite including a 'severe but plausible' downside used for the going-concern/viability test, and report first covenant breach, first lock-up and equity IRR under each.

- **Why:** This is the standard auditors and boards apply. Gresham House's auditors reviewed directors' forecast covenant compliance and assessed the HoldCo's ability to meet covenants 'even under the severe but plausible' downside scenario. Listed funds publish sensitivities of +/-1% on the discount rate and on revenue.
- **Error if omitted:** A model with only a base case cannot support an investment decision or a credit paper. Without a first-breach output, the model cannot tell the investor how much revenue decline the structure absorbs before distributions stop.
- **How to implement:** Define a named, versioned stress set at minimum: (1) revenue at the low case for the whole life; (2) revenue at the low case for the first five years only (near-term stress, which is where DSCR bites); (3) capex +10-20%; (4) opex +20%; (5) interest rate +200bps on unhedged debt; (6) COD delay of 6-12 months; (7) augmentation cost +30%; (8) degradation at the unwarranted rate; (9) host exit at year 5 / 10 (BTM only); (10) charge-avoidance value to zero from the next charging review (BTM only); (11) discount rate +/-1%. For each, report min DSCR, first lock-up period, first default period, equity IRR and NPV. Report the breakeven revenue level (£/MW/yr) at which min DSCR = 1.00x.
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — auditors reviewed directors' forecast covenant compliance under a severe but plausible downside; discount rate +/-1% sensitivity disclosed, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf
- *Source:* Gore Street Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Audited Full Year Results to 31 March 2025', 2025 — discount rate +1% sensitivity by geography, https://www.gsenergystoragefund.com/docs/librariesprovider22/archive/results/prelims-2025.pdf

#### H-19 · MUST · Require the sponsor to fund a high proportion of development costs with equity and model the equity-first drawdown order.

- **Why:** The GB legal literature lists 'a high percentage of the development costs to be funded by equity' as one of the specific enhancements lenders require for battery storage financings.
- **Error if omitted:** Modelling debt drawing pro-rata from day one overstates the debt balance during construction (raising IDC) and understates the sponsor's cash requirement and timing, which distorts equity IRR through the time value of the earlier outflow.
- **How to implement:** Drawdown order: equity first up to EquityFirstPct of total funding (or all development and pre-FID costs), then debt pro-rata or debt-last. Model an equity bridge only if explicitly assumed, and charge its cost. Report peak equity requirement and its timing.
- *Source:* Osborne Clarke / Practical Law, 'Structuring a bankable project: energy storage', 2022, https://www.osborneclarke.com/system/files/documents/22/06/15/Structuring%20a%20bankable%20project%20energy%20storage%20(w-025-5057)%202022.pdf

#### H-20 · MUST · Test the Smart Secure Electricity Systems (SSES) load-control licensing regime and NIS/cyber duties against the actual asset and against the OPTIMISER, and record the result. On the published scope this is a documented NON-ISSUE for the asset itself — the Load Control Licence covers domestic-scale energy smart appliances, not 0.1-5 MW C&I batteries — but it is a live obligation for the aggregator/optimiser layer if that layer also controls domestic-scale assets, and the NIS Operator of Essential Services threshold at 300 MW of controlled load is a real gate for any optimiser at scale. Separately, treat the host's own IT security policy as a hard constraint on the control connection, with its own capex and its own single point of failure.

- **Why:** DESNZ's SSES consultation response defines the in-scope appliances as 'private Electric Vehicles (EVs) and EVSCPs, heat technologies within the scope of the smart mandate, and domestic-scale BESS, and, where appropriate, ancillary devices used to control load on these technologies', with the aim of supporting 'domestic and small non-domestic consumer confidence'. A commercial or industrial battery of 0.1-5 MW is not a domestic-scale BESS, so the asset is out of scope. The residual is precisely located: 'All load controllers within the SSES programme licensing scope i.e., load controllers managing domestic scale EV chargers, heat pumps, BESS etc. managing under 300MW of load will be required to meet specific cyber security licence conditions via the Load Control Licence. For load controllers managing equal to or above 300MW of load, we additionally intend to bring them within scope of the Network and Information Systems (NIS) Regulations as Operators of Essential Services.' All in-scope controllers must meet a Cyber Assessment Framework profile proportionate to the risk they pose. Timing is inside the underwriting life: applications open March 2027, licensing requirement effective March 2028.
- **Error if omitted:** For the asset, nothing — which is itself the useful result, and worth recording so the risk register does not carry a phantom item. For the counterparty stack, two real exposures. (1) If the optimiser is a mixed-fleet aggregator that also controls domestic assets, it needs a Load Control Licence from March 2028 and a CAF profile; failure to obtain one interrupts the route to market for every asset it optimises, including this one. (2) NESO's own telemetry obligations already imply a security posture — Response Services require 20 Hz performance data for DC, DM and Stacking Services (DR 20 Hz or 2 Hz) plus operational data — so a compliant control path is a technical prerequisite regardless of licensing. The genuinely BTM-specific risk is the third one: many C&I hosts will not permit an unmanaged third-party control connection onto their corporate network, which forces a separate comms path (dedicated 4G/5G router or fibre) with its own capex, its own opex, and its own unmodelled single point of failure that maps directly onto the availability requirement.
- **How to implement:** Record three booleans and one cost line. (1) asset_in_sses_scope = FALSE for a C&I BTM battery on the published scope; document the citation so the item is closed rather than open. (2) optimiser_requires_load_control_licence = TRUE if the optimiser controls domestic-scale ESAs; if TRUE, require evidence of licence application (from March 2027) or a credible plan, and treat absence as a route-to-market counterparty risk in the change-in-law matrix. (3) optimiser_controlled_load_MW >= 300 triggers NIS OES duties and a heavier CAF profile — ask the optimiser for its controlled-load figure and its CAF self-assessment. Cost line: a dedicated out-of-band comms path (router, SIM/fibre, installation, monthly data) plus its failure rate and MTTR, fed into the timed-outage model as a distinct 'comms link' cause with its own MTBF and repair distribution. Add the host's IT security sign-off as a documentation gate before financial close, since a refusal forces the dedicated path.
- *Source:* DESNZ, 'Delivering a smart and secure electricity system: consultation response', https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/6808a2630324470d6a394eb2/SSES-consultation-response.pdf — in-scope ESAs are private EVs/EVSCPs, smart-mandate heat technologies and 'domestic-scale BESS'; load controllers under 300MW of load require cyber security licence conditions via the Load Control Licence; those at or above 300MW additionally brought within NIS as Operators of Essential Services; Cyber Assessment Framework profile proportionate to risk; Elexon minded-to as enduring governance body via BSC modifications
- *Source:* DESNZ, 'Smart Secure Electricity Systems (SSES) Programme: draft Load Control Licence Regulations and conditions', consultation 10 December 2025 - 18 February 2026, government response 7 August 2026, https://www.gov.uk/government/consultations/smart-secure-electricity-systems-sses-programme-draft-load-control-licence-regulations-and-conditions — applications open March 2027; licensing requirement effective March 2028; three proposed class exemptions
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Smart Secure Electricity Systems: implementing the load control licensing regime', https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/consultation/smart-secure-electricity-systems-implementing-load-control-licensing-regime
- *Source:* PAS 1878:2021 (Energy smart appliances — system functionality and architecture) and PAS 1879:2021 (Energy smart appliances — demand side response operation), BSI — referenced throughout the SSES response; note DESNZ states it is 'sponsoring work to develop alternatives to PAS 1878' and exploring approaches beyond it
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services (DC/DM/DR) Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026 — performance data at 20 Hz for DC, DM and Stacking Services; DR at 20 Hz or 2 Hz
- *Source:* ETSI EN 303 645 (consumer IoT cyber security) — referenced in the SSES response in the context of alignment with other domestic and EU legislation

#### H-21 · SHOULD · Apply an explicit, sourced haircut to convert perfect-foresight MILP output into an underwritable P50, and never present the perfect-foresight objective as expected revenue.

- **Why:** A deterministic MILP over a full year of realised prices is an upper bound on achievable revenue, and every author who computes both says so. The published gap is large enough to move an IRR by several points.
- **Error if omitted:** Revenue overstated by 5-25% depending on market and time resolution, before any other error. On a levered project this is the difference between a bankable and an unbankable DSCR.
- **How to implement:** Compute both: (a) full-year perfect-foresight MILP as the theoretical ceiling; (b) a rolling-horizon MILP driven by a genuine forecast (or a naive backcast) as the realistic case, and report their ratio as the model's own trading-efficiency assumption. Published values: two-week backcast dispatch captured 'about 85% or more' of perfect-foresight arbitrage value in each of six years, PJM 2002-2007, 12-hour device, hourly prices (Sioshansi et al. 2009, Fig. 6; the year-by-year accuracy runs from roughly 84.5% in 2003 to roughly 89.5% in 2005). A forecast-driven rolling-window strategy earned EUR 146,237/yr for a 1 MW / 1 MWh system in the German continuous intraday market in 2023, 'only 11% below perfect foresight' (Hornek et al. 2025). For the UK, Staffell and Rustomji (2016) report that 75-95% of optimal profits are obtained without perfect foresight of future prices.
- *Source:* Sioshansi, R., Denholm, P., Jenkin, T., Weiss, J., 'Estimating the value of electricity storage in PJM: Arbitrage and some welfare effects', Energy Economics 31(2):269-277, 2009, Section 3 and Fig. 6, DOI 10.1016/j.eneco.2008.10.005, https://www.osti.gov/servlets/purl/1353367
- *Source:* Hornek, T., Lee, Y., Potenciano Menci, S., Pavic, I., IEEE PowerTech 2025, abstract and Section V, arXiv:2501.07121v2
- *Source:* Staffell, I., Rustomji, M., 'Maximising the value of electricity storage', Journal of Energy Storage 8:212-225, 2016, DOI 10.1016/j.est.2016.08.010

#### H-22 · SHOULD · Produce P50/P90 from a distribution over scenarios, not from a single deterministic MILP with a judgemental haircut.

- **Why:** A deterministic MILP produces one number. P90 is a quantile of a distribution and requires either scenario-based stochastic programming, a chance-constrained/robust formulation, or a Monte Carlo wrapper over price and load paths.
- **Error if omitted:** The 'P90' is a fabricated number with no statistical content, which is exactly what a lender's technical advisor will test. It also hides the fact that ancillary availability revenue and wholesale arbitrage revenue have very different variances and are not perfectly correlated.
- **How to implement:** Sample-average approximation over n_sc price/load scenarios: min (1/n_sc) * sum_j [ objective(scenario j) ] with first-stage variables (build sizing, and ancillary commitments if committed ahead) shared across scenarios and second-stage dispatch scenario-specific. Convergence guidance from Engels et al.: with n_U = 1e5 and n_L = 10, alpha = 0.005, the optimality gap falls below 3% by roughly n_sc >= 250 scenarios and below 1% from about 1000 scenarios; with Kantorovich-distance backward scenario reduction, below 1% from about 500 reduced scenarios. Gap estimator: gap = gbar_U - gbar_L + z_alpha * sigma_U/sqrt(n_U) + t_{alpha,n_L-1} * sigma_L/sqrt(n_L).
- *Source:* Engels, J., Claessens, B., Deconinck, G., IEEE Transactions on Smart Grid 10(2):1971-1981, 2019, Section V-B/V-D and eq. (13), and Section VI-B-1 for the scenario-count results, arXiv:1710.08331

#### H-23 · SHOULD · Where a deliverability obligation exists (frequency response availability, Capacity Market stress events), express it as a chance constraint or robust constraint with a stated violation probability, not as a deterministic average.

- **Why:** Availability payments are contingent on being able to deliver at all times in the contracted window. The correct object is a probability of constraint violation, and the robust counterpart is tractable as a second-order cone constraint, so there is no computational excuse for the deterministic shortcut.
- **Error if omitted:** Over-contracting reserve. The model sells the maximum reserve capacity that is feasible on average and then fails to deliver in the tail, incurring penalties and reputational derating that the deterministic model cannot see. Conversely, naive heuristics under-contract: Engels et al. show a 10 kWh / 7 kW battery at 90% round-trip efficiency can offer 6.37 kW of primary frequency reserve at a violation probability of 1e-4 with an optimised recharging policy, versus 4.66 kW from a published heuristic for the same battery — a 37% difference in sellable capacity.
- **How to implement:** Chance constraint: Pr( a_i^T * Delta_f <= b_i ) >= 1 - epsilon for i = 1..n_c, with epsilon on the order of 1e-4 to 1e-5. Robust counterpart as an SOCP: a_i^T * mean(Delta_f) + sqrt(-2 * ln(epsilon)) * ||u_i||_2 <= b_i, with u_i = max( Q * a_i^T * W^-1, -R * a_i^T * W^-1 ) elementwise, W the Cholesky factor of the inverse covariance and Q, R the forward/backward deviation matrices. Pair with a linear recharging policy P^rc_k = sum_{i=1}^{k-1} d_ki * Delta_f_i (strictly lower triangular D, so the recharge does not interfere with the instantaneous response) and its equivalent state feedback form P_rc = (I + (1/r) D)^-1 * (1/r) * D * Delta_E_bat.
- *Source:* Engels, J., Claessens, B., Deconinck, G., IEEE Transactions on Smart Grid 10(2):1971-1981, 2019, eqs (2), (4), (6)-(9), (14), Fig. 2, Section VI-A-3, arXiv:1710.08331

#### H-24 · SHOULD · Apply a dual coverage test: size debt as the minimum of (P50 CFADS / target P50 DSCR) and (P90 CFADS / target P90 DSCR), rather than a single ratio on a single case.

- **Why:** Storage underwriting convention runs two ratios simultaneously because the merchant distribution is wide and asymmetric; a single P50 ratio does not constrain the downside and a single P90 ratio over-penalises contracted structures.
- **Error if omitted:** A model that sizes only off P50 will pass structures that breach covenant in any plausible downside; a model that sizes only off a haircut P90 will under-lever contracted structures and wrongly reject bankable tolls. Either way the recommended structure is wrong.
- **How to implement:** Debt = min( NPV_i(CFADS_P50 / DSCR_P50), NPV_i(CFADS_P90 / DSCR_P90), GearingCap x Capex ). Indicative market ratios (US-sourced, treat as structure not as GB calibration): tolling 1.20x P90; capacity-contracted 1.25x P90; hybrid hedge 1.30x P50 and 1.10x P90; pure merchant 1.40x P50. A separate convention sizes merchant at 1.75x-2.00x P50 with a 1.00x P99 floor test. Store the ratio set as a named, sourced parameter block, not a hard-coded constant.
- *Source:* SunRaise Capital (N. Jovanelly), 'Standalone Battery Storage Project Finance Underwriting in 2026', 2026 — BLOG, US market, low authority, https://sunraisecapital.com/blog/standalone-battery-storage-project-finance-underwriting-2026
- *Source:* GreenBridge Infrastructure, 'Debt Sizing', https://greenbridgeinfra.com/resources/project-finance/debt-sizing
- *Source:* Morgan Lewis, 'Project Financing Trends for Global Energy Storage Projects in 2026 and Beyond', 2026, https://www.morganlewis.com/pubs/2026/03/project-financing-trends-for-global-energy-storage-projects-in-2026-and-beyond

#### H-25 · SHOULD · Fund and model the full reserve-account package: a debt service reserve (DSRA), an O&M/maintenance reserve, an augmentation/degradation reserve, and, in GB, a VAT facility during construction.

- **Why:** Observed GB deals fund these explicitly. Econergy's Immingham financing comprised a £38.4m main facility plus a VAT revolving facility and a debt-service reserve facility within a £42.7m total. Morgan Lewis records that lenders require O&M reserves specifically for anticipated degradation and augmentation costs.
- **Error if omitted:** Omitting reserves overstates equity IRR twice: once by understating the day-one funding requirement (reserves are funded from the facility or from equity at financial close) and once by ignoring the cash trapped over the debt life. The augmentation reserve is the storage-specific one and is routinely forgotten.
- **How to implement:** Model each reserve as a balance-sheet account with a funding rule and a release rule. DSRA target = next 6 or 12 months of scheduled debt service; fund at COD from the facility or equity; release at final repayment. Augmentation reserve: accrue an annual amount per kWh (a US benchmark is $4-12 per kWh-year, chemistry dependent) or fund to the modelled augmentation capex date. VAT facility: size to peak recoverable input VAT during construction. Reserve movements are a CFADS adjustment, and reserve funding is a use of funds in the sources-and-uses statement.
- *Source:* Solar Power Portal, 'Santander provides debt finance for Econergy, Brockwell BESS', 2026 — Immingham 80MW/240MWh, c.£42.7m total, £38.4m main facility plus VAT revolving facility and debt-service reserve facility, https://www.solarpowerportal.co.uk/battery-storage/santander-provides-debt-finance-for-econergy-brockwell-energy-bess
- *Source:* Morgan Lewis, 'Project Financing Trends for Global Energy Storage Projects in 2026 and Beyond', 2026, https://www.morganlewis.com/pubs/2026/03/project-financing-trends-for-global-energy-storage-projects-in-2026-and-beyond
- *Source:* SunRaise Capital, 'Standalone Battery Storage Project Finance Underwriting in 2026' — BLOG, US, augmentation reserve $4-12/kWh-yr, https://sunraisecapital.com/blog/standalone-battery-storage-project-finance-underwriting-2026

#### H-26 · SHOULD · Construct the revenue distribution so that forecast-curve (vintage) uncertainty dominates, not within-year price noise. Include a systematic curve-level shift as the principal uncertainty term.

- **Why:** The observed dispersion in GB storage outcomes is overwhelmingly driven by the whole forward curve moving, not by weather noise around a stable curve. Gresham House discloses that declining third-party revenue forecasts reduced NAV per share by 15.61p in 2025 and by 66.30p cumulatively since 31 December 2022 — 'the largest impact on the NAV over the year'. Outturn GB BESS revenue has ranged from £123k/MW/yr (2021) to £25,380/MW/yr (Q1 2024) to £88k/MW/yr (Jan 2025) to £41k/MW/yr (Feb 2026).
- **Error if omitted:** A Monte Carlo that resamples half-hourly prices around a fixed forward curve will produce a P90/P50 ratio close to 1 (dispersion of an annual average of ~17,520 half-hours is small). That is the classic error: it will report a spuriously tight band, pass a merchant DSCR test that reality would fail, and give false comfort on P90.
- **How to implement:** Decompose revenue uncertainty into: (i) a curve-level multiplicative factor L ~ distribution calibrated to the historical dispersion of successive forecast vintages and to outturn-vs-forecast error (this is the dominant term and is highly serially correlated across years); (ii) a within-year shape/volatility term; (iii) an asset-specific execution term (availability, foresight, competition). Then Revenue_y = L_y x BaseCurve_y x ShapeFactor_y x ExecutionFactor. Calibrate L from at least the 2022-2026 curve history; a defensible minimum is that the band spans the observed range of published GB curves for the same duration. Report the P90/P50 ratio the model produces and sanity-check it against the observed year-on-year outturn range rather than asserting a conventional value.
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — third-party revenue forecast declines reduced NAV/share by 15.61p in 2025 and 66.30p cumulatively since 31 Dec 2022, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'The evolution of the GB battery energy storage revenue stack' — 2020 £65k/MW/yr, 2021 £123k/MW/yr, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/5023
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'GB BESS: the five headlines from Q1 2024' — Q1 2024 £25,380/MW/yr, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/q1-2024-roundup-gb-battery-energy-storage-revenues-balancing-mechanism-ancillary-services
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'ME BESS GB: Revenues fall to £41k/MW/year in February 2026' — down 47% year on year, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/me-bess-gb-revenues-february-2026-wholesale-battery-energy-storage-balancing-mechanism

#### H-27 · SHOULD · Model UK corporation tax, capital allowances on the battery and associated plant, and the corporate interest restriction, on the actual cash-tax timing.

- **Why:** CEPA's hurdle rates are quoted pre-tax precisely because the tax wedge is technology- and structure-specific; converting between pre- and post-tax requires the effective tax rate. For a UK SPV the timing of capital allowances materially shifts early-year CFADS, which is exactly where DSCR sizing bites.
- **Error if omitted:** Ignoring capital allowances understates early CFADS and under-sizes debt; ignoring the interest restriction overstates the shield on a highly geared SPV. Both distort the sizing and the equity IRR, and the errors do not cancel.
- **How to implement:** Compute taxable profit = accounting profit +/- adjustments - capital allowances; carry forward losses; apply the prevailing UK corporation tax rate; restrict net interest deductions per the corporate interest restriction where applicable. Feed cash tax paid (not accrued) into CFADS with the correct payment lag. Flag whether the battery qualifies for full expensing / annual investment allowance versus special-rate pool treatment, because the answer changes early-year cash materially and is a genuine open question for BESS.
- *Source:* CEPA for DESNZ, 'Hurdle rate estimates for electricity sector technologies', 2025 — Section 4.4.6 tax rate; pre-/post-tax variants, https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/68cd1d818c44a661b4995d9f/cepa-desnz-hurdle-rates-electricity.pdf

#### H-28 · SHOULD · Apply an explicit merchant-tail treatment: revenue after the expiry of any floor, toll or CM agreement must be haircut or excluded from debt sizing, and the debt must repay before the tail begins wherever possible.

- **Why:** The merchant tail is the uncontracted portion of a project's life after contracts expire; it is the standard project finance concept for exactly this exposure. GB storage contracts are shorter than asset life — tolls of two years, floors of ten years, CM of up to fifteen — while asset lives are modelled at 15-25 years, so a tail always exists.
- **Error if omitted:** Sizing debt against a fully merchant year 12-15 at the same DSCR as a floored year 1-10 is the classic error. It produces a facility that cannot be repaid without a refinancing that may not be available, and it is the mechanism by which storage financings actually fail.
- **How to implement:** Define TailStart = min over contracts of (contract expiry). For periods >= TailStart, either (a) exclude the revenue from debt sizing entirely, or (b) apply a merchant haircut (a commonly cited lender practice is a 30-50% haircut on the merchant component) and a higher DSCR. Require DebtBalance(TailStart) to be low enough that the tail revenue at the merchant DSCR repays it, or require a contracted extension as a condition. Report the percentage of total debt service falling after TailStart.
- *Source:* Osborne Clarke / Practical Law, 'Structuring a bankable project: energy storage', 2022 — differing periods of contracted revenue under the various offtake agreements and the resulting fluctuating cashflows, https://www.osborneclarke.com/system/files/documents/22/06/15/Structuring%20a%20bankable%20project%20energy%20storage%20(w-025-5057)%202022.pdf
- *Source:* Morgan Lewis, 'Project Financing Trends for Global Energy Storage Projects in 2026 and Beyond', 2026 — lenders may finance merchant cashflows but with less leverage, subject to detailed market studies, cash sweeps and other protections, https://www.morganlewis.com/pubs/2026/03/project-financing-trends-for-global-energy-storage-projects-in-2026-and-beyond
- *Source:* SunRaise Capital, 'Standalone Battery Storage Project Finance Underwriting in 2026' — BLOG, US: 30-50% merchant haircut applied by lenders, https://sunraisecapital.com/blog/standalone-battery-storage-project-finance-underwriting-2026

#### H-29 · SHOULD · State and justify terminal value separately from the operating cashflows, and default to a conservative treatment (zero, decommissioning-net, or a repowering-based value) rather than a perpetuity.

- **Why:** GB practice does not support a large terminal value. Modo assumes a 15-year BESS project life against 25 years for generation. Investment companies describe time horizons of 25 years. Ofgem's LDES framework recognises residual value explicitly as something that lowers the required floor. Gresham House values operational assets at £742k/MW (£734k/MW excluding working capital) as at 31 December 2025 — a market observation, not a perpetuity calculation.
- **Error if omitted:** An unstated or aggressive terminal value can be the majority of NPV and is invisible in the IRR. Underwriters discount it heavily or ignore it; if the model relies on it the equity case is not real.
- **How to implement:** TerminalValue = max(0, ResidualAssetValue - DecommissioningCost - LeaseReinstatement), where ResidualAssetValue is either zero, a repowering option value (cost of new cells against remaining site/connection/consent value), or a market comparable. Report NPV excluding terminal value as the headline and NPV including it as a sensitivity. Include decommissioning and site reinstatement as a funded cost — Ofgem's LDES model carries decommissioning cost as an explicit percentage of capex.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'Modelling Assumptions' (UK/Europe, Jul 2026 vintage) — BESS project life 15 years vs 25 years for generation, https://docs.modoenergy.com/pages/jul-2026/uk-europe/model-assumptions/
- *Source:* Ofgem, 'Decision - Financial Framework: LDES Window 1 Cap and Floor regime', September 2025 — decommissioning cost as a percentage of capex; residual value recognised as lowering the floor, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2025-09/LDES%20Window%201%20Financial%20Framework%20Decision.pdf
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026 — operational assets valued at £742k/MW (£734k/MW ex working capital) at 31 December 2025, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf

#### H-30 · SHOULD · Report a revenue breakeven against a published investment hurdle, and check the project against the market's observed required revenue level rather than only against its own IRR.

- **Why:** Modo estimated in October 2024 that at then-current capex, GB batteries required £74k/MW/year to £85k/MW/year 'to make an acceptable return on investment', against an outturn of £58k/MW/year that month. That is a clean, external cross-check on whether a project is investable at all.
- **Error if omitted:** A model can produce a passing IRR from an optimistic revenue curve without ever being challenged on whether that curve is above or below the level the market believes is required. The external benchmark catches curve optimism that internal consistency checks cannot.
- **How to implement:** Compute RequiredRevenue = the £/MW/yr that yields exactly the hurdle-rate equity IRR, and report it alongside: (a) the modelled base-case revenue; (b) the current GB index level; (c) the published required-revenue benchmark of £74-85k/MW/yr (Oct 2024 capex vintage — re-derive at current capex). If modelled revenue exceeds the index by more than the calibration factor explains, flag it.
- *Source:* Modo Energy, 'October 2024: GB battery energy storage research roundup' — 'At current Capex levels, this exceeds the £74k/MW/year to £85k/MW/year revenues that we estimate are required to make an acceptable return on investment'; October 2024 outturn £58k/MW/year, https://modoenergy.com/research/en/battery-energy-storage-research-roundup-great-britain-october-2024-revenues-buildout-forecast-capex-winter

#### H-31 · SHOULD · Recognise that a 0.1-5 MW BTM asset is below the size at which GB non-recourse project finance is available, and model the realistic financing route (corporate facility, lease/asset finance, portfolio aggregation, or all-equity) rather than assuming SPV project debt.

- **Why:** Every observed GB BESS project financing is at least an order of magnitude larger: Immingham 80 MW/240 MWh with a c.£42.7m facility; Harmony Energy a £60m facility with a £70m accordion on a five-year tenor; Zenobe £235m non-recourse from a five-bank club; Gresham House £240m. KBRA records that battery storage transactions are typically funded 'on a short-term basis, taking into account corporate risk rather than on a pure stand-alone, nonrecourse basis' by a narrow pool of specialised funders, and that fixed transaction costs make small deals uneconomic.
- **Error if omitted:** Applying grid-scale non-recourse debt terms to a 1 MW BTM project produces an equity IRR that cannot be achieved, because no lender will underwrite a £600k facility on project-finance diligence. The gearing, margin and fee assumptions are all wrong, and the fixed cost of the financing (legal, technical DD, model audit, insurance advisory) can be a double-digit percentage of a small project's capex.
- **How to implement:** Add a FinancingRoute enum {non-recourse project finance, portfolio/platform facility, corporate/balance-sheet, lease or asset finance, all-equity} with a minimum deal size gate (observed GB non-recourse deals are >= c.£35m facility size). Model fixed transaction costs as a lump sum (legal, technical adviser, model audit, insurance adviser, agency fees) that does not scale with deal size, so its percentage impact rises sharply below c.£20m. For portfolio routes, model cross-collateralisation and the diversification benefit on the revenue distribution explicitly.
- *Source:* KBRA, 'Battery Storage Funding Critical to Europe's Energy Transition', 28 June 2022, https://www.ipfa.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/Battery-Storage-Funding.pdf
- *Source:* Solar Power Portal, 'Santander provides debt finance for Econergy, Brockwell BESS', 2026 — c.£42.7m total for 80MW/240MWh, https://www.solarpowerportal.co.uk/battery-storage/santander-provides-debt-finance-for-econergy-brockwell-energy-bess
- *Source:* NatWest, 'The Battery Energy Storage System (BESS) market: delivering the backbone for a greener future' — Harmony Energy £60m facility (5-year tenor) with £70m accordion, later £130m total; Zenobe £235m syndicated, https://www.natwest.com/corporates/about-us/case-studies/the-battery-energy-storage-system-market-delivering-the-backbone-for-a-greener-future.html
- *Source:* Zenobe, 'Zenobe secures £235 million of non-recourse project finance debt from five banks', https://www.zenobe.com/news-and-events/zenobe-235-million-project-finance-five-banks-energy-storage/

#### H-32 · SHOULD · Add siting, fire safety, planning consent and the HOST's own property and business-interruption insurance as dated, evidenced project gates that must clear before financial close, and let them feed back into capex and layout. Require: written confirmation from the host's property insurer that the installation is acceptable and on what conditions (separation distances, test evidence, detection, suppression); UL 9540A cell/module/unit/installation-level test reports and IEC 62619 / BS EN IEC 62933-5-2 certification; a DSEAR assessment; a pre-application discussion with the local Fire and Rescue Service; and local planning permission or a documented permitted-development analysis. Track the live UK policy movement on bringing BESS within environmental permitting as a change-in-law item inside the asset's life.

- **Why:** For a BTM asset the battery sits next to an occupied commercial or industrial building whose insurer has a view on lithium-ion, and this is the most common practical killer of C&I BTM projects. The NFCC's updated guidance (approved December 2025, superseding the 2023 version) proposes an initial 30 m minimum separation between a BESS and occupied buildings before mitigation such as blast walls, a minimum 3 ft (0.914 m) between BESS units unless UL 9540A testing demonstrates no propagation, no vertical stacking, automatic detection with continuous combustible-gas monitoring, and a water supply of at least 25 L/s at site hydrants or roughly 180,000 L of static storage (120 minutes). On a constrained C&I site, a 30 m standoff is frequently decisive for whether the project exists at all, and it dictates cable runs, transformer siting and metering topology — all of which are capex and all of which change the electrical model. On planning: SI 2020/1218 (made 4 November 2020, in force 2 December 2020) removed electricity storage other than pumped hydro from the NSIP regime by disapplying the 50 MW threshold in England and 350 MW in Wales, so a BTM battery is always a local planning matter, never an NSIP.
- **Error if omitted:** Binary project failure, discovered late. An insurer refusal or a 30 m standoff requirement on a site that does not have 30 m of clear space kills the project after development spend. Where it does not kill it, it moves the battery — which changes cable length and losses, may require a new transformer or a different metering position, and can invalidate the whole avoided-import case if the battery ends up on the wrong side of a meter. Because FRSs are not statutory planning consultees, the absence of an objection is not evidence of acceptability; the risk surfaces at the insurer, after consent. None of this is a percentage adjustment to revenue — it is a gate, and gates must be modelled as gates.
- **How to implement:** Represent as a set of hard boolean gates with dated evidence, each of which must be TRUE with a document reference before the model is permitted to produce a bankable output: insurer_written_acceptance (with conditions listed and their capex costed); ul9540a_report_on_file (state which levels: cell, module, unit, installation); iec62619_cert; bs_en_iec_62933_5_2_cert; dsear_assessment_complete; frs_pre_application_held; planning_consent_granted OR permitted_development_opinion_on_file. Feed the geometric constraints into the layout: enforce separation_to_occupied_building >= 30 m unless a mitigation package is evidenced and its capex is in the build; enforce inter-unit spacing >= 0.914 m absent UL 9540A no-propagation evidence; no vertical stacking. Cost the fire-water provision explicitly (25 L/s hydrant flow, or ~180,000 L static storage) as a line item. Then propagate: recompute DC and AC cable runs and losses from the final position, and re-derive the metering topology (which determines whether discharge is avoided import or metered export — see the MPAN requirement). Add an environmental-permitting change-in-law scenario with a cost and a date range, and allocate it in the change-in-law matrix.
- *Source:* National Fire Chiefs Council, 'Grid Scale Battery Energy Storage System planning — Guidance for Fire and Rescue Services', approved for publication December 2025 (supersedes 2023 version), https://nfcc.org.uk/our-services/building-safety/grid-scale-energy-storage-system-planning-guidance-for-fire-and-rescue-services/ — 30 m initial separation from occupied buildings; 3 ft (0.914 m) between units absent UL 9540A no-propagation evidence; no vertical stacking; automatic detection plus continuous combustible-gas monitoring; 25 L/s hydrant flow or ~180,000 L static storage (120 minutes); FRS not a statutory planning consultee but early engagement advised
- *Source:* The Infrastructure Planning (Electricity Storage Facilities) Order 2020, SI 2020/1218, made 4 November 2020, in force 2 December 2020, https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2020/1218/made — removes electricity storage (except pumped hydro) from the NSIP regime; England 50 MW and Wales 350 MW thresholds disapplied
- *Source:* Defra / DESNZ, consultation on modernising environmental permitting including BESS in England — status: government considering consultation responses as at February 2026, https://questions-statements.parliament.uk/written-questions/detail/2026-02-23/115041; see also SLR Consulting, 'Preparing for change: new environmental permitting regulations for BESS', https://slrconsulting.com/insights/preparing-for-change-new-environmental-permitting-regulations-battery-energy-storage-solutions/ — 'BESS are not currently required to obtain an environmental permit under the Environmental Permitting Regulations 2016' [consultancy commentary, secondary]
- *Source:* Solar Energy UK, 'Defra progresses with BESS permitting plan', 3 June 2026, https://solarenergyuk.org/defra-progresses-with-bess-permitting-plan/ [trade body, secondary]
- *Source:* House of Commons Library research briefing on BESS safety and planning rules, June 2025 [as reported by International Fire and Safety Journal, https://internationalfireandsafetyjournal.com/battery-safety-and-planning-rules-examined-in-new-bess-briefing/ — secondary]
- *Source:* NFPA 855, 'Standard for the Installation of Stationary Energy Storage Systems' — source of the 100 ft remote-installation distance underlying the NFCC 30 m figure [referenced via NFCC guidance; not independently verified]

#### H-33 · SHOULD · Rate, disclose and stress the HOST as a credit obligor, and cost the loss-given-default of stranding. Require: the host's statutory accounts and any credit score; an explicit probability of default with its source; a stress case in which the host fails, closes the site or is acquired and the avoided-import revenue goes to zero; and a costed relocation/residual-value case covering de-installation, transport, reinstallation, revenue downtime, AND the permanent loss of location-tied contracts on a move. Model the location-tied losses explicitly: a Capacity Market agreement is tied to a Connection Capacity at a site, a DNO flexibility contract is tied to a defined zone, a DFS Unit's meter points must all sit in one DFS Zone, and response aggregation is only permitted within the same GSP Group.

- **Why:** For a BTM asset the avoided-import line is usually the largest revenue and is presented as the most 'contracted' — but it is an unsecured trade receivable from a single, typically unrated C&I corporate, ranking behind that corporate's secured lenders. A grid-scale model has no analogue for this and therefore no machinery to handle it. The location-tie is documented rather than assumed: NESO's Response Services guidance states 'Aggregation is possible when assets sit behind same GSP Group (subject to technical assurance being carried out on the risks associated with moving to GSP Group)', and the DFS Procurement Rules require that 'a DFS Unit shall only have allocated to it Unit Meter Points located in the same DFS Zone'. A move across a GSP Group boundary or a DFS Zone boundary is therefore a contract-terminating event, not a logistics cost. Base-rate anchoring is available: the UK company insolvency rate was 52.5 per 10,000 companies in 2025, 'equivalent to one in 190 companies entering insolvency'.
- **Error if omitted:** An unrated SME carries the whole DSCR with no capital held against it. Using the population base rate of ~0.53% per year, cumulative probability of at least one host default over a 15-year term is roughly 1 - 0.9947^15 = 7.7% for an average-risk company — and a single-site C&I host in a cyclical sector is not an average-risk company, so the true figure is materially higher and sector-specific. At that frequency the expected loss is not a rounding error, and because the loss is concentrated (the whole savings stream to zero at once, not a haircut) it drives the tail, which is exactly what the P90 and the DSCR are supposed to capture. Omitting the LGD side compounds it: relocation is treated as costless when it is capex plus downtime plus permanent loss of the CM agreement and any zone-tied flexibility revenue.
- **How to implement:** Inputs: host Companies House filings, a commercial credit score (Creditsafe/Experian) and the sector default rate for the host's SIC code. Set PD_annual from the credit score, floored at the population base rate of 0.53%/yr. Model as a hazard: in each project year, with probability PD, the host defaults; on default, avoided-import revenue goes to zero from that date and the model branches to a recovery case. Recovery case cashflows: -de-installation cost, -transport, -reinstallation at a replacement site, -revenue downtime for the outage duration (assume a realistic re-siting timeline, not weeks), -loss of the Capacity Market agreement for the remainder of its term including any termination fee, -loss of any DNO flexibility contract if the new site is outside the zone, -loss of response revenue if the new site is outside the original GSP Group, +residual value of the moved asset. Anchor the de-installation cost using published decommissioning benchmarks (~$50/kW demolition plus ~$38/kW material disposition at a 120 MW site) and note that small BTM projects will not achieve that unit cost — apply a small-scale multiplier and state it. Run the DSCR with the host-default hazard live and report the resulting minimum and average DSCR alongside the no-default case. Underwriting gate: refuse to treat avoided-import revenue as contracted unless the host's PD is documented and the LGD case is costed; consider requiring a parent guarantee, security over the site, or a step-in deed as a condition precedent.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services (DC/DM/DR) Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download — 'Aggregation is possible when assets sit behind same GSP Group (subject to technical assurance being carried out on the risks associated with moving to GSP Group)'
- *Source:* NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service Procurement Rules', v5.0, 18 May 2026, para 4.3.2(k) — 'a DFS Unit shall only have allocated to it Unit Meter Points located in the same DFS Zone'
- *Source:* The Insolvency Service, 'Company Insolvency Statistics', December 2025 (published 27 January 2026), https://www.gov.uk/government/collections/insolvency-service-official-statistics — company insolvency rate 52.5 per 10,000 companies, 'equivalent to one in 190 companies entering insolvency'
- *Source:* POWER Magazine, 'BESS Decommissioning and End-of-Life Planning: Why Care Today?', https://www.powermag.com/blog/bess-decommissioning-and-end-of-life-planning-why-care-today/ — for a 120 MW BESS site, $1.4m decommissioning and demolition plus $4.6m material disposition, i.e. ~$50/kW and ~$38/kW respectively [industry trade press, secondary; US costs, not GB, and at grid scale not BTM scale]
- *Source:* EMR Delivery Body, 'Capacity Market Rules' (current version) — Connection Capacity and site-linked agreements, transfer and termination provisions [primary code document; obtain current version]

#### H-34 · SHOULD · Model availability as TIMED OUTAGE EVENTS, not as an annual scalar derate on revenue. Simulate outages with a forced outage rate and a mean time to repair decomposed by cause — cell/module, PCS, transformer, HVAC, comms link, aggregator platform, host site planned shutdown, DNO planned outage — and evaluate each outage against the actual non-linear penalty regime in force at the moment it occurs. Explicitly include the two BTM-specific causes that no grid-scale model contains: the host's own planned site shutdown, which takes the battery with it, and the comms/aggregator platform as a shared dependency across the fleet.

- **Why:** An annual availability percentage applied to annual revenue is valid only if revenue is uniform in time and penalties are linear in downtime, and neither holds in GB. NESO's Response Services rules are explicit: the availability factor F is zero where there is 'any period or periods of unavailability within Settlement Period j', and F is computed as zero if availability within the settlement period is below 0.999 — on 20 Hz sampled data a full settlement period contains 36,000 rows, so 0.1% is 1.8 seconds. The EFA-block performance factor is K_e = min_j k_j, the minimum across the block. So 1.8 seconds of unavailability zeroes a settlement period, and a single bad settlement period sets the performance factor for the whole four-hour block. Capacity Market exposure is structurally different again: a Storage Generating Technology Class CMU must demonstrate Extended Performance during at least one Satisfactory Performance Day once every three years, so the risk is failing a specific dated test and being terminated — a function of WHEN the outage falls, not how many hours it lasted.
- **Error if omitted:** A 97% availability assumption applied as a 3% revenue haircut can conceal a 100% loss of a winter's ancillary revenue, and it cannot represent a CM termination at all. The error is not symmetric: because F is a step function at 99.9% within the settlement period and K is a block minimum, short frequent faults are punished far more than the hours-based average implies, while long rare faults during low-value periods are punished less. An annual scalar averages these into a number that is right for neither. The BTM-specific causes make it worse: a host's annual planned shutdown is typically several consecutive days, is scheduled (so its timing is knowable and may or may not fall in a high-value window), and is entirely absent from any grid-scale availability dataset; the aggregator platform is a correlated, fleet-wide failure that violates the independence assumption behind any per-asset availability figure.
- **How to implement:** For each cause c, specify a failure process (Poisson with rate lambda_c, or an empirical inter-arrival distribution) and a repair-time distribution (lognormal is standard for MTTR), plus a spares-holding and warranty-response SLA that truncates the repair tail. Simulate outage timelines across the same contemporaneous scenario blocks used for prices, so a forced outage can coincide with a high-price winter evening. Then evaluate penalties event-by-event, not in aggregate: (i) frequency response — for each settlement period touched by an outage set F=0, and set K_e = min over the block, so compute the EFA-block loss as the full block availability payment where any settlement period in it is degraded; (ii) DFS — recompute RV_ie and hence K_ie under the piecewise schedule, including the negative branch where K can reach -1; (iii) Capacity Market — check whether the outage window overlaps a Satisfactory Performance Day or Extended Performance Test obligation and, if so, branch to the termination case with its termination fee and loss of all remaining agreement revenue; (iv) BM — reduce MEL/MIL. Treat the host planned shutdown as a deterministic scheduled outage taken from the host's actual shutdown calendar (obtain it), not as a random event. Treat the aggregator platform as a common-mode failure applied simultaneously to all assets in the portfolio. Contract inputs to obtain: the O&M/LTSA response-time SLA, the spares strategy, the availability guarantee and its liquidated-damages cap — the LD cap is what actually bounds the recovery and it is usually far below the revenue at risk. Report both the annual availability percentage AND the revenue-weighted availability, and quote the difference.
- *Source:* NESO, 'Response Services (DC/DM/DR) Provider Guidance', v14, June 2026, https://www.neso.energy/document/276606/download — 'F_ije is zero where there is any period or periods of unavailability within Settlement Period j during the relevant Contracted EFA Block e, otherwise is F_ije 1'; availability factor is 0 where availability < 0.999 within the settlement period; 'For 20Hz sampled data, a full SP contains 36000 rows of data'; K_e = min_j k_j
- *Source:* NESO, 'Demand Flexibility Service Service Terms', v4.0, 9 April 2026, Schedule 1 — piecewise K_ie including K = -1 for RV < -100 (penalty branch)
- *Source:* EMR Delivery Body, 'Agreement Management — Existing', https://emrdeliverybody.nationalenergyso.com/IG/s/article/Agreement-Management-Existing — 'A Capacity Committed CMU with a Storage Generating Technology Class ... must demonstrate Extended Performance during at least one Satisfactory Performance Day' once every three years
- *Source:* Elexon CMAG, 'CP370 — Changes to Extended Performance Test', https://www.elexon.co.uk/cmag/change-proposals/cp370/ — proposal to revise the EPT methodology to better reflect storage contracted volume and duration
- *Source:* The Electricity Capacity (Amendment) Regulations 2025, SI 2025/183, explanatory memorandum, https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2025/183/pdfs/uksiem_20250183_en_001.pdf — 'EPTs are a subfunction of the SPD requirement'; standard satisfactory performance days during winter
- *Source:* DNV, '2024 Battery Scorecard', https://www.dnv.com/publications/2024-battery-scorecard/ — real-world battery performance and degradation benchmarking
- *Source:* Energy-Storage.news, 'DNV verifies 98.7% availability of Fluence's global battery energy storage fleet', https://www.energy-storage.news/dnv-verifies-98-7-availability-of-fluences-global-battery-energy-storage-fleet/ — 98.7% MW-weighted fleet availability [vendor-supplied, DNV-verified; selection bias likely]
- *Source:* IEEE, 'Forced Outage Rate of Utility-Scale Energy Storage Units', https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/11438461 — notes that 'forced outage characteristics of ESS remain largely unexplored'
- *Source:* EPRI Storage Wiki, 'Performance and Reliability Foresight', https://storagewiki.epri.com/index.php/Performance_and_Reliability_Foresight; EPRI BESS Failure Incident Database — incidents per GW falling from ~10 in 2018 to under 1 by 2023 [as reported by StorPeak, https://storpeak.com/insights/bess-failure-database-epri/ — secondary]

#### H-35 · CONSIDER · Treat the forward-looking DUoS charge structure as an explicit structural risk with a scenario, because Ofgem has paused the DUoS Significant Code Review pending REMA.

- **Why:** Ofgem's April 2025 update states 'we don't intend to prioritise DUoS Reform work until more is known about the direction that the REMA project will take'. The Access and Forward-Looking Charges SCR final decision (May 2022) covered only the connection charging boundary and access rights; forward-looking DUoS and TNUoS charge design were removed from its scope and moved to separate, now-paused, processes. The red/amber/green structure a 10–15 year case depends on is therefore explicitly undecided.
- **Error if omitted:** An underwriting case that assumes today's red/amber/green band structure and spreads persist for 15 years carries an unpriced regulatory risk on its principal avoidable revenue line. Lenders should see this quantified, not assumed away.
- **How to implement:** Add a DUoS-structure scenario axis: (a) status quo bands; (b) flattened bands (red-green spread halved, mirroring the DCP228 direction of travel which rebalanced CDCM recovery away from unit rates on 1 April 2018); (c) capacity-led charging (unit spread → ~0, capacity rate up). Report DSCR and equity IRR under each; require the base case to survive scenario (b) at P90.
- *Source:* Ofgem, Distribution Use of System charges: Significant Code Review update, 22 April 2025, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/policy/distribution-use-system-charges-significant-code-review-update
- *Source:* Ofgem, Access and Forward-Looking Charges Significant Code Review: Decision and Direction, May 2022, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2022-05/Access%20SCR%20-%20Final%20Decision.pdf
- *Source:* Ofgem, DCUSA DCP228 'Revenue matching in the CDCM' decision — implemented 1 April 2018, https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/publications/distribution-connection-and-use-system-agreement-dcp228-revenue-matching-cdcm-0

#### H-36 · CONSIDER · Use GB-specific, sourced cost of capital, and apply different discount rates to contracted and merchant cashflow streams within the same asset.

- **Why:** This is settled GB valuation practice, not a refinement. Gresham House applies 7.5% to floor-contracted cashflows, 11.35% to merchant upside above the floor and 10.85% to wholly uncontracted merchant cashflow, for a weighted average of 10.33% at 31 December 2025 (10.73% at 31 December 2024). Gore Street applies 7.25-9.25% to energised contracted income, 8.75-9.50% to energised uncontracted income and 10.75-12.00% to pre-construction assets, weighted average 10.2%.
- **Error if omitted:** A single blended discount rate misprices the effect of adding a floor or toll: it will show contracting as value-destructive (because it caps upside) when in the market it is value-accretive (because it re-rates the discount rate on the contracted portion by 300-400bps). The model will then recommend staying merchant when the market pays for contracting.
- **How to implement:** NPV = sum_t [ ContractedCF_t / (1+r_c)^t ] + sum_t [ MerchantUpsideCF_t / (1+r_mu)^t ] + sum_t [ UncontractedMerchantCF_t / (1+r_m)^t ]. GB reference set (Dec 2025): r_c = 7.5%, r_mu = 11.35%, r_m = 10.85%. Alternative GB reference set (Mar 2025): r_c = 7.25-9.25%, r_m = 8.75-9.50% for energised assets, 10.75-12.00% pre-construction. Add a construction-phase premium: Gresham House applies +50bps during the 30-day proving period after practical completion. Sources disagree by 100-200bps on the merchant rate — carry both and report the NPV range, do not average.
- *Source:* Gresham House Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Annual Report and Financial Statements 2025', 2026, https://greshamhouse.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRID-Annual-Review_25_20.04-FINAL.pdf
- *Source:* Gore Street Energy Storage Fund plc, 'Audited Full Year Results to 31 March 2025', 2025 — Discount Rate Matrix, https://www.gsenergystoragefund.com/docs/librariesprovider22/archive/results/prelims-2025.pdf


---

## 2. What the literature does not settle

- No published skip-rate statistics decompose acceptance by BID/OFFER PRICE BAND. NESO and LCP Delta both measure skip volumetrically among already-in-merit actions, so by construction the metric is price-blind. There is therefore no published basis for a price-elasticity-of-acceptance function. Any such function in a model is an assumption, not literature.
- No published skip-rate statistics are broken down by GSP GROUP or DNO region with numbers. Modo states North Scotland has the highest battery skip rates and LCP Delta documents zonal desk allocation as a cause, but neither publishes a regional table. A model that needs a locational skip factor must derive it from the raw NESO In-Merit PSA and Exclusion Reasons datasets, or state the factor as an assumption.
- No published skip-rate statistics are broken down by BM UNIT SIZE or by BATTERY DURATION. LCP Delta's stakeholder interviews report a perceived 'bias towards prioritising larger assets over smaller, distributed ones' and note the duration limitation 'unfairly penalise[s] smaller assets', but this is qualitative. Whether a 1 MW BTM Secondary BMU faces a materially higher skip rate than a 50 MW front-of-meter BMU is unquantified — and it is the single most important unknown for this asset class.
- It is unclear whether a BTM C&I battery routed via a VLP is measured in NESO's battery technology skip-rate dataset or in the separate 'In Merit PSA for Demand Side Flexibility Unit' and 'Stage 5 DSF Technology-Specific Skip Rate' datasets. If DSF, the applicable skip rate may differ materially from the published battery figure. This should be resolved against the raw datasets before parameterising.
- The 2026 skip-rate figures disagree across sources by 7–8 percentage points (49%→38% per NESO/ESS News vs 41%→31% per Modo for overlapping windows). The cause is presumably a different variant/stage tag, but neither source states which. Until reconciled, both should be carried as a range.
- NESO's skip-rate reduction TARGET is not yet public. Ofgem has directed absolute numerical dispatch targets and 'technological parity' by end of the financial year, with a final proposal due February 2026 and root-cause recommendations April 2026. The terminal skip rate a model should assume is therefore genuinely unknown.
- The outcome of NESO's constraint-retrading consultation (Options 3A / 3AA / 3C, live as of July 2026) is unresolved. Option 3A would remove storage bid revenue inside active constraints entirely. This is a live binary risk with no published decision date found.
- No source read gives the historical distribution of BM bid/offer PRICES achieved by batteries in a form usable for revenue modelling. Modo reports batteries and CCGTs 'receive similar prices' with CCGT offers 6% lower and CCGT bids 19% higher than batteries, and pumped storage receiving a premium in both directions — but no absolute £/MWh distribution by period or price band was found in public sources.
- The interaction between GC0166 MDO/MDB and skip rates is untested. GC0166's stated purpose is 'increased economic dispatch of Electricity Storage Modules', but no published estimate of the expected skip-rate reduction from MDO/MDB was found. NESO's own roadmap defers root-cause analysis recommendations to April 2026.
- Whether the Gale et al. (2026) skip-rate elasticity (~£12,000/MW/yr per 10 percentage points) scales down to a 0.1–5 MW BTM asset was not established — the paper's asset assumptions and duration were not accessible (ScienceDirect returned 403 for full text; figures are from the abstract and search indexing). The elasticity should be treated as indicative for a front-of-meter merchant asset and re-derived for a BTM case.
- No public source quantifies the all-in cost of BM access for a small BTM asset (WA API or EDT/EDL connectivity, control telephony, 1 Hz operational metering, asset metering under P375, MOA and HHDC appointments, VLP qualification). Without this, the break-even MW at which BM participation becomes NPV-positive for a BTM site cannot be established from the literature.
- NESO stated an intention to relax operational metering accuracy, refresh rate and latency requirements for <1 MW aggregated assets in early 2026, but the specific relaxed values were not found in the sources read.
- Is the one-year DSR route bankable for a behind-the-meter battery? On the face of Rules 2.3.4(b) and 2.3.4(e), a Proven DSR CMU whose component is 'import reduction achieved by behind-the-meter Storage' is de-rated at the DSR factor (0.8558 for 2026/27 T-1) rather than the storage EFC (0.1361 at 1h) — a 6.3x difference for identical hardware. Rule 2.3.4(e) closes this only for Unproven DSR CMUs bidding multi-year. No public document states whether the Delivery Body would reject such an application under its Rule 4.4 discretion, or whether DESNZ intends to close the one-year case. This is the single largest unresolved economic question for the BTM segment and needs legal advice before it enters a base case.
- The exact monthly weighting factors WF_mx per delivery year could not be retrieved: EMR Settlement Ltd returned HTTP 403 to automated fetch. The formula is settled (share of GB monthly demand over a rolling 3-year period, to 3 decimal places) but the published values must be obtained directly for accurate monthly cashflow and minimum-DSCR modelling.
- The inference that the plateau of the published EFC curve equals the Storage class TCWAA (0.9216 for the 2026/27 T-1 and 2029/30 T-4 vintages) is derived from Schedule 3B rather than stated. Since Adjusted Connection Capacity for the Extended Performance Test is Connection Capacity x TCWAA, this number sets the EPT feasibility constraint and should be confirmed against the relevant Auction Guidelines.
- Which minimum durations correspond to each named Storage Generating Technology Class, and the exact rounding convention applied when an applicant's usable energy divided by nominated Connection Capacity falls between bins, are set out in the Auction Guidelines rather than the Rules. The NESO EMR Delivery Body guidance pages are JavaScript-rendered and were not retrievable. The 0.5h binning and a floor-to-nearest-bin convention are inferred from the published factor table structure.
- The published de-rating factors are exactly linear in duration for short durations in every vintage checked, but the point at which the curve becomes concave differs between vintages (about 4.5h for the 2026/27 T-1, about 8h for the 2029/30 T-4). No public documentation explains the shape difference. A model should read the table rather than fit a functional form.
- What NESO's forward de-rating trajectory will be is genuinely contested. Modo Energy's July 2026 view is 'downward'; the observed record shows a sharp fall for 2026/27 and 2027/28 followed by partial recovery for 2028/29 and 2029/30. The Rule 2.3.4B 5%-of-Loss-of-Load-Occurrences trigger and the underlying stochastic simulation make the outcome dependent on the whole future capacity mix, which no public forecast pins down.
- The precise remaining embedded-benefit stack for a post-TCR behind-the-meter C&I site — specifically whether any triad-linked locational TNUoS demand exposure persists for half-hourly settled sites, and the current BSUoS treatment — was not resolved from the sources read. Ofgem's TCR decision is clear on residual charges moving to fixed bands and on removal of the Transmission Generation Residual for generators, but the residual non-residual/locational demand charging base for HH sites has been subject to subsequent CUSC modifications not covered here. This needs the network-charging lens, not the CM lens.
- There is no public price index or liquidity data for secondary trading of Capacity Obligations, so the achievable price and bid-ask spread for shedding or acquiring obligation are unsourced. A model must treat any secondary-trading mitigation value as an assumption.
- Ofgem's annual reports on the operation of the Capacity Market were retrieved but the 2024/25 edition was not available at the URL found (the file served covered 2023-24). Statistics on how many CMUs actually fail Satisfactory Performance Days or Extended Performance Tests each year — the key empirical calibration for the realistic downside — were therefore not captured and should be extracted from the correct Ofgem annual report.
- Whether NESO or the Delivery Body applies any additional scrutiny to a battery that nominates Connection Capacity at the Rule 3.5.6 floor of 50% purely to optimise its duration bin is not addressed in any document read. The Rule permits it on its face; whether it is routinely accepted in practice is unknown.
- Can a battery physically located behind a C&I customer's supply meter be prequalified as an Eligible Asset at all, and if so under which registration model? The Procurement Rules define Relevant Metering as 'the BSC accredited half hourly metering registered to each Eligible Asset' and make inclusion of all response energy volumes in ABSVD a pre-condition to participation, but no public NESO document addresses the case where the asset sits inside a customer's existing MPAN rather than having its own. NESO's December 2025 co-location guidance addresses metering topology but assumes the site is already a participant.
- How is the physical response energy settled for the C&I host? ABSVD adjusts the BSC party's imbalance position, but a BTM battery's discharge reduces the site's metered import, which is settled under the customer's retail supply contract. Whether the value accrues to the host (avoided import at the all-in retail rate), to the optimiser, or is netted out is not addressed in any public document reviewed, and the answer moves per-MW economics by six figures.
- Can Response and Reserve (QR/BR/SR) be split across the same asset in the same window today? The Response Service Terms and QR Service Terms contain mirror-image 'our service prevails, the other is deemed unavailable' clauses, each subject to a NESO determination of no conflict. The Quick Reserve design says splitting with Response is a future intention subject to EBR Article 18 consultation. No published document states the current permission unambiguously, and the EAC co-optimises all four service families in one auction, which invites the assumption that they can be stacked.
- Is there an express Capacity Market carve-out for DC/DM/DR equivalent to QR Service Terms paragraph 17.6? The Response Service Terms paragraph 12.1 lists third-party agreements 'pursuant to the Capacity Market Rules or otherwise' among the things that must not impair availability, with no visible carve-out, whereas Quick Reserve has one and has been added to the list of Relevant Balancing Services.
- What are the current Maximum Market Price and Minimum Market Price for the response auctions? Both are defined as 'such price as may be notified in writing by NESO from time to time' and are not published in the Procurement Rules. The 2021Q3 DCL clearing price of exactly £17.00/MW/h suggests a binding cap at that time, but the current values could not be sourced.
- What is the empirical distribution of the K factor for a well-run BESS? NESO publishes the methodology and reserves the right to publish non-anonymised performance data, but no public dataset of realised K factors by unit was located, so the expected penalty drag on availability revenue must be assumed rather than measured.
- What price compression should be assumed when Dynamic Response moves from 4-hour EFA blocks to 30-minute procurement blocks, and what share of volume migrates to the instructed real-time market? NESO has announced the direction but not the timetable or the volume split.
- Does the Reserved Capacity requirement bind in the bidding direction for a single-directional contract? The Procurement Rules state an aggregate test across both directions; the SOE Monitoring Guidance states that for a single-directional contract there is 'ample capacity available in the opposite direction' so no reserve is needed in the bidding direction. These are reconcilable but the drafting is not identical, and the difference is 40 % of DR capacity.
- What are typical aggregator commercial terms (share of revenue, minimum term, performance guarantees) for a sub-1 MW BTM asset accessing DC/DM/DR? No public source was found, yet for a 0.1–5 MW asset this is often the single largest deduction from gross ancillary revenue.
- What replaces Mandatory Frequency Response, and does the replacement open a route for distribution-connected or BTM assets? NESO issued a Request for Information on MFR and DR on 28 May 2026 and has committed to 'a compliant, accessible replacement', but the design is not published.
- How much of the observed DC price is being set by assets that are simultaneously capturing Capacity Market and wholesale value, and therefore bidding below their standalone cost? Without unit-level cost data the merit order cannot be reconstructed, so the floor price for a new entrant with no other revenue is unknown.
- No published study quantifies the perfect-foresight premium for a GB behind-the-meter C&I battery at half-hourly resolution co-optimising DUoS red-band avoidance, peak shaving, wholesale and the co-optimised ancillary auction. The available anchors are from different markets and resolutions and disagree in a way that matters: 85%+ (PJM hourly, 12h device, 2002-2007), 89% (German continuous intraday, 1MW/1MWh, 2023), 75-95% (UK, 2016). Choosing a haircut requires either an in-house backtest or an explicit, defended assumption.
- Whether Garifi et al.'s exactness conditions survive in a GB BTM model that simultaneously contains negative wholesale prices, a free PV curtailment variable, an export limit, and capacity-payment terms is not addressed anywhere in the literature. Each of these individually is known or suspected to break a sufficient condition. Nobody has published the combined case.
- Pozo (2023) gives the tightest linear (convex hull) formulation of a storage system with complementarity, but the paper is closed access and it was not possible to verify whether that convex hull is an exact relaxation (i.e. integral) or merely the tightest linear outer approximation. Arroyo (2022) published a counterexample to a claimed binary-free convex model. This should be resolved before any decision to drop binaries on tightness grounds.
- No public cycle-depth stress function exists for the LFP grid modules actually being installed in GB in 2025-26. The available published functions are for NMC 18650 cells (Xu et al., pre-2018) and a single LFP cell (Hesse et al., 2019). A model using either is extrapolating chemistry and vintage, and the degradation cost per MWh should be treated as a sensitivity, not a constant.
- The correct terminal state-of-charge valuation for a rolling-horizon controller in a half-hourly GB market is unresolved. The literature uses cyclic conditions, fixed terminal energy, or long horizons with short commitment windows, but no source quantifies the residual bias for a 1-4 hour asset with a 24-48 hour horizon on GB prices.
- Whether NESO's Linked Service Windows (default 2-hour minimum, live January 2026) will in practice be the binding granularity, or whether flexible 30-minute windows will dominate, is not yet observable. This determines how much interleaving between frequency response and arbitrage is genuinely available and therefore directly affects the optimal duration in the build decision.
- NESO's EBR Article 18 consultation on Dynamic Response Services (submitted to Ofgem 1 May 2026, decision expected July 2026) proposes changes to performance monitoring. The resulting penalty and derating regime is unknown and sits inside the underwriting horizon.
- No source found gives the auxiliary/thermal-management parasitic load for a 0.1-5 MW containerised GB BESS as a percentage of rated power. Hesse et al. explicitly exclude EMS and TMS from their otherwise measurement-based efficiency model, so the published round-trip efficiencies are all optimistic by an unstated margin.
- How much of a pay-as-clear co-optimised auction price a small (sub-5 MW) price-taking BTM asset realistically captures, net of aggregator margin, is not published. The aggregator revenue share is a contractual term that the literature does not cover and that materially changes equity IRR.
- The interaction between the 2025 co-optimised ancillary auction and the wholesale trading decision has no published treatment. Because ancillary volumes are fixed at 14:00 D-1 and energy can be traded afterwards, the correct model is a two-stage stochastic program; every published GB stacking study predates this structure and treats the two as either separate or jointly co-optimised with common foresight.
- Does the skip-rate improvement trend continue, plateau, or reverse? NESO's own published data shows battery Stage-5 PSA skip rates falling from 49.1% (H1 2025) to 38.0% (H1 2026), but the partial H2 2026 data (July–August: 44.9%) suggests the trend may have stalled or reversed, and NESO has stated that 'the low hanging fruit has already been picked'. No source gives a credible terminal skip rate.
- What is the correct forward locational skip differential? The Scotland penalty was ~10pp in January 2025 and ~15pp in June 2026 — it widened rather than narrowed. Whether NESO's constraint optimisation (April 2026), the OBP–NCMS interface (early 2026) and Reformed National Pricing narrow, preserve or invert this is unresolved.
- No public benchmark exists for aggregator/optimiser commercial shares in GB. Every consulted source (BCLP, Pexapark, Energy-Storage.news, Modo) describes the structures (revenue share, floor, toll, swap) but none discloses a percentage or £/MW/year fee. This is a material gross-to-net gap that must be filled from the actual executed contract.
- How much of the residual skip rate is genuinely irreducible? NESO distinguishes avoidable from unavoidable skips but has not published the split. Its Root Cause Analysis programme findings are only due April 2026 and the Independent Dispatch Strategic Review is ongoing.
- Will the sub-10 MW and VLP-route skip penalties stay closed? They narrowed sharply between January 2025 and June 2026 in my analysis of NESO's data, but no published source explains the mechanism, so there is no basis for asserting the convergence is structural rather than a sampling artefact of a rapidly growing fleet.
- How should the price-independence of skip probability conditional on being in-merit be reconciled with rational bidding? If bidding more aggressively does not improve acceptance, the equilibrium bidding strategy is unclear and the literature offers no model of it. Gale et al. note imperfect price capture under pay-as-bid but do not derive an optimal bidding rule under a skip constraint.
- How do MDO/MDB/FSoE actually change dispatch once fully implemented? Modo's 24%→69% addressable-volume estimate is a forecast, not an observation. Ofgem itself required NESO to publish a roadmap and to run a post-implementation review within 12 months, explicitly to establish the impact of MDO/MDB on control-room decision making and the accuracy of FSoE models.
- What is the magnitude of imbalance exposure for a BTM battery that is instructed but under-delivers because the host site's load moved? No source quantifies delivery-failure rates for BTM BM Units, and NESO's operational metering and dynamic-parameter accuracy expectations make this a compliance as well as a financial risk.
- Is NESO's published skip-rate dataset itself stable? NESO revised the data on 18 November 2025 to correct a long-notice unit exclusion bug 'affecting consistency'. There is no published error bar on the metric, and no statement of how future methodology changes will be back-applied.
- What is the effect of P462 on constraint-driven bid revenue in £/MW/year for a specific location? The direction is clear (charging prices move from deeply negative toward £0/MWh) and the consumer saving is claimed at £518m by 2030, but no source quantifies the per-asset revenue loss by constraint zone.
- Does the 300 MW Small Aggregated Asset metering cohort still have headroom, and what happens to assets admitted under it if the cap binds or the derogation is withdrawn? NESO publishes the cap but not the utilisation.
- How should skip rate interact with Balancing Reserve? A unit holding Balancing Reserve has committed headroom that is delivered via BOA; whether reserve-holding units experience systematically lower skip rates (because the control room is contractually pointed at them) is not addressed by any source consulted, and LCP explicitly list ancillary-reserved assets as an unexcluded limitation of the skip metric.
- The Capacity Market supplier charge rate in £/kW of peak-period demand. The statutory basis is unambiguous (16:00–19:00 on working days in November–February; apportioned by share of Total Relevant Demand from delivery year 2024/25), but no authoritative public per-kW rate was locatable; EMR Settlement's supplier charge schedules and Ofgem's Capacity Market annual reports both returned 403 or unparseable content. A model needs this number and it should be obtained directly from EMR Settlement or from the customer's own invoice.
- What fraction of C&I supply contracts in the 0.1–5 MW segment actually pass DUoS red-band, TNUoS Triad and the CM supplier charge through on their native time basis, versus flattening them into a p/kWh unit rate. This single parameter determines whether most of the claimed BTM network savings are realisable at all, and no published survey of GB C&I contract structures was found.
- The DCP161 exceeded-capacity rate multiplier across all fourteen DNOs. NGED sets it equal to the capacity rate for 2026/27; 2017-era commentary claims up to 4× at other DNOs. The full cross-DNO picture requires reading all fourteen charging statements.
- The Feed-in Tariff levelisation cost expressed in p/kWh. Ofgem publishes the apportionment method (share of GB supply market volume, with EII and Exempt Supply exclusions) but not a headline unit rate; the figure must be derived from total scheme cost and total supply volume, or taken from the customer's contract.
- The forward structure of DUoS. Ofgem paused the DUoS Significant Code Review in April 2025 pending REMA, TNUoS reform and the Energy Cost Allocation and Recovery Review. Whether red/amber/green unit-rate banding survives to the end of a 15-year underwriting horizon, and in what form, is genuinely undecided.
- Whether the TNUoS demand locational element will remain Triad-based. NESO's 2026/27 methodology still charges HH demand on Triad volumes, but TNUoS reform is an active Ofgem workstream and a move away from Triads would eliminate the last remaining triad value.
- Whether a BTM battery co-located with rooftop PV can be structured to obtain any part of the Non-Final Demand Site or Final Consumption Levy exemptions through separate metering of the storage asset, and whether the additional MPAN's own TCR band and fixed charges would exceed the levy saving. This is a site-specific optimisation that the published rules permit in principle but do not resolve.
- Peer-reviewed quantification of GB BTM battery value under the post-2022 charging regime. Academic sources located (Journal of Energy Storage, Applied Energy, IEEE, MDPI) were behind 403 access controls and, in any case, most pre-date the TCR and BSUoS reforms; the peer-reviewed literature appears not yet to have caught up with the 2022–2026 GB charging changes, so regulatory primary sources currently outrank it for this lens.
- Baseline treatment for an I&C battery differs materially between DNOs and the NGED convention is not unambiguous from the published documents.
