UKPN applies a Zero baseline to Storage across SAOU, LT-SU and DA-SU. NGED's baseline workbook maps I&C 'Stored energy' to BL_AsCap, which resolves to 'Asset Capacity' for the default DTD/GTU direction and 'Zero' for DTU/GTD — but NGED's Revenue Estimator separately instructs that 'FSPs with batteries should input as generation where assets are commercial', which under the ENA signage convention (generation baseline positive) would make an Asset Capacity baseline unachievable for a discharging battery. The economically sensible reading is that the Asset Capacity baseline reflects NGED's network-planning assumption that a BTM battery would otherwise be CHARGING at peak, so ceasing to charge counts as delivery. This must be confirmed in writing with NGED before any revenue is underwritten; the difference is potentially a factor of two on deliverable MWh.
- Whether DNO flexibility services are, as at 2026, included in the Capacity Market's list of Relevant Balancing Services. Cornwall Insight's December 2023 review found they were not, exposing a CMU delivering a DSO service during a stress event to non-delivery penalties, and noted the list is periodically reviewed with a lag. The Market Facilitator's Revenue Stacking Requirements rule (referenced by NGED in March 2026) may have changed this. The current Capacity Market Rules must be checked directly.
- The NGED vs UKPN price divergence for the nominally identical SAOU product (NGED per-CMZ availability ceiling median GBP 3.16/MW/h; UKPN indicative availability median GBP 65-93/MW/h) is unexplained by any document found. It may reflect different ceiling-setting methodologies (present value of deferred reinforcement per zone), different zone economics, or different unit conventions. Do not average; model per DNO.
- The unit convention for 'Total Availability Instructions in 25/26 (MWh)' in SSEN's SLC31E report. If this is MW multiplied by hours of instructed availability, the derived ~14 instructed availability hours per contracted MW per year for VAOU is a devastating figure for any availability-led revenue case. Confirm with SSEN.
- How much of the observed collapse in NGED clearing prices (SU_SEP dispatch median GBP 958/MWh in 2024/25 vs SU-HV awarded median GBP 91.93/MWh for autumn-2025 delivery) is a genuine repricing versus a product-definition change. The two are different product codes; a like-for-like series is not published.
- Whether the DNO's ability to derate or reject bids on past delivery performance (UKPN) and to prioritise on historical performance at the acceptance cut-off (SSEN) is applied quantitatively, and if so with what formula. No DNO publishes the derating function.
- The realised award probability and realised clearing prices in UKPN's Localflex day-ahead and long-term auctions. UKPN publishes competition data (requirements and indicative ranges) and a 'Flexibility Dispatches' dataset on its Open Data Portal, but the dispatch dataset returned no records to the public API at the time of this survey, so UKPN realised prices could not be verified independently. NGED and SSEN realised data was obtainable; UKPN's was not.
- Whether DNO flexibility contracts are assignable to a lender or a step-in party. The ENA Standard Agreement paragraph 11.2 makes the agreement personal to the parties and requires prior written consent to assign (not to be unreasonably withheld). No DNO publishes a direct agreement or step-in regime, which is a live question for any financing that gives the DNO revenue line credit.
- Whether NGED's move from a 15-minute Operational Utilisation dispatch notice to Day-Ahead notice for long-term HV (2024) is being mirrored by all DNOs, and therefore whether any product still requires sub-BM-gate-closure response from a BTM asset. NGED retains OU_15 in its product list but reported zero dispatch across 60 OU-15 zones in 2024/25.
- The extent to which Ofgem's Future of Distributed Flexibility programme and the Market Facilitator's flexibility market rules will standardise products, baselines and settlement across DNOs, and on what timetable. NGED cites the Market Facilitator's Revenue Stacking Requirements rule and a 'Defining Markets More Accurately to Support Stacking' working group as at March 2026, but the rule texts themselves could not be retrieved during this survey (Elexon and ENA both returned HTTP 403).
- Minimum offer sizes, baselining conventions and realised clearing prices for SP Energy Networks, Northern Powergrid and Electricity North West were not separately verified. SPEN, NPg and SSEN share the Flexible Power platform and are likely aligned to the NGED conventions, and ENWL signposts on Piclo Flex, but this should be confirmed before underwriting a site in those licence areas.
- No public source gives a GB-specific 'percent of perfect foresight' figure. Modo's methodology page confirms it re-optimises in 2-hour increments with injected forecast errors and outages, but the resulting haircut versus a perfect-foresight run is not published. The TBx capture ratios (2.0× TB1, 1.42× TB2) are the only public calibration handle, and they conflate forecast quality with multi-venue access and churn. A model cannot currently separate 'how good is my forecast' from 'how many venues do I trade' using public data.
- Modo publishes at least two GB indices whose numbers disagree materially — the revenue-stack evolution series (2024 £60.7k, 2025 £80.1k/MW/yr) and the de-risking article (2024 £51k, 2025 £72k/MW/yr). The basis difference (duration, asset population, Capacity Market inclusion) is not fully documented. Which one an underwriting should benchmark to is unresolved.
- Cornwall Insight (Oct 2024) forecast £96/kW for a 2-hour GB battery in 2025 against a realised ~£72–80k/MW/yr. Whether this is a one-off miss or a systematic forecaster optimism bias cannot be established from a single comparison, but it is large enough that running a single third-party curve is indefensible.
- No published GB dataset gives realised revenue for behind-the-meter batteries in the 0.1–5 MW band. Every benchmark is calibrated on BMU-registered assets of 7 MW and above. The correct derate from the fleet benchmark to a 1 MW BTM asset is an open question; Modo itself recommends custom forecasting runs for such assets.
- The P415 baseline eligibility rule (≥5 non-event days in any 60 days) implies a hard ceiling on dispatch frequency, but no source quantifies the resulting annual revenue ceiling for a battery that would otherwise cycle daily. Whether a VTP's own alternative baselining methodology (permitted under the rules) relaxes this, and by how much, is not publicly documented.
- Whether P415 supplier compensation remains mutualised is uncertain. Ofgem directed mutualisation over the BSC Panel's unanimous preference for VLP-pays, explicitly acknowledged the uneven playing field it creates between suppliers and VLPs, and said it expects industry to progress changes. A VLP-pays outcome would reduce BTM wholesale margin by the Price Cap Methodology sourcing cost per Deviation Volume MWh.
- The REMA proposals to realign the market trading deadline with gate closure and require PNs to match traded positions would remove a chunk of post-gate-closure optimisation and NIV chasing, but no source quantifies the revenue impact on a battery. Timing is unspecified beyond 'this Parliament' for TNUoS and 'later this year' for the balancing reform consultation.
- Whether the imbalance settlement period will shorten to 15 or 5 minutes is under active consideration with no decision or date. This would materially change the value of fast-responding storage and the granularity every model must run at — a 5-minute settlement period makes half-hourly optimisation obsolete.
- Public sources give optimiser revenue shares of 3–10% (a vendor source) and confirm there is no standard GB agreement, but no dataset of actual GB optimiser fees exists in the public domain. Fixed-fee, SaaS and hybrid structures are described without pricing.
- No public source states the typical floor or toll price achievable for a single sub-5 MW behind-the-meter asset. Both GB reference transactions are portfolio-scale (568 MW and 789 MW) with investment-grade counterparties.
- The interaction between avoided-import value and P415 deviation-volume value is asserted to be substitutive (Orkestra: P415's marginal value falls at pass-through-tariff sites) but not quantified. The correct allocation rule for a half-hour in which both are available is not established in the literature.
- Elexon's own primary documentation (imbalance pricing guidance, P375, P415, P442 modification records) could not be retrieved directly — the site blocks automated access. Several parameters here (MHHS dates, P442 implementation, P375 implementation) rest on secondary law-firm or search-index sources and should be confirmed against the Elexon primary record before being relied on for underwriting.
- NO published GB study quantifies the stacking discount for a behind-the-meter C&I battery — i.e. what fraction of the naive sum of standalone service values survives joint optimisation. Seward et al. (2022) is the closest peer-reviewed GB BTM work but evaluates five mutually-exclusive strategies rather than decomposing a joint stack. This is the single most important gap for the lens and it means any discount used must be derived from the model itself, not imported.
- No GB-specific published post-installation study comparing modelled to realised revenue for BTM C&I storage was located. The available evidence on the modelled-versus-realised gap is US (Carmona et al. 2024, showing ~45% on demand charges between rule-based and stochastic-optimal control; Berkeley Lab's PV+storage hybrid work, which I could not access — emp.lbl.gov and eta-publications.lbl.gov both returned HTTP 403). The Berkeley Lab briefing (Seel, Mulvaney Kemp, Cheyette, Gorman, Chuang, Millstein, 'Identifying Barriers to Solar and Storage Hybrids', 2026) appears from search snippets to report observed PV+S operations realising $39/MWh, or 62% of modelled value, in 2024 — I could NOT verify this against the primary document and it must be treated as UNVERIFIED until fetched.
- Actual DUoS red/amber/green unit rates in p/kWh per DNO for 2025/26 and 2026/27 could not be retrieved: the rates live in the Annex 1 spreadsheets attached to each DNO's charging statement rather than in the statement PDF. Only directional year-on-year changes and three example red-band windows were obtainable. Any model must pull these from the DNO's published 'Schedule of charges and other tables' spreadsheet directly.
- The actual £/site/day values of the TDR and DUoS fixed residual by band were not obtained — only the band THRESHOLDS. These are needed to size the band-jump penalty when a site uprates its MIC for a battery.
- Whether battery storage, and specifically grid-charged electricity subsequently exported, is eligible for Smart Export Guarantee payments is not addressed on Ofgem's SEG page. This materially affects the co-located solar+storage case and must be confirmed with the specific SEG licensee.
- The DUoS Significant Code Review is explicitly PAUSED (Ofgem update, 22 April 2025) pending REMA, TNUoS reform and the Energy Cost Allocation and Recovery review, with no implementation dates. The entire volumetric red-band value proposition — the main genuinely shaveable network charge — therefore rests on a methodology the regulator has said it intends to redesign, on an unknown timetable.
- Whether NESO will activate the 'Scenario 3b' stacking mode (allowing BM acceptances to erode Dynamic Containment provision, priced at the DC availability opportunity cost) is undecided; it is marked INACTIVE pending NESO satisfaction with stacking performance. Activation would materially increase BM revenue for response-contracted assets.
- Ofgem approved amendments to the Dynamic Response Services terms and conditions effective by 31 July 2026 (Ofgem decision, June 2026). I read the June 2026 Provider Guidance but did not read the amended Service Terms or Procurement Rules themselves, so specific commercial parameters (auction gate closure times, EFA block clock times, availability payment units) remain unverified against the contractual documents.
- Degradation modelling is genuinely contested: throughput-proportional cost (the common 2 $/MWh simplification) versus rainflow cycle-depth models that imply up to a 10x difference in degradation per unit throughput between shallow and deep cycles. The literature does not settle which is adequate for a GB peak-shaving duty cycle specifically, where cycles are deep, infrequent and highly regular. This materially affects optimal battery duration.
- The GB capital cost of 0.1–5 MW behind-the-meter C&I lithium-ion systems in £/kWh (2025/26) was not obtained from an authoritative source. The DESNZ/Carbon Trust Energy Innovation Needs Assessment: Energy Storage (June 2025) addresses 4–8 hour and longer-duration grid-scale technologies, not the C&I BTM segment. Seward et al.'s £445–1,315/kWh range for small-scale systems is a 2022 vintage and almost certainly stale.
- Typical savings-share percentage splits, availability guarantees and capacity-maintenance obligations in GB C&I energy-as-a-service contracts are not published in any source I could reach at better than vendor-marketing quality. Davis Wright Tremaine (Feb 2026) covers PPA and tolling structures for grid-scale BESS and defers performance provisions (degradation management, availability guarantees) to a promised follow-up.
- Whether a BTM battery at a Final Demand Site can be separately metered and registered so that only the storage sits behind a Non-Final Demand certificate — i.e. whether a second connection point or a dedicated storage MPAN pair can be created at an existing C&I site, and at what cost — is a live commercial question the charging statements do not answer directly, and it is potentially worth the entire fixed residual.
- How is P90 actually constructed for GB merchant storage revenue? No provider consulted publishes a probabilistic revenue distribution. Modo explicitly states its scenarios are worldviews, not percentiles; Cornwall Insight and Aurora publish forecasts without disclosed percentile methodology; and no GB lender term sheet in the public domain states its P90 definition. There is therefore no published GB P90/P50 ratio for merchant storage revenue. Anyone quoting one is either quoting a US convention or inventing it.
- What DSCR levels do GB lenders actually require for merchant versus floored storage? No authoritative GB source published a number during this survey. The only quantified DSCR sets found are US (SOFR/ITC-based) or generic renewables. GB deals disclose facility size, tenor and amortisation profile but not coverage ratios. This is the single largest evidential gap for a GB underwriting model.
- What is the actual GB gearing achieved on floored BESS? Immingham suggests roughly two-thirds against a Modo-derived capex, but neither the project cost nor the gearing was disclosed, so this is inference. Gresham House's fund-level 50%-of-NAV cap and Gore Street's 30%-of-GAV cap are fund policies, not project gearing.
- Does a behind-the-meter battery behind a customer's supply meter have any route to the final-consumption-levy exemptions? Modo states it does not. Whether a separately metered, separately settled BTM battery with its own MPAN and a licence (or class exemption) can obtain a non-final-demand declaration is not settled in the public sources consulted, and the answer is worth tens of pounds per MWh of charging.
- How is a behind-the-meter battery assessed for business rates - as a separate hereditament, or merged into the host's assessment, and who bears the cost? The VOA rating manual assigns a special category code to battery storage but publishes no scheme for BTM installations, and the 2026 revaluation resets values against April 2024 costs.
- What is the correct capex split for a sub-5 MW BTM installation? Every GB benchmark found (£580k/MW average, £650k/MW 2-hour median) is drawn from a grid-scale survey population averaging 1.9 hours, and the survey explicitly finds economies of scale. The scale penalty for 0.1-5 MW is not published.
- Is the merchant discount rate for GB BESS 8.75-9.50% (Gore Street) or 10.85-11.35% (Gresham House)? Both are 2025 GB observations from independently valued portfolios and they differ by 100-200bps, which is a large NPV difference on a 15-year asset. Neither publishes the derivation.
- What terminal or residual value, if any, is credited by GB lenders and valuers at the end of a 15-year BESS life? Modo assumes a 15-year project life, listed funds describe 25-year horizons, Ofgem's LDES framework recognises residual value as reducing the required floor, and Gore Street refers to 'updated battery cell costs for repowering'. There is no published convention.
- Whether the battery and its associated plant qualify for full expensing, the annual investment allowance, or special-rate pool treatment for UK capital allowances - this materially changes early-year cash tax and therefore DSCR-sized debt, and no authoritative source consulted addressed it.
- How much diversification benefit does a portfolio of BTM sites deliver on the revenue distribution? Since the dominant uncertainty is curve-level and therefore common to all sites, the benefit may be much smaller than a naive independence assumption implies, but no source quantifies the correlation.
- What is the observed distribution of GB BESS availability, and how do toll and floor agreements define and test the minimum availability target? Modo attributes ~5% to availability but publishes no distribution; Ofgem's LDES regime uses a Minimum Availability Target but for a different asset class.
- Whether current GB revenue-floor pricing of roughly £44,000/MW/yr for ten years is duration-specific, indexed, and available at 0.1-5 MW scale. Every observed floor is on a portfolio of tens to hundreds of MW with investment-grade counterparties; whether a single small BTM asset can access the same product at the same level is unknown.
- The authoritative bin-by-bin Capacity Market de-rating factor table for storage (0.5 h increments) for a NAMED recent auction could not be retrieved this session — only the indicative shape from 2023-vintage secondary reporting. The DESNZ auction parameters letter URL returned 404 and the NESO Electricity Capacity Report table was not obtainable in text form. Before any CM revenue is modelled, take the table directly from the NESO Electricity Capacity Report and the DESNZ-to-NESO auction parameters letter for the specific target auction, and re-derive the local De-rated MW per MWh slope rather than reusing a borrowed constant.
- Published DNO ANM/flexible-connection curtailment statistics as a DISTRIBUTION (per zone, per half hour, correlated with system conditions) were not obtainable. The queue principle is documented (Last In, First Out at NGED) and UKPN publishes a Curtailment Data Dashboard, but no headline curtailment percentage or its variance was extractable. This must be a site-specific DNO assessment obtained as a model input gate; there is no defensible literature constant.
- Whether the Ofgem Access SCR's 'clear curtailment limits and end-dates for non-firm access arrangements' have actually been implemented as contractually binding caps in current DNO flexible connection offers, and what those caps are numerically, could not be established. This determines whether curtailment is a bounded, contractible risk or an open-ended one.
- No source quantifies the marginal degradation shadow price (£/%SoH or £/MWh throughput) for a GB behind-the-meter duty cycle. The method is sound and citable (dual on the end-of-life constraint; Xu et al. degradation model; Shi et al. convexity result) but the NUMBER must be produced by the model and reported, not imported from literature. There is also no published guidance on how many fixed-point iterations are typically needed for the assumed and realised end-of-life dates to converge.
- A canonical, citable reference for the polygonal inner approximation of the inverter apparent-power constraint in a MILP context was not located. The construction itself is elementary and its error bound (1 - cos(pi/K)) is exact, so the requirement is implementable, but the specific literature attribution suggested by the panel (Akbari & Tavakoli Bina; Zhang, Gatsis & Giannakis) was not verified and should not be cited without checking.
- Whether a behind-the-meter storage installation can, in any configuration, obtain the DCUSA Non-Final Demand Site certificate and the Final Consumption Levy exemption is not settled by any source found. Industry analysis states BTM batteries are 'currently unlikely to qualify', but the precise test (Schedule 18 Single Site test) and whether a separately metered storage MPAN at an existing C&I connection satisfies it could not be confirmed from the primary code documents. This is the single highest-value unresolved question in this sweep — it is worth roughly £80/MWh on charging energy.
- DNO process, cost and lead time for issuing an additional MPAN at an existing connection, and which TCR band a new storage-only MPAN would fall into with what £/site/day residual, are not published in any comparable form. Must be obtained per DNO from a connections team, and a worked comparison built for at least one real site before the second-MPAN branch can be priced.
- Realised DFS clearing prices and acceptance rates under the post-9-April-2026 zonal, bi-directional design are not yet available — the service terms and procurement rules are new (v4.0 effective 9 April 2026; procurement rules v5.0 effective 18 May 2026) and no outturn analysis from Modo Energy or LCP Delta covering the new design was located. The DFS revenue line must therefore be modelled with an explicit and wide price distribution, and flagged as an emerging rather than established revenue stream. The Guaranteed Acceptance Price applies only to DFS Tests and 'may be zero'.
- The current contents of the DFS Stacking List — the document that actually determines whether DFS can be stacked with a Capacity Market agreement, with DC/DM/DR, or with DNO flexibility and ANM participation — were not retrieved. Everything about DFS revenue stacking is conditional on this list, and it 'may be modified by NESO from time to time'. It must be obtained and re-checked at each model refresh.
- Forced outage rates and mean time to repair DECOMPOSED BY CAUSE for behind-the-meter C&I batteries do not appear to exist in the public literature. Vendor fleet availability is published but is an annual MW-weighted scalar at utility scale with obvious selection bias, and IEEE work confirms that 'forced outage characteristics of ESS remain largely unexplored'. The two BTM-specific causes (host planned site shutdown, aggregator platform as a shared dependency) have no published statistics at all and must be elicited from the host's shutdown calendar and the optimiser's own incident history.
- The value of deferred GB Capacity Market payments during the 2018-2019 standstill (reported at the order of £1 billion) could not be confirmed from a primary source. The dates and the fact of suspension are firmly sourced; the magnitude is not.
- Whether the NFCC's grid-scale BESS guidance (30 m standoff, 25 L/s water) is applied by fire authorities and property insurers to sub-5 MW behind-the-meter installations, or whether a proportionate alternative is accepted, is unresolved. This is decisive for site feasibility on constrained C&I sites and can only be answered by early engagement with the specific FRS and the host's insurer.
- Actual C&I supply-contract volume tolerance bands and change-of-consumption reconciliation formulas are only available from broker and supplier commentary (indicating 80/120), not from published standard terms. Supplier standard terms were not obtainable. The executed contract is the only authoritative source and must be read.
- Whether Ofgem, a FiT licensee, or an RO generator would accept a DC-coupled battery configuration (where the battery sits on the PV's DC bus, upstream of the generation meter) as compliant with the single-net-calculating-meter rule is not addressed in the co-location guidance. This directly determines whether DC coupling is available to accredited sites, which in turn changes round-trip efficiency, clipping recapture and metering topology. Requires written confirmation from the licensee before financial close.

---
